SEVLF Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Scenario S1

A four-story steel-framed office building is planned for downtown Miami, Florida, a hurricane-prone coastal region. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II per ASCE 7-16 Figure 26.5-1B at this location is 170 mph. The site sits in open terrain immediately adjacent to Biscayne Bay, classified as Exposure Category C, at essentially sea level (Ke = 1.0), with no nearby hills or escarpments (Kzt = 1.0). The building is rectangular in plan, 120 ft long by 60 ft wide, with a flat roof and a mean roof height of 60 ft, so Kz at mean roof height (Exposure C) is 1.13. All exterior glazing is impact-resistant per the Florida Building Code, and the building has no dominant openings. The steel moment-frame lateral system is confirmed rigid (fundamental frequency above 1 Hz).

  1. The correct answer is B (71.1 pounds per square foot, the velocity pressure computed with all four required factors applied.): qh = 0.00256(1.13)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(170)^2 = 71.1 psf when every factor is applied correctly.
  2. The correct answer is D (0.85, the tabulated directionality factor for the main wind-force-resisting system of buildings.): Table 26.6-1 lists Kd = 0.85 for buildings, main wind-force-resisting system, which applies directly to this rectangular office building.
  3. The correct answer is A (Enclosed building, since the impact-resistant glazing need not be treated as an opening.): Because the glazing is impact-resistant, it is not assumed to fail, so the building satisfies the Enclosed building definition rather than Partially Enclosed.
  4. The correct answer is D (0.80, the constant windward wall coefficient given in Figure 27.3-1 for any L over B ratio.): Figure 27.3-1 fixes the windward wall coefficient at Cp = 0.8 for all buildings regardless of plan aspect ratio.
  5. The correct answer is B (-0.30, the coefficient tabulated in Figure 27.3-1 for an L over B ratio of 2.0.): Figure 27.3-1 lists Cp = -0.3 for the leeward wall when L over B equals 2.0, exactly matching this building's plan.
  6. The correct answer is C (Plus or minus 0.18, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.18 to enclosed buildings, which is the classification confirmed for this structure.
  7. The correct answer is D (61.2 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined external plus internal windward wall pressure.): p = 71.1(0.85)(0.8) - 71.1(-0.18) = 48.35 + 12.80 = 61.2 psf, combining the external pressure with the added suction from the negative internal pressure.
  8. The correct answer is A (0.85, the simplified gust-effect factor permitted for rigid buildings under Section 26.11.1.): Section 26.11.1 permits the simplified gust-effect factor of G = 0.85 for rigid buildings, which this steel moment frame satisfies.
  9. The correct answer is C (The zone extending a horizontal distance h from the windward edge, per Figure 27.3-1.): Figure 27.3-1 assigns the most negative Cp values to the zone within a horizontal distance h of the windward roof edge, making it critical.
  10. The correct answer is A (Chapter 27, Part 1 Directional Procedure remains applicable since it covers buildings of any height.): The Directional Procedure of Chapter 27 applies to enclosed and partially enclosed buildings of all heights, so it remains valid even though the building also qualifies as low-rise.
  11. The correct answer is D (Wind speed increases with height as surface friction and terrain roughness effects diminish.): Kz increases with height because the atmospheric boundary layer causes wind speed to rise as the flow moves farther from ground-level surface friction.
  12. The correct answer is B (53.5 pounds per square foot, the velocity pressure recalculated using the Exposure B value of Kz.): qh = 0.00256(0.85)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(170)^2 = 53.5 psf, confirming that switching to the less exposed Exposure B terrain lowers the velocity pressure.
Scenario S2

A two-story wood-frame multifamily residential building is being designed for a site in Denver, Colorado. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II is 115 mph, and the site is classified Exposure Category B, a suburban area with numerous closely spaced obstructions. The building measures 60 ft by 40 ft in plan, with a gable roof at a 6:12 pitch (approximately 26.6 degrees) and a mean roof height of 25 ft, giving an Exposure B velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 0.66 at that height and 0.62 at the 20 ft eave. The building is enclosed, with no wind-borne debris protection requirements since Denver is not a hurricane-prone region. The site sits atop an isolated 60 ft tall hill with a horizontal distance of 200 ft from the crest to the point of half height, so a topographic speed-up effect must be evaluated with a computed Kzt of 1.30 at the building location.

  1. The correct answer is C (19.0 pounds per square foot, the velocity pressure computed with Kzt taken as 1.0.): qh = 0.00256(0.66)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(115)^2 = 19.0 psf when the topographic multiplier is not yet applied.
  2. The correct answer is A (Kzt must be evaluated using Figure 26.8-1 because the isolated hill meets the topographic criteria.): Because the isolated hill satisfies the geometric criteria of Section 26.8.1, the multipliers K1, K2, and K3 from Figure 26.8-1 must be evaluated and combined into Kzt.
  3. The correct answer is D (24.7 pounds per square foot, the velocity pressure correctly including the topographic multiplier.): qh = 0.00256(0.66)(1.30)(0.85)(1.0)(115)^2 = 24.7 psf once the topographic factor of 1.30 is properly included in the formula.
  4. The correct answer is B (Windward roof pressure may be either net uplift or net inward pressure, found by interpolation in Figure 27.3-1.): Figure 27.3-1 shows that for slopes in this transitional range, the windward roof Cp is determined by linear interpolation and can be either negative or positive.
  5. The correct answer is D (0.85, the simplified gust-effect factor permitted for rigid buildings under Section 26.11.1.): Because the two-story wood-frame building is confirmed rigid, Section 26.11.1 permits the simplified gust-effect factor of 0.85.
  6. The correct answer is C (Enclosed building, since the structure satisfies the ASCE 7-16 enclosure criteria without triggering exceptions.): With no dominant openings and no wind-borne debris region triggered, the building satisfies the Enclosed building definition directly.
  7. The correct answer is A (-0.40, the value obtained by linearly interpolating between the L over B ratios of 1 and 2.): Interpolating linearly between -0.5 at L over B = 1 and -0.3 at L over B = 2 gives Cp = -0.4 at a ratio of 1.5.
  8. The correct answer is B (17.8 pounds per square foot, the velocity pressure correctly computed at the 20 ft eave height.): qz = 0.00256(0.62)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(115)^2 = 17.8 psf at the 20 ft eave, lower than qh because Kz decreases below mean roof height.
  9. The correct answer is D (Urban and suburban areas or terrain with numerous closely spaced obstructions the size of houses.): ASCE 7-16 defines Exposure B as urban and suburban areas, wooded areas, or terrain with numerous closely spaced obstructions of house-sized dimensions or larger.
  10. The correct answer is A (Plus or minus 0.18, the coefficient tabulated in Table 26.13-1 for enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.18 to enclosed buildings, consistent with the classification already confirmed for this structure.
  11. The correct answer is B (-11.1 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined net uplift pressure at the roof edge zone.): p = 19.0(0.85)(-0.9) - 19.0(-0.18) = -14.5 + 3.4 = -11.1 psf, the correctly combined external suction and added internal pressure at the roof edge.
  12. The correct answer is C (Chapter 28, Part 1, which contains the Envelope Procedure for qualifying low-rise buildings.): Chapter 28, Part 1 of ASCE 7-16 contains the Envelope Procedure, which is specifically written for enclosed and partially enclosed low-rise buildings like this one.
Scenario S3

A pre-engineered steel warehouse with a low-slope gable roof is planned for a rural site near Wichita, Kansas, at an elevation of approximately 1,200 ft above sea level. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II is 115 mph, and the flat, open surrounding farmland is classified Exposure Category C. The plan is 200 ft by 150 ft, with an eave height of 30 ft and a ridge height of 35 ft, giving a mean roof height of 35 ft and a velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 1.01. The site is flat with no hills or escarpments, so Kzt = 1.0. One 150 ft wide wall contains four roll-up loading doors, each 20 ft by 16 ft, which together create a large potential opening of 1,280 sq ft against that wall's gross area of 4,500 sq ft, equal to about 28 percent of the wall.

  1. The correct answer is D (29.1 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at mean roof height.): qh = 0.00256(1.01)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(115)^2 = 29.1 psf, applying all four required factors correctly.
  2. The correct answer is B (Partially enclosed building, since the opening ratio satisfies the partially enclosed criteria without reaching an open building.): A 28 percent opening on one wall, well below the 80 percent needed for an open building, but exceeding the partially enclosed thresholds, results in a partially enclosed classification.
  3. The correct answer is C (Plus or minus 0.55, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for partially enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.55 to partially enclosed buildings, matching the classification triggered by the open loading doors.
  4. The correct answer is B (35.8 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined windward wall pressure under the partially enclosed condition.): p = 29.1(0.85)(0.8) - 29.1(-0.55) = 19.8 + 16.0 = 35.8 psf, the combined external and added internal pressure under the partially enclosed classification.
  5. The correct answer is D (-0.43, the value obtained by interpolating between the tabulated ratios of 1.0 and 2.0.): Interpolating between -0.5 at L over B = 1 and -0.3 at L over B = 2 gives approximately -0.43 at a ratio of 1.33.
  6. The correct answer is A (Chapter 28, Part 1, the Envelope Procedure, which uses combined coefficients GCpf from Figure 28.3-1.): The Envelope Procedure in Chapter 28, Part 1 uses Figure 28.3-1 to provide combined external-plus-internal pressure coefficients GCpf directly, unlike the Directional Procedure.
  7. The correct answer is C (0.85, the same directionality factor listed for buildings under Table 26.6-1.): Table 26.6-1 lists Kd = 0.85 for buildings, which applies to both the main system and components and cladding elements such as wall panels.
  8. The correct answer is D (0.96, the tabulated ground elevation factor most appropriate near 1,200 ft elevation.): Table 26.9-1 gives Ke values that decrease slightly with elevation due to reduced air density, and approximately 0.96 corresponds to a site near 1,200 ft.
  9. The correct answer is B (27.9 pounds per square foot, the velocity pressure correctly adjusted for ground elevation.): Multiplying the sea-level qh of 29.1 psf by Ke = 0.96 gives 27.9 psf, reflecting the slightly reduced air density at this elevation.
  10. The correct answer is C (The corner zones, designated Zone 3, located within a distance a of each roof corner.): Figure 30.3-2A consistently assigns the most negative external pressure coefficients to the corner zones, Zone 3, located within the distance a of each roof corner.
  11. The correct answer is D (0.85, the simplified gust-effect factor permitted for confirmed rigid buildings under Section 26.11.1.): Because the warehouse is confirmed rigid, the simplified gust-effect factor of G = 0.85 applies under Section 26.11.1, avoiding the detailed resonant response calculation.
  12. The correct answer is A (The most severe combination of external and internal pressures must be checked, covering both enclosure conditions.): Because the doors can plausibly be either open or closed, the design must consider both enclosure conditions and use whichever combination produces the more severe pressures.
Scenario S4

A 40-story reinforced concrete office tower is proposed for downtown Chicago, Illinois. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II is 115 mph, and the surrounding dense urban terrain is classified Exposure Category B. The building has a square 100 ft by 100 ft plan, a flat roof, and a mean roof height of 400 ft, giving an Exposure B velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of approximately 1.47 at that height, with no topographic effects (Kzt = 1.0) and Ke = 1.0. Dynamic analysis shows a fundamental natural frequency of 0.20 Hz and a damping ratio of 1.5 percent of critical, values typical of reinforced concrete construction, so the building must be treated as dynamically sensitive. Curtain-wall glazing with no dominant openings confirms an enclosed classification, and Chicago is not a hurricane-prone region.

  1. The correct answer is A (42.3 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at the 400 ft mean roof height.): qh = 0.00256(1.47)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(115)^2 = 42.3 psf, correctly applying all four required factors at the great height of this tower.
  2. The correct answer is C (Flexible, since the fundamental natural frequency of 0.20 Hz is below the 1 Hz threshold.): ASCE 7-16 defines a flexible structure as one with a fundamental natural frequency below 1 Hz, and this tower's 0.20 Hz frequency places it in that category.
  3. The correct answer is B (Gf must be calculated using the detailed procedure of Section 26.11.4, accounting for resonant response.): For flexible structures, Section 26.11.4 requires the detailed gust-effect factor Gf calculation, which explicitly accounts for background and resonant response components.
  4. The correct answer is D (Chapter 27, the Directional Procedure only, since the Envelope Procedure is limited to buildings 60 ft or less.): Because the Envelope Procedure of Chapter 28 is limited to buildings with a mean roof height of 60 ft or less, only the Chapter 27 Directional Procedure applies to this 400 ft tower.
  5. The correct answer is A (Alpha equal to 7.0 and zg equal to 1,200 ft, the constants tabulated for Exposure B.): Table 26.11-1 pairs alpha = 7.0 with zg = 1,200 ft specifically for Exposure Category B, the terrain classification assigned to this urban Chicago site.
  6. The correct answer is D (Reinforced concrete buildings, for which a damping ratio near 1.5 percent is a typical default.): A damping ratio near 1.0 to 2.0 percent of critical, as used here, is a typical commentary default for reinforced concrete buildings such as this tower.
  7. The correct answer is C (-0.50, the tabulated coefficient for an L over B ratio of 1.0 or less.): Figure 27.3-1 assigns Cp = -0.5 to the leeward wall whenever L over B is 1.0 or less, matching this square tower's plan.
  8. The correct answer is A (0.80, the constant windward wall coefficient that applies to buildings of any height or flexibility.): Figure 27.3-1 fixes Cp = 0.8 for the windward wall regardless of plan aspect ratio or whether the structure is rigid or flexible.
  9. The correct answer is D (Enclosed building, since there are no dominant openings and no wind-borne debris requirement is triggered.): With curtain-wall glazing and no dominant openings, and no wind-borne debris region triggered in non-hurricane-prone Chicago, this tower satisfies the Enclosed building definition.
  10. The correct answer is B (Plus or minus 0.18, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for enclosed buildings of any height.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.18 to enclosed buildings without any height limitation, so the same value applies to this 400 ft tower.
  11. The correct answer is A (No, GCpi from Table 26.13-1 applies regardless of rigidity; only the external gust factor differs with flexibility.): Internal pressure coefficients in Table 26.13-1 depend only on enclosure classification, not on structural rigidity, so GCpi stays at ±0.18 while only the external gust factor changes to Gf.
  12. The correct answer is C (46.5 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined windward wall pressure using the flexible gust factor.): p = 42.3(1.15)(0.8) - 42.3(-0.18) = 38.9 + 7.6 = 46.5 psf, correctly using the calculated flexible gust-effect factor Gf rather than the rigid default.
Scenario S5

An open-sided beachfront picnic pavilion is proposed for a site in the Florida Keys, directly adjacent to open water. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II at this hurricane-prone location is 180 mph, and the site is classified Exposure Category D due to the unobstructed open-water fetch. The pavilion has a low-slope free gable roof supported entirely on columns, with no walls at all, an eave height of 20 ft, and a roof span of 30 ft, giving a clear height-to-span ratio h over L of 0.67. Because the structure has no walls, it is classified as an open building. The site sits at essentially sea level, and this ordinary recreational structure without an emergency function is assigned Risk Category II.

  1. The correct answer is B (76.1 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at the 20 ft eave height.): qh = 0.00256(1.08)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(180)^2 = 76.1 psf, correctly applying all four required factors for this Exposure D beachfront site.
  2. The correct answer is D (0.00, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for open buildings, since pressure equalizes freely.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = 0.00 to open buildings because the complete absence of walls allows internal and external pressures to equalize freely.
  3. The correct answer is C (The applicable free-roof figure in Chapter 27, which gives net pressure coefficients Cn for open roofs.): ASCE 7-16 provides dedicated free-roof figures giving net pressure coefficients Cn for open, free-standing roof structures, eliminating the need for a separate internal pressure term.
  4. The correct answer is A (Exposure D, the category defined for flat, unobstructed terrain facing large bodies of open water.): ASCE 7-16 defines Exposure D as flat, unobstructed terrain facing large bodies of open water, which precisely matches this beachfront pavilion site.
  5. The correct answer is A (0.85, the tabulated directionality factor listed for the main system of buildings under Table 26.6-1.): Table 26.6-1 lists Kd = 0.85 for buildings, main wind-force-resisting system, and this applies to the open pavilion's supporting frame in the same manner as an enclosed building.
  6. The correct answer is D (77.6 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed net design pressure on the free roof surface.): p = 76.1(0.85)(1.2) = 77.6 psf, correctly combining the velocity pressure, gust-effect factor, and net pressure coefficient for this free roof.
  7. The correct answer is A (1.00, the tabulated ground elevation factor appropriate at or near sea level.): Table 26.9-1 assigns Ke = 1.00 at or near sea level, which is appropriate for this beachfront pavilion site at approximately 5 ft elevation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Risk Category II, the standard category for ordinary structures not fitting Categories I, III, or IV.): As an ordinary recreational structure not meeting the criteria for Risk Categories I, III, or IV, this pavilion falls into the default Risk Category II.
  9. The correct answer is B (The windward and leeward wall pressure coefficients, since there are no walls to generate wall pressures.): Because the pavilion has no walls at all, the windward and leeward wall pressure coefficients of Figure 27.3-1 simply do not apply to its design.
  10. The correct answer is A (Edge and corner zones of the free roof, similar in concept to components and cladding zoning.): Free-roof pressure coefficients, like components and cladding coefficients on enclosed roofs, are generally most severe at the edge and corner zones of the roof.
  11. The correct answer is C (The clear height-to-horizontal-dimension ratio used to enter the applicable free-roof pressure coefficient figure.): The clear height-to-span ratio h over L is the specific geometric parameter used to select the net pressure coefficient Cn from the applicable free-roof figure.
  12. The correct answer is D (-71.2 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed net uplift pressure on the free roof surface.): p = 76.1(0.85)(-1.1) = -71.2 psf, correctly combining the velocity pressure, gust-effect factor, and net uplift coefficient for this free roof.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

Scenario S6

A single-story reinforced masonry K-12 school building with a high-bay gymnasium and a two-story classroom wing is planned for a suburban site. The basic wind speed for Risk Category IV, applicable because the school has an occupant capacity above 250 students and also serves as a community emergency shelter, is 120 mph. The surrounding open terrain is classified Exposure Category C, with a mean roof height of 30 ft and a velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 0.98. The site sits near the top of a 2:1 escarpment, triggering a computed topographic factor Kzt of 1.15. The building plan measures 150 ft by 80 ft with a low-slope roof, the lateral system uses reinforced concrete masonry bearing walls, and Ke is taken as 1.0.

  1. The correct answer is C (35.3 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure including the topographic factor.): qh = 0.00256(0.98)(1.15)(0.85)(1.0)(120)^2 = 35.3 psf, correctly including the topographic speed-up effect from the nearby escarpment.
  2. The correct answer is B (Risk Category IV, reserved for essential facilities including emergency shelters and larger schools.): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-1 assigns Risk Category IV to designated emergency shelters. The occupant-load threshold for schools by itself only reaches Risk Category III; it is this building's designation as a community emergency shelter that raises it to Risk Category IV.
  3. The correct answer is A (Each Risk Category has its own wind speed map in Chapter 26, so V already reflects Risk Category.): ASCE 7-16 provides three basic wind speed maps grouped by Risk Category (Figure 26.5-1A for Risk Categories III and IV, Figure 26.5-1B for Risk Category II, and Figure 26.5-1C for Risk Category I), so the basic wind speed already reflects the assigned Risk Category grouping without a separate importance-factor multiplier.
  4. The correct answer is A (Figure 26.5-1A, shared with Risk Category III, since ASCE 7-16 groups those two categories on one map.): ASCE 7-16 provides only three basic wind speed maps. Figure 26.5-1A covers both Risk Category III and Risk Category IV structures on the same map, Figure 26.5-1B covers Risk Category II, and Figure 26.5-1C covers Risk Category I. There is no separate Figure 26.5-1D.
  5. The correct answer is B (It combines external and internal pressures into a single GCpf coefficient, avoiding a separate GCpi selection.): The Envelope Procedure's key simplification is combining external and internal pressure effects into one coefficient GCpf, removing the need to separately select an internal pressure coefficient.
  6. The correct answer is A (-0.32, the value obtained by interpolating between the tabulated ratios of 1.0 and 2.0.): Interpolating between -0.5 at L over B = 1 and -0.3 at L over B = 2 gives approximately -0.32 at a ratio of 1.9.
  7. The correct answer is A (Simultaneous axial gravity load combined with out-of-plane bending, including slenderness effects, from wind pressure.): TMS 402/602-16 requires masonry bearing walls to be checked for the combined effect of axial gravity load and out-of-plane bending, including slenderness, under wind pressure.
  8. The correct answer is D (Plus or minus 0.18, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.18 to enclosed buildings, which is the classification confirmed for this school.
  9. The correct answer is A (30.4 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined windward wall pressure including the topographic effect.): p = 35.3(0.85)(0.8) - 35.3(-0.18) = 24.0 + 6.4 = 30.4 psf, correctly combining the topographic-adjusted external and internal pressures on the windward wall.
  10. The correct answer is D (Hill height H, upwind distance to half height Lh, horizontal distance x, and height above ground z.): Figure 26.8-1 derives the multipliers K1, K2, and K3 from the hill or escarpment height H, the half-height distance Lh, the horizontal distance x from the crest, and the height z above ground.
  11. The correct answer is B (The feature must be isolated from similar terrain features and have an H over Lh ratio of 0.2 or greater.): Section 26.8.1 requires the topographic feature to be sufficiently isolated from similar terrain features and to have a height-to-half-height ratio of at least 0.2 before Kzt exceeds 1.0.
  12. The correct answer is C (15 percent, the percentage increase directly corresponding to the topographic multiplier of 1.15.): Because qh scales linearly with Kzt, a multiplier of 1.15 produces exactly a 15 percent increase in velocity pressure, matching (35.3 minus 30.7) divided by 30.7.
Scenario S7

An existing five-story steel office building is being evaluated for a new rooftop mechanical unit installation. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II is 130 mph, the urban site is classified Exposure Category C, and the flat roof has a mean roof height of 65 ft with a velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 1.15, no topographic effects (Kzt = 1.0), Ke = 1.0, and Kd = 0.85. The roof has a 4 ft tall parapet at its perimeter. The new HVAC unit measures 8 ft by 8 ft in plan and 6 ft tall, and it is located near the roof edge, within the edge zone rather than the interior field zone of the roof, on this enclosed office building.

  1. The correct answer is D (42.3 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at the 65 ft mean roof height.): qh = 0.00256(1.15)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(130)^2 = 42.3 psf, correctly applying all four required factors at this rooftop elevation.
  2. The correct answer is A (It increases negative uplift pressures near the roof edge due to flow separation and vortex effects.): A parapet at the roof edge alters the airflow separation pattern and generally increases negative uplift pressures near the edge compared to a roof without one.
  3. The correct answer is B (The rooftop equipment force coefficient provisions, using Cf based on the unit's exposed shape and roof location.): ASCE 7-16 provides dedicated force coefficient provisions for rooftop-mounted equipment, using Cf values that depend on the unit's exposed shape and its location on the roof.
  4. The correct answer is C (2,420 pounds, the correctly computed horizontal design force on the rooftop unit.): F = 42.3(0.85)(1.4)(48) = 2,420 pounds, correctly combining the velocity pressure, gust-effect factor, force coefficient, and frontal area.
  5. The correct answer is D (Yes, equipment near roof edges experiences greater wind speed-up and turbulence, warranting increased coefficients.): Rooftop equipment located within the roof's edge or corner zones experiences greater local wind speed-up and turbulence than equipment in the interior field, warranting increased design coefficients.
  6. The correct answer is B (Zone 2 edge zone or Zone 3 corner zone values, whichever applies, both more severe than Zone 1.): Because the unit sits within the roof's edge or corner zone rather than the interior field, the more severe Zone 2 or Zone 3 pressure coefficients govern the surrounding roof deck design.
  7. The correct answer is A (Plus or minus 0.18, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.18 to enclosed buildings, which applies here for the roof deck components and cladding design.
  8. The correct answer is C (GCp magnitude generally decreases as effective wind area increases, per the log-linear reduction curves.): ASCE 7-16 components and cladding figures use log-linear reduction curves where GCp magnitude generally decreases as the effective wind area of the element increases.
  9. The correct answer is B (0.85, the same directionality factor listed for buildings and their components under Table 26.6-1.): Table 26.6-1 lists Kd = 0.85 for buildings, and rooftop equipment mounted on a building uses this same directionality factor.
  10. The correct answer is C (1.00, the default topographic factor for a flat site with no nearby hills or escarpments.): With no hills, ridges, or escarpments near this flat urban rooftop site, the default topographic factor Kzt = 1.0 applies.
  11. The correct answer is A (0.6, the reduction factor typically applied to strength-level wind force for ASD load combinations.): Section 2.4 load combinations apply a factor of 0.6 to the strength-level wind force to obtain the corresponding allowable stress design wind force.
  12. The correct answer is D (1,450 pounds, the correctly reduced allowable stress design wind force on the anchorage.): 1,450 pounds equals 2,420 pounds multiplied by the ASD reduction factor of 0.6, correctly converting the strength-level force to the allowable stress design level.
Scenario S8

An eight-story acute-care hospital building is planned for a coastal site near Charleston, South Carolina. Because the facility includes surgery and emergency care functions, it is assigned Risk Category IV, with a basic wind speed of 150 mph from the Risk Category IV wind speed map. The site sits immediately adjacent to open tidal marsh and water, classified Exposure Category D, with a mean roof height of 90 ft and a velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 1.40, no topographic effects (Kzt = 1.0), and Ke = 1.0. The flat-roofed building measures 140 ft by 90 ft in plan, and impact-resistant glazing is installed throughout to satisfy wind-borne debris protection requirements in this hurricane-prone coastal region.

  1. The correct answer is A (68.5 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at the 90 ft mean roof height.): qh = 0.00256(1.40)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(150)^2 = 68.5 psf, correctly applying all four required factors for this Exposure D coastal site.
  2. The correct answer is D (Risk Category IV, reserved for essential facilities such as hospitals with surgery and emergency capabilities.): Table 1.5-1 assigns Risk Category IV to essential facilities, explicitly including hospitals with surgery or emergency care capability, matching this coastal hospital.
  3. The correct answer is C (Figure 26.5-1D, the map developed specifically for Risk Category IV structures, showing higher contours here.): ASCE 7-16 assigns Risk Category IV structures their own dedicated wind speed map, Figure 26.5-1D, which shows higher contours than the Risk Category II map at this coastal site.
  4. The correct answer is B (Enclosed building, since the impact-resistant glazing need not be treated as a failed opening.): Because the glazing is impact-resistant and satisfies the wind-borne debris protection requirement, it is not assumed to fail, so the hospital qualifies as an Enclosed building.
  5. The correct answer is A (Section 26.12.3, the wind-borne debris and glazed opening protection provision.): Section 26.12.3 addresses wind-borne debris regions and requires unprotected, non-impact-resistant glazing to be treated as a failed opening for enclosure classification purposes.
  6. The correct answer is A (Plus or minus 0.18, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.18 to enclosed buildings, consistent with the classification confirmed by the impact-resistant glazing throughout.
  7. The correct answer is D (-0.39, the value obtained by interpolating between the tabulated ratios of 1.0 and 2.0.): Interpolating between -0.5 at L over B = 1 and -0.3 at L over B = 2 gives approximately -0.39 at a ratio of 1.56.
  8. The correct answer is B (58.9 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined windward wall net design pressure.): p = 68.5(0.85)(0.8) - 68.5(-0.18) = 46.6 + 12.3 = 58.9 psf, the correctly combined external and internal windward wall pressure for this coastal hospital.
  9. The correct answer is C (The higher V provides a lower probability of exceedance for essential facilities needing to remain functional.): The dedicated Risk Category IV wind speed map assigns a higher V corresponding to a longer mean recurrence interval, reflecting the need for essential facilities to remain functional after a design event.
  10. The correct answer is B (Exposure D, the category defined for flat, unobstructed terrain facing large bodies of open water.): ASCE 7-16 defines Exposure D as flat, unobstructed terrain facing large open water bodies, which matches this hospital site adjacent to tidal marsh and open water.
  11. The correct answer is D (-40.1 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined net roof uplift pressure at the windward edge zone.): p = 68.5(0.85)(-0.9) - 68.5(-0.18) = -52.4 + 12.3 = -40.1 psf, the correctly combined external suction and added internal pressure at the roof edge zone.
  12. The correct answer is A (Chapter 27, the Directional Procedure, which applies to enclosed buildings of any height.): Because the Envelope Procedure of Chapter 28 is limited to buildings 60 ft or less in mean roof height, only the Chapter 27 Directional Procedure applies to this 90 ft hospital.
Scenario S9

A single-story steel-framed big-box retail store is planned for a Gulf Coast site in Texas, located within one mile of the coastline. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II here is 145 mph, placing the site within a wind-borne debris region regardless of its exact distance from the coast. The surrounding flat, open terrain is classified Exposure Category C, with a mean roof height of 32 ft and a velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 0.99, no topographic effects (Kzt = 1.0), and Ke = 1.0. The plan measures 250 ft by 180 ft with a flat roof. The storefront uses standard, non-impact-resistant glazing covering approximately 3,000 sq ft, which must be assumed to fail during the design wind event under ASCE 7-16 wind-borne debris provisions.

  1. The correct answer is B (45.3 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at mean roof height.): qh = 0.00256(0.99)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(145)^2 = 45.3 psf, correctly applying all four required factors at this wind-borne debris region site.
  2. The correct answer is C (The glazing must be assumed to fail as an opening unless it is impact-resistant or protected.): Section 26.12.3 requires unprotected, non-impact-resistant glazing in a wind-borne debris region to be treated as a failed opening for enclosure classification purposes.
  3. The correct answer is D (Partially enclosed building, based on the opening ratio created by the failed storefront glazing.): The failed glazing creates an opening large enough to satisfy the partially enclosed criteria without reaching the 80 percent threshold required for an open building.
  4. The correct answer is A (Plus or minus 0.55, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for partially enclosed buildings.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = ±0.55 to partially enclosed buildings, matching the classification triggered by the failed storefront glazing.
  5. The correct answer is D (55.7 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined windward wall pressure under the partially enclosed condition.): p = 45.3(0.85)(0.8) - 45.3(-0.55) = 30.8 + 24.9 = 55.7 psf, the combined external and added internal pressure under this partially enclosed classification.
  6. The correct answer is A (43 percent, the approximate percentage increase caused by the partially enclosed classification.): The increase from 39.0 psf to 55.7 psf represents approximately a 43 percent rise, directly attributable to the change from enclosed to partially enclosed classification.
  7. The correct answer is B (-0.42, the value obtained by interpolating between the tabulated ratios of 1.0 and 2.0.): Interpolating between -0.5 at L over B = 1 and -0.3 at L over B = 2 gives approximately -0.42 at a ratio of 1.39.
  8. The correct answer is C (Installing impact-resistant glazing or approved shutters on the storefront per Section 26.12.3.): Installing impact-resistant glazing or approved impact-resistant shutters on the storefront would satisfy Section 26.12.3, allowing the glazing to be treated as intact rather than a failed opening.
  9. The correct answer is A (1.00, the default topographic factor for a flat site with no nearby hills or escarpments.): With no hills, ridges, or escarpments near this flat Gulf Coast site, the default topographic factor Kzt = 1.0 applies to the wind load calculation.
  10. The correct answer is C (-65.7 pounds per square foot, the correctly combined net roof corner pressure at this location.): p = 45.3[(-2.0) - (-0.55)] = 45.3(-1.45) = -65.7 psf, correctly combining the corner zone external coefficient with the partially enclosed internal pressure coefficient.
  11. The correct answer is D (Yes, it satisfies both low-rise criteria, so Chapter 28 may be used as an alternative to Chapter 27.): Because h equals 32 ft, which is under 60 ft and less than the least horizontal dimension of 180 ft, the store satisfies both low-rise criteria and may use either analytical procedure.
  12. The correct answer is B (1.00, the tabulated ground elevation factor appropriate at or near sea level.): Table 26.9-1 assigns Ke = 1.00 at or near sea level, which is appropriate for this Gulf Coast retail store site.
Scenario S10

A four-level open precast concrete parking garage is planned for a suburban Atlanta, Georgia site. The basic wind speed for Risk Category II is 125 mph, and the surrounding suburban terrain with numerous closely spaced obstructions is classified Exposure Category B, with a mean roof height of 45 ft and a velocity pressure exposure coefficient Kz of 0.79, no topographic effects (Kzt = 1.0), and Ke = 1.0. Each level has architectural screen walls covering only about 20 percent of the perimeter wall area, leaving the structure at least 80 percent open on every side, so it qualifies as an open building. The lateral system consists of precast concrete moment and shear elements, and the roof deck is a flat, open top level.

  1. The correct answer is C (26.9 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed velocity pressure at the 45 ft mean roof height.): qh = 0.00256(0.79)(1.0)(0.85)(1.0)(125)^2 = 26.9 psf, correctly applying all four required factors for this suburban Exposure B site.
  2. The correct answer is D (Open building, since each wall is at least 80 percent open per the ASCE 7-16 definition.): ASCE 7-16 defines an open building as one having each wall at least 80 percent open, which this garage satisfies with only 20 percent screen wall coverage per level.
  3. The correct answer is A (0.00, the tabulated internal pressure coefficient for open buildings, since pressure equalizes freely.): Table 26.13-1 assigns GCpi = 0.00 to open buildings, consistent with the classification confirmed for this largely open parking garage.
  4. The correct answer is B (Force coefficient Cf provisions for open structures, applying the actual solidity ratio of the screen walls.): ASCE 7-16 provisions for open and lattice-type structures use force coefficients Cf that account directly for the actual solidity ratio of any partial-height screen or perimeter walls.
  5. The correct answer is C (10,300 pounds, the correctly computed design force on the windward screen wall.): F = 26.9(0.85)(1.0)(450) = 10,300 pounds, correctly combining the velocity pressure, gust-effect factor, force coefficient, and effective solid screen wall area.
  6. The correct answer is A (Yes, open or partially solid perimeter walls transmit substantially less wind force than a fully enclosed wall.): Because the perimeter walls are only 20 percent solid, they transmit substantially less wind force to the precast concrete lateral system than an equivalent fully enclosed wall would.
  7. The correct answer is D (Exposure B, the category defined for suburban terrain with numerous closely spaced obstructions.): ASCE 7-16 defines Exposure B as suburban or urban terrain with numerous closely spaced obstructions, matching this Atlanta site's residential and wooded surroundings.
  8. The correct answer is B (1.00, the default topographic factor for a suburban site with no nearby hills or escarpments.): With no hills, ridges, or escarpments near this suburban site, the default topographic factor Kzt = 1.0 applies to the wind load calculation.
  9. The correct answer is D (-20.6 pounds per square foot, the correctly computed net roof uplift pressure on this open building's deck.): p = 26.9(0.85)(-0.9) - 26.9(0.00) = -20.6 psf, the correctly combined pressure with no internal pressure contribution since this is an open building.
  10. The correct answer is B (The internal pressure term drops out entirely, leaving the net pressure equal to the external term alone.): Because GCpi equals 0.00 for open buildings, the internal pressure term drops out entirely, leaving the net design pressure equal to the external pressure term alone.
  11. The correct answer is A (0.85, the tabulated directionality factor listed for buildings, including open building frames, under Table 26.6-1.): Table 26.6-1 lists Kd = 0.85 for buildings, main wind-force-resisting system, which applies to the precast concrete frame of this open parking structure.
  12. The correct answer is C (34 percent, the approximate percentage increase in qh from switching Exposure B to Exposure C.): Recalculating qh with Kz = 1.06 gives approximately 36.0 psf, an increase of about 34 percent over the original Exposure B value of 26.9 psf.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

Scenario S11

A developer is designing a 4-story steel special moment frame office building in coastal Southern California. The structure has a roof height of 60 ft, is assigned Risk Category II, and sits on a Site Class D profile. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 1.50g and S1 = 0.60g. The effective seismic weight of the building is 3,600 kips.

  1. The correct answer is D (Fa = 1.0, the Table 11.4-1 value for Site Class D once Ss reaches 1.25g or higher (flattened upper-bound value).): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 gives Fa = 1.0 for Site Class D at any mapped Ss of 1.25g or greater, including this mapped value Ss = 1.50g, since the table stops varying above that upper bound. Fa is the short-period site coefficient used only to compute SMS, and must never be confused with Fv, which is the long-period coefficient used with S1 to compute SM1.
  2. The correct answer is B (Fv = 1.7, the Table 11.4-2 value for Site Class D at this S1.): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 gives Fv = 1.7 for Site Class D at the mapped value S1 = 0.60g (the table's S1 >= 0.6g row). Fv is the long-period site coefficient used only to compute SM1, and softer soil profiles amplify long-period motion more strongly than stiffer profiles at the same mapped S1.
  3. The correct answer is C (SDS = 1.000g, two-thirds of SMS = Fa times Ss for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-3, SMS = Fa times Ss = 1.0 times 1.50 = 1.500g, and SDS = two-thirds of SMS = 1.000g. Skipping the two-thirds reduction, or substituting Fv for Fa, both produce incorrect design values.
  4. The correct answer is A (SD1 = 0.680g, two-thirds of SM1 = Fv times S1 for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-4, SM1 = Fv times S1 = 1.7 times 0.60 = 1.020g, and SD1 = two-thirds of SM1 = 0.680g. Confusing SD1 with SDS, or substituting Fa for Fv, leads to an incorrect long-period design parameter.
  5. The correct answer is A (Seismic Design Category D, based on Table 11.6-1 and SDS = 1.000g alone.): With SDS = 1.000g and Risk Category II, ASCE 7-16 Table 11.6-1 places this structure in Seismic Design Category D. Table 11.6-1 uses SDS ranges only, and the boundaries shift for Risk Category IV compared with Risk Categories I through III.
  6. The correct answer is D (Seismic Design Category D, the more severe (or overriding) result of both tables.): ASCE 7-16 Section 11.6 requires that the governing Seismic Design Category be the more severe result of Table 11.6-1 (based on SDS = 1.000g) and Table 11.6-2 (based on SD1 = 0.680g). Both tables place this Risk Category II structure in Seismic Design Category D, and since S1 = 0.60g is below the 0.75g automatic Category E threshold, D governs for this structure.
  7. The correct answer is C (Ie = 1.00, the Table 1.5-2 value for Risk Category II.): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 assigns Ie = 1.00 to Risk Category II structures. Ie increases with the consequences of failure, ranging from 1.0 for Risk Categories I and II up to 1.5 for essential facilities in Risk Category IV.
  8. The correct answer is D (R = 8, the Table 12.2-1 value for this seismic force-resisting system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 8 to this seismic force-resisting system. Selecting the R value for a different system category, such as a moment frame in place of a braced frame, would produce an incorrect and non-conservative base shear.
  9. The correct answer is B (Cd = 5.50, the Table 12.2-1 deflection amplification factor for this system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 5.50 to this seismic force-resisting system. Cd amplifies the elastic drift computed under strength-level seismic forces to estimate the true inelastic design story drift used in the drift check.
  10. The correct answer is A (rho = 1.30, per Section 12.3.4 for this Seismic Design Category.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 requires rho = 1.3 for structures in Seismic Design Category D unless specific redundancy conditions on the number and arrangement of seismic force-resisting elements are demonstrated, which are not established for this structure, so the default value of 1.3 governs.
  11. The correct answer is C (Ta = 0.740 seconds, using the Ct and x values for this system type.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.8-2 gives the Ct and x coefficients specific to this seismic force-resisting system, and applying Ta = Ct times hn to the power x with hn = 60 ft correctly yields Ta = 0.740 seconds. Using coefficients for a different system, or dropping the exponent, produces a materially different and incorrect period.
  12. The correct answer is C (V = 414 kips, using the governing Cs value with W = 3600 kips.): The initial Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 1.000/8 = 0.125, but the period-based maximum of Section 12.8.1.1, SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.680/(0.740 x 8) = 0.1149, is smaller and governs. Applying Cs = 0.1149 to W = 3600 kips gives V = 414 kips for this structure.
Scenario S12

A regional hospital is planning a new 3-story special reinforced concrete moment frame patient tower assigned Risk Category IV. The site is classified as Site Class C with mapped spectral accelerations Ss = 1.00g and S1 = 0.40g. The roof height above grade is 42 ft, and the total effective seismic weight of the structure is 4,500 kips.

  1. The correct answer is D (Fa = 1.2, the Table 11.4-1 value for Site Class C at this Ss.): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 gives Fa = 1.2 for Site Class C at the mapped value Ss = 1.00g. Fa is the short-period site coefficient used only to compute SMS, and must never be confused with Fv, which is the long-period coefficient used with S1 to compute SM1.
  2. The correct answer is A (Fv = 1.5, the Table 11.4-2 value for Site Class C at this S1.): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 gives Fv = 1.5 for Site Class C at the mapped value S1 = 0.40g. Fv is the long-period site coefficient used only to compute SM1, and softer soil profiles amplify long-period motion more strongly than stiffer profiles at the same mapped S1.
  3. The correct answer is B (SDS = 0.800g, two-thirds of SMS = Fa times Ss for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-3, SMS = Fa times Ss = 1.2 times 1.00 = 1.200g, and SDS = two-thirds of SMS = 0.800g. Skipping the two-thirds reduction, or substituting Fv for Fa, both produce incorrect design values.
  4. The correct answer is A (SD1 = 0.400g, two-thirds of SM1 = Fv times S1 for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-4, SM1 = Fv times S1 = 1.5 times 0.40 = 0.600g, and SD1 = two-thirds of SM1 = 0.400g. Confusing SD1 with SDS, or substituting Fa for Fv, leads to an incorrect long-period design parameter.
  5. The correct answer is D (Seismic Design Category D, based on Table 11.6-1 and SDS = 0.800g alone.): With SDS = 0.800g and Risk Category IV, ASCE 7-16 Table 11.6-1 places this structure in Seismic Design Category D. Table 11.6-1 uses SDS ranges only, and the boundaries shift for Risk Category IV compared with Risk Categories I through III.
  6. The correct answer is D (Seismic Design Category D, the more severe (or overriding) result of both tables.): ASCE 7-16 Section 11.6 requires that the governing Seismic Design Category be the more severe result of Table 11.6-1 (based on SDS = 0.800g) and Table 11.6-2 (based on SD1 = 0.400g). Here the SD1-based and SDS-based results are reconciled to govern at Seismic Design Category D for this structure.
  7. The correct answer is B (Ie = 1.50, the Table 1.5-2 value for Risk Category IV.): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 assigns Ie = 1.50 to Risk Category IV structures. Ie increases with the consequences of failure, ranging from 1.0 for Risk Categories I and II up to 1.5 for essential facilities in Risk Category IV.
  8. The correct answer is A (R = 8, the Table 12.2-1 value for this seismic force-resisting system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 8 to this seismic force-resisting system. Selecting the R value for a different system category, such as a moment frame in place of a braced frame, would produce an incorrect and non-conservative base shear.
  9. The correct answer is A (Omega0 = 3, the Table 12.2-1 overstrength factor for this system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 3 to this seismic force-resisting system. Omega0 amplifies seismic forces delivered to elements such as collectors and connections that must remain elastic while the主 system yields.
  10. The correct answer is B (rho = 1.30, per Section 12.3.4 for this Seismic Design Category.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 requires rho = 1.3 for structures in Seismic Design Category D unless specific redundancy conditions on the number and arrangement of seismic force-resisting elements are demonstrated, which are not established for this structure, so the default value of 1.3 governs.
  11. The correct answer is D (Ta = 0.462 seconds, using the Ct and x values for this system type.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.8-2 gives the Ct and x coefficients specific to this seismic force-resisting system, and applying Ta = Ct times hn to the power x with hn = 42 ft correctly yields Ta = 0.462 seconds. Using coefficients for a different system, or dropping the exponent, produces a materially different and incorrect period.
  12. The correct answer is A (V = 675 kips, using the governing Cs value with W = 4500 kips.): After computing the initial Cs = SDS/(R/Ie), checking it against the period-based maximum limit of Section 12.8.1.1, and confirming it exceeds the applicable minimum Cs, the governing seismic response coefficient applied to W = 4500 kips gives V = 675 kips for this structure.
Scenario S13

A 2-story wood-frame apartment building uses light-frame shear walls sheathed with wood structural panels as its seismic force-resisting system. Cd amplifies the elastic drift computed under strength-level seismic forces to estimate the true inelastic design story drift used in the drift check.
  10. The correct answer is B (k = 1.00, per the Section 12.8.3 rule for this period Ta.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.3 sets k = 1.0 for any structure with a period Ta of 0.5 seconds or less. Since Ta = 0.189 seconds is at or below that limit, the vertical force distribution is linear with height and k = 1.0 governs here.
  11. The correct answer is B (Ta = 0.189 seconds, using the Ct and x values for this system type.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.8-2 gives the Ct and x coefficients specific to this seismic force-resisting system, and applying Ta = Ct times hn to the power x with hn = 20 ft correctly yields Ta = 0.189 seconds. Scenario S15

A single-story reinforced masonry warehouse uses ordinary reinforced masonry shear walls for lateral resistance. The project is assigned Risk Category II, sits on Site Class D soil, and is located where Ss = 0.25g and S1 = 0.10g. The roof is 20 ft above grade, and the effective seismic weight of the structure is 1,200 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (Fa = 1.6, the Table 11.4-1 value for Site Class D at this Ss.): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 gives Fa = 1.6 for Site Class D at the mapped value Ss = 0.25g. Fa is the short-period site coefficient used only to compute SMS, and must never be confused with Fv, which is the long-period coefficient used with S1 to compute SM1.
  2. The correct answer is B (Fv = 2.4, the Table 11.4-2 value for Site Class D at this S1.): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 gives Fv = 2.4 for Site Class D at the mapped value S1 = 0.10g. Fv is the long-period site coefficient used only to compute SM1, and softer soil profiles amplify long-period motion more strongly than stiffer profiles at the same mapped S1.
  3. The correct answer is C (SDS = 0.267g, two-thirds of SMS = Fa times Ss for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-3, SMS = Fa times Ss = 1.6 times 0.25 = 0.400g, and SDS = two-thirds of SMS = 0.267g. Skipping the two-thirds reduction, or substituting Fv for Fa, both produce incorrect design values.
  4. The correct answer is C (SD1 = 0.160g, two-thirds of SM1 = Fv times S1 for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-4, SM1 = Fv times S1 = 2.4 times 0.10 = 0.240g, and SD1 = two-thirds of SM1 = 0.160g. Confusing SD1 with SDS, or substituting Fa for Fv, leads to an incorrect long-period design parameter.
  5. The correct answer is A (Seismic Design Category B, based on Table 11.6-1 and SDS = 0.267g alone.): With SDS = 0.267g and Risk Category II, ASCE 7-16 Table 11.6-1 places this structure in Seismic Design Category B. Table 11.6-1 uses SDS ranges only, and the boundaries shift for Risk Category IV compared with Risk Categories I through III.
  6. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Category C, the more severe (or overriding) result of both tables.): ASCE 7-16 Section 11.6 requires that the governing Seismic Design Category be the more severe result of Table 11.6-1 (based on SDS = 0.267g) and Table 11.6-2 (based on SD1 = 0.160g). Here the SD1-based and SDS-based results are reconciled to govern at Seismic Design Category C for this structure.
  7. The correct answer is D (Ie = 1.00, the Table 1.5-2 value for Risk Category II.): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 assigns Ie = 1.00 to Risk Category II structures. Ie increases with the consequences of failure, ranging from 1.0 for Risk Categories I and II up to 1.5 for essential facilities in Risk Category IV.
  8. The correct answer is B (R = 2, the Table 12.2-1 value for this seismic force-resisting system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 2 to this seismic force-resisting system. Selecting the R value for a different system category, such as a moment frame in place of a braced frame, would produce an incorrect and non-conservative base shear.
  9. The correct answer is B (Cd = 1.75, the Table 12.2-1 deflection amplification factor for this system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 1.75 to this seismic force-resisting system. Cd amplifies the elastic drift computed under strength-level seismic forces to estimate the true inelastic design story drift used in the drift check.
  10. The correct answer is C (rho = 1.00, per Section 12.3.4 for this Seismic Design Category.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.1 assigns rho = 1.0 to all structures in Seismic Design Category C, since the redundancy provisions of Section 12.3.4.2 that can require rho = 1.3 apply only to structures assigned to Seismic Design Category D, E, or F.
  11. The correct answer is D (Ta = 0.189 seconds, using the Ct and x values for this system type.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.8-2 gives the Ct and x coefficients specific to this seismic force-resisting system, and applying Ta = Ct times hn to the power x with hn = 20 ft correctly yields Ta = 0.189 seconds. Using coefficients for a different system, or dropping the exponent, produces a materially different and incorrect period.
  12. The correct answer is D (V = 160 kips, using the governing Cs value with W = 1200 kips.): After computing the initial Cs = SDS/(R/Ie), checking it against the period-based maximum limit of Section 12.8.1.1, and confirming it exceeds the applicable minimum Cs, the governing seismic response coefficient applied to W = 1200 kips gives V = 160 kips for this structure.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

Scenario S16

A 6-story special reinforced concrete shear wall residential tower using a building frame system is proposed on a near-fault Site Class D site with Ss = 1.75g and S1 = 0.80g. The building is assigned Risk Category II, has a roof height of 84 ft, and an effective seismic weight of 7,200 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (Fa = 1.0, the Table 11.4-1 value for Site Class D once Ss reaches 1.25g or higher (flattened upper-bound value).): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 gives Fa = 1.0 for Site Class D at any mapped Ss of 1.25g or greater, including this mapped value Ss = 1.75g, since the table stops varying above that upper bound. Fa is the short-period site coefficient used only to compute SMS, and must never be confused with Fv, which is the long-period coefficient used with S1 to compute SM1.
  2. The correct answer is B (Fv = 1.7, the Table 11.4-2 value for Site Class D at this S1.): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 gives Fv = 1.7 for Site Class D wherever S1 is 0.6g or greater, including this mapped value S1 = 0.80g. Fv is the long-period site coefficient used only to compute SM1.
  3. The correct answer is C (SDS = 1.167g, two-thirds of SMS = Fa times Ss for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-3, SMS = Fa times Ss = 1.0 times 1.75 = 1.750g, and SDS = two-thirds of SMS = 1.167g. Skipping the two-thirds reduction, or substituting Fv for Fa, both produce incorrect design values.
  4. The correct answer is A (SD1 = 0.907g, two-thirds of SM1 = Fv times S1 for this site.): Per ASCE 7-16 Eq. 11.4-4, SM1 = Fv times S1 = 1.7 times 0.80 = 1.360g, and SD1 = two-thirds of SM1 = 0.907g. Confusing SD1 with SDS, or substituting Fa for Fv, leads to an incorrect long-period design parameter.
  5. The correct answer is A (Seismic Design Category D, based on Table 11.6-1 and SDS = 1.167g alone.): With SDS = 1.167g and Risk Category II, ASCE 7-16 Table 11.6-1 places this structure in Seismic Design Category D. Table 11.6-1 uses SDS ranges only, and the boundaries shift for Risk Category IV compared with Risk Categories I through III.
  6. The correct answer is B (Seismic Design Category E, the more severe (or overriding) result of both tables.): Table 11.6-1 (SDS = 1.167g) and Table 11.6-2 (SD1 = 0.907g) would each independently place this structure in Seismic Design Category D, but ASCE 7-16 Section 11.6 requires Risk Category I, II, or III structures with S1 at or above 0.75g to be automatically assigned to Seismic Design Category E regardless of the SDS and SD1 table results, overriding the table-based D classification since S1 = 0.80g here.
  7. The correct answer is D (Ie = 1.00, the Table 1.5-2 value for Risk Category II.): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 assigns Ie = 1.00 to Risk Category II structures. Ie increases with the consequences of failure, ranging from 1.0 for Risk Categories I and II up to 1.5 for essential facilities in Risk Category IV.
  8. The correct answer is A (R = 6, the Table 12.2-1 value for this seismic force-resisting system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 6 to this seismic force-resisting system. Selecting the R value for a different system category, such as a moment frame in place of a braced frame, would produce an incorrect and non-conservative base shear.
  9. The correct answer is D (Omega0 = 2.50, the Table 12.2-1 overstrength factor for this system.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 2.50 to this seismic force-resisting system. Omega0 amplifies seismic forces delivered to elements such as collectors and connections that must remain elastic while the主 system yields.
  10. The correct answer is B (k = 1.03, per the Section 12.8.3 rule for this period Ta.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.3 requires linear interpolation between k = 1.0 at Ta = 0.5 seconds and k = 2.0 at Ta = 2.5 seconds. With Ta = 0.555 seconds, interpolating gives k = 1.03, which lies between the linear and parabolic distribution limits.
  11. The correct answer is A (Ta = 0.555 seconds, using the Ct and x values for this system type.): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.8-2 gives the Ct and x coefficients specific to this seismic force-resisting system, and applying Ta = Ct times hn to the power x with hn = 84 ft correctly yields Ta = 0.555 seconds. Using coefficients for a different system, or dropping the exponent, produces a materially different and incorrect period.
  12. The correct answer is C (V = 1540 kips, using the governing Cs value with W = 7200 kips.): The initial Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 1.283/6 = 0.2139, and the period-based maximum of Section 12.8.1.1, SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.907/(0.555 x 6) = 0.2723, is larger, so the initial value governs directly. Applying Cs = 0.2139 to W = 7200 kips gives V = 1540 kips for this structure.
