Scenario S20

A 5-story steel special moment frame office building is Risk Category II and sits on Site Class D soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 1.5g and S1 = 0.6g. The approximate fundamental period is T = 0.79 seconds, the effective seismic weight is W = 4,200 kips, and the moment frame bays are arranged with at least two bays resisting lateral force on each side of every story in both directions.

  1. The correct answer is C (Fa equals 1.0, since Ss of 1.5g meets or exceeds the table's 1.25g limit): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 lists Fa equal to 1.0 for Site Class D whenever Ss reaches or exceeds 1.25g, since the table stops varying above that upper bound. No interpolation is required because 1.5g already lies above the highest listed row. This flattened coefficient reflects reduced amplification at very high shaking intensities on stiffer soil profiles.
  2. The correct answer is A (Fv equals 1.5, since S1 = 0.6g meets or exceeds the table's 0.5g upper limit): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 lists Fv equal to 1.5 for Site Class D once S1 reaches or exceeds 0.5g, the highest tabulated row for that site class. Since 0.6g already exceeds this threshold, the value is read directly with no interpolation needed. Fv governs the long-period design spectrum through SD1.
  3. The correct answer is B (SDS equals 1.00g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): ASCE 7-16 Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.0 and Ss = 1.5g gives (2/3)(1.0)(1.5g), which equals 1.00g. This two-thirds factor converts the maximum considered earthquake spectrum into the design-level spectrum used for the ELF procedure.
  4. The correct answer is A (SD1 equals 0.60g, obtained by applying the two-thirds factor to Fv and S1): ASCE 7-16 Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 1.5 and S1 = 0.6g gives (2/3)(1.5)(0.6g), which equals 0.60g. SD1 controls the response spectrum's constant-velocity region and directly sets the maximum permitted seismic base shear coefficient.
  5. The correct answer is D (Seismic Design Category D, since both SDS and SD1 exceed their 0.50g and 0.20g limits): Table 11.6-1 assigns SDC D to Risk Category II structures once SDS reaches 0.50g, and Table 11.6-2 independently assigns SDC D once SD1 reaches 0.20g. Both SDS = 1.00g and SD1 = 0.60g clear these limits, and since S1 = 0.6g is below 0.75g the automatic Category E trigger does not apply, leaving SDC D as the more severe governing result.
  6. The correct answer is A (Ie equals 1.0, the baseline importance factor assigned to Risk Category I and II structures): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 lists an importance factor of 1.0 for both Risk Category I and Risk Category II structures, reflecting standard occupancy with no elevated hazard or essential-facility function. Higher factors of 1.25 and 1.5 apply only to Risk Category III and IV structures, respectively, neither of which describes this ordinary office building.
  7. The correct answer is D (R equals 8.0, the coefficient specifically assigned to steel special moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 8 for steel special moment frames, reflecting their high ductility capacity from the strong-column-weak-beam detailing and protected zones required by AISC 341. Ordinary and intermediate moment frames carry the lower values of 3.5 and 4.5, reflecting their reduced ductile detailing.
  8. The correct answer is C (Cd equals 5.5, the deflection amplification factor assigned to steel special moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns a deflection amplification factor Cd = 5.5 to steel special moment frames. This factor multiplies the elastic displacements from the design seismic forces to estimate actual inelastic drift, which is then compared against the story drift limits in Table 12.12-1.
  9. The correct answer is D (Cs equals 0.095, since the period-based upper limit is smaller and governs instead): Equation 12.8-2 first gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 1.00/8 = 0.125. Equation 12.8-3 caps Cs at SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.60/(0.79 x 8) = 0.60/6.32, which equals approximately 0.095. Since this period-based value is smaller than the initial 0.125, it governs, and it also exceeds the minimum Cs floors, so Cs = 0.095 is used for design.
  10. The correct answer is B (V equals approximately 399 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.095 by W of 4): ASCE 7-16 Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of 0.095 by the effective seismic weight of 4,200 kips gives approximately 399 kips. This total lateral force is then distributed vertically over the building's height per Section 12.8.3.
  11. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equals 3.0, the overstrength factor assigned to steel special moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists an overstrength factor Omega0 = 3.0 for steel special moment frames. Section 12.10.2.1 requires collector elements to be designed for seismic forces amplified by this overstrength factor, since collectors must remain elastic while the ductile moment frame yields and absorbs the intended inelastic demand.
  12. The correct answer is B (Rho equals 1.0, since the frame configuration satisfies the redundancy condition in Table 12.3-3): For Seismic Design Categories D through F, ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 permits rho = 1.0 when each story resisting more than 35 percent of the base shear has at least two bays of seismic force-resisting frames on each side of the structure. Since this frame meets that layout condition, the reduced redundancy factor of 1.0 applies rather than the default penalty of 1.3.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

Scenario S21

A 3-story special reinforced concrete shear wall residential building is Risk Category II and is located on Site Class C soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 1.0g and S1 = 0.35g. The approximate fundamental period is T = 0.3 seconds, the effective seismic weight is W = 3,000 kips, and only two shear wall lines resist lateral force in each orthogonal direction of the building.

  1. The correct answer is A (Fa equals 1.0, the tabulated Site Class C value corresponding to Ss = 1.0g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 1.0 for Site Class C at Ss = 1.0g exactly, requiring no interpolation. Site Class C represents very dense soil or soft rock, which amplifies short-period ground motion less than the softer Site Class D or E profiles at this same shaking level.
  2. The correct answer is D (Fv equals 1.45, the linear interpolation midpoint between the 0.3g and 0.4g rows): Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 1.5 for Site Class C at S1 = 0.3g and Fv = 1.4 at S1 = 0.4g. Since S1 = 0.35g sits exactly halfway between these two mapped values, linear interpolation per ASCE 7-16 Section 11.4.3 gives the midpoint value of Fv = 1.45.
  3. The correct answer is B (SDS equals 0.667g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.0 and Ss = 1.0g gives (2/3)(1.0)(1.0g), which equals approximately 0.667g. This value sets the plateau level of the short-period design response spectrum used throughout the ELF procedure.
  4. The correct answer is A (SD1 equals 0.338g, obtained by applying the two-thirds factor to Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 1.45 and S1 = 0.35g gives (2/3)(1.45)(0.35g), which equals approximately 0.338g. SD1 controls the long-period portion of the design spectrum and the ELF equivalent lateral force upper-bound coefficient.
  5. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Category D, since both SDS and SD1 exceed their 0.50g and 0.20g thresholds): Table 11.6-1 assigns SDC D to Risk Category II structures once SDS reaches 0.50g, and Table 11.6-2 independently assigns SDC D once SD1 reaches 0.20g. Both SDS = 0.667g and SD1 = 0.338g exceed these respective limits, so SDC D governs even though S1 remains well below the 0.75g automatic Category E trigger.
  6. The correct answer is D (R equals 5, the coefficient specifically assigned to special reinforced concrete shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 5 for bearing wall systems using special reinforced concrete shear walls (the building frame system variant of the same wall type instead carries R = 6). The ordinary shear wall variant, detailed to lower ductility requirements, is assigned only R = 4, since it lacks the special boundary element and confinement detailing required under ACI 318-14 Chapter 18.
  7. The correct answer is D (Cs equals 0.133, obtained as SDS divided by R over Ie, exceeding the minimum floor): Equation 12.8-2 gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 0.667/(5/1.0), which equals approximately 0.133. The minimum floor from Equation 12.8-5 is 0.044 x SDS x Ie, or about 0.029, which is well below 0.133, so the calculated value governs rather than the minimum floor.
  8. The correct answer is A (Cs equals 0.133, since the period-based limit of about 0.225 is larger and does not govern): Equation 12.8-3 gives an upper limit of SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.338/(0.3 x 5), which equals approximately 0.225. Since this period-based limit exceeds the previously calculated Cs of 0.133, it does not reduce the design value, and the initial 0.133 coefficient remains the governing value for base shear calculations.
  9. The correct answer is D (V equals approximately 400 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.133 by W of 3): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of 0.133 by the effective seismic weight of 3,000 kips gives approximately 400 kips, the total lateral force distributed vertically over the building's three stories per Section 12.8.3.
  10. The correct answer is C (Cd equals 5, the deflection amplification factor assigned to special reinforced concrete shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 5 to special reinforced concrete shear walls. This factor scales the elastic displacement results from the design seismic forces up to an estimate of true inelastic drift, which is then checked against the applicable story drift limit for this Risk Category II structure.
  11. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equals 2.5, the overstrength factor specifically assigned to special reinforced concrete shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Omega0 = 2.5 for special reinforced concrete shear walls. Section 12.10.2.1 requires that collector elements transferring diaphragm forces into this wall system be designed using seismic forces amplified by this overstrength factor, ensuring the collector stays essentially elastic while the wall yields.
  12. The correct answer is B (Rho equals 1.3, the default penalty value applying when the redundancy condition is not met): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 assigns rho = 1.3 to structures in Seismic Design Categories D through F whenever the layout does not satisfy the redundancy conditions of Table 12.3-3, such as having fewer than two bays or wall lines per side resisting more than 35 percent of story shear. With only two wall lines per direction, this building falls short of that requirement and must use the full 1.3 penalty.
Scenario S22

A hospital building is Risk Category IV and is located on Site Class E soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 1.8g and S1 = 0.75g. The lateral system is steel special concentrically braced frames (SCBF) arranged with adequate perimeter bracing to avoid extreme torsional irregularity. The approximate fundamental period is T = 0.5 seconds and the effective seismic weight is W = 8,000 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (Fa equals 0.9, the tabulated Site Class E value for Ss at or above 1.25g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 0.9 for Site Class E once Ss reaches 1.25g or higher, the lowest tabulated point for this soft soil class. Site Class E soils actually de-amplify short-period motion at very high shaking intensities, unlike the strong amplification seen at lower Ss values on this same soil class.
  2. The correct answer is C (Fv equals 2.4, the tabulated Site Class E value for S1 at or above 0.5g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 2.4 for Site Class E once S1 reaches 0.5g or higher. Since 0.75g exceeds this upper threshold, the coefficient is read directly at 2.4 without interpolation, reflecting the flattened long-period amplification behavior of this soft soil class at high shaking intensities.
  3. The correct answer is A (SDS equals 1.08g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 0.9 and Ss = 1.8g gives (2/3)(0.9)(1.8g), which equals 1.08g. This value establishes the short-period plateau of this hospital's design response spectrum for the ELF procedure.
  4. The correct answer is B (SD1 equals 1.20g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 2.4 and S1 = 0.75g gives (2/3)(2.4)(0.75g), which equals 1.20g. This unusually high SD1 reflects the combination of very high mapped seismicity with a soft Site Class E soil profile.
  5. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Category F, the automatic assignment required for Risk Category IV once S1 reaches 0.75g): ASCE 7-16 Section 11.6 requires that structures be automatically assigned to Seismic Design Category E, or Category F for Risk Category IV structures, whenever S1 is 0.75g or greater, bypassing the standard SDS and SD1 table lookups entirely. Since this hospital is Risk Category IV and S1 equals exactly 0.75g, SDC F governs directly.
  6. The correct answer is C (Ie equals 1.5, the highest importance factor, reserved for essential Risk Category IV facilities): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 assigns Ie = 1.5 to Risk Category IV structures, the highest importance factor in the code, applied to essential facilities such as hospitals that must remain operational to provide emergency response after a design earthquake.
  7. The correct answer is C (R equals 6, the coefficient specifically assigned to steel special concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 6 for steel special concentrically braced frames, reflecting their ductile brace detailing and capacity-designed connections under AISC 341. This is lower than the R = 8 given to special moment frames, since braced frame ductility relies on a different, less flexible yielding mechanism.
  8. The correct answer is A (Omega0 equals 2.0, the overstrength factor specifically assigned to steel special concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Omega0 = 2.0 for steel special concentrically braced frames. AISC 341 capacity design provisions use this factor to size brace connections and adjoining members so they remain essentially elastic while the intended ductile brace yielding and buckling mechanism absorbs the seismic energy.
  9. The correct answer is A (Cs equals 0.27, since the initial value is smaller than the period-based upper limit of 0.60): Equation 12.8-2 gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 1.08/(6/1.5) = 1.08/4.0, which equals 0.27. The period-based upper limit from Equation 12.8-3 is SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 1.20/(0.5 x 4.0), which equals 0.60. Since the initial 0.27 is already smaller than this 0.60 cap, it governs directly, and it also comfortably clears the applicable minimum coefficient floors.
  10. The correct answer is D (V equals approximately 2,160 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.27 by W of 8): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of 0.27 by the effective seismic weight of 8,000 kips gives approximately 2,160 kips, a very large base shear reflecting the combination of high seismicity, Site Class E soil, and the elevated Risk Category IV importance factor.
  11. The correct answer is D (Rho equals 1.0, since the perimeter bracing configuration satisfies the Table 12.3-3 redundancy condition): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 permits rho = 1.0 for Seismic Design Categories D through F when the structure has sufficient redundancy in its lateral system, such as adequate perimeter bracing lines with no extreme torsional irregularity. Since this hospital's SCBF layout satisfies that condition, the reduced factor of 1.0 applies instead of the default 1.3 penalty.
  12. The correct answer is B (Cd equals 5.0, the deflection amplification factor assigned to steel special concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 5.0 to steel special concentrically braced frames. This factor amplifies the elastic drift computed from the design seismic forces into an estimate of true inelastic drift, which is then compared against the tighter drift limits Table 12.12-1 imposes on this essential Risk Category IV facility.
Scenario S23

A one-story wood-frame elementary school building is Risk Category III and is located on Site Class D soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 0.9g and S1 = 0.3g. The lateral system is wood structural panel shear walls per SDPWS 2015, the approximate fundamental period is T = 0.25 seconds, the effective seismic weight is W = 2,500 kips, and only two shear wall lines resist lateral force in each direction.

  1. The correct answer is B (Fa equals 1.14, the interpolated value between the Ss = 0.75g and Ss = 1.0g rows): Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 1.2 for Site Class D at Ss = 0.75g and Fa = 1.1 at Ss = 1.0g. Since Ss = 0.9g sits sixty percent of the way from 0.75g toward 1.0g, linear interpolation per Section 11.4.3 gives Fa = 1.2 minus 0.6 times 0.1, which equals 1.14.
  2. The correct answer is A (Fv equals 1.8, the tabulated Site Class D value listed exactly at S1 = 0.3g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 1.8 for Site Class D at S1 = 0.3g exactly, so no interpolation is needed. This coefficient reflects the moderate amplification of long-period ground motion expected for stiff soil at this level of mapped seismicity.
  3. The correct answer is A (SDS equals 0.684g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.14 and Ss = 0.9g gives (2/3)(1.14)(0.9g), which equals approximately 0.684g. This value defines the short-period plateau of the design response spectrum for this school building.
  4. The correct answer is C (SD1 equals 0.360g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 1.8 and S1 = 0.3g gives (2/3)(1.8)(0.3g), which equals 0.360g. SD1 governs the long-period portion of the response spectrum used in the ELF procedure for this school.
  5. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Category D, since both SDS and SD1 exceed their 0.50g and 0.20g thresholds): For Risk Category I, II, or III structures, Table 11.6-1 assigns SDC D once SDS reaches 0.50g, and Table 11.6-2 independently assigns SDC D once SD1 reaches 0.20g. Both SDS = 0.684g and SD1 = 0.360g exceed these thresholds, so SDC D governs, and S1 = 0.3g remains far below the 0.75g automatic Category E trigger.
  6. The correct answer is C (Ie equals 1.25, the importance factor specifically assigned to Risk Category III structures): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-2 lists Ie = 1.25 for Risk Category III structures, which includes schools with occupant loads greater than 250. This intermediate importance factor reflects the substantial hazard to human life posed by school buildings in the event of failure, without reaching the essential-facility level of Risk Category IV.
  7. The correct answer is A (R equals 6.5, the coefficient specifically assigned to light-framed wood structural panel shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 6.5 for bearing wall systems consisting of light-framed walls sheathed with wood structural panels. This relatively high coefficient reflects the good ductility and energy dissipation these shear walls exhibit through nail yielding, as recognized by SDPWS 2015 design provisions.
  8. The correct answer is B (Cs equals 0.1315, obtained as SDS divided by the quantity R divided by Ie): Equation 12.8-2 gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 0.684/(6.5/1.25) = 0.684/5.2, which equals approximately 0.1315. This initial value must still be checked against the period-based upper limit and the applicable minimum coefficient floors before it can be confirmed as the governing design value.
  9. The correct answer is A (Cs equals 0.1315, since the period-based limit of about 0.277 is larger and does not govern): Equation 12.8-3 gives an upper limit of SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.360/(0.25 x 5.2), which equals approximately 0.277. Since this period-based limit exceeds the previously calculated Cs of 0.1315, it does not reduce the design value, and 0.1315 remains the governing coefficient after also clearing the applicable minimum floors.
  10. The correct answer is C (V equals approximately 329 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.1315 by W of 2): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of approximately 0.1315 by the effective seismic weight of 2,500 kips gives approximately 329 kips, the total design lateral force for this one-story school.
  11. The correct answer is A (A maximum aspect ratio of 2 to 1, height to width, without a strength reduction): SDPWS 2015 Section 4.3.4 permits a blocked wood structural panel shear wall to reach a height-to-width aspect ratio of 2 to 1 while using its full tabulated unit shear capacity. Above 2 to 1 and up to the code's absolute limit of 3.5 to 1 for blocked walls, a strength reduction factor of 2w/h must be applied to the nominal capacity, so 3.5 to 1 is the absolute maximum permitted, not the threshold below which no reduction applies.
  12. The correct answer is C (Rho equals 1.3, the default penalty value applying when the redundancy condition is not met): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 assigns rho = 1.3 to structures in Seismic Design Categories D through F when the lateral system does not satisfy the redundancy layout conditions of Table 12.3-3. With only two shear wall lines per direction, this school does not meet the required configuration and must use the full 1.3 penalty on its seismic design forces.
Scenario S24

A single-story steel warehouse building is Risk Category II and is located on Site Class D soil in a region of moderate seismicity. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 0.4g and S1 = 0.15g. The lateral system is steel ordinary concentrically braced frames (OCBF), the approximate fundamental period is T = 0.4 seconds, and the effective seismic weight is W = 5,000 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (Fa equals 1.48, the interpolated value between the Ss = 0.25g and Ss = 0.5g rows): Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 1.6 for Site Class D at Ss = 0.25g and Fa = 1.4 at Ss = 0.5g. Since Ss = 0.4g sits sixty percent of the way from 0.25g toward 0.5g, linear interpolation per Section 11.4.3 gives Fa = 1.6 minus 0.6 times 0.2, which equals 1.48.
  2. The correct answer is D (Fv equals 2.2, the linear interpolation midpoint between the 0.1g and 0.2g table rows): Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 2.4 for Site Class D at S1 = 0.1g and Fv = 2.0 at S1 = 0.2g. Since S1 = 0.15g sits exactly halfway between these two mapped values, linear interpolation per Section 11.4.3 gives the midpoint value of Fv = 2.2.
  3. The correct answer is A (SDS equals 0.395g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.48 and Ss = 0.4g gives (2/3)(1.48)(0.4g), which equals approximately 0.395g. This moderate value reflects the region's relatively lower mapped seismicity compared to the higher-hazard scenarios elsewhere in this book.
  4. The correct answer is C (SD1 equals 0.220g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 2.2 and S1 = 0.15g gives (2/3)(2.2)(0.15g), which equals 0.220g. SD1 sets the long-period portion of this warehouse's design response spectrum for the ELF procedure.
  5. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Category D, since SD1 alone already exceeds the 0.20g threshold for that category): Table 11.6-1 places this SDS = 0.395g in Seismic Design Category C for a Risk Category II structure, since it falls between 0.33g and 0.50g. However, Table 11.6-2 places SD1 = 0.220g in Seismic Design Category D, since it meets the 0.20g threshold. ASCE 7-16 Section 11.6 requires taking the more severe of the two results, so SDC D governs overall.
  6. The correct answer is A (R equals 3.25, the coefficient specifically assigned to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 3.25 for steel ordinary concentrically braced frames. This much lower coefficient than the special variant's R = 6 reflects the reduced ductility detailing permitted for ordinary braced frames, which lack the strict brace slenderness and connection capacity design requirements of AISC 341 special systems.
  7. The correct answer is B (Omega0 equals 2.0, the overstrength factor specifically assigned to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Omega0 = 2.0 for steel ordinary concentrically braced frames, the same overstrength value used for the special variant. This factor amplifies seismic forces on connections and collectors so they remain elastic while the braces themselves are the intended yielding elements.
  8. The correct answer is C (Cs equals 0.1215, since the initial value is smaller than the period-based upper limit of 0.169): Equation 12.8-2 gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 0.395/3.25, which equals approximately 0.1215. The period-based upper limit from Equation 12.8-3 is SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.220/(0.4 x 3.25), which equals approximately 0.169. Since the initial 0.1215 is already smaller than this cap, it governs directly for design.
  9. The correct answer is D (V equals approximately 608 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.1215 by W of 5): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of approximately 0.1215 by the effective seismic weight of 5,000 kips gives approximately 608 kips, the total design lateral force for this single-story warehouse.
  10. The correct answer is C (Cd equals 3.25, the deflection amplification factor specifically assigned to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 3.25 to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames, the same numeric value as their R coefficient in this case. This factor is used to amplify elastic drift results up to expected inelastic drift for comparison against the applicable story drift limit.
  11. The correct answer is B (k equals 1.0, the exponent used for structures with a period of 0.5 seconds or less): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.3 specifies k = 1.0 for structures with a fundamental period of 0.5 seconds or less, producing a linear vertical distribution of seismic force. Since this warehouse's period of 0.4 seconds falls below that threshold, k = 1.0 applies directly with no interpolation required.
  12. The correct answer is D (Table 12.3-3, which lists the specific redundancy configuration requirements for each system type): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.3-3 sets out the specific bay, wall line, or braced frame bay configuration requirements that a structure in Seismic Design Category D, E, or F must satisfy to qualify for the reduced redundancy factor of 1.0 rather than the default penalty of 1.3. Tables 11.6-1, 12.2-1, and 12.6-1 govern unrelated aspects of the seismic design process.
Scenario S25

A 20-story special reinforced concrete moment frame office tower is Risk Category II and is located on Site Class D soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 1.25g and S1 = 0.5g. The approximate fundamental period is T = 1.2 seconds, the effective seismic weight is W = 12,000 kips, and the moment frame plan provides at least two bays of frames resisting lateral force on each side of every story.

  1. The correct answer is C (Fa equals 1.0, the tabulated Site Class D value for Ss at or above 1.25g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 1.0 for Site Class D once Ss reaches 1.25g or higher, the flattened upper-bound value for this site class. Since this tower's mapped Ss equals exactly 1.25g, the coefficient is read directly at 1.0 with no interpolation required.
  2. The correct answer is D (Fv equals 1.5, the tabulated Site Class D value for S1 at or above 0.5g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 1.5 for Site Class D once S1 reaches 0.5g or higher, the flattened upper-bound value for this site class. Since S1 for this tower equals exactly 0.5g, the coefficient is read directly at 1.5 with no interpolation required.
  3. The correct answer is A (SDS equals 0.833g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.0 and Ss = 1.25g gives (2/3)(1.0)(1.25g), which equals approximately 0.833g. This value sets the short-period plateau of the design response spectrum used in this tower's ELF calculations.
  4. The correct answer is B (SD1 equals 0.500g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 1.5 and S1 = 0.5g gives (2/3)(1.5)(0.5g), which equals exactly 0.500g. This SD1 value governs the long-period portion of the tower's design response spectrum and its period-based base shear cap.
  5. The correct answer is D (Seismic Design Category D, since both SDS and SD1 exceed their 0.50g and 0.20g thresholds): Table 11.6-1 assigns SDC D to Risk Category II structures once SDS reaches 0.50g, and Table 11.6-2 independently assigns SDC D once SD1 reaches 0.20g. Both SDS = 0.833g and SD1 = 0.500g clear these limits, and S1 = 0.5g remains below the 0.75g automatic Category E trigger, leaving SDC D as the governing classification.
  6. The correct answer is D (R equals 8, the coefficient specifically assigned to special reinforced concrete moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 8 for special reinforced concrete moment frames, the same high ductility coefficient assigned to steel special moment frames. This reflects the strong-column-weak-beam capacity design and confinement detailing that ACI 318-14 Chapter 18 requires for these frames.
  7. The correct answer is B (Cs equals 0.052, since the period-based upper limit is smaller and governs the design instead): Equation 12.8-2 first gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 0.833/8, which equals approximately 0.104. Equation 12.8-3 caps Cs at SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.500/(1.2 x 8), which equals approximately 0.052. Since this period-based value is smaller than the initial 0.104, it governs, and it exceeds the applicable minimum floor of about 0.037, so Cs = 0.052 is used for design.
  8. The correct answer is D (V equals approximately 625 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.052 by W of 12): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of approximately 0.052 by the effective seismic weight of 12,000 kips gives approximately 625 kips, a relatively modest base shear for this tall tower because the period-based cap significantly reduced the initial coefficient.
  9. The correct answer is C (Cd equals 5.5, the deflection amplification factor assigned to special reinforced concrete moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 5.5 to special reinforced concrete moment frames, matching the value given to steel special moment frames. This factor amplifies the elastic displacement computed from the design seismic forces up to an estimate of the tower's true inelastic drift for code compliance checks.
  10. The correct answer is D (Rho equals 1.0, since the frame configuration satisfies the redundancy condition in Table 12.3-3): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 permits rho = 1.0 for Seismic Design Categories D through F when each story resisting more than 35 percent of the base shear has at least two bays of moment frames on each side of the structure. Since this tower's plan meets that condition, the reduced factor of 1.0 applies rather than the default 1.3 penalty.
  11. The correct answer is A (k equals 1.35, obtained by linearly interpolating between k = 1.0 at 0.5 seconds and k): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.3 requires k = 1.0 for T at or below 0.5 seconds, k = 2.0 for T at or above 2.5 seconds, and linear interpolation between these bounds for intermediate periods. For T = 1.2 seconds, k = 1.0 plus (1.2 minus 0.5) divided by (2.5 minus 0.5), which equals 1.0 plus 0.35, or 1.35.
  12. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equals 3.0, the overstrength factor specifically assigned to special reinforced concrete moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Omega0 = 3.0 for special reinforced concrete moment frames. Section 12.4.3 requires certain elements, including base columns subject to overturning-induced axial forces, to be designed using seismic forces amplified by this overstrength factor to ensure they remain essentially elastic under the frame's full ductile response.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

Scenario S26

A 2-story special reinforced masonry shear wall retail building is Risk Category II and is located on Site Class C soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 0.75g and S1 = 0.25g. The approximate fundamental period is T = 0.35 seconds, the effective seismic weight is W = 3,500 kips, and three masonry shear wall lines resist lateral force in each orthogonal direction, satisfying ASCE 7-16 Table 12.3-3.

  1. The correct answer is A (Fa equals 1.2, the tabulated Site Class C value listed exactly at Ss = 0.75g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 1.2 for Site Class C at Ss = 0.75g exactly, so no interpolation is needed. This reflects the moderate amplification expected on very dense soil or soft rock at this shaking intensity.
  2. The correct answer is B (Fv equals 1.50, the flat Site Class C value that applies across this range of S1): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 1.5 for Site Class C at every mapped S1 from 0.1g through 0.5g, only dropping to 1.4 at S1 of 0.6g or greater. Since the table does not actually change value between S1 = 0.2g and 0.3g, no interpolation is needed at S1 = 0.25g, and assuming a false linear interpolation between two different tabulated values produces an incorrect result.
  3. The correct answer is A (SDS equals 0.600g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.2 and Ss = 0.75g gives (2/3)(1.2)(0.75g), which equals 0.600g. This value sets the short-period plateau of the design response spectrum for this retail building.
  4. The correct answer is A (SD1 equals 0.250g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 1.5 and S1 = 0.25g gives (2/3)(1.5)(0.25g), which equals exactly 0.250g. SD1 governs the long-period portion of this masonry building's design response spectrum.
  5. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Category D, since both SDS and SD1 exceed their 0.50g and 0.20g thresholds): Table 11.6-1 assigns SDC D to Risk Category II structures once SDS reaches 0.50g, and Table 11.6-2 independently assigns SDC D once SD1 reaches 0.20g. Both SDS = 0.600g and SD1 = 0.250g clear these limits, so SDC D governs even though S1 = 0.25g remains far below the 0.75g automatic Category E trigger.
  6. The correct answer is D (R equals 5, the coefficient specifically assigned to special reinforced masonry shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists R = 5 for special reinforced masonry shear walls. This coefficient reflects the enhanced ductility from the special reinforcement detailing, minimum steel ratios, and confinement requirements that TMS 402/602-16 imposes beyond what ordinary or intermediate masonry shear walls require.
  7. The correct answer is A (Cd equals 3.5, the deflection amplification factor specifically assigned to special reinforced masonry shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 3.5 to special reinforced masonry shear walls. This factor amplifies elastic drift results from the design seismic forces up to an estimate of true inelastic drift, which is then checked against the applicable story drift limit for this Risk Category II retail building.
  8. The correct answer is C (Cs equals 0.120, since the initial value is smaller than the period-based upper limit of 0.143): Equation 12.8-2 gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 0.600/5, which equals 0.120. The period-based upper limit from Equation 12.8-3 is SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.250/(0.35 x 5), which equals approximately 0.143. Since the initial 0.120 is already smaller than this cap, it governs directly for design after also clearing the applicable minimum floor.
  9. The correct answer is A (V equals approximately 420 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.120 by W of 3,500 kips): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of 0.120 by the effective seismic weight of 3,500 kips gives 420 kips, the total design lateral force for this two-story masonry retail building.
  10. The correct answer is B (Out-of-plane forces acting perpendicular to the wall, pushing it away from the structure): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.11 requires exterior wall panels, including masonry veneer, to be positively anchored to the structure to resist out-of-plane seismic forces, since walls are far more vulnerable to falling away from a building under perpendicular shaking than to in-plane shear, which the shear wall system itself is designed to resist.
  11. The correct answer is B (Rho equals 1.0, since the three wall lines per direction satisfy the Table 12.3-3 condition): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 permits rho = 1.0 for Seismic Design Categories D through F when the lateral system has sufficient bays or wall lines per side resisting more than 35 percent of story shear. With three masonry shear wall lines in each direction, this building satisfies that redundancy condition, so the reduced factor of 1.0 applies.
  12. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equals 2.5, the overstrength factor specifically assigned to special reinforced masonry shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Omega0 = 2.5 for special reinforced masonry shear walls. Section 12.10.2.1 requires collector elements transferring diaphragm shear into this wall system to be designed for seismic forces amplified by this overstrength factor, keeping the collector essentially elastic while the wall provides the intended ductile response.
Scenario S27

A steel pipe rack supporting process piping is classified as a nonbuilding structure similar to a building, Risk Category II, on Site Class D soil. Mapped spectral accelerations are Ss = 1.0g and S1 = 0.4g. The lateral system is steel ordinary concentrically braced frames, the approximate fundamental period is T = 0.3 seconds, and the effective seismic weight is W = 800 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (Fa equals 1.1, the tabulated Site Class D value listed exactly at Ss = 1.0g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-1 lists Fa = 1.1 for Site Class D at Ss = 1.0g exactly, so no interpolation is needed. This directly tabulated value reflects the amplification expected on stiff soil at this specific shaking intensity.
  2. The correct answer is B (Fv equals 1.9, the tabulated Site Class D value listed exactly at S1 = 0.4g): ASCE 7-16 Table 11.4-2 lists Fv = 1.9 for Site Class D at S1 = 0.4g exactly, so no interpolation is needed. This directly tabulated value governs the long-period amplification for this pipe rack's design spectrum.
  3. The correct answer is A (SDS equals 0.733g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fa and Ss): Equation 11.4-3 defines SDS as (2/3) times Fa times Ss. Substituting Fa = 1.1 and Ss = 1.0g gives (2/3)(1.1)(1.0g), which equals approximately 0.733g. This value sets the short-period plateau of the design response spectrum used to design this pipe rack under ASCE 7-16 Chapter 15.
  4. The correct answer is D (SD1 equals 0.507g, obtained as two-thirds of the product of Fv and S1): Equation 11.4-4 defines SD1 as (2/3) times Fv times S1. Substituting Fv = 1.9 and S1 = 0.4g gives (2/3)(1.9)(0.4g), which equals approximately 0.507g. This SD1 value governs the long-period portion of the design spectrum used for this nonbuilding structure.
  5. The correct answer is A (Seismic Design Category D, since both SDS and SD1 exceed their 0.50g and 0.20g thresholds): Table 11.6-1 assigns SDC D to Risk Category II structures once SDS reaches 0.50g, and Table 11.6-2 independently assigns SDC D once SD1 reaches 0.20g. Both SDS = 0.733g and SD1 = 0.507g clear these limits, so SDC D governs even though S1 = 0.4g remains well below the 0.75g automatic Category E trigger.
  6. The correct answer is A (Table 15.4-1, which lists coefficients for nonbuilding structures similar to buildings): ASCE 7-16 Table 15.4-1 provides response modification coefficients, overstrength factors, and deflection amplification factors specifically for nonbuilding structures similar to buildings, such as this pipe rack, listing R = 3.25 for steel ordinary concentrically braced frames in that category, consistent with the value used for equivalent building systems in Table 12.2-1.
  7. The correct answer is B (Cs equals 0.226, since the initial value is smaller than the period-based upper limit of 0.520.): Equation 12.8-2 gives Cs = SDS/(R/Ie) = 0.733/3.25, which equals approximately 0.226. The period-based upper limit from Equation 12.8-3 is SD1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.507/(0.3 x 3.25), which equals approximately 0.520. Since the initial 0.226 is already smaller than this cap, it governs directly for design.
  8. The correct answer is D (V equals approximately 181 kips, obtained by multiplying Cs of 0.226 by W of 800 kips): Equation 12.8-1 defines base shear as V = Cs x W. Multiplying the governing coefficient of approximately 0.226 by the effective seismic weight of 800 kips gives approximately 181 kips, the total design lateral force for this relatively light nonbuilding pipe rack structure.
  9. The correct answer is C (Chapter 15, which contains seismic design requirements specific to nonbuilding structures): ASCE 7-16 Chapter 15 sets out the seismic design provisions specific to nonbuilding structures, including structural systems similar to buildings like this pipe rack, as well as tanks, vessels, and other process equipment supports, supplementing rather than replacing the general ELF procedure in Chapter 12.
  10. The correct answer is A (The component amplification factor ap, the component response modification factor Rp): ASCE 7-16 Equation 13.3-1 computes the seismic design force Fp for a nonstructural component such as this piping using SDS, the component weight, its height within the structure, and three component-specific factors, ap, Rp, and Ip, which are tabulated separately from the building's own R, Cd, and Omega0 values used for the main lateral system.
  11. The correct answer is B (Rho equals 1.0, since the bracing configuration satisfies the Table 12.3-3 redundancy condition): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4 applies the same redundancy factor framework used for buildings to nonbuilding structures similar to buildings. Since this pipe rack's braced frame configuration satisfies the Table 12.3-3 condition for adequate bays resisting more than 35 percent of story shear on each side, the reduced redundancy factor of 1.0 applies rather than the default 1.3 penalty.
  12. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equals 2.0, the overstrength factor specifically assigned to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 15.4-1 assigns Omega0 = 2.0 to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames used in nonbuilding structures similar to buildings, matching the value used for the equivalent building system in Table 12.2-1. This factor amplifies connection design forces so they remain elastic while the braces yield.
Scenario S28

A 4-story steel office building in Riverside, California (Risk Category II, Ie = 1.0) uses special concentrically braced frames (SCBF) as the seismic force-resisting system in both orthogonal directions. The floor and roof diaphragms are 3 in. normal-weight concrete over 2 in. composite metal deck. Site Class D; SDS = 1.10g. The braced frame on Grid Line A has a lateral stiffness of 15,000 kip/in and the braced frame on Grid Line D has a lateral stiffness of 25,000 kip/in, both resisting shear in the transverse (E-W) direction. The roof diaphragm is rectangular, 120 ft by 180 ft. Design story shear at the roof level in the transverse direction is 400 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (Rigid, so story shear is distributed to Grids A and D by their relative lateral stiffness): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2, diaphragms constructed of concrete fill over metal deck are permitted to be idealized as rigid without further deflection calculation. A rigid diaphragm distributes lateral force to the vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area. This is the standard assumption used throughout the rest of this scenario's calculations.
  2. The correct answer is C (Grid Line D resists approximately 250 kips of the total transverse story shear): With rigid diaphragm behavior, the story shear is split by relative stiffness, so Grid D carries 400 kips times 25,000/(15,000+25,000), or 400 times 0.625, which equals 250 kips. The stiffer frame line always attracts a proportionally larger share of the total shear. This matches the equilibrium check performed in the next question.
  3. The correct answer is B (Grid Line A resists approximately 150 kips of the total transverse story shear): Grid A carries 400 kips times 15,000/40,000, or 400 times 0.375, which equals 150 kips. This is the complement of the 250 kips computed for Grid D, so the two values together equal the full applied story shear. Relative stiffness distribution always splits the total shear proportionally between the resisting elements.
  4. The correct answer is A (Grid A carries 150 kips and Grid D carries 250 kips, summing to 400 kips): This pair matches both the 15,000:25,000 stiffness ratio and sums exactly to the 400-kip applied story shear. Options that only satisfy the arithmetic sum without matching the correct stiffness ratio are not valid distributions. A correct relative-stiffness solution must satisfy both the ratio and the equilibrium requirement simultaneously.
  5. The correct answer is B (The center of rigidity is located approximately 93.75 ft from Grid Line A): The center of rigidity is the stiffness-weighted average position, computed as (15,000 times 0 plus 25,000 times 150) divided by 40,000, which equals 93.75 ft from Grid A. Locating the center of rigidity is a prerequisite step before torsional eccentricity can be evaluated. The stiffer frame pulls the center of rigidity closer to its own location.
  6. The correct answer is A (The inherent torsional eccentricity is approximately 3.75 ft between the two points): Inherent eccentricity is simply the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity, or 93.75 ft minus 90 ft, which equals 3.75 ft. This eccentricity exists even without any code-required accidental offset and results purely from the building's actual mass and stiffness layout. It must still be combined with accidental eccentricity for the full design torsion check.
  7. The correct answer is A (The accidental eccentricity is approximately 9 ft, computed as 5 percent of 180 ft): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2 requires the center of mass to be displaced by 5 percent of the plan dimension perpendicular to the direction of the applied force. Since transverse force acts along the 180-ft dimension in this layout, the accidental eccentricity equals 0.05 times 180 ft, or 9 ft. This value is added to the inherent eccentricity to obtain the governing design eccentricity.
  8. The correct answer is C (The governing total torsional eccentricity is approximately 12.75 ft for the design check): ASCE 7-16 requires both eccentricities to be combined for the most severe torsional demand, so 3.75 ft plus 9 ft equals 12.75 ft. Using only the accidental or only the inherent component understates the true torsional demand on the frame lines. This combined eccentricity is carried forward into the torsional moment calculation.
  9. The correct answer is B (The torsional moment is approximately 5,100 kip-ft applied to the roof diaphragm): The torsional moment equals the design story shear times the governing eccentricity, or 400 kips times 12.75 ft, which equals 5,100 kip-ft. This moment must be resolved into additional shear on each vertical resisting element beyond the direct translational share already computed. Larger eccentricities and larger story shears both directly increase this torsional demand.
  10. The correct answer is A (The equivalent lateral force procedure remains a permitted analysis option for this structure): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.6-1 permits the equivalent lateral force procedure for regular structures assigned to SDC D provided the fundamental period and irregularity conditions of the table are satisfied. A 4-story building with no disqualifying horizontal or vertical irregularities meets these conditions. More rigorous dynamic procedures remain optional but are not mandatory here.
  11. The correct answer is A (A collector, or drag strut, framing member along the Grid Line D bay): A collector, also called a drag strut, is required whenever a shear wall or braced bay occupies only part of a diaphragm edge, so it can gather shear from the unbraced portion of the diaphragm and deliver it into the resisting element. Without a collector, diaphragm shear outside the braced bay would have no defined load path into the bracing. This maintains continuity of the seismic load path required by ASCE 7-16.
  12. The correct answer is B (The design story drift is approximately 1.0 in at the roof level): Design story drift equals Cd times the elastic drift divided by Ie, or 5.0 times 0.20 in divided by 1.0, which equals 1.0 in. This amplified drift, not the raw elastic analysis drift, is the value compared against the ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1 allowable story drift limit. Cd amplification accounts for inelastic deformation beyond the elastic analysis result.
Scenario S29

A five-story steel special moment frame (SMF) office building, Risk Category II, is located in a region with Ss = 1.15g and S1 = 0.50g on Site Class D, placing it in Seismic Design Category D. The building plan is 96 ft wide by 150 ft long, with a rigid reinforced concrete slab on composite metal deck diaphragm at each floor. Three steel moment frame lines resist lateral force in the transverse direction, spaced along the 96 ft plan dimension. R = 8, Cd = 5.5, Ie = 1.0.

  1. The correct answer is C (Rigid diaphragm; a concrete slab or concrete-filled deck may be idealized rigid without a deflection check.): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2, a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm may be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so story shear at this level is distributed to the 3 vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is C (x_cm = 48.0 ft, the geometric center of the 96 ft plan dimension.): For a plan symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls at the geometric centroid of the 96 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 48.0 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is D (x_r = 57.6 ft, from the k_i times x_i sum over the k_i sum.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity, sum of k_i = 500, gives x_r = 57.6 ft, a location offset from the geometric center because the 3 lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is B (e = 9.6 ft, the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity, giving e = absolute value of 48.0 minus 57.6 equals 9.6 ft, a value that must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is C (e_acc = 4.8 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 96 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 96 ft = 4.8 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is B (Mt = 6624 kip-ft, from Mt equal to V times the combined eccentricity of 14.4 ft.): Adding the two eccentricities for the worst-case torsional direction gives a total eccentricity of 9.6 plus 4.8 equals 14.4 ft, so Mt equals 460 kips times 14.4 ft = 6624 kip-ft, the moment used to compute torsional shear on each resisting line.
  7. The correct answer is D (211.6 kips, from V times k for that line divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 460 kips times (230 divided by 500) equals 211.6 kips carried by Frame Line 3.
  8. The correct answer is B (Approximately 56.5 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term, the sum of k_j times d_j squared over all lines; for Frame Line 1 this evaluates to approximately 56.5 kips, which adds to that line's direct shear.
  9. The correct answer is D (Approximately 290.3 kips, the sum of its direct shear and torsional shear acting together.): The controlling line receives both its direct shear share and its torsional shear increment acting in the same rotational sense, giving a combined demand of approximately 290.3 kips on Frame Line 3, which governs that line's design.
  10. The correct answer is C (Delta = 10.45 in, which exceeds Delta_a = 3.0 in, so the drift check fails.): The amplified design story drift equals Cd times delta_xe divided by Ie, giving 5.5 times 1.9 divided by 1.0 = 10.45 in, compared against Delta_a = 3.0 in for Risk Category II (all other structures), so the drift check fails and the lateral system must be stiffened.
  11. The correct answer is C (theta = 0.0826, from Px times Delta over V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equal to Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (3000 times 10.45) divided by (460 times 150 times 5.5) = 0.0826, a value checked against the 0.10 threshold that triggers required P-delta amplification of member forces.
  12. The correct answer is B (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since the period stays below 3.5Ts here.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T stays below 3.5Ts and the height limit is satisfied, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted for this structure.
Scenario S30

A seven-story reinforced concrete building with special reinforced concrete shear walls, Risk Category III (a public assembly space on the ground floor), is assigned to Seismic Design Category D with Ss = 1.30g and S1 = 0.58g on Site Class D. The plan is 84 ft wide with three shear wall lines resisting lateral force in the transverse direction, connected by a rigid post-tensioned concrete slab diaphragm at each level. R = 6, Cd = 5, Ie = 1.25.

  1. The correct answer is D (Rigid diaphragm; a concrete slab or concrete-filled deck may be idealized rigid without a deflection check.): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2, a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm may be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so story shear at this level is distributed to the 3 vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is D (x_cm = 42.0 ft, the geometric center of the 84 ft plan dimension.): For a plan symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls at the geometric centroid of the 84 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 42.0 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is A (x_r = 52.8 ft, from the k_i times x_i sum over the k_i sum.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity, sum of k_i = 380, gives x_r = 52.8 ft, a location offset from the geometric center because the 3 lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is D (e = 10.8 ft, the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity, giving e = absolute value of 42.0 minus 52.8 equals 10.8 ft, a value that must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is C (e_acc = 4.2 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 84 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 84 ft = 4.2 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is A (Mt = 8844 kip-ft, from Mt equal to V times the combined eccentricity of 15.0 ft.): Adding the two eccentricities for the worst-case torsional direction gives a total eccentricity of 10.8 plus 4.2 equals 15.0 ft, so Mt equals 590 kips times 15.0 ft = 8844 kip-ft, the moment used to compute torsional shear on each resisting line.
  7. The correct answer is B (302.8 kips, from V times k for that line divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 590 kips times (195 divided by 380) equals 302.8 kips carried by Shear Wall C.
  8. The correct answer is C (Approximately 75.2 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term, the sum of k_j times d_j squared over all lines; for Shear Wall A this evaluates to approximately 75.2 kips, which adds to that line's direct shear.
  9. The correct answer is B (Approximately 426.6 kips, the sum of its direct shear and torsional shear acting together.): The controlling line receives both its direct shear share and its torsional shear increment acting in the same rotational sense, giving a combined demand of approximately 426.6 kips on Shear Wall C, which governs that line's design.
  10. The correct answer is A (Delta = 2.72 in, which exceeds Delta_a = 2.0 in, so the drift check fails.): The amplified design story drift equals Cd times delta_xe divided by Ie, giving 5 times 0.68 divided by 1.25 = 2.72 in, compared against Delta_a = 2.0 in for Risk Category III (all other structures), so the drift check fails and the lateral system must be stiffened.
  11. The correct answer is A (theta = 0.03, from Px times Delta over V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equal to Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (4300 times 2.72) divided by (590 times 132 times 5) = 0.03, a value checked against the 0.10 threshold that triggers required P-delta amplification of member forces.
  12. The correct answer is B (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since the period stays below 3.5Ts here.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T stays below 3.5Ts and the height limit is satisfied, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted for this structure.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

Scenario S31

A nine-story steel building with special concentrically braced frames (SCBF), Risk Category II, is located in a hurricane-prone coastal region with a basic wind speed of 155 mph and Exposure Category D, and is also assigned to Seismic Design Category D with Ss = 1.05g and S1 = 0.47g on Site Class D, since wind governs the strength design of several brace lines while seismic drift governs others. A rigid composite concrete slab diaphragm spans a 115 ft plan dimension with three brace lines. R = 6, Cd = 5, Ie = 1.0.

  1. The correct answer is B (Rigid diaphragm; a concrete slab or concrete-filled deck may be idealized rigid without a deflection check.): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2, a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm may be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so story shear at this level is distributed to the 3 vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is B (x_cm = 57.5 ft, the geometric center of the 115 ft plan dimension.): For a plan symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls at the geometric centroid of the 115 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 57.5 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is A (x_r = 69.5 ft, from the k_i times x_i sum over the k_i sum.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity, sum of k_i = 550, gives x_r = 69.5 ft, a location offset from the geometric center because the 3 lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is A (e = 12.0 ft, the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity, giving e = absolute value of 57.5 minus 69.5 equals 12.0 ft, a value that must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is B (e_acc = 5.8 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 115 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 115 ft = 5.8 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is D (Mt = 14414 kip-ft, from Mt equal to V times the combined eccentricity of 17.8 ft.): Adding the two eccentricities for the worst-case torsional direction gives a total eccentricity of 12.0 plus 5.8 equals 17.8 ft, so Mt equals 810 kips times 17.8 ft = 14414 kip-ft, the moment used to compute torsional shear on each resisting line.
  7. The correct answer is C (397.6 kips, from V times k for that line divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 810 kips times (270 divided by 550) equals 397.6 kips carried by Brace Line 3.
  8. The correct answer is D (Approximately 103.7 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term, the sum of k_j times d_j squared over all lines; for Brace Line 1 this evaluates to approximately 103.7 kips, which adds to that line's direct shear.
  9. The correct answer is B (Approximately 538.5 kips, the sum of its direct shear and torsional shear acting together.): The controlling line receives both its direct shear share and its torsional shear increment acting in the same rotational sense, giving a combined demand of approximately 538.5 kips on Brace Line 3, which governs that line's design.
  10. The correct answer is C (Delta = 11.0 in, which exceeds Delta_a = 3.0 in, so the drift check fails.): The amplified design story drift equals Cd times delta_xe divided by Ie, giving 5 times 2.2 divided by 1.0 = 11.0 in, compared against Delta_a = 3.0 in for Risk Category II (all other structures), so the drift check fails and the lateral system must be stiffened.
  11. The correct answer is D (theta = 0.0978, from Px times Delta over V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equal to Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (5400 times 11.0) divided by (810 times 150 times 5) = 0.0978, a value checked against the 0.10 threshold that triggers required P-delta amplification of member forces.
  12. The correct answer is B (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since the period stays below 3.5Ts here.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T stays below 3.5Ts and the height limit is satisfied, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted for this structure.
Scenario S32

A thirteen-story reinforced concrete special moment frame building, Risk Category II, is assigned to Seismic Design Category D with Ss = 0.98g and S1 = 0.44g on Site Class D. A rigid two-way post-tensioned slab diaphragm spans a 135 ft plan dimension at each level, with three lateral frame lines contributing stiffness. The computed fundamental period of this building is long relative to its Seismic Design Category peers, prompting a direct comparison between the equivalent lateral force procedure and modal response spectrum analysis. R = 8, Cd = 5.5, Ie = 1.0.

  1. The correct answer is D (Rigid diaphragm; a concrete slab or concrete-filled deck may be idealized rigid without a deflection check.): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2, a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm may be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so story shear at this level is distributed to the 3 vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is D (x_cm = 67.5 ft, the geometric center of the 135 ft plan dimension.): For a plan symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls at the geometric centroid of the 135 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 67.5 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is B (x_r = 89.4 ft, from the k_i times x_i sum over the k_i sum.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity, sum of k_i = 575, gives x_r = 89.4 ft, a location offset from the geometric center because the 3 lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is A (e = 21.9 ft, the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity, giving e = absolute value of 67.5 minus 89.4 equals 21.9 ft, a value that must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is B (e_acc = 6.8 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 135 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 135 ft = 6.8 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is D (Mt = 26956 kip-ft, from Mt equal to V times the combined eccentricity of 28.7 ft.): Adding the two eccentricities for the worst-case torsional direction gives a total eccentricity of 21.9 plus 6.8 equals 28.7 ft, so Mt equals 940 kips times 28.7 ft = 26956 kip-ft, the moment used to compute torsional shear on each resisting line.
  7. The correct answer is B (506.8 kips, from V times k for that line divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 940 kips times (310 divided by 575) equals 506.8 kips carried by Frame Line 3.
  8. The correct answer is D (Approximately 150.0 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term, the sum of k_j times d_j squared over all lines; for Frame Line 1 this evaluates to approximately 150.0 kips, which adds to that line's direct shear.
  9. The correct answer is B (Approximately 743.8 kips, the sum of its direct shear and torsional shear acting together.): The controlling line receives both its direct shear share and its torsional shear increment acting in the same rotational sense, giving a combined demand of approximately 743.8 kips on Frame Line 3, which governs that line's design.
  10. The correct answer is B (Delta = 21.45 in, which exceeds Delta_a = 2.8 in, so the drift check fails.): The amplified design story drift equals Cd times delta_xe divided by Ie, giving 5.5 times 3.9 divided by 1.0 = 21.45 in, compared against Delta_a = 2.8 in for Risk Category II (all other structures), so the drift check fails and the lateral system must be stiffened.
  11. The correct answer is D (theta = 0.2044, from Px times Delta over V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equal to Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (6800 times 21.45) divided by (940 times 138 times 5.5) = 0.2044, a value checked against the 0.10 threshold that triggers required P-delta amplification of member forces.
  12. The correct answer is C (Modal response spectrum analysis is required, since the period exceeds 3.5Ts for this category and height.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T exceeds 3.5Ts for this Seismic Design Category and height, modal response spectrum analysis is required instead.
Scenario S33

A six-story steel building with eccentrically braced frames (EBF), Risk Category II, is assigned to Seismic Design Category D with Ss = 1.20g and S1 = 0.52g on Site Class D. A rigid concrete slab on composite metal deck diaphragm spans a 92 ft plan dimension at each floor, with three EBF lines resisting lateral force in the transverse direction, each detailed with a shear link per the AISC Seismic Design Manual, 3rd edition. R = 8, Cd = 4, Ie = 1.0.

  1. The correct answer is D (Rigid diaphragm; a concrete slab or concrete-filled deck may be idealized rigid without a deflection check.): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2, a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm may be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so story shear at this level is distributed to the 3 vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is C (x_cm = 46.0 ft, the geometric center of the 92 ft plan dimension.): For a plan symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls at the geometric centroid of the 92 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 46.0 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is A (x_r = 57.7 ft, from the k_i times x_i sum over the k_i sum.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity, sum of k_i = 475, gives x_r = 57.7 ft, a location offset from the geometric center because the 3 lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is B (e = 11.7 ft, the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and the center of rigidity, giving e = absolute value of 46.0 minus 57.7 equals 11.7 ft, a value that must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is A (e_acc = 4.6 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 92 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 92 ft = 4.6 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is C (Mt = 8468 kip-ft, from Mt equal to V times the combined eccentricity of 16.3 ft.): Adding the two eccentricities for the worst-case torsional direction gives a total eccentricity of 11.7 plus 4.6 equals 16.3 ft, so Mt equals 520 kips times 16.3 ft = 8468 kip-ft, the moment used to compute torsional shear on each resisting line.
  7. The correct answer is B (262.7 kips, from V times k for that line divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 520 kips times (240 divided by 475) equals 262.7 kips carried by EBF Line 3.
  8. The correct answer is A (Approximately 71.1 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term, the sum of k_j times d_j squared over all lines; for EBF Line 1 this evaluates to approximately 71.1 kips, which adds to that line's direct shear.
  9. The correct answer is D (Approximately 369.5 kips, the sum of its direct shear and torsional shear acting together.): The controlling line receives both its direct shear share and its torsional shear increment acting in the same rotational sense, giving a combined demand of approximately 369.5 kips on EBF Line 3, which governs that line's design.
  10. The correct answer is B (Delta = 5.6 in, which exceeds Delta_a = 2.9 in, so the drift check fails.): The amplified design story drift equals Cd times delta_xe divided by Ie, giving 4 times 1.4 divided by 1.0 = 5.6 in, compared against Delta_a = 2.9 in for Risk Category II (all other structures), so the drift check fails and the lateral system must be stiffened.
  11. The correct answer is D (theta = 0.0636, from Px times Delta over V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equal to Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (3400 times 5.6) divided by (520 times 144 times 4) = 0.0636, a value checked against the 0.10 threshold that triggers required P-delta amplification of member forces.
  12. The correct answer is C (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since the period stays below 3.5Ts here.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T stays below 3.5Ts and the height limit is satisfied, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted for this structure.

Scenario S37

A four-story steel special moment frame (SMF) office building, Risk Category II, is located in a region with Ss = 1.25g and S1 = 0.55g on Site Class D, placing it in Seismic Design Category D. The building plan is 100 ft wide by 140 ft long, with a rigid reinforced concrete slab on composite metal deck diaphragm at each floor. Three steel moment frame lines resist lateral force in the north-south direction, spaced along the 100 ft plan dimension, with R = 8 and Cd = 5.5.

  1. The correct answer is C (Rigid diaphragm, since concrete slab construction may be idealized as rigid without a deflection comparison.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2 permits a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm to be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so the story shear at this level is distributed to the vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is B (x_cm = 50.0 ft, the geometric center of the 100 ft plan dimension.): For a plan that is symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls exactly at the geometric centroid of the 100 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 50.0 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is A (x_r = 62.0 ft, from x_r = Sum(k_i x_i) divided by Sum(k_i) for the three lines.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity Sum(k_i) = 500 gives x_r = 62.0 ft, a location that does not coincide with the geometric center because the three lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is C (e = 12.0 ft, the absolute distance between the center of mass and rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and center of rigidity, |50.0 minus 62.0| = 12.0 ft, and this value must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is D (e_acc = 5.0 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 100 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 100 ft = 5.0 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is A (250.0 kips, from V times k divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 500 times (250/500) equals 250.0 kips carried by Frame Line 3.
  7. The correct answer is C (Approximately 45.5 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i over the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term Sum(k_j d_j squared); for Frame Line 1 this evaluates to approximately 45.5 kips, added to or subtracted from its direct shear.
  8. The correct answer is C (Approximately 319.7 kips, the sum of its direct and torsional shear acting together.): Frame Line 3's direct shear of 250.0 kips and its torsional shear increment of approximately 69.7 kips act in the same rotational sense on this line, so the combined design shear is 250.0 + 69.7 = approximately 319.7 kips, which governs Frame Line 3's connection design as the most heavily loaded line.
  9. The correct answer is B (Yes, Type 1a torsional irregularity exists, since the drift ratio 1.3 exceeds 1.2.): Dividing the maximum drift of 1.45 in by the average drift of 1.1 in gives a ratio of 1.3, which exceeds 1.2, so Type 1a torsional irregularity is present per Table 12.3-1 and must be addressed in the design.
  10. The correct answer is B (Delta_a = 3.1 in, using the Table 12.12-1 coefficient for Risk Category II.): Table 12.12-1 assigns an allowable drift coefficient based on Risk Category II and structure type; multiplying that coefficient by the 156 in story height gives Delta_a = 3.1 in for this story.
  11. The correct answer is B (theta = 0.016, from theta equals Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equals Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (3200 times 2.1) divided by (500 times 156 times 5.5) = 0.016, which must be checked against the 0.10 threshold.
  12. The correct answer is A (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since the period stays below 3.5Ts with no prohibited condition.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T stays below 3.5Ts and the height limit is satisfied, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted for this structure.
