Scenario S39

A two-story wood light-frame apartment building, Risk Category II, is assigned to Seismic Design Category C with Ss = 0.65g and S1 = 0.28g on Site Class D. The roof and floor diaphragms are wood structural panel sheathing over open web wood trusses, supported on a series of parallel light-frame shear wall lines spaced across a 60 ft diaphragm width. Wall sheathing is 15/32 in wood structural panel with 8d common nails.

  1. The correct answer is B (Flexible diaphragm, so shear is distributed to each wall line by its own tributary roof area.): A wood structural panel diaphragm supported on shear walls is permitted to be idealized as flexible per Section 12.3.1.1, so each wall line simply receives the shear tributary to its own supported roof or floor area rather than a stiffness-based share.
  2. The correct answer is A (26 over 60, equal to 0.4 of the total width.): For a flexible diaphragm, each wall line's share of the total shear equals its own tributary width divided by the total diaphragm width, giving 0.4 for Wall Line A.
  3. The correct answer is C (60.7 kips, from V times the tributary width ratio for that line.): Multiplying the total shear by the tributary width ratio gives 140 times (26/60) equals 60.7 kips carried by Wall Line A.
  4. The correct answer is D (2757.6 plf, from the design shear in pounds divided by the 22 ft length.): Unit shear equals the design shear force divided by the actual sheathed wall length: 60666 lb divided by 22 ft equals 2757.6 plf, compared to the tabulated capacity of the selected nailing schedule per SDPWS 2015.
  5. The correct answer is C (2833.3 plf, from the tributary shear in pounds divided by the 28 ft length.): Unit shear equals the tributary design shear divided by the actual sheathed length of Wall Line B: 79333 lb divided by 28 ft equals 2833.3 plf, compared against the tabulated nailing schedule capacity per SDPWS 2015.
  6. The correct answer is D (Approximately 17.5 kips, from the diaphragm moment divided by the 60 ft chord depth.): The total diaphragm force V = 140 kips must first be converted to a distributed load w = V/L = 140/60 = 2.333 kip/ft. Treating the diaphragm as a simple span beam, the maximum moment M equals wL squared over 8, which is 1050 kip-ft at midspan, and dividing by the 60 ft chord depth gives a chord force of approximately 17.5 kips.
  7. The correct answer is A (Approximately 22.1 kips, from the unit shear times the 8 ft opening length.): The collector element must carry diaphragm shear across the 8 ft opening: 2757.6 plf times 8 ft equals 22.1 kips, engineered as a continuous drag strut tying into the adjacent full-height wall segments.
  8. The correct answer is A (Accidental torsion applies; a flexible diaphragm cannot redistribute it, so each line uses its own shear.): Per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2, the 5 percent accidental eccentricity requirement still applies to structures with flexible diaphragms, but because a flexible diaphragm cannot redistribute torsional shear among its wall lines, each line continues to be designed for its own tributary shear rather than a torsionally redistributed value.
  9. The correct answer is B (Delta_a = 2.2 in, using the Table 12.12-1 coefficient for Risk Category II.): Table 12.12-1 assigns an allowable drift coefficient by Risk Category and structure type; multiplying that coefficient by the 108 in story height gives Delta_a = 2.2 in for this story.
  10. The correct answer is A (theta = 0.003, from Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting the given values into theta equals Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd yields theta = (380 times 0.55) divided by (140 times 108 times 4) = 0.003, checked against the 0.10 threshold that triggers required P-delta amplification.
  11. The correct answer is C (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since period stays below 3.5Ts with no exceedance here.): Table 12.6-1 governs the permitted analysis procedure using Seismic Design Category, the period relative to 3.5Ts, and structural height; since all of those criteria are satisfied here, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted.
  12. The correct answer is D (Diaphragm to collector, collector to shear wall, shear wall to anchorage, anchorage to foundation.): The correct load path begins with the diaphragm collecting the applied lateral force, continues through collectors and drag struts that gather shear into the shear wall lines, proceeds down through the shear walls, and ends with the sill plate and anchorage transferring the accumulated shear and overturning reaction into the foundation.
Scenario S40

An existing three-story steel building with concentrically braced frames is being evaluated for a change of occupancy that raises it to Risk Category III. The existing rigid concrete slab on metal deck diaphragm is retained. The building is assigned to Seismic Design Category C with Ss = 0.70g and S1 = 0.30g on Site Class D. Two ordinary concentrically braced frame (OCBF) lines resist lateral force in the direction under evaluation, spaced along a 70 ft plan dimension, with R = 3.25 and Cd = 3.25.

  1. The correct answer is B (Rigid diaphragm, since concrete slab construction may be idealized as rigid without a deflection comparison.): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.2 permits a concrete slab or concrete-filled metal deck diaphragm to be idealized as rigid without an explicit deflection comparison, so the story shear at this level is distributed to the vertical elements in proportion to their relative lateral stiffness rather than by tributary area.
  2. The correct answer is C (x_cm = 35.0 ft, the geometric center of the 70 ft plan dimension.): For a plan that is symmetric in mass distribution, the center of mass falls exactly at the geometric centroid of the 70 ft plan dimension, giving x_cm = 35.0 ft measured from Line 1.
  3. The correct answer is D (x_r = 44.8 ft, from x_r = Sum(k_i x_i) divided by Sum(k_i) for the two lines.): Summing k_i times x_i over the total rigidity Sum(k_i) = 250 gives x_r = 44.8 ft, a location that does not coincide with the geometric center because the two lines have unequal relative stiffness.
  4. The correct answer is D (e = 9.8 ft, the absolute distance between the center of mass and rigidity.): Inherent eccentricity equals the distance between the center of mass and center of rigidity, |35.0 minus 44.8| = 9.8 ft, and this value must still be combined with accidental eccentricity per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2.
  5. The correct answer is B (e_acc = 3.5 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 70 ft perpendicular plan dimension.): ASCE 7-16 requires assuming the center of mass is displaced 5 percent of the plan dimension measured perpendicular to the applied force, giving e_acc = 0.05 times 70 ft = 3.5 ft for this direction of loading.
  6. The correct answer is D (134.4 kips, from V times k divided by the sum of all rigidities.): For a rigid diaphragm, direct shear is distributed in proportion to relative rigidity: 210 times (160/250) equals 134.4 kips carried by Brace Line 2.
  7. The correct answer is C (Approximately 29.4 kips, from Mt times k_i times d_i over the polar stiffness sum.): Torsional shear on an element equals Mt times k_i times d_i divided by the polar stiffness term Sum(k_j d_j squared); for Brace Line 1 this evaluates to approximately 29.4 kips, added to or subtracted from its direct shear.
  8. The correct answer is B (Approximately 105.0 kips, the sum of its direct and torsional shear acting together.): Brace Line 2 sits on the side of the center of rigidity where the inherent torsional moment opposes its direct shear, so its torsional increment of approximately 29.4 kips subtracts from its direct shear of 134.4 kips, giving a combined demand of 134.4 - 29.4 = approximately 105.0 kips. Not every resisting line adds torsion to its direct shear; the sign depends on which side of the center of rigidity the line sits on relative to the direction of the inherent eccentricity.
  9. The correct answer is D (Yes, Type 1a torsional irregularity exists, since the drift ratio 1.24 exceeds 1.2.): Dividing the maximum drift of 1.3 in by the average drift of 1.05 in gives a ratio of approximately 1.24, which exceeds the 1.2 threshold, so Type 1a torsional irregularity is present per Table 12.3-1 and must be addressed in the design.
  10. The correct answer is A (Delta_a = 2.2 in, using the Table 12.12-1 coefficient for Risk Category III.): Table 12.12-1 assigns an allowable drift coefficient based on Risk Category III and structure type; multiplying that coefficient by the 144 in story height gives Delta_a = 2.2 in for this story.
  11. The correct answer is A (theta = 0.011, from theta equals Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd.): Substituting into theta equals Px times Delta divided by V times hsx times Cd gives theta = (1800 times 0.6) divided by (210 times 144 times 3.25) = 0.011, which must be checked against the 0.10 threshold.
  12. The correct answer is B (Equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted, since the period stays below 3.5Ts with no prohibited condition.): Table 12.6-1 ties the permitted analysis procedure to Seismic Design Category, structural height, and the ratio of the fundamental period to 3.5Ts; since T stays below 3.5Ts and the height limit is satisfied, the equivalent lateral force procedure remains permitted for this structure.

Scenario S41

A one-story reinforced masonry warehouse, Risk Category II, is assigned to Seismic Design Category C with Ss = 0.60g and S1 = 0.25g on Site Class D. Scenario S46

A 6-story Risk Category II office building located in Los Angeles, California uses steel special moment frames (SMF) as the seismic force-resisting system in both orthogonal directions, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site parameters are SDS = 1.10g and SD1 = 0.60g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category D. The ELF base shear is V = 850 kips. A typical second-floor beam-column joint uses a W24x76 beam (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi) framing into a W14x159 column (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi) through a reduced beam section (RBS) moment connection prequalified per AISC 358. The computed design story drift at this level is 0.021 times the story height.

  1. The correct answer is B (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 8): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel special moment frames (SMF) a response modification coefficient R = 8, reflecting their high ductility from distributed beam plastic hinging. Intermediate moment frames use R = 4.5 and ordinary moment frames use R = 3.5, both lower because they rely on less stringent connection detailing. The higher R for SMF permits a proportionally lower design base shear for the same seismic mass.
  2. The correct answer is D (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 5.5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 5.5 for steel special moment frames, used to amplify the drift computed under the reduced seismic forces to approximate the true inelastic drift demand. This amplified drift is then compared against the allowable story drift limits in Table 12.12-1. Cd is smaller than R because elastic drift already underestimates inelastic response by a lesser ratio than the force reduction.
  3. The correct answer is A (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 3): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 3 to steel special moment frames. This overstrength factor is multiplied by the code-level horizontal seismic force to estimate the maximum force the frame can deliver once beam hinges reach their probable strength, used for capacity-protected elements. SCBF and EBF use a lower Omega0 = 2 because their braces and links yield at a more predictable, lower force level.
  4. The correct answer is C (No structural height limit is imposed under this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 places no structural height limit on steel special moment frames in Seismic Design Categories D, E, or F, reflecting their high ductility and the stringent detailing required by AISC 341. Steel ordinary moment frames and intermediate moment frames carry much stricter or prohibitive height limits in SDC D through F because their connections cannot reliably sustain large inelastic rotations.
  5. The correct answer is B (An interstory drift angle of 0.04 rad at this connection): AISC 341 requires special moment frame connections, including the reduced beam section detail, to be qualified by testing to sustain an interstory drift angle of at least 0.04 radians while maintaining at least 80 percent of the beam's nominal plastic moment. Intermediate moment frame connections need only qualify to 0.02 radians, reflecting their lower ductility demand and correspondingly lower R factor.
  6. The correct answer is A (The beam segment from the column face through the reduced beam section cutout): AISC 341 defines the protected zone at an RBS connection as the region of the beam extending from the face of the column through the reduced section, since this is where the plastic hinge is engineered to form and undergo large inelastic strains. Welds, decking attachments, and other discontinuities are prohibited there because they could trigger premature fracture. The panel zone and midspan regions are not part of this protected zone.
  7. The correct answer is D (A calculated value of 1.0 for this design check): AISC 341 Section E3.4a requires the sum of the projected column moment strengths above and below the joint to exceed the sum of the projected beam moment strengths framing into the joint, expressed as a ratio greater than 1.0. This strong column-weak beam requirement forces the intended plastic hinging to occur in the beams rather than the columns, preserving overall frame stability during large inelastic response.
  8. The correct answer is C (The highly ductile member classification of AISC 341 Table D1.1): AISC 341 requires the beam flanges and web in a special moment frame to satisfy the highly ductile member width-to-thickness limits of Table D1.1, the most restrictive classification, because the beam must sustain large inelastic rotations at the plastic hinge without local buckling. Intermediate and ordinary moment frame members are only required to meet the less restrictive moderately ductile limits.
  9. The correct answer is B (The sum of the expected moments, Mpr, delivered by the beams framing into the joint): AISC 341 requires the panel zone shear demand to be computed from the probable maximum moments, Mpr, that the beams can deliver to the joint once they reach their expected plastic hinge strength, projected to the column face. This capacity-design approach ensures the panel zone can develop the full beam hinging mechanism without shearing prematurely, which would compromise the frame's intended ductile behavior.
  10. The correct answer is D (To force the plastic hinge to form within the reduced section, away from the weld): Trimming the beam flanges at the RBS deliberately reduces the beam's flexural strength at that location so the plastic hinge forms there rather than at the complete-joint-penetration weld at the column face, which was identified after the Northridge earthquake as prone to brittle fracture. This relocates yielding to a ductile, unwelded region of the beam, protecting the connection.
  11. The correct answer is C (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 2550 kips): ASCE 7-16 assigns Omega0 = 3 to steel special moment frames, so the amplified seismic load equals 850 kips multiplied by 3, which equals 2550 kips. Collectors and other elements requiring overstrength-level design must use this amplified force rather than the code-level seismic force to remain elastic while the moment frame beams hinge and dissipate energy as intended.
  12. The correct answer is A (It does not comply, since 0.021 exceeds the 0.020 allowable limit): For Risk Category II structures other than masonry cantilever shear wall or masonry wall structures four stories or less, ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1 sets the allowable story drift at 0.020 times the story height. The computed design story drift of 0.021 exceeds this limit, so the frame stiffness or member sizes must be revised until the drift ratio complies, regardless of the frame's overall ductility.
Scenario S47

A 3-story Risk Category IV fire station and emergency operations center in Seattle, Washington uses steel special concentrically braced frames (SCBF) with single-diagonal HSS braces as the seismic force-resisting system, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 1.00g and SD1 = 0.55g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category D, with an importance factor Ie = 1.5. The first-story braces are HSS8x8x1/2 (ASTM A500 Grade C, Fy = 50 ksi, Ry = 1.4, gross area Ag = 13.5 in^2), with a diagonal brace length of 19.8 ft in a story 14 ft tall. The frame resists a first-story seismic shear of 420 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 6): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 6 to steel special concentrically braced frames. This value reflects SCBF's reliance on brace yielding and buckling for ductility, which is less than the distributed beam hinging ductility of a special moment frame (R = 8) but considerably more than an ordinary concentrically braced frame (R = 3.25), which lacks the highly ductile brace detailing SCBF requires.
  2. The correct answer is A (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 5 for steel special concentrically braced frames. Applying this factor to the drift computed from the code-reduced seismic forces gives the anticipated actual inelastic drift, which must then be checked against the allowable story drift limit for this Risk Category IV structure.
  3. The correct answer is D (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 2): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 2 to steel special concentrically braced frames, lower than the Omega0 = 3 assigned to moment frames because brace yielding and controlled buckling produce a more predictable, lower peak force than distributed beam hinging. Elements connected to the braces, such as columns and collectors, must be designed for this amplified load.
  4. The correct answer is B (A structural height limit of 160 ft for this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 limits steel special concentrically braced frames to a structural height of 160 feet in Seismic Design Categories D, E, and F. This is more restrictive than the unlimited height permitted for special moment frames, but far more permissive than the 35-foot limit imposed on ordinary concentrically braced frames in the same seismic design categories.
  5. The correct answer is A (A maximum permitted slenderness ratio, KL/r, of approximately 96.3): AISC 341 Section F2.5a limits SCBF diagonal brace slenderness to KL/r no greater than 4.0 times the square root of E/Fy. Substituting E = 29,000 ksi and Fy = 50 ksi gives the square root of 580, which is 24.08, and 4.0 times that value equals 96.3. Since the actual computed slenderness of this brace falls well below 96.3, this limit governs the maximum allowable brace slenderness rather than a general column provision.
  6. The correct answer is C (The highly ductile member classification of AISC 341 Table D1.1): AISC 341 requires SCBF diagonal braces to satisfy the highly ductile member width-to-thickness limits of Table D1.1, since the braces must survive repeated buckling and straightening cycles without local wall fracture. This is the same restrictive classification required for SMF beams, reflecting the large inelastic demand both elements must sustain during a design-level earthquake.
  7. The correct answer is B (Tension braces must resist between about 30 and 70 percent of the force): AISC 341 Section F2.3 requires this tension-compression balance along any line of single-diagonal or X-braced bays so the frame does not rely almost entirely on brace buckling in one direction, which would produce unsymmetrical stiffness and strength. This provision does not apply the same way to chevron configurations, which are instead governed by the unbalanced-force beam design provisions.
  8. The correct answer is D (Each end of the brace, plus the brace midlength buckling region): AISC 341 Section F2.5b designates each end of the SCBF diagonal brace, as well as the region near its midlength where the brace is expected to buckle and form a plastic hinge, as the protected zone. Attachments or discontinuities within this zone could initiate fracture at exactly the location intended to deform in a stable, ductile manner during repeated inelastic cycles.
  9. The correct answer is A (An Ry value of 1.4 for this hollow structural section material): AISC 341 Table A3.1 assigns Ry = 1.4 to ASTM A500 Grade C hollow structural sections, reflecting the typical margin between actual and specified minimum yield stress for this product. This Ry value multiplies the specified Fy when computing the expected yield strength, RyFy, used to determine the probable tensile capacity that must be delivered to the brace connection design.
  10. The correct answer is B (An expected tensile strength of 945 kips delivered to the connection): The expected tensile strength equals RyFyAg, which is 1.4 times 50 ksi times 13.5 square inches, giving 945 kips. AISC 341 requires the brace connection, including the gusset plate and its welds or bolts, to develop this expected strength so that yielding of the brace itself controls the inelastic response rather than a brittle connection failure.
  11. The correct answer is D (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 840 kips): ASCE 7-16 assigns Omega0 = 2 to steel special concentrically braced frames, so the amplified seismic load equals 420 kips multiplied by 2, which equals 840 kips. Columns supporting braced frame connections are capacity-protected elements designed for this amplified force so they remain essentially elastic while the braces themselves yield or buckle to dissipate seismic energy.
  12. The correct answer is C (Providing a linear clearance, about twice the plate thickness, so the gusset can flex): This detailing, commonly called the 2t linear clearance model, allows the gusset plate to flex out of plane and accommodate the brace's end rotation as it buckles, preventing the plate from tearing at a restrained corner. A rigidly restrained gusset edge concentrates rotation demand and has caused documented gusset plate fractures in past earthquakes.
Scenario S48

A 4-story Risk Category II parking structure in San Francisco, California uses steel special concentrically braced frames (SCBF) in a chevron, or inverted-V, configuration, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 1.20g and SD1 = 0.70g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category D. At the roof level, the chevron braces are HSS6x6x3/8 (ASTM A500 Grade C, Fy = 50 ksi). Each brace has an expected tensile strength with a vertical, beam-perpendicular component of 300 kips, and an expected post-buckling compressive strength with a vertical component of 80 kips. The continuous beam above the chevron point is a W18x50 (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi) spanning a 24-foot braced bay.

  1. The correct answer is D (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 6): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 6 to steel special concentrically braced frames regardless of whether the bracing is arranged as single-diagonal, X-braced, or chevron, since the coefficient reflects the system's ductility source, brace yielding and buckling, rather than its geometric layout.
  2. The correct answer is B (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 5 for steel special concentrically braced frames, the same value used for any SCBF geometric configuration including this chevron arrangement. Applying Cd to the reduced-force elastic drift gives the anticipated actual inelastic drift for comparison against the allowable limit.
  3. The correct answer is C (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 2): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 2 to steel special concentrically braced frames, the same value used for this chevron configuration as for single-diagonal or X-braced SCBF layouts, since Omega0 reflects the predictable overstrength of yielding braces rather than their geometric arrangement.
  4. The correct answer is D (A structural height limit of 160 ft for this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 limits steel special concentrically braced frames to a structural height of 160 feet in Seismic Design Category D, the same limit that applies regardless of whether the bracing is arranged in a chevron, single-diagonal, or X pattern, since the height limit is tied to the SCBF system classification rather than its bay geometry.
  5. The correct answer is D (An expected tensile strength of 220 kips delivered to the connection): The net unbalanced vertical force equals the tension brace's vertical component minus the buckled compression brace's reduced post-buckling vertical component, or 300 kips minus 80 kips, which equals 220 kips. AISC 341 requires the chevron beam to be designed for this unbalanced condition because the compression brace loses most of its capacity once it buckles, while the tension brace continues pulling.
  6. The correct answer is A (The beam must be designed continuous, using full section properties, for this unbalanced force): AISC 341 Section F2.6a requires the chevron beam to be designed as a continuous member between its actual end supports, using its full section capacity, to carry the unbalanced vertical force that results once one brace buckles and loses most of its strength. The beam cannot be assumed to gain any vertical support from the braces themselves at the chevron point under this condition.
  7. The correct answer is B (The moderately ductile member classification of AISC 341 Table D1.1): AISC 341 requires SCBF beams and columns to satisfy the moderately ductile member width-to-thickness limits of Table D1.1, a less restrictive classification than the highly ductile limits required for the braces themselves, because the beams and columns are intended to remain essentially elastic while the braces yield or buckle to dissipate seismic energy.
  8. The correct answer is C (Both beam flanges need lateral bracing there, sized per stability provisions): Because a buckled compression brace can no longer be relied on to laterally restrain the beam at the chevron point, AISC 341 requires supplemental lateral bracing of both the top and bottom flanges there, proportioned using the stability bracing provisions, to prevent lateral-torsional buckling of the beam under the unbalanced brace condition.
  9. The correct answer is D (A maximum permitted slenderness ratio, KL/r, of approximately 96.3): The slenderness limit is independent of the specific HSS size chosen and depends only on E and Fy, so it remains 4.0 times the square root of 29,000 divided by 50, which is 4.0 times 24.08, or 96.3. The engineer must still verify the actual computed KL/r of the HSS6x6x3/8 brace at its true unbraced length falls below this limit.
  10. The correct answer is C (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 520 kips): The amplified seismic axial load equals the code-level force of 260 kips multiplied by Omega0 = 2 for steel special concentrically braced frames, which equals 520 kips. This capacity-design force ensures the column remains essentially elastic while the braces above yield or buckle to dissipate seismic energy.
  11. The correct answer is A (Computing the required strength directly from the adjusted, expected strengths of the yielding brace elements themselves): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.4.3.1 allows the required strength of an element to be based on the actual expected, adjusted strength of the brace or other yielding element that delivers force to it, rather than the blanket Omega0 multiplier, when that calculation can be reliably performed. This capacity-limited approach can be more accurate than the generic overstrength factor for elements directly connected to a brace.
  12. The correct answer is B (Because this local region can yield and rotate like the brace ends): AISC 341 extends the protected zone concept to the local beam region near the chevron gusset connection because this area can undergo local yielding and rotation demand similar to what occurs at the brace ends themselves, particularly under the unbalanced vertical force condition. Restricting welded attachments and discontinuities there helps avoid initiating a premature fracture at this highly stressed detail.
Scenario S49

A 5-story Risk Category II office building in Portland, Oregon uses steel eccentrically braced frames (EBF) with moment connections between the link and the column, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 0.95g and SD1 = 0.50g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category D. At the third floor, the link beam is a W16x36 (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi, plastic section modulus Zx = 64.0 in^3, web shear area Aw = 4.69 in^2) with a link length e = 30 inches, bounded by diagonal braces on each side.

  1. The correct answer is A (A plastic moment strength, Mp, of 3200 kip-in): The plastic moment strength Mp equals Zx multiplied by Fy, which is 64.0 in^3 times 50 ksi, giving 3200 kip-in. This value is used together with the plastic shear strength Vp to classify the link as a shear, intermediate, or flexural link under AISC 341 Section F3.5a.
  2. The correct answer is C (A plastic shear strength, Vp, of 140.7 kips): The plastic shear strength Vp equals 0.6 times Fy times Aw, which is 0.6 times 50 ksi times 4.69 square inches, giving 140.7 kips. This value, together with the plastic moment strength Mp, is used to compute the shear and flexural link length thresholds specified in AISC 341 Section F3.5a.
  3. The correct answer is B (A threshold link length of approximately 36.4 in): The shear link threshold equals 1.6 times Mp divided by Vp, which is 1.6 times 3200 kip-in divided by 140.7 kips, giving approximately 36.4 inches. A link with a length e at or below this value yields primarily in shear along its length rather than through flexural hinging at its ends.
  4. The correct answer is D (A threshold link length of approximately Shear link): Since e = 30 inches is less than 1.6Mp/Vp = 36.4 inches, AISC 341 classifies this as a shear link, meaning it yields predominantly in shear along its full length rather than through flexural hinging at its ends. Shear links are permitted the largest inelastic rotation angle of any EBF link category because shear yielding of the web is a very ductile, stable mechanism.
  5. The correct answer is C (A link rotation angle limit of 0.08 rad for this link category): AISC 341 permits shear links, which yield primarily through stable web shear yielding, a maximum link rotation angle of 0.08 radians, the most generous limit among the link categories. Flexural links are limited to 0.02 radians because flange local buckling under flexural hinging is a less ductile mechanism, and intermediate links are assigned a value interpolated between these two limits based on link length.
  6. The correct answer is B (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 8): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 8 to steel eccentrically braced frames with moment-resisting connections between the link and the column, the highest coefficient among the braced frame systems, reflecting the substantial ductility of the shear-yielding link combined with the added redundancy of a moment connection. Frames using non-moment connections at the column are instead assigned R = 7.
  7. The correct answer is A (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 4): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 4 to steel eccentrically braced frames, whether the connection between the link and the column is a moment connection or a non-moment connection, since Cd depends on the system's overall ductile link-yielding behavior rather than the specific column connection type.
  8. The correct answer is D (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 2): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 2 to steel eccentrically braced frames, the same overstrength factor used for concentrically braced frames, reflecting the relatively predictable overstrength that develops once the link reaches its expected shear or flexural capacity, including strain hardening.
  9. The correct answer is B (A structural height limit of 160 ft for this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 limits steel eccentrically braced frames to a structural height of 160 feet in Seismic Design Category D, the same limit applied to steel special concentrically braced frames, since both systems rely on well-detailed, highly ductile yielding elements suitable for taller structures than ordinary braced or moment frame systems permit.
  10. The correct answer is A (The link itself and the portion of the diagonal brace adjacent to the link connection): AISC 341 designates the link itself, along with the portion of the diagonal brace and beam adjacent to the link connection, as the protected zone in an eccentrically braced frame, since this is where large inelastic shear or flexural yielding is intended to occur. Welded attachments or other discontinuities elsewhere on the brace or beam outside this zone are not similarly restricted.
  11. The correct answer is D (Web doubler plates are not permitted, and holes for penetrations are not permitted within the link): AISC 341 prohibits both web doubler plates and penetration holes within the link length because either modification would alter the link's calculated shear strength and disrupt the stable, ductile shear-yielding mechanism the design relies on. These restrictions help ensure the link performs as analyzed under repeated large inelastic cycles.
  12. The correct answer is C (The link's expected strength, including strain hardening, so these stay elastic): AISC 341 requires the diagonal brace and the beam segment outside the link to be designed for the forces corresponding to the link's expected strength, including strain hardening effects, so that these adjacent elements remain essentially elastic while the link itself yields and dissipates seismic energy through stable inelastic cycling.
Scenario S50

A 4-story Risk Category III public school building in Anchorage, Alaska uses steel special moment frames (SMF) as the seismic force-resisting system, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 1.25g and SD1 = 0.80g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category E. A typical roof-level beam-column joint uses a W21x62 beam (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi, Zx = 144 in^3) framing into a W14x193 column through a bolted flange plate (BFP) moment connection prequalified per AISC 358. The frame resists a diaphragm collector force of 310 kips at this level, and the joint's projected sum of beam plastic moments, sum Mpb*, is 620 kip-ft.

  1. The correct answer is B (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 8): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 8 to steel special moment frames regardless of Risk Category or Seismic Design Category, since this coefficient depends on the system's inherent ductility from beam plastic hinging rather than the building's occupancy classification or seismicity level.
  2. The correct answer is D (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 5.5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 5.5 for steel special moment frames. This amplification factor is applied to the drift computed under the code-reduced seismic forces to estimate the true inelastic drift demand, which is then compared against the allowable story drift limit appropriate for this Risk Category III structure.
  3. The correct answer is A (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 3): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 3 to steel special moment frames. Diaphragm collectors and other capacity-protected elements in this school must be designed using this overstrength factor to remain essentially elastic while the moment frame beams hinge and dissipate seismic energy as intended.
  4. The correct answer is C (No structural height limit is imposed under this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 places no structural height limit on steel special moment frames in any Seismic Design Category, including Category E, because their highly ductile detailing under AISC 341 is considered adequate for use at any building height, unlike ordinary or intermediate steel moment frames, which face strict height limits or outright prohibition once the seismic design category becomes severe.
  5. The correct answer is A (An interstory drift angle of 0.04 rad at this connection): AISC 341 requires special moment frame connections, including a bolted flange plate detail, to be qualified to sustain an interstory drift angle of at least 0.04 radians while maintaining the required percentage of the beam's nominal plastic moment strength. This is the same 0.04 radian requirement that applies to any special moment frame connection type, including the reduced beam section detail.
  6. The correct answer is D (A calculated value of Just greater than 620 kip-ft for this design check): AISC 341 Section E3.4a requires the ratio of sum Mpc* to sum Mpb* to exceed 1.0, meaning the sum of the projected column moment strengths above and below the joint must be greater than the sum of the projected beam moment strengths, or greater than 620 kip-ft in this case. This margin ensures inelastic action concentrates in the beams rather than the columns.
  7. The correct answer is C (A calculated value of AISC 358 for this design check): AISC 358, Prequalified Connections for Special and Intermediate Steel Moment Frames for Seismic Applications, contains prequalification data, design procedures, and limits of applicability for connections such as the bolted flange plate and reduced beam section details, allowing their use in SMF and IMF without project-specific testing when the applicability limits are satisfied.
  8. The correct answer is B (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 930 kips): The amplified seismic load equals the code-level collector force of 310 kips multiplied by Omega0 = 3 for steel special moment frames, which equals 930 kips. Collectors are capacity-protected elements that must remain essentially elastic while the moment frame's beams hinge and dissipate the design earthquake's energy.
  9. The correct answer is D (The highly ductile member classification of AISC 341 Table D1.1): AISC 341 requires the beam flanges and web in a special moment frame to satisfy the highly ductile member width-to-thickness limits of Table D1.1, the most restrictive classification available, since the plastic hinge region must sustain large inelastic rotations without local flange or web buckling that could degrade the frame's strength prematurely.
  10. The correct answer is C (A calculated value of AISC 341 Section E3.6e for this design check): AISC 341 Section E3.6e governs the required shear strength check of the column panel zone in a special moment frame, requiring the demand to be computed from the probable moments the framing beams can deliver to the joint. Section F2.5a instead governs braced frame brace slenderness, and Section F3.5b addresses eccentrically braced frame link stiffener requirements, neither of which applies to this panel zone check.
  11. The correct answer is B (The top story of a multi-story special moment frame): AISC 341 Section E3.4a exempts columns in the top story of a multi-story special moment frame from the strong column-weak beam check, along with certain other limited conditions such as low axial load columns in one-story buildings, because a column hinge at the roof level does not create the same soft-story collapse mechanism risk as a hinge at a lower story.
  12. The correct answer is A (A redundancy factor, rho, having a design value of 1.3): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4 assigns a redundancy factor rho = 1.3 to structures assigned to Seismic Design Category D, E, or F unless the structure satisfies specific conditions demonstrating adequate redundancy, such as no single element whose failure would cause more than a 33 percent reduction in story strength or create an extreme torsional irregularity. When those conditions are met, rho may be taken as 1.0 instead.

Practice Test 11 Answer Key

Scenario S51

A 2-story Risk Category II retail building in Sacramento, California uses steel intermediate moment frames (IMF) as the seismic force-resisting system, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 0.90g and SD1 = 0.45g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category D. The building height is 30 feet, within the applicable IMF height limit. A typical beam-column joint uses a W18x40 beam (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi) framing into a W12x87 column through a bolted flange plate connection prequalified per AISC 358 for intermediate moment frames. The frame resists a code-level seismic base shear of 180 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 4.5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 4.5 to steel intermediate moment frames, a value between the R = 3.5 assigned to ordinary moment frames and the R = 8 assigned to special moment frames, reflecting an intermediate level of ductility detailing and connection qualification demand.
  2. The correct answer is B (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 4): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 4 for steel intermediate moment frames, between the Cd = 3 assigned to ordinary moment frames and Cd = 5.5 assigned to special moment frames, reflecting IMF's intermediate ductile drift behavior compared to the two other steel moment frame systems.
  3. The correct answer is D (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 3): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 3 to steel intermediate moment frames, the same overstrength factor assigned to steel special and ordinary moment frames, since all three moment frame systems share the same general beam-hinging overstrength characteristic despite their differing R and Cd values.
  4. The correct answer is A (A limit of 35 feet applies, and this building complies): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 limits steel intermediate moment frames to a structural height of 35 feet in Seismic Design Category D, reflecting their more limited ductility compared to special moment frames, which have no height limit. Since this building's height of 30 feet is less than the 35-foot limit, the IMF system is permitted for this structure.
  5. The correct answer is B (An interstory drift angle of 0.02 rad at this connection): AISC 341 requires intermediate moment frame connections to be qualified to sustain an interstory drift angle of at least 0.02 radians, half the 0.04 radian requirement for special moment frame connections. This lower qualification demand reflects the reduced ductility expected of an IMF system, consistent with its lower R factor and correspondingly higher design seismic force.
  6. The correct answer is D (The moderately ductile member classification of AISC 341 Table D1.1): AISC 341 requires intermediate moment frame beams and columns to satisfy the moderately ductile member width-to-thickness limits of Table D1.1, a less restrictive classification than the highly ductile limits required for special moment frame members, consistent with the lower inelastic rotation demand expected of an intermediate moment frame.
  7. The correct answer is A (A calculated value of AISC 358 for this design check): AISC 358 provides prequalification data and applicability limits for connections such as the bolted flange plate detail for use in both special and intermediate steel moment frames, allowing their use without project-specific cyclic qualification testing whenever the connection falls within the standard's stated limits of applicability.
  8. The correct answer is C (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 540 kips): The amplified seismic load equals the code-level base shear of 180 kips multiplied by Omega0 = 3 for steel intermediate moment frames, which equals 540 kips. Foundation and other elements supporting discontinuous portions of the seismic force-resisting system must be designed for this amplified force under ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.3.3.
  9. The correct answer is A (No, this explicit ratio check in AISC 341 applies only to SMF): This capacity design provision is specific to the special moment frame chapter of AISC 341 because SMF systems are designed to develop a full beam-hinging mechanism with high ductility demand. Intermediate moment frame systems have lower ductility demand and correspondingly less restrictive detailing requirements, and the standard does not extend this particular ratio check to them.
  10. The correct answer is D (Either an AISC 358 prequalified connection or cyclic testing per Chapter K): AISC 341 requires an intermediate moment frame connection to satisfy one of two acceptable qualification pathways: use of a connection prequalified per AISC 358 within its stated applicability limits, or qualification of the specific connection by cyclic testing performed according to the protocols of AISC 341 Chapter K.
  11. The correct answer is C (Parameters such as beam and column depth, weight, and material grade): AISC 358's prequalification limits of applicability restrict parameters such as beam depth, column depth, member weight, flange thickness, and material grade to the ranges actually demonstrated adequate by the cyclic test data underlying the prequalification, ensuring the connection is only used within a proven range of conditions.
  12. The correct answer is B (The amplified load need not exceed the true maximum deliverable force): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.4.3.1 recognizes that the Omega0-amplified seismic load is sometimes more conservative than the true maximum force the connected ductile elements can deliver, so it permits the engineer to cap the design force at this rationally determined capacity-limited value instead. This prevents overdesign of elements connected to elements with a well-defined yielding mechanism.
Scenario S52

A single-story Risk Category II industrial pipe rack support structure in Amarillo, Texas uses steel ordinary moment frames (OMF) as the seismic force-resisting system, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 0.35g and SD1 = 0.18g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category C. The frame is a single bay, single story, 22 feet tall, using a W16x31 beam (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi) framing into a W10x60 column through a fully restrained welded connection meeting the prescriptive requirements of AISC 341 Section E1. The frame resists a code-level seismic base shear of 65 kips.

  1. The correct answer is D (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 3.5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 3.5 to steel ordinary moment frames, the lowest coefficient among the three steel moment frame systems, reflecting the minimal ductile detailing OMF connections require compared to intermediate or special moment frames.
  2. The correct answer is A (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 3): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 3 for steel ordinary moment frames, the lowest deflection amplification factor among the steel moment frame systems, consistent with OMF's lower expected inelastic drift demand relative to intermediate or special moment frames.
  3. The correct answer is C (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 3): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 3 to steel ordinary moment frames, the same value assigned to intermediate and special steel moment frames, since all three moment frame systems share a comparable beam-hinging overstrength characteristic even though their R and Cd values differ substantially.
  4. The correct answer is B (No structural height limit is imposed under this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 places no structural height limit on steel ordinary moment frames in Seismic Design Categories B and C. The stricter limits, such as 35 feet with specific exceptions in Seismic Design Category D and outright prohibition in E and F, apply only once the site reaches the higher seismic design categories, where OMF's minimal ductility detailing becomes inadequate.
  5. The correct answer is D (Meeting the prescriptive strength requirements of AISC 341 Section E1.6b): AISC 341 allows ordinary moment frame connections to satisfy prescriptive strength-based criteria in lieu of the interstory drift angle qualification testing required for SMF and IMF connections, reflecting the lower ductility demand OMF systems are designed for. This is one reason OMF connections can be simpler and less costly than SMF or IMF connections.
  6. The correct answer is C (No highly ductile or moderately ductile classification; standard AISC 360 compact section requirements apply instead): AISC 341 Section E1 does not impose the highly ductile or moderately ductile width-to-thickness classifications used for SMF and IMF members, because OMF systems are designed for much lower ductility demand. Members need only satisfy the standard compact section limits of AISC 360, consistent with OMF's lower R factor and correspondingly higher design seismic force.
  7. The correct answer is A (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 195 kips): The amplified seismic load equals the code-level base shear of 65 kips multiplied by Omega0 = 3 for steel ordinary moment frames, which equals 195 kips. Column base connections and other capacity-protected elements must be designed for this amplified force under ASCE 7-16 Section 12.4.3.
  8. The correct answer is B (Lower seismic demand here does not require SMF or IMF detailing): Because this site's modest seismic demand and Seismic Design Category C classification do not require the high ductility detailing of SMF or IMF, an ordinary moment frame with its simpler, prescriptively qualified connections is an appropriate and more economical choice, even though it carries a higher design force from its lower R factor.
  9. The correct answer is C (A redundancy factor, rho, having a design value of 1.0): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4 requires the redundancy factor rho to be evaluated only for structures assigned to Seismic Design Category D, E, or F. Structures in Seismic Design Category B or C are automatically permitted a redundancy factor of 1.0, since the redundancy provisions were developed to address the higher seismic risk of the more severe seismic design categories.
  10. The correct answer is A (It is rigid enough that negligible rotation occurs between beam and column): A fully restrained moment connection is idealized in analysis as rigid enough that the relative rotation between the connected beam and column is negligible, allowing it to transfer moment essentially without rotational flexibility, unlike a simple shear connection, which is idealized as a pin transferring negligible moment.
  11. The correct answer is B (Wind loads must also be checked, and the governing case used): ASCE 7-16 requires the engineer to independently determine wind loads on the structure and to design for whichever load combination, wind or seismic, produces the more critical demand on each element, since assigning a Seismic Design Category does not exempt a structure from also satisfying the applicable wind load provisions.
  12. The correct answer is D (A collector element that gathers diaphragm force and delivers it to this ordinary moment frame): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1 requires collector elements, along with their connections to the seismic force-resisting system, to be designed for the amplified seismic load using Omega0 in structures assigned to Seismic Design Category C or higher above a certain size threshold. Typical gravity-only members not part of the seismic load path do not require this amplification.
Scenario S53

A 7-story Risk Category II mixed-use residential tower in San Diego, California uses steel eccentrically braced frames (EBF) with non-moment, simple shear-only connections between the link beam and the column, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has SDS = 1.05g and SD1 = 0.55g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category D. At the fourth floor, the link beam is a W21x44 (ASTM A992, Fy = 50 ksi, plastic section modulus Zx = 95.8 in^3, web shear area Aw = 5.83 in^2) with a link length e = 72 inches.

  1. The correct answer is A (A plastic moment strength, Mp, of 4790 kip-in): The plastic moment strength Mp equals Zx multiplied by Fy, which is 95.8 in^3 times 50 ksi, giving 4790 kip-in. This value is used together with the plastic shear strength Vp to determine whether the link behaves primarily as a shear-yielding or flexure-yielding element.
  2. The correct answer is D (A plastic shear strength, Vp, of 174.9 kips): The plastic shear strength Vp equals 0.6 times Fy times Aw, which is 0.6 times 50 ksi times 5.83 square inches, giving 174.9 kips. This value, together with Mp, determines the shear link and flexural link length thresholds under AISC 341 Section F3.5a for this frame.
  3. The correct answer is B (A threshold link length of approximately 71.2 in): The flexural link threshold equals 2.6 times Mp divided by Vp, which is 2.6 times 4790 kip-in divided by 174.9 kips, giving approximately 71.2 inches. A link with a length e at or above this value yields predominantly through flexural hinging at its ends rather than through shear along its length.
  4. The correct answer is C (A threshold link length of approximately Flexural link): Since e = 72 inches is greater than 2.6Mp/Vp = 71.2 inches, AISC 341 classifies this as a flexural link, meaning it yields predominantly through flexural plastic hinging at its ends rather than through web shear yielding. Flexural links are assigned a more restrictive rotation angle limit than shear links because flange local buckling under repeated flexural hinging is a less stable, less ductile failure mode.
  5. The correct answer is D (A link rotation angle limit of 0.02 rad for this link category): AISC 341 limits flexural links, which yield primarily through flange plastic hinging, to a maximum link rotation angle of 0.02 radians, considerably more restrictive than the 0.08 radian limit for shear links, because flange local buckling under repeated flexural cycling is a less ductile, less stable failure mode than web shear yielding.
  6. The correct answer is A (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 7): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 7 to steel eccentrically braced frames with non-moment connections between the link and the column, one point lower than the R = 8 assigned to EBF systems with moment connections at the column, since the non-moment connection provides one less redundant load path once the link has fully yielded.
  7. The correct answer is C (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 4): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Cd = 4 to steel eccentrically braced frames regardless of whether the link connects to the column with a moment connection or a non-moment connection, since Cd depends on the system's overall link-yielding ductile behavior rather than the specific column connection detail.
  8. The correct answer is B (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 2): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 2 to steel eccentrically braced frames, the same value used for concentrically braced frames, regardless of whether the link-to-column connection is a moment connection or a non-moment connection, since Omega0 reflects the link's expected overstrength rather than the column connection type.
  9. The correct answer is A (A structural height limit of 160 ft for this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 limits steel eccentrically braced frames to a structural height of 160 feet in Seismic Design Category D, the same limit applied to steel special concentrically braced frames, reflecting the well-detailed, highly ductile yielding elements both systems rely on for taller construction than ordinary systems permit.
  10. The correct answer is C (The link's expected strength, including strain hardening, so these stay elastic): AISC 341 requires the diagonal brace and the beam segment outside the link to be designed for the forces corresponding to the link's expected strength, including strain hardening effects, so that these adjacent elements remain essentially elastic while the link itself yields and dissipates the earthquake's energy through stable inelastic cycling.
  11. The correct answer is D (Intermediate link, with rotation limit interpolated between the two values): AISC 341 classifies a link with a length between 1.6Mp/Vp and 2.6Mp/Vp as an intermediate link, which yields through a combination of shear and flexural action. Its permitted rotation angle is linearly interpolated between the 0.08 radian limit for shear links and the 0.02 radian limit for flexural links, based on where the actual link length falls within that range.
  12. The correct answer is B (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 550 kips): The amplified seismic load equals the code-level collector force of 275 kips multiplied by Omega0 = 2 for steel eccentrically braced frames, which equals 550 kips. Collectors delivering diaphragm force into an EBF must be designed for this amplified force so they remain essentially elastic while the link yields.
Scenario S54

A single-story Risk Category II pre-engineered metal warehouse building near Corpus Christi, Texas uses steel ordinary concentrically braced frames (OCBF) as the lateral force-resisting system, designed per ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341-16. The site has a basic wind speed of 145 mph for Risk Category II per ASCE 7-16 Figure 26.5-1B, with Exposure Category C, while the seismic parameters are modest, SDS = 0.20g and SD1 = 0.09g, placing the structure in Seismic Design Category B. Wind governs the lateral design. The braces are single-diagonal HSS6x6x1/4 (ASTM A500 Grade C, Fy = 50 ksi), and the frame resists a wind base shear of 95 kips at the eave level, well above the seismic base shear.

  1. The correct answer is B (A response modification coefficient, R, equal to 3.25): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 3.25 to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames, the lowest R value among the steel braced frame systems, reflecting the minimal ductile brace detailing OCBF requires compared to the highly ductile bracing of a special concentrically braced frame.
  2. The correct answer is C (A deflection amplification factor, Cd, equal to 3.25): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists Cd = 3.25 for steel ordinary concentrically braced frames, equal to the R value for this system, unlike most other systems where Cd differs from R, reflecting a design relationship specific to this lower-ductility braced frame category.
  3. The correct answer is A (An overstrength factor, Omega0, having a design value of 2): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega0 = 2 to steel ordinary concentrically braced frames, the same value used for special concentrically braced frames and eccentrically braced frames, reflecting a comparable expected brace overstrength once the diagonal braces reach their tension or compression capacity.
  4. The correct answer is D (No structural height limit is imposed under this seismic design category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 places no structural height limit on steel ordinary concentrically braced frames in Seismic Design Categories A, B, and C. The more restrictive limits, such as 35 feet with exceptions in Seismic Design Category D and prohibition in E and F, apply only in the higher seismic design categories where OCBF's minimal ductility detailing becomes inadequate.
  5. The correct answer is C (The assigned R factor still requires this system's seismic detailing): Once a structure is assigned an R factor from a specific seismic force-resisting system category, ASCE 7-16 and AISC 341 require the corresponding detailing requirements to be satisfied regardless of whether wind or seismic forces ultimately control the design force magnitude, since the seismic detailing provisions govern the ductility and load path behavior the system must provide under any lateral demand.
  6. The correct answer is A (No, OCBF braces instead follow the standard AISC 360 compression provisions): OCBF systems are permitted less restrictive detailing than SCBF because they are assigned a lower R factor and are intended for use where less inelastic ductility demand is expected. The stringent 4.0 times the square root of E/Fy brace slenderness limit, along with the highly ductile member classification, is a special requirement unique to the SCBF provisions of AISC 341 Section F2.
  7. The correct answer is D (No, Omega0 amplifies only the seismic term, never the wind term): Omega0 is a seismic overstrength factor that amplifies only the horizontal seismic load term within the seismic load combinations of ASCE 7-16 Section 12.4.3. Wind design forces are computed and factored entirely separately under ASCE 7-16's wind provisions, so Omega0 has no role in amplifying wind loads even when wind governs the overall lateral design of this warehouse.
  8. The correct answer is B (Open terrain with scattered obstructions generally under 30 feet in height): ASCE 7-16 Section 26.7 defines Exposure Category C as open terrain with scattered obstructions generally less than 30 feet in height, including flat open country, grasslands, and shorelines in non-hurricane-prone regions. This exposure results in higher wind pressures than the more sheltered Exposure Category B because less surface roughness exists to slow the approaching wind.
  9. The correct answer is D (Figure 26.5-1B, which applies to Risk Category II structures): ASCE 7-16 provides separate basic wind speed maps for each Risk Category, with Figure 26.5-1B applying specifically to Risk Category II structures. Risk Category III uses Figure 26.5-1A, and Risk Category IV uses Figure 26.5-1C, each map reflecting the different mean recurrence interval appropriate to that category's consequence of failure.
  10. The correct answer is B (Amplified seismic load = 36 kips, wind still governs since it exceeds this amplified seismic value): The amplified seismic load equals the code-level seismic base shear of 18 kips multiplied by Omega0 = 2, which equals 36 kips. Since the 95 kip wind base shear still exceeds this amplified seismic value, the wind load combination continues to govern the design of the brace connection, illustrating that computing the seismic overstrength load is still necessary to confirm which case actually controls.
  11. The correct answer is A (Low seismic demand here does not justify SCBF's costlier detailing): Because this site's low seismic demand and Seismic Design Category B classification do not require the highly ductile brace detailing of an SCBF, an ordinary concentrically braced frame with its simpler, less restrictive connection and slenderness requirements is an appropriate and more economical choice, particularly since wind, not seismic, controls the governing lateral design force here.
  12. The correct answer is A (A drag strut that collects diaphragm shear into the braced bay): A drag strut, also called a collector, gathers diaphragm shear along its length wherever the diaphragm boundary does not align directly with a vertical braced bay, and delivers that accumulated shear into the braced frame. Without a collector, the diaphragm cannot fully engage the braced bays that are not aligned with the loaded diaphragm edge.
Scenario S55

An 8-story steel office building in Seattle, Washington (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 1.00g, SD1 = 0.60g) uses Special Moment Frames (SMF) as its seismic force-resisting system in both orthogonal directions. Seismic Design Category D governs. Typical bay spacing is 30 ft, story height is 13 ft, and beam-to-column moment connections use reduced beam section (RBS) details per AISC 358 with demand critical welds at the column flanges.

  1. The correct answer is A (R equal to 8.0, the value tabulated for steel special moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel special moment frames an R of 8.0 because of their high ductility capacity from strong-column weak-beam behavior and prequalified connections. SCBF, IMF, and OMF are separate system lines with lower R values that do not apply to this SMF building. Using the wrong R would misstate the design base shear for the actual system installed. The engineer must match the R value to the specific detailed system, not just the general frame type.
  2. The correct answer is B (Cd equal to 5.5, matched to the special moment frame system): Table 12.2-1 pairs steel SMF with Cd = 5.5, which is applied to the elastic displacements from the reduced design seismic forces to estimate actual expected inelastic drift. Cd is deliberately smaller than R because inelastic drift amplification differs from strength reduction, so setting Cd equal to R, as one wrong choice does, is a common but incorrect assumption. The Cd values for OMF and SCBF belong to different system rows and do not apply here. Selecting the wrong Cd would understate or overstate the drift check against the ASCE 7-16 Chapter 12 limit.
  3. The correct answer is C (The overstrength factor Omega0 equal to 3.0 for steel special moment frames): ASCE 7-16 requires the overstrength seismic load, using Omega0 = 3.0 for SMF systems, when checking elements such as column splices and foundations that must remain essentially elastic under the true maximum force the ductile system can deliver. Cd is used only for drift, and R is used only to reduce elastic demand for strength design of the ductile members themselves. The importance factor scales the base shear itself but is not the capacity-design overstrength multiplier. Applying Omega0 protects the non-yielding elements from a force larger than the frame's actual overstrength capacity.
  4. The correct answer is D (Forcing flexural yielding to occur away from the column face): The RBS detail intentionally trims the beam flanges over a short length so that the beam's plastic hinge forms in that weakened zone rather than at the highly stressed column face weld, reducing strain demand on the brittle-prone connection region. This does not increase stiffness, since removing flange material actually reduces local section stiffness slightly. It is not primarily a material-saving measure, and continuity plates are still typically required based on separate panel zone and flange thickness criteria. The RBS strategy directly addresses the pre-Northridge lesson that weld fractures concentrated at the column face.
  5. The correct answer is B (Welded shear studs and other attachments are not permitted): AISC 341 prohibits welded attachments such as shear studs, decking supports, or other appurtenances within the designated protected zone because notches or discontinuities there can trigger fracture at the very location expected to undergo large inelastic strain. Continuity plates are a panel zone detail tied to the column flange, not something added inside the beam's protected zone. Field bolting the web is unrelated to the protected zone restriction itself. Doubler plates address panel zone shear strength and are placed on the column web, not within the beam's protected region.
  6. The correct answer is A (Sum of column plastic moments must exceed sum of beam plastic moments): The strong-column weak-beam check requires the sum of the projected column flexural strengths above and below the joint, adjusted for axial load, to exceed the sum of the projected beam flexural strengths framing into that joint, with a required ratio typically at least 1.0. This forces plastic hinging into the beams rather than the columns, preserving the vertical load-carrying columns during the earthquake. Reversing the inequality would permit soft-story column hinging, which is the exact failure mode this provision is written to prevent. Shear and axial strength comparisons are separate checks and do not substitute for this flexural hierarchy requirement.
  7. The correct answer is D (The k-area has reduced toughness from the cold-rolling process): The k-area, where the web and flange fillet region is cold-worked during rolling, has been shown to have locally reduced notch toughness and increased hardness, making it susceptible to cracking when welding or punching introduces additional strain concentration nearby. This is a material property concern, not simply an issue of the region carrying no load, since the k-area still participates in force transfer. It has no connection to visual inspection requirements or to the location of the RBS cut, which is made in the flange further from the column face. AISC 341 and AWS guidance specifically limit fabrication operations in this zone to avoid brittle fracture initiation.
  8. The correct answer is C (The column flange thickness cannot resist the beam flange force): Continuity plates are required when the column flange and web are not thick or strong enough on their own to transfer the concentrated beam flange force into the column without excessive local flange bending or web crippling, based on AISC 358 and AISC 360 strength checks. A beam deeper than the column is a geometric concern addressed separately and does not by itself define the continuity plate trigger. The RBS cut percentage affects the beam's own flexural strength, not the column's local strength check. Weld inspection status is a quality control step unrelated to whether continuity plates are structurally necessary.
  9. The correct answer is A (The beam and column sizes fall within the prequalification limits): AISC 358 prequalification for RBS connections applies only within specific limits on beam depth, weight, flange thickness, and column size documented in that standard, so the engineer must confirm the actual project members fall inside those tested parameter ranges. Architectural approval of appearance has no bearing on structural prequalification validity. A fabricator's prior experience with the detail is a workmanship consideration, not a code prequalification requirement. Prequalification does not depend on the bay's location within the building footprint.
  10. The correct answer is C (0.020 times the story height for this structure and risk category): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1 sets the allowable story drift for Risk Category I or II structures other than masonry shear wall structures, four stories or less with certain conditions, at 0.020 times the story height, which governs typical moment frame buildings like this one. The 0.010 limit is more restrictive and applies to different structure and risk category combinations, not this case. The 0.025 limit is associated with other structural types, and 0.015 corresponds to Risk Category III and IV general buildings, not II. Applying the wrong drift limit could either wrongly fail or wrongly pass this frame's serviceability check.
  11. The correct answer is B (Splices are protected elements needing extra strength margin there): Column splices in SMF systems are designed as capacity-protected elements located away from the beam flange region where the largest inelastic column moment and shear demands concentrate, and AISC 341 requires the splice to develop the required strength associated with the plastic hinging mechanism rather than being placed at a point of peak demand. It is not true that splices are non-structural, and their location is chosen specifically because failure there would be highly consequential to load path continuity. Splice placement has no meaningful effect on overall building seismic weight. Locating splices at reduced-demand elevations reduces the chance of splice fracture during the design earthquake.
  12. The correct answer is D (The column web region bounded by the beam flanges at the joint): The panel zone is the column web region between the top and bottom beam flanges at a moment connection, and AISC 341 requires this region to have adequate shear strength, often supplemented with doubler plates, to participate appropriately in the frame's ductile behavior without excessive shear distortion. The beam web away from the joint and the RBS cut are governed by separate beam flexural and shear checks, not the panel zone provisions. Anchor bolts and diaphragm chords are entirely different structural elements addressed by foundation and diaphragm design provisions respectively. Panel zone shear yielding, when properly proportioned, contributes ductility to the overall frame mechanism.

Practice Test 12 Answer Key

Scenario S56

A 4-story steel distribution warehouse near Memphis, Tennessee (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.95g, SD1 = 0.55g) is assigned Seismic Design Category D. The lateral system consists of Special Concentrically Braced Frames (SCBF) with a mix of two-story X-braced bays at the exterior and single-story chevron (inverted-V) braced bays at the interior, using HSS brace members and bolted gusset plate connections to wide-flange beams and columns.

  1. The correct answer is B (R equal to 6.0, the value tabulated for special concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel SCBF systems an R of 6.0, reflecting moderate ductility from the highly ductile brace detailing and capacity-designed connections, which is lower than the moment frame value but higher than the ordinary braced frame value. The OMF and SMF rows do not apply because this building uses braced frames, not moment frames. OCBF's lower R of 3.25 applies only to ordinary detailing, which this project exceeds by using SCBF requirements. Using R = 6.0 correctly reflects the actual detailed ductility level installed in this warehouse.
  2. The correct answer is D (Cd equal to 5.0, tabulated for special concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 pairs SCBF with Cd = 5.0, distinct from its R = 6.0, because the drift amplification behavior of a braced frame differs from its strength reduction behavior and the two coefficients are calibrated independently. Setting Cd equal to R, as one wrong choice does, ignores this deliberate distinction the table makes for every system line. The OCBF and SMF Cd values belong to different system rows that do not match this project's SCBF detailing. Using the correct Cd is essential for an accurate ASCE 7-16 Chapter 12 story drift check.
  3. The correct answer is A (Tension braces must resist between 30 and 70 percent of the total horizontal force): AISC 341 requires SCBF configurations to be arranged so that, along any line of bracing, braces in tension resist between 30 and 70 percent of the total horizontal force, ensuring both tension and compression braces meaningfully participate rather than the system relying on only one direction of action. Requiring all force in tension only ignores the real post-buckling behavior of the compression brace, which still contributes reduced but nonzero capacity. Similarly, assuming the compression brace alone resists everything is inconsistent with brace buckling behavior. This balanced participation requirement is a defining feature that distinguishes SCBF from less regulated bracing arrangements.
  4. The correct answer is C (It delays local buckling so the brace can sustain large inelastic cycles): The highly ductile width-to-thickness limits in AISC 341 Table D1.1 restrict how slender the HSS walls can be, which delays local wall buckling and fracture so the brace can undergo the repeated inelastic tension-compression cycles expected during the design earthquake without premature failure. This classification is a structural performance requirement, not primarily a cost-reduction or appearance measure. It also does not directly simplify gusset plate geometry, which is governed by separate connection design provisions. Meeting the highly ductile limit is essential to achieving the ductile brace behavior assumed when R = 6.0 was selected.
  5. The correct answer is C (A linear clearance in the gusset plate that permits brace end rotation): AISC 341 requires that gusset plates be detailed with adequate clearance, commonly evaluated using an elliptical or linear clearance model, so the brace can rotate at its end as it buckles without overstressing the plate through restrained bending at that hinge line. Eliminating rotation entirely with a rigid weld would force damaging bending stresses into the plate rather than allowing the intended ductile buckling mechanism. Simply reducing bolt count does not address the geometric clearance requirement and could compromise connection strength. Increasing plate thickness without providing clearance does not solve the rotational restraint problem and can actually worsen it.
  6. The correct answer is A (An unbalanced vertical force applied when one brace buckles and the other yields in tension): In a chevron configuration, the compression brace loses much of its strength after buckling while the tension brace continues to develop its full expected yield strength, producing a net unbalanced vertical force at the beam midspan connection that the beam must be designed to resist while remaining essentially elastic. A two-story X-brace configuration does not concentrate this unbalanced force at a single midspan beam point in the same way. The chevron geometry does not reduce required beam depth, since the beam must still span the full bay under this unbalanced demand. Beam flexural design is not eliminated but rather becomes more demanding due to this specific capacity design load case.
  7. The correct answer is D (The expected yield strength of the brace, using the material overstrength factor Ry): AISC 341 requires brace connections to be designed for the expected strength of the brace, computed using Ry times the specified minimum yield stress, so the connection can deliver the brace's full ductile capacity without failing first in a brittle manner. Using the reduced code-level seismic force from the R factor would understate the true force the yielding brace can deliver to its connection. Wind load combinations and gravity dead load are separate load cases that do not represent the governing capacity design demand for a seismic brace connection. This expected-strength approach ensures the connection is stronger than the brace itself, forcing ductile behavior into the brace.
  8. The correct answer is B (The buckled brace retains reduced but nonzero post-buckling compression capacity): AISC 341's capacity design provisions recognize that a buckled brace continues to provide a reduced, but not zero, post-buckling compression resistance through several inelastic cycles, and this residual behavior is accounted for when checking connected elements like the chevron beam for unbalanced force. Assuming the brace loses all capacity the instant it buckles would understate the demand transferred to adjacent members in some load combinations. A buckled concentric brace does not transform into a moment connection, since its end connections remain pinned or near-pinned by design. Strain hardening can increase tension capacity somewhat but does not describe the compression buckling behavior being addressed here.
  9.
