The correct answer is D (Both configurations rely on the same tension-compression brace ductility mechanism): Whether braces are arranged as a two-story X or a single-story chevron, SCBF detailing is applied uniformly because both configurations rely on the same fundamental mechanism of ductile brace yielding in tension and controlled post-buckling behavior in compression to dissipate seismic energy. Beam depths are a separate sizing decision driven by span and unbalanced force demands, not a detailing category trigger. Neither configuration is exempt from capacity design connection rules, since both require expected-strength-based connection design. Column continuity checks still apply to both configurations because columns remain capacity-protected elements in either brace arrangement.
  10. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equal to 2.0, tabulated for special concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns SCBF systems an overstrength factor Omega0 of 2.0, which must amplify the horizontal seismic load effect when checking capacity-protected elements such as foundations, collectors, and brace connections against the maximum force the braced frame can realistically deliver. The SMF Omega0 of 3.0 belongs to a different system row and does not apply to this braced frame. OMF's Omega0 value is also unrelated to this braced system. Overstrength load effects are required for essentially every seismic force-resisting system in ASCE 7-16, so assuming it does not apply is incorrect.
  11. The correct answer is B (Braced frames are typically stiffer than moment frames of comparable size, reducing drift): Concentrically braced frames generally provide substantially greater lateral stiffness than moment frames of similar member size and bay geometry because the diagonal braces resist lateral load primarily through axial action rather than flexure, which is an efficient mechanism for controlling drift in a warehouse where large open floor areas are still needed. It is not accurate to claim braced frames are always more flexible, since that contradicts typical axial versus flexural stiffness behavior. Drift results between the two system types are not identical, since their load paths and Cd values differ. Braced frame stiffness depends heavily on brace cross-sectional area and orientation, not just beam depth.
  12. The correct answer is A (Axial force amplified by system overstrength combined with concurrent flexure): Columns in SCBF systems are capacity-protected elements and must be checked for the axial force amplified using the system overstrength factor Omega0, combined with any concurrent flexure from brace eccentricities or frame action, ensuring the column remains essentially elastic while the braces yield. Using only the unamplified code-level force would understate the true demand the yielding braces can transmit into the column. Assuming wind always governs ignores that seismic overstrength combinations frequently control column design in high SDC regions like this Memphis site. Considering only gravity loads ignores the lateral force path entirely, which is inappropriate for a primary lateral system column.
Scenario S57

A 3-story steel hospital support annex in Los Angeles, California (Risk Category IV, Site Class D, SDS = 1.10g, SD1 = 0.65g) is assigned Seismic Design Category D. The lateral system is an Eccentrically Braced Frame (EBF) with a single diagonal brace framing into a short shear link at midspan of each 32 ft beam bay, with moment connections between the beam and column located away from the link.

  1. The correct answer is B (R equal to 8.0, tabulated for EBF with moment connections at the column away from the link): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 distinguishes two EBF configurations, and the one with moment connections between beam and column away from the link is assigned R = 8.0, reflecting the higher ductility contribution from that additional moment frame action combined with link yielding. The alternative EBF configuration without moment connections at the column carries the lower R = 7.0 value, which does not match this project's described connections. SCBF's R = 6.0 belongs to a concentric bracing system, not this eccentric configuration. Correctly identifying which EBF sub-type governs is essential because the two configurations carry different R, Cd, and Omega0 values in the table.
  2. The correct answer is D (Cd equal to 4.0, matched to this EBF configuration): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 pairs the EBF configuration with moment connections away from the link with Cd = 4.0, independent of its R = 8.0 value, since drift amplification and strength reduction are calibrated separately for each system row. Setting Cd equal to R conflates two distinct coefficients that the table intentionally differentiates. The SCBF and OMF Cd values belong to entirely different system lines that do not describe this eccentrically braced hospital frame. Using Cd = 4.0 correctly scales the elastic displacement results to estimate the expected inelastic story drift for the Chapter 12 drift check.
  3. The correct answer is A (A shear link, which yields primarily through shear deformation of the web): When the link length e is short enough that e is less than or equal to 1.6 times the ratio of plastic moment Mp to plastic shear strength Vp, the link is classified as a shear link, meaning its web yields in shear before the flanges reach their flexural capacity, which is the intended ductile mechanism for this configuration. A flexural link instead has a longer length where flexural yielding at the link ends governs, corresponding to a different length criterion. An intermediate link falls between these two length thresholds, exhibiting combined behavior rather than clean shear yielding. This link is not rigid, since the entire EBF concept depends on the link yielding to dissipate seismic energy.
  4. The correct answer is C (0.08 radian, the limit specified for shear links): AISC 341 limits the inelastic rotation angle of a shear link to 0.08 radian, reflecting the substantial ductility capacity of web shear yielding under cyclic loading, which is significantly larger than what is permitted for flexural links. The 0.02 radian limit instead applies to flexural links, where flange local buckling limits achievable rotation capacity, and using it here would understate this shear link's actual permitted ductility. There is no code basis for an arbitrary 0.20 radian limit in this context. The 0.005 radian value describes an essentially elastic rotation, not the inelastic capacity this ductile fuse element is designed to achieve.
  5. The correct answer is A (Intermediate full-depth web stiffeners are required at spacing based on link length and rotation angle): AISC 341 requires shear links to include intermediate full-depth web stiffeners spaced according to the link's length and the design rotation angle demand, since these stiffeners delay web buckling and allow the link to achieve its full inelastic rotation capacity through repeated cycles. Omitting stiffeners entirely would risk premature web buckling well before the link reaches its intended ductile capacity. Stiffeners must extend the full depth of the web, not just attach to one flange side, to be effective against shear buckling. A single midspan stiffener alone is generally insufficient for longer links and does not satisfy the spacing-based requirement tied to rotation demand.
  6. The correct answer is D (Welded attachments such as decking supports are not permitted on the link): The link is designated a protected zone because it is expected to undergo large inelastic rotations, so AISC 341 prohibits welded attachments such as deck supports or other appurtenances that could introduce notches or discontinuities that trigger premature fracture during cyclic yielding. It is not correct that attachments are required at the link ends, since the goal is to keep the link free of such details, not to add them. The restriction targets welding specifically because of heat-affected zone and notch concerns, not bolting alone. This protected zone restriction applies to the link regardless of its specific length, since all shear and intermediate links undergo significant cyclic inelastic demand.
  7. The correct answer is C (The expected shear strength of the link, amplified using the link overstrength factor): AISC 341 requires the brace, beam segment outside the link, and column to be designed as capacity-protected elements using the axial and moment demands generated by the link's expected shear strength, which includes strain hardening effects captured by a link overstrength factor, ensuring these elements remain essentially elastic while the link yields. Using only the unamplified code-level seismic force would significantly understate the true force the yielding link delivers to the brace. Gravity live load reactions and wind load combinations are separate load cases that do not represent this seismic capacity design demand. This approach concentrates ductile behavior exclusively within the link, protecting the surrounding frame elements.
  8. The correct answer is B (Sizing the outside beam segment for forces from the link's expected strength): The beam segment outside the link is a capacity-protected element and must be proportioned to remain essentially elastic under the axial force and moment generated by the link's expected shear strength, consistent with the same capacity design philosophy applied to the brace and column. Designing it for only the unreduced elastic seismic force does not correctly capture the specific mechanism-based demand transmitted from the yielding link. Allowing this beam segment to yield alongside the link would defeat the purpose of concentrating ductile behavior in a single, well-detailed fuse element. This beam segment is very much part of the lateral load path and cannot be ignored in the design.
  9. The correct answer is C (The design story drift, the bay geometry, and the link length): The link rotation angle is estimated geometrically from the design story drift combined with the bay width, story height, and link length, since the overall frame racking deformation is concentrated into the much shorter link length, amplifying the rotation demand there relative to the story drift angle. Building height alone does not capture the local bay and link geometry needed for this calculation. The link's cross-sectional area affects its strength and stiffness but is not itself the geometric quantity used to compute rotation angle. Base shear is a force quantity used elsewhere in design and does not directly yield the geometric rotation angle relationship.
  10. The correct answer is A (Forces corresponding to the expected strength of all links above the column): EBF columns must be designed for the axial forces and moments generated by the expected strength of the links at and above that column's location, consistent with the general capacity design principle of protecting non-yielding elements from the maximum force the ductile links can actually deliver. Using only the unamplified code-level lateral force would understate this true capacity-based demand in a high seismic region like Los Angeles. Gravity dead load alone ignores the lateral force path this column must also resist. It is not accurate that seismic never controls, since Los Angeles's high SDS and SD1 values for this Risk Category IV hospital annex make seismic overstrength combinations likely to govern column design.
  11. The correct answer is D (A higher importance factor Ie increases the design seismic base shear): ASCE 7-16 assigns Risk Category IV structures, including hospitals and essential facilities relied upon after an earthquake, a higher seismic importance factor Ie than Risk Category II structures, which directly increases the computed design base shear to provide additional life-safety and post-event functionality margin. It is incorrect that the importance factor decreases for a higher risk category, since the intent is the opposite. Risk Category does meaningfully affect seismic base shear through this importance factor mechanism, so claiming no effect is wrong. While Risk Category also affects wind design parameters, it is not true that it affects only wind and not seismic design.
  12. The correct answer is B (Visible flange or web buckling and yielding patterns within the link region): Because the link is intentionally designed as the ductile fuse element, visible signs of local buckling, yielding, or paint flaking concentrated within the link's web and flanges are the most direct structural indicator that the link engaged its intended inelastic behavior during a significant earthquake. Exterior architectural finish cracking far from the frame is a nonstructural indicator unrelated to the link's own performance. Floor slab scuffing in a parking garage is not a reliable structural indicator of link demand. Roof mechanical equipment paint discoloration is unrelated to the seismic performance of the EBF link and does not indicate structural engagement.
Scenario S58

A 2-story steel strip mall building in Tulsa, Oklahoma (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.30g, SD1 = 0.18g) is assigned Seismic Design Category B. The lateral system consists of Ordinary Moment Frames (OMF) with bolted flange-plate moment connections along the 24 ft wide storefront bays, selected for their lower cost and reduced connection detailing requirements relative to a special moment frame.

  1. The correct answer is D (R equal to 3.5, tabulated for steel ordinary moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel OMF systems the lower R value of 3.5, reflecting their limited ductility detailing and connection qualification requirements compared to intermediate or special moment frames. SMF's R = 8.0 and IMF's R = 4.5 both belong to more heavily detailed moment frame systems that this project's connections do not satisfy. SCBF's R = 6.0 applies to a braced frame system entirely different from this moment frame building. Using R = 3.5 correctly reflects the reduced ductility this OMF system is expected to provide during a design earthquake.
  2. The correct answer is B (Cd equal to 3.0, tabulated for steel ordinary moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 pairs steel OMF with Cd = 3.0, a distinct value from its R = 3.5, since the two coefficients are separately calibrated for each system's expected drift amplification and strength reduction behavior. Setting Cd equal to R would incorrectly conflate these two separately tabulated coefficients. The Cd values for SMF and IMF belong to different, more ductile system rows that do not describe this OMF strip mall. Using Cd = 3.0 correctly scales the elastic analysis results for the Chapter 12 story drift check on this building.
  3. The correct answer is C (The lower seismic demand allows less stringent, lower-cost connection detailing): In a low seismic demand region like this SDC B site, engineers often select OMF because the reduced connection prequalification and detailing requirements translate to lower fabrication cost while still satisfying code strength and drift requirements at this modest demand level. Building height alone does not mandate OMF use, since taller OMF buildings are permitted within code height limits when seismic demand is low. OMF actually provides less ductility than SMF, not more, which is precisely why it carries a lower R value. Selecting OMF has no bearing on eliminating wind load design, which remains a separate governing load case for the storefront bays.
  4. The correct answer is A (OMF connections have less stringent qualification testing requirements): AISC 341 permits OMF moment connections to satisfy a reduced set of qualification requirements compared to the rigorous cyclic testing and prequalification demanded for SMF connections, reflecting the lower ductility demand OMF systems are expected to experience given their lower R value and typical use in lower seismic hazard applications. It is not accurate that identical AISC 358 SMF-level prequalification is always required for OMF, since the standards specifically differentiate connection qualification by system type. Bolted flange-plate connections are a recognized connection type for OMF, not a prohibited one. Full-scale cyclic testing for every project is not a blanket OMF requirement, unlike some non-prequalified custom SMF connections.
  5. The correct answer is D (Excessive panel zone shear yielding can still affect frame stability and strength): Even with OMF's reduced ductility detailing, the column web panel zone at each beam-to-column joint still carries significant shear from the connection moments, and inadequate panel zone strength can lead to excessive local distortion that compromises the frame's overall lateral strength and stability under design loads. It is not accurate to call this check a mere formality, since panel zone behavior directly affects real load transfer at the joint. Panel zone checks are relevant to moment frame joints generally, not exclusively to braced frames, which do not have this same beam-to-column moment transfer condition. The check has a structural strength basis, not an architectural clearance purpose.
  6. The correct answer is C (SDC B imposes no height limitation on OMF for this building's structural system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1's structural system height limits for OMF become restrictive primarily in higher Seismic Design Categories, while in SDC B this two-story strip mall is well within the permitted range and effectively unrestricted by that height provision. It is not true that SDC B prohibits moment frames outright, since OMF remains a permitted system there. The strict height caps that apply to SDC D and above do not carry over to SDC B, since the table's limits are tied to the specific SDC and system combination. Nothing in ASCE 7-16 mandates converting to a braced frame purely because of low SDC classification.
  7. The correct answer is B (Wind loads are more likely to govern given the low seismic demand at this site): Because this Tulsa site has relatively low SDS and SD1 values placing it in SDC B, it is common for wind loads to govern the lateral strength and drift design of a low-rise building like this strip mall, so the engineer must check both load cases rather than assuming seismic automatically controls. It is incorrect to assume seismic always governs, since that ignores the site-specific hazard levels described for this project. Both wind and seismic must be checked regardless of Risk Category assignment, not skipped. The seismic overstrength combination is one specific check within the seismic load case, not a substitute for comparing against wind loads generally.
  8. The correct answer is A (Ensuring adequate load path continuity through the column under design forces): Even for the reduced ductility detailing category of OMF, column splices must still be designed with adequate strength and appropriate location to maintain continuous load path for axial, shear, and moment transfer through the column under the applicable design load combinations. It is not the purpose of these provisions to eliminate the column's structural function above the splice, since the column must continue to carry load throughout its height. There is no code requirement fixing splice location strictly at building mid-height, since actual location depends on practical fabrication and shipping length considerations. Splices cannot be designed for zero required strength, since they must transfer real forces present in the frame.
  9. The correct answer is B (Reduced field welding demand, which can lower cost and construction schedule risk): Bolted flange-plate connections shift much of the critical force transfer to shop-fabricated plates and field bolting rather than field welding, which can reduce weld inspection burden, schedule risk from weather-dependent field welding, and overall connection cost for a lower ductility demand project like this strip mall. These connections still require full engineering design calculations, so claiming they eliminate calculations entirely is false. Using this connection type does not automatically satisfy the strong-column weak-beam ratio, which remains a separate check tied to member sizing, not connection type when applicable. Connection type selection does not change the tabulated R value for the overall system, which is fixed at 3.5 for OMF regardless of specific bolted detail chosen.
  10. The correct answer is C (Seismic Design Categories D, E, and F require rho to be computed): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4 requires the redundancy factor rho to be evaluated using the specified conditions only for structures assigned to Seismic Design Categories D, E, or F, while structures in SDC B or C, including this Tulsa strip mall, are permitted to use rho equal to 1.0 without further evaluation. It is incorrect that SDC A and B always require the detailed rho evaluation, since the code specifically exempts those lower categories from this additional check. Rho is not computed for every building regardless of SDC, since the provision is scoped to higher hazard categories. The redundancy factor provision is not limited to wood light-frame construction and applies broadly across applicable structural systems in the higher SDCs.
  11. The correct answer is D (A more restrictive height limit or a requirement to use a different system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 imposes progressively more restrictive height limits, or in some cases outright prohibition, on OMF systems as Seismic Design Category increases toward D, E, and F, reflecting the system's limited ductility becoming less appropriate for taller buildings in higher seismic hazard regions. It is not true that OMF height limits are identical across every SDC, since the table's limits are specifically tied to SDC. Using OMF does not automatically raise its R value to match SMF performance, since R remains fixed at 3.5 for the OMF system line regardless of building height. There is no removal of height limitation for OMF in higher SDCs, since the trend runs in the opposite, more restrictive direction.
  12. The correct answer is A (The low Seismic Design Category combined with the building's limited height): OMF remains permitted for this strip mall specifically because the combination of SDC B, a relatively low seismic hazard category, and the modest two-story height falls comfortably within the system's applicable height and use limitations tabulated in ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1. Building occupancy type as a strip mall does not by itself dictate which seismic force-resisting system must be used. Storefront glazing is an architectural feature that may influence bay layout but does not mandate a specific structural system by code. There is no blanket rule tying OMF requirement to a specific bay spacing threshold like 20 ft.
Scenario S59

A 5-story steel mixed-use building in Salt Lake City, Utah (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.65g, SD1 = 0.40g) is assigned Seismic Design Category C. The lateral system consists of Intermediate Moment Frames (IMF) with bolted end-plate moment connections prequalified per AISC 358, framing 28 ft bays at the building perimeter.

  1. The correct answer is C (R equal to 4.5, tabulated for steel intermediate moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel IMF systems an R value of 4.5, positioned between the more ductile SMF and the less ductile OMF, reflecting the intermediate level of connection qualification and detailing this system requires. OMF's R = 3.5 and SMF's R = 8.0 both belong to different moment frame ductility categories that do not match this building's IMF detailing. SCBF's R = 6.0 applies to a braced frame system, not this moment frame building. Using R = 4.5 correctly reflects the intermediate ductility level this system is designed and detailed to achieve.
  2. The correct answer is B (Cd equal to 4.0, tabulated for steel intermediate moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 pairs steel IMF systems with Cd = 4.0, a value distinct from the system's R = 4.5, since drift amplification and strength reduction are independently calibrated coefficients for each tabulated system. Setting Cd equal to R would incorrectly merge two separately defined values in the table. The OMF and SMF Cd values belong to different ductility categories that do not describe this IMF building. Using Cd = 4.0 correctly scales the elastic analysis output for the required Chapter 12 story drift check on this Salt Lake City structure.
  3. The correct answer is A (IMF connections must demonstrate a smaller qualifying interstory drift angle capacity): AISC 358 prequalifies IMF connections to demonstrate an interstory drift angle capacity of at least 0.02 radian through cyclic testing, which is a smaller ductility demand than the 0.04 radian capacity required for SMF connections, reflecting the intermediate ductility level assigned to this system. It is not accurate that IMF and SMF require identical qualifying drift angles, since the standard specifically differentiates these thresholds by system type. IMF connections are not exempt from cyclic qualification testing, since AISC 358 prequalification is itself based on such testing. IMF's qualifying drift angle is smaller, not larger, than SMF's, consistent with its intermediate ductility classification.
  4. The correct answer is D (The strong-column weak-beam check is not required for IMF systems generally): AISC 341 reserves the mandatory strong-column weak-beam flexural strength ratio check specifically for SMF systems, reflecting their higher ductility demand and reliance on a well-defined beam-hinging mechanism, while IMF systems are not subject to this same mandatory provision given their lower expected ductility demand. It is not accurate that IMF requires an identical strong-column weak-beam check to SMF, since the two systems are governed by different detailing provisions in AISC 341. There is no story-based threshold within a building, such as above the fourth story, that triggers this SMF-specific check for an IMF system. The drift limit is a separate serviceability provision and does not substitute for or replace the strong-column weak-beam flexural strength relationship.
  5. The correct answer is B (Preventing excessive shear distortion in the column web at the joint region): The panel zone shear check for this IMF system, similar in concept to SMF, ensures the column web region between the beam flanges at each joint has adequate strength to prevent excessive shear distortion that could compromise joint stiffness and strength under the design seismic forces. This check does not prevent beam flexural yielding, since beam hinging is actually part of the intended ductile mechanism for a properly designed moment frame. The connection must still transfer moment for the frame to function as a moment-resisting system, so the panel zone check does not eliminate that function. Panel zone shear strength is a seismic and gravity lateral force consideration, not a wind roof-level resistance factor specifically.
  6. The correct answer is C (Welded attachments that could introduce notches are not permitted there): Consistent with the general protected zone concept applied across ductile steel seismic systems, AISC 341 prohibits welded attachments such as shear studs or other appurtenances within the IMF beam's protected zone near the moment connection, since these could introduce notches that trigger premature fracture during the connection's expected inelastic demand. It is not correct that the protected zone requires additional welded studs, since the intent is the opposite, keeping the region free of such attachments. There is no code requirement to paint the protected zone a distinct color for inspection purposes. The protected zone concept for IMF applies to the beam flange region near the connection, not exclusively to columns.
  7. The correct answer is D (Forces amplified using the Omega0 value of 3.0 tabulated for moment frame systems): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel moment frame systems, including IMF, an overstrength factor Omega0 of 3.0, which must be used to amplify the horizontal seismic load effect for capacity-protected elements such as column splices, ensuring they remain adequate under the realistic maximum force the frame's overstrength can generate. Using only the unamplified code-level seismic force would understate this capacity design demand for the splice. It is incorrect that seismic overstrength never applies to IMF, since Omega0 based combinations remain part of ASCE 7-16's general capacity design framework for this moment frame system. Considering only gravity dead load ignores the lateral force path this column splice must also accommodate.
  8. The correct answer is A (IMF trades some ductility capacity for reduced connection qualification cost): Selecting IMF over SMF for this moderate seismic demand SDC C site allows the engineer to use connections with a smaller qualifying drift angle capacity and generally less extensive detailing requirements, trading some ductility margin for potential cost and schedule savings appropriate to the lower seismic hazard compared to a high SDC D or higher project. It is not accurate that IMF provides identical ductility to SMF, since the two systems have different qualifying drift angles and R values reflecting genuinely different ductility levels. IMF still requires meaningful seismic detailing, including prequalified connections and panel zone checks, so it does not eliminate seismic detailing altogether. IMF connection qualification testing requirements are generally less extensive than SMF's, not more extensive.
  9. The correct answer is C (Prequalification validity is based on the tested parameter ranges being matched): AISC 358 prequalification for a given connection type, including IMF bolted end-plate connections, is valid only within the documented beam depth, weight, and other parameter limits established through the cyclic testing program used to develop that prequalification, so the engineer must verify the actual project members fall within those tested ranges. These limits are code-referenced requirements, not merely suggested guidelines, when the connection is being used under prequalified status rather than project-specific testing. The prequalification limits specifically apply to the seismic moment-resisting beams using that connection type, not to unrelated gravity-only beams. Prequalification is not automatically satisfied by any wide-flange shape, since sizes outside the tested range require project-specific qualification testing instead.
  10. The correct answer is A (Moment frames avoid diagonal braces that would obstruct open floor plans): Moment frames, including this project's IMF system, resist lateral load through beam-column flexural action without diagonal bracing members, which preserves clear sightlines and flexible floor plans desirable for retail storefronts and residential layouts, unlike a concentrically or eccentrically braced frame that would introduce diagonal members within the bay. It is not true that moment frames always require more floor area than braced frames, since floor area consumption depends on member sizing and layout specifics, not an inherent system rule. Moment frames still require perimeter columns to form the frame, so eliminating columns entirely is not accurate. Moment frames generally provide less lateral stiffness than braced frames of comparable size, not identical stiffness, which is part of the actual tradeoff being made here.
  11. The correct answer is D (A comparison of the column flange local strength against the applied beam flange force): Continuity plate requirements at IMF joints, similar to the general moment connection provisions in AISC 358 and AISC 360, are triggered by comparing the column flange's local strength, including flange bending and web crippling limit states, against the concentrated force delivered by the connecting beam flange, providing plates only where that local strength is insufficient. The number of stories above a joint does not directly determine this local strength comparison at that specific connection. The building's compass orientation has no bearing on this structural strength check. Architectural cladding color is entirely unrelated to the structural continuity plate determination at a steel moment connection.
  12. The correct answer is B (It avoids the time and expense of a full-scale cyclic testing program): Using an AISC 358 prequalified connection allows the engineer to rely on previously completed cyclic qualification testing performed by others within the standard's documented parameter limits, avoiding the significant time and expense a project-specific full-scale testing program would otherwise require before the connection could be used. Prequalification does not eliminate the need for project-specific structural calculations, since the engineer must still verify demand, capacity, and that the actual members fall within the prequalified limits. It does not guarantee this connection is cheaper than every other bolted alternative, since cost depends on many project-specific factors beyond qualification status alone. Special inspection requirements during construction remain in force for prequalified connections and are not removed by prequalification status.
Scenario S60

A 6-story steel industrial processing building in St. Louis, Missouri (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.55g, SD1 = 0.32g) is assigned Seismic Design Category C. The lateral system consists of Ordinary Concentrically Braced Frames (OCBF) using single-diagonal braces in alternating bays, selected because the moderate SDC does not require the more stringent ductility detailing of an SCBF.

  1. The correct answer is A (R equal to 3.25, tabulated for steel ordinary concentrically braced frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel OCBF systems an R value of 3.25, reflecting the reduced ductility detailing this system carries compared to an SCBF, which is appropriate for this moderate SDC C site where the more stringent SCBF detailing is not mandated. SCBF's R = 6.0 requires highly ductile brace detailing this project's ordinary bracing does not provide. SMF's R = 8.0 and IMF's R = 4.5 both belong to moment frame systems, not this braced frame building. Using R = 3.25 correctly reflects the actual ductility level of the ordinary bracing detailing installed here.
  2. The correct answer is B (Cd equal to 3.25, numerically equal to the system's own R value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel OCBF a Cd value of 3.25, which happens to numerically equal its own R value, unlike most other tabulated systems such as SMF or SCBF where Cd and R are distinct numbers reflecting independently calibrated behavior. SCBF's Cd = 5.0 belongs to the more ductile braced frame system, not this ordinary bracing configuration. Cd is never matched to the Omega0 overstrength factor, since these are separately defined coefficients serving different design purposes. The EBF Cd value of 4.0 belongs to a different bracing configuration entirely, not this concentric bracing system.
  3. The correct answer is D (OCBF systems are designed for lower ductility demand under a lower R value): Because OCBF is assigned a lower R value than SCBF, the system is designed for correspondingly lower ductility demand, which allows AISC 341 to specify less restrictive brace width-to-thickness and connection detailing requirements than the highly ductile classification mandated for SCBF braces expected to undergo more extensive inelastic cycling. OCBF braces still experience compression and can buckle during an earthquake, so claiming they never experience compression force is incorrect. OCBF braces are not required to be a fundamentally different steel material, and typically use the same structural steel grades as SCBF. OCBF is not exempt from AISC 341 provisions entirely, since it remains a seismic force-resisting system subject to its own specific set of requirements in that standard.
  4. The correct answer is C (K-bracing, where diagonal braces connect to the column between floor levels): K-bracing is generally prohibited for seismic force-resisting systems because the diagonal braces connect to the column at a point between floor levels rather than at a beam-column joint, meaning brace buckling or yielding imposes a significant unbalanced lateral force directly on the column at that intermediate point, potentially triggering column instability. X-bracing, chevron bracing, and simple diagonal bracing are all recognized configurations permitted for SCBF or OCBF systems when properly detailed, unlike K-bracing. The concern with K-bracing is specifically its unique unbalanced force transfer mechanism into the column, not a general prohibition on braced frames overall. This restriction applies broadly across braced frame systems in AISC 341, protecting the column from an undesirable failure mode.
  5. The correct answer is B (A connection strength based on the amplified seismic load using Omega0): AISC 341 permits certain OCBF brace connections to be designed for the amplified seismic load, using the tabulated Omega0 overstrength factor applied to the code-level force, as an alternative to the full expected-strength capacity design basis required for SCBF connections, reflecting the reduced ductility demand expected from this system. OCBF is not exempt from seismic connection design, so assuming zero seismic demand is incorrect. Connection design is an engineering strength requirement, not a matter of architectural aesthetic preference. Wind load alone does not substitute for the required seismic design check on this braced frame's connections in a Seismic Design Category C building.
  6. The correct answer is D (The compression brace still loses strength relative to the tension brace after buckling): Regardless of whether the system is detailed as SCBF or OCBF, a chevron-configured compression brace still experiences buckling and a corresponding loss of capacity relative to the tension brace's sustained strength, so the supporting beam in an OCBF chevron bay must still be checked for the resulting unbalanced vertical force under the applicable design load combination. It is not accurate that OCBF braces never buckle, since ordinary bracing is still a compression member subject to buckling under sufficient axial load. The chevron beam in an OCBF bay does carry meaningful seismic force through this same unbalanced load mechanism. The unbalanced force concern is a geometric consequence of the chevron configuration itself, not something limited exclusively to SCBF detailing.
  7. The correct answer is C (Omega0 equal to 2.0, tabulated for concentrically braced frame systems): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns both SCBF and OCBF an Omega0 value of 2.0, which must amplify the horizontal seismic load effect for capacity-protected elements such as columns in this OCBF industrial building, even though the two systems differ in R and Cd values. The moment frame Omega0 of 3.0 belongs to an entirely different system category and does not apply to this braced frame. Omega0 is a separately tabulated coefficient and is not set equal to the system's own R value of 3.25. OCBF columns are not exempt from overstrength-based capacity design, so assuming an Omega0 of 1.0 with no check required is incorrect.
  8. The correct answer is A (Reduced brace and connection detailing requirements can lower fabrication cost): Choosing OCBF for this moderate SDC C site allows the engineer to use less restrictive brace slenderness classification and connection design provisions than SCBF requires, which can meaningfully reduce fabrication complexity and cost while still satisfying code strength and drift requirements at this hazard level. OCBF does not inherently provide greater stiffness than SCBF, since stiffness depends primarily on brace area and configuration rather than the ductility detailing category. Brace connections must still be designed for OCBF, just potentially using a different, generally less demanding capacity basis, so connection design is not eliminated. OCBF generally has more restrictive height limits than SCBF in higher Seismic Design Categories, not the reverse, since it is the less ductile system.
  9. The correct answer is D (The estimated actual inelastic lateral displacement expected during the design earthquake): Multiplying the elastic displacement computed under the reduced design seismic forces by Cd = 3.25 produces the design story drift, which represents the engineer's best estimate of the actual inelastic lateral displacement the structure would experience during the design earthquake, used to check against the Chapter 12 drift limit. This design story drift is not the unreduced elastic displacement itself, since Cd is specifically applied to amplify that elastic value toward a realistic inelastic estimate. It does not represent a wind serviceability displacement, which would be computed under separate wind load combinations. Foundation settlement under gravity load is an entirely different geotechnical consideration unrelated to this seismic drift amplification concept.
  10. The correct answer is B (The bay's tension and compression capacity differ between the two loading directions): With only a single diagonal brace in a given bay, that brace acts in tension under load applied in one direction and in compression under load applied in the reverse direction, so the bay's effective capacity differs between the two loading directions, which is why the alternating bay layout across the building is used to provide reasonably balanced overall lateral resistance in both directions. It is not accurate that a single-diagonal bay provides identical capacity regardless of direction, since tension and compression capacities of the same brace member are generally different. Compression buckling must still be checked for the brace when it acts in compression under the reverse loading direction. A single diagonal configuration does not automatically satisfy the highly ductile classification, which depends on width-to-thickness ratios and is generally an SCBF-specific requirement rather than an OCBF one.
  11. The correct answer is A (Collector elements, sometimes called drag struts, extending along the diaphragm): Collector elements, also called drag struts, run along the diaphragm to gather distributed diaphragm shear over a tributary length and deliver it as a concentrated force into the vertical braced frame, forming an essential link in the seismic load path between the horizontal diaphragm and this OCBF industrial building's braced bays. Roof mechanical equipment curbs serve an entirely different, non-structural purpose and do not perform this diaphragm force collection function. Architectural partition walls are explicitly excluded from the primary lateral force-resisting load path in this context. Exterior cladding attachments handle out-of-plane wind pressure on the building envelope, not the in-plane diaphragm shear collection this question addresses.
  12. The correct answer is C (Premature elastic buckling well below the material yield strength of the brace): A compression member with excessive slenderness, meaning a large effective length to radius of gyration ratio, is governed by elastic buckling per AISC 360 and can buckle at a load well below the material's yield strength, so the engineer must still size this OCBF brace to satisfy general compression member slenderness provisions even without the more restrictive SCBF highly ductile detailing. Slenderness does not increase tension capacity, since tension yielding is governed by cross-sectional area and yield stress, not slenderness ratio. An appropriately sized brace remains fully useful in tension regardless of its compression slenderness classification. Slenderness very much affects buckling behavior, which is precisely why AISC 360 includes specific compression member design provisions tied to this ratio.

Practice Test 13 Answer Key

Scenario S61

A 10-story steel office high-rise in San Francisco, California (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 1.20g, SD1 = 0.70g) is assigned Seismic Design Category D and rests on a mat foundation over deep piles. Special Moment Frames (SMF) resist lateral loads in both directions, with RBS beam-to-column connections, field-welded column splices, and doubler-plate-reinforced panel zones at the lower six stories where shear demands are highest.

  1. The correct answer is D (No height limit is imposed on steel special moment frames in SDC D): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists steel SMF as having no structural system height limit even in Seismic Design Category D, E, or F, reflecting its high ductility and the extensive detailing and capacity design provisions AISC 341 requires, which is precisely why this system was selected for a 10-story high-rise on this high seismic hazard San Francisco site. The 160 ft and 65 ft height limits in the table apply to other, less ductile systems such as certain braced frames or moment frames, not to SMF. SMF is not prohibited in SDC D; rather it is one of the systems best suited to taller buildings in high seismic regions specifically because of its lack of a height restriction. Selecting the correct height limit is essential to confirming this system remains a code-compliant choice for the full 10-story height.
  2. The correct answer is A (Ensuring the weld metal resists brittle fracture under seismic strain demand): AISC 341 requires demand critical welds to use filler metals meeting a minimum Charpy V-Notch toughness value at specified test temperatures, which ensures the weld metal has sufficient fracture toughness to resist brittle fracture under the high strain rates and large inelastic demands expected at these critical connection welds during a design earthquake. This requirement is a structural fracture mechanics consideration, not related to electrical conductivity or grounding. It is not intended to reduce filler metal weight, since toughness and deposition volume are separate weld properties. Weld cosmetic appearance is unrelated to this fracture toughness requirement, which addresses structural safety, not visual quality.
  3. The correct answer is C (Retained backing bars can act as an unintended notch initiating fracture): A steel backing bar left in place at the bottom flange weld can create a small gap or notch condition that acts as a stress concentration and potential fracture initiation site under the large cyclic inelastic strains expected at an SMF moment connection, which is why AISC 341 requires its removal and back-gouging with reinforcing fillet weld at this critical bottom flange location. Removal is a fracture-critical detailing requirement, not primarily a material savings measure, since the amount of steel involved is minor. Backing bar retention does meaningfully affect weld quality and fatigue or fracture performance at this location, so claiming no effect is incorrect. Backing bars are not required to remain permanently at every joint; the top flange backing bar is often permitted to remain when properly detailed, while the bottom flange location has the more stringent removal requirement.
  4. The correct answer is B (Locating splices away from maximum beam-induced column moment demand): AISC 341 requires column splices to be located at a minimum distance above the finished floor level, keeping them away from the region of highest column moment demand generated by the adjacent beam-to-column connections, so the splice experiences reduced demand relative to that peak location and can be designed with an appropriate strength margin as a capacity-protected element. This offset requirement is a structural performance consideration, not primarily intended to simplify ceiling height coordination. It does not reduce the overall steel quantity used, since the splice location shift does not change member sizing requirements elsewhere. Column splices have no functional relationship to fire-rated floor penetration sealing, which is addressed by separate fire protection provisions.
  5. The correct answer is C (The distance from the column face, the length of the cut, and its depth): In the standard RBS geometry defined by AISC 358, parameter a controls the distance from the column face to the start of the cut, parameter b controls the length of the reduced cut along the flange, and parameter c controls the depth of material removed from the flange width, together defining the reduced section's location and severity along the beam. These parameters do not describe the number of bolts in a beam web shear connection, which is a separate detailing item. They are unrelated to continuity plate thickness, which is governed by column flange strength requirements. RBS geometry parameters also do not describe web stiffener spacing, which is a different detail entirely used in contexts such as EBF links, not RBS beam flange cuts.
  6. The correct answer is D (Increasing the panel zone's shear strength to resist joint distortion): Doubler plates are welded to the column web within the panel zone to increase its effective thickness and thus its shear strength, addressing cases like the lower stories of this high-rise where the panel zone shear demand generated by high beam moments exceeds what the bare column web alone can resist. Doubler plates address the column web's shear capacity, not the beam's flexural strength at the RBS location, which is a separate design consideration. They do not replace the separate continuity plate requirement, which addresses column flange local strength under concentrated beam flange force rather than panel zone shear. Doubler plates add steel to the column web rather than reducing overall column flange weight.
  7. The correct answer is A (Omega0 equal to 3.0, tabulated for steel special moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel SMF systems an Omega0 of 3.0, which must amplify the horizontal seismic load effect when checking the mat foundation for overturning, sliding, and bearing under the maximum force this ductile frame can realistically deliver at its true overstrength capacity. The braced frame Omega0 of 2.0 belongs to an entirely different system category and does not apply to this moment frame high-rise. Omega0 is a separately tabulated coefficient and is never set equal to the system's own R value of 8.0. Foundation overturning checks are specifically one of the capacity-protected element checks ASCE 7-16 requires the overstrength load effect for, so assuming no overstrength applies is incorrect.
  8. The correct answer is B (Yes, since the ratio of 9,600 to 7,200 equals 1.33, which exceeds 1.0): Dividing the sum of projected column plastic moments, 9,600 kip-ft, by the sum of projected beam plastic moments, 7,200 kip-ft, gives a ratio of 1.33, which exceeds the AISC 341 minimum required ratio of 1.0 for the strong-column weak-beam check, confirming this joint satisfies the provision and columns remain stronger than beams at this location. The claim that the ratio equals 0.75 incorrectly inverts the numerator and denominator of the actual calculation. It is not true that the beam moment exceeds the column moment here, since 7,200 is clearly less than 9,600. AISC 341 requires the ratio to be at least 1.0, meaning exceeding 1.0 is acceptable and expected, not a violation of an exact-equality requirement.
  9. The correct answer is D (Secondary moments from gravity loads acting through lateral displacement amplify demand): As story drift increases in the upper stories of this high-rise, the gravity load acting through that lateral displacement generates additional secondary overturning moment, known as the P-delta effect, which can amplify story shear and drift demand beyond the first-order analysis results if not properly accounted for in the design. P-delta is a general structural stability phenomenon affecting both wind and seismic load cases, not something limited to reducing wind demand only. Using RBS connections addresses beam-column moment connection ductility and has no bearing on eliminating P-delta effects, which are a global stability phenomenon. P-delta effects apply to steel frame buildings just as they do to concrete buildings, since the phenomenon depends on gravity load and lateral displacement, not material type.
  10. The correct answer is B (The connection must remain adequate while the beam achieves its full plastic hinge capacity): Because the RBS beam is expected to reach its full expected plastic moment capacity at the hinge location during the design earthquake, the shear tab and web bolts must be checked against the corresponding expected shear demand associated with that fully yielded condition, ensuring the connection does not fail in shear before the intended ductile flexural mechanism is achieved. The shear tab is a genuine structural element transferring beam shear into the column and is not merely architectural. Expected shear demand associated with full yielding is generally higher, not lower, than a simple nominal code-level demand estimate, which is precisely why the capacity design check is necessary. This shear transfer check is a seismic capacity design consideration specific to the ductile connection behavior, not a wind-only check.
  11. The correct answer is A (The computed design base shear would be understated relative to the true seismic demand): SDS directly scales the design response spectrum and the resulting seismic base shear, so using an incorrectly lower SDS value corresponding to a less severe site condition than the actual Site Class D soil profile at this San Francisco site would understate the true design base shear and undersize the SMF system relative to the real seismic hazard. Site class assumptions directly affect Fa and thus SDS, so claiming no effect on base shear is incorrect. The R factor is a separate reduction applied after the elastic demand is established and does not correct for an error in the underlying site-specific ground motion parameters. SDS is a seismic design parameter and has no direct bearing on the separate wind design calculation for this building.
  12. The correct answer is C (The design base shear strength check and the allowable story drift limit): For a steel SMF high-rise like this one, the engineer typically confirms the design base shear produces adequate member and connection strength throughout the frame while separately checking that the resulting design story drift, amplified by Cd, remains within the ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1 allowable drift limit, together addressing both life-safety strength and serviceability performance objectives. Wind speed and rainfall are unrelated to this seismic strength and drift verification process. Snow load parameters address a separate gravity load case and are not part of the seismic strength and drift check pairing described here. Soil bearing capacity and groundwater elevation are geotechnical foundation design inputs, not the structural strength and drift checks this question addresses for the steel frame itself.
Scenario S62

A 4-story steel research building in Portland, Oregon (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.85g, SD1 = 0.50g) is assigned Seismic Design Category D. Special Concentrically Braced Frames (SCBF) resist lateral load in the north-south direction along solid perimeter walls, while Eccentrically Braced Frames (EBF) with 6 ft long shear links resist lateral load in the east-west direction, where large storefront glazing requires open bays.

  1. The correct answer is A (R equal to 6.0 for the SCBF direction and R equal to 8.0 for the EBF direction): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns steel SCBF an R of 6.0 and assigns the EBF configuration with moment connections away from the link, as described for this building's shear links, an R of 8.0, so each orthogonal direction is designed using its own system-specific R value rather than a shared or averaged value. Reversing that assignment, as one wrong choice does, would incorrectly swap the two systems' tabulated coefficients. The 3.25 value belongs to OCBF, a different braced frame category not used in either direction of this building. The 4.5 and 3.5 values belong to IMF and OMF respectively, neither of which is the lateral system used in this project.
  2. The correct answer is B (Different systems are permitted along different axes when each is designed for its own applicable R, Cd, and Omega0 values): ASCE 7-16 permits combinations of different seismic force-resisting systems along different orthogonal axes of the same structure, provided each direction is independently designed using the R, Cd, and Omega0 values applicable to the specific system used in that direction, which is exactly the approach used for this building's SCBF and EBF combination. It is not true that different systems are prohibited along different axes, since this combination approach is explicitly addressed and permitted by the code. Averaging the two systems' R values is not the correct method, since each direction retains its own system-specific coefficients. Using the lower R value in both directions would be unnecessarily conservative and does not reflect the code's actual approach of directional independence for this configuration.
  3. The correct answer is C (Combining 100 percent of the force in one direction with 30 percent in the perpendicular direction): ASCE 7-16 requires certain columns and other elements common to intersecting seismic force-resisting systems, such as this corner column shared by the SCBF and EBF frames, to be designed for the combination of 100 percent of the seismic force in one orthogonal direction plus 30 percent of the force in the perpendicular direction, applied in the orientation producing the most critical effect. Applying 100 percent in both directions simultaneously at full value would substantially overestimate the realistic combined demand beyond what the code requires. Using a flat 50 percent in each direction does not match the specific 100-30 percent combination rule ASCE 7-16 actually prescribes. Ignoring one direction entirely at this shared corner column would fail to capture the genuine biaxial demand this element experiences.
  4. The correct answer is D (EBF allows the brace to offset from the beam-column joint, creating clear opening space): The defining feature of an EBF is that the diagonal brace intentionally frames into the beam at an offset from the column, at a location such as the shear link position, rather than directly into the beam-column joint corner, which creates a larger unobstructed opening within the bay compared to configurations where a brace would otherwise block a corner needed for a doorway or window. It is not accurate that EBF requires braces to cross directly through window openings, since the offset link position is chosen specifically to avoid such conflicts. EBF still requires vertical framing, including columns, along the wall line, so it does not eliminate vertical framing entirely. There is no code restriction limiting EBF use only to walls without any glazing.
  5. The correct answer is B (The link will yield primarily in shear, achieving up to 0.08 radian rotation capacity): Confirming that the link length e falls below the 1.6 Mp over Vp threshold classifies it as a shear link, meaning its web yields in shear before flexural hinging governs at the flanges, which allows this link to be designed for the larger 0.08 radian inelastic rotation angle limit permitted for shear links under AISC 341. A flexural link, corresponding to a longer length exceeding a different threshold, is instead limited to the smaller 0.02 radian rotation capacity due to flange local buckling concerns. The link is specifically intended to yield as the ductile fuse element of the EBF system, so remaining fully elastic would defeat its structural purpose. Link classification directly determines the applicable rotation angle limit, so it clearly does affect permitted rotation capacity rather than having no effect.
  6. The correct answer is A (A significant stiffness difference between directions can contribute to torsional irregularity): When one direction of a building, such as the more solid SCBF perimeter walls here, is substantially stiffer than the other direction with its more open EBF bays, and if this stiffness is not distributed symmetrically about the center of mass, the resulting eccentricity between center of mass and center of rigidity can contribute to torsional irregularity that must be checked per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.2. It is not accurate that differences in bay openness between directions never matter for torsional response, since relative stiffness distribution is central to torsional behavior. Torsional behavior depends on both mass distribution and lateral stiffness distribution together, not mass alone. Torsional irregularity can occur in buildings using multiple different systems just as readily as in buildings using a single system type, since it depends on stiffness and mass distribution, not system count.
  7. The correct answer is D (Collector elements must be sized appropriately for the specific demand of each system): Because the SCBF and EBF frames have different R, Cd, and Omega0 values and therefore different capacity design force levels, the collector elements delivering diaphragm shear into each respective frame type must be individually sized for the specific amplified force demand associated with that particular system, rather than assuming one design serves both conditions equally. Using a single collector size regardless of destination system risks under-designing the collector feeding into whichever system generates the larger capacity design force. Diaphragms with multiple lateral systems still require collectors wherever diaphragm shear must be gathered and delivered to a discrete vertical element, so claiming collectors are unnecessary is incorrect. Diaphragm and collector design is very much affected by which specific lateral system is receiving the collected force, given the differing overstrength bases involved.
  8. The correct answer is C (EBF is generally stiffer than a moment frame but more flexible than a fully concentric braced frame): EBF systems are typically understood to provide lateral stiffness intermediate between a pure moment frame and a fully concentric braced frame, since the diagonal brace still engages significant axial stiffness while the shorter link introduces additional flexibility compared to a direct concentric brace connection, which is a key reason the two systems in this building carry different Cd values. It is not accurate that EBF is always stiffer than SCBF, since the concentric bracing in SCBF generally provides greater lateral stiffness for comparable member sizes due to the more direct axial load path. EBF and SCBF do not provide identical stiffness, given their different structural mechanisms and load paths. EBF is not stiffness-less, since the brace and beam segments outside the link still meaningfully contribute to the frame's overall lateral stiffness.
  9. The correct answer is A (SCBF forces derive from brace expected strength, EBF forces derive from link strength): In the SCBF direction, brace connections are designed for forces corresponding to the brace's own expected yield and buckling strength, while in the EBF direction, the brace, beam segment outside the link, and column are instead designed for forces generated by the link's expected shear strength, reflecting that each system concentrates its intended ductile yielding in a different structural element. It is not accurate that both systems share an identical capacity design basis, since the specific yielding element driving the capacity design forces differs between the two systems. SCBF does require a capacity design basis, tied to brace expected strength, so claiming none exists is incorrect. EBF likewise requires its own capacity design basis, tied to link expected strength, so claiming EBF has none is also incorrect.
  10. The correct answer is C (SCBF and EBF are assigned different Omega0 values in ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns SCBF an Omega0 of 2.0 and assigns this particular EBF configuration an Omega0 of 2.0 as well in many editions, but because the two systems are governed by separate table rows, the engineer must confirm and apply the specific tabulated value for whichever system's reaction is being checked at the foundation, rather than assuming a single universal overstrength factor applies to the whole building regardless of source direction. Omega0 is not a single universal value across all systems, since the table differentiates values by system type specifically for this reason. Foundation design does require the overstrength seismic load combination for capacity-protected elements like footings beneath a braced or eccentrically braced frame. Omega0 is tied to the seismic force-resisting system generating the load, not to the foundation type itself, which is a separate design consideration.
  11. The correct answer is B (Each orthogonal direction applies its own system-specific Cd to its own elastic displacement): Because the SCBF direction uses Cd = 5.0 and the EBF direction uses Cd = 4.0, each direction's own elastic displacement result from the reduced seismic force analysis in that direction must be amplified by its own respective Cd value, producing generally different design story drift values for the two orthogonal directions even if the underlying elastic displacements happened to be numerically similar. Design story drift is not automatically identical in both directions, since it depends on both the elastic displacement and the system-specific Cd, which differ here. Applying only the larger Cd value to both directions would incorrectly overstate the EBF direction's actual expected drift. Cd values remain essential after the elastic analysis, since they are precisely the multiplier used to convert that elastic result into the design story drift for the applicable drift limit check.
  12. The correct answer is D (The EBF base connection could be undersized relative to its own true link-based demand): Because the EBF direction's capacity design forces are derived from the expected shear strength of its links while the SCBF direction's forces are derived from expected brace strength, these two force sets are calculated independently and are not interchangeable, so mistakenly applying the SCBF direction's base forces to the EBF columns could leave those EBF base connections undersized relative to the true demand generated by the yielding links in that direction. It is not guaranteed that substituting one system's forces for another's would always be conservative, since the actual magnitude and combination of forces differ by system and direction. The two systems do not always generate identical base forces, given their different R, Cd, and Omega0 values and different governing yielding mechanisms. This is fundamentally a seismic capacity design issue, not a wind-only design concern.
Scenario S63

A 10-story residential building assigned to Risk Category II and Seismic Design Category D uses special reinforced concrete shear walls as its seismic force-resisting system, classified as a bearing wall system per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1. A typical wall segment at the first story has length lw = 20 ft and thickness t = 14 in, with total wall height hw = 100 ft above the critical section, giving an aspect ratio hw/lw = 5.0. The wall uses normalweight concrete with f'c = 5,000 psi and Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi. At the base of the wall, factored demands from analysis are Pu = 800 kips (compression), Mu = 12,000 kip-ft, and Vu = 250 kips. Vertical and horizontal web reinforcement each provide a ratio rho_t = 0.0025, and a strain compatibility analysis at the design displacement gives a neutral axis depth c = 48 in. Design is performed per ACI 318-14 Chapter 18.

  1. The correct answer is C (alpha_c equals 2.0, since the aspect ratio exceeds 2.0): ACI 318-14 18.10.4.4 sets alpha_c equal to 3.0 for hw/lw less than or equal to 1.5, alpha_c equal to 2.0 for hw/lw greater than or equal to 2.0, and requires linear interpolation for intermediate aspect ratios. Since hw/lw equals 5.0 here, the wall falls well above the 2.0 threshold, so the lower bound coefficient of 2.0 applies directly and no interpolation is needed.
  2. The correct answer is C (Vn is approximately 979 kips): Acv equals 14 in times 240 in, or 3,360 sq in. Vn equals Acv times (alpha_c times lambda times the square root of f'c, plus rho_t times fy), which is 3,360 times (2.0 times 70.7 plus 0.0025 times 60,000), giving 3,360 times 291.4, or about 979,000 lb, which is approximately 979 kips.
  3. The correct answer is C (phiVn is approximately 588 kips, calculated from the demands given for this member): The design shear strength is phi times Vn, which equals 0.60 times 979 kips, or about 588 kips. ACI 318-14 assigns phi = 0.60 to structural walls whose nominal shear strength is less than the shear corresponding to the development of their nominal flexural strength, which governs this wall.
  4. The correct answer is D (The upper limit is about 1,901 kips, so Vn does not exceed it): The upper limit equals 8 times 3,360 times the square root of 5,000, or 8 times 3,360 times 70.7, which is about 1,901,000 lb, or 1,901 kips. Since the computed Vn of 979 kips is well below this limit, the wall cross section and web reinforcement are not governed by the maximum shear cap.
  5. The correct answer is C (The extreme fiber stress is approximately 1,310 psi): The axial stress is Pu divided by Ag, or 800,000 divided by 3,360, equal to about 238 psi. The flexural stress is Mu times c divided by Ig, or 144,000,000 lb-in times 120 in divided by 16,128,000 in^4, equal to about 1,071 psi. Adding these gives roughly 1,309 psi, or about 1,310 psi.
  6. The correct answer is B (Boundary elements are required, since 1,310 psi exceeds 1,000 psi): The threshold for requiring special boundary elements under the stress-based method is 0.2 times f'c, which equals 0.2 times 5,000, or 1,000 psi. Because the computed extreme fiber compressive stress of about 1,310 psi exceeds this 1,000 psi threshold, special boundary elements are required at the compression boundary of this wall per ACI 318-14 18.10.6.3.
  7. The correct answer is A (The boundary elements may be discontinued below 750 psi): ACI 318-14 18.10.6.3 allows special boundary elements to be discontinued where the calculated compressive stress is less than 0.15 times f'c. With f'c = 5,000 psi, this discontinuation stress equals 0.15 times 5,000, or 750 psi, providing a buffer below the 0.2 f'c requirement threshold.
  8. The correct answer is D (R equals 5.0 for this bearing wall system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls used as a bearing wall system a response modification coefficient R equal to 5.0. This value reflects the ductile detailing provided by ACI 318-14 Chapter 18, though it is lower than the R = 6.0 assigned when the same wall type instead serves as a building frame system.
  9. The correct answer is C (Cd equals 5.0 for this bearing wall system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 lists a deflection amplification factor Cd equal to 5.0 for special reinforced concrete shear walls in a bearing wall system. This factor is applied to the elastic drift computed under reduced design seismic forces to estimate the actual expected inelastic story drift for serviceability and separation checks.
  10. The correct answer is D (Omega0 equals 2.5 for special reinforced concrete shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls an overstrength factor Omega0 equal to 2.5, regardless of whether the wall is classified as a bearing wall or building frame system. This factor amplifies seismic demands on force-controlled elements like collectors and wall-to-diaphragm connections designed to remain essentially elastic.
  11. The correct answer is B (The provided ratio of 0.0025 exactly satisfies the minimum requirement): ACI 318-14 18.10.2.1 requires a minimum reinforcement ratio of 0.0025 in both the longitudinal and transverse directions for special structural walls once the factored shear demand exceeds the small threshold noted. Since the wall provides exactly rho_t = 0.0025 in each direction, it just meets, and does not fall below, this mandatory minimum web reinforcement ratio.
  12. The correct answer is B (The required extent is 24 in from the extreme compression fiber): The first quantity, c minus 0.1lw, equals 48 minus 24, or 24 in. The second quantity, c/2, equals 48 divided by 2, or 24 in as well. ACI 318-14 18.10.6.4(a) requires the boundary element to extend horizontally the larger of these two values, and since both equal 24 in here, the required extent is 24 in from the extreme compression fiber.
Scenario S64

A 15-story hotel building, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D, uses coupled special reinforced concrete shear walls as its seismic force-resisting system. A typical coupling beam linking two wall piers has a clear span ln = 6 ft, total depth h = 30 in, and width bw = 16 in, giving an aspect ratio ln/h = 2.4. The beam uses normalweight concrete with f'c = 5,000 psi and Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi. The factored shear demand on the coupling beam is Vu = 180 kips. A proposed diagonal reinforcement layout provides two intersecting groups of diagonal bars, each group with area Avd = 4.74 sq in, oriented at an angle to the horizontal determined by the beam's depth and clear span geometry. Design follows ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4.

  1. The correct answer is B (The aspect ratio ln/h is approximately 2.4): Converting the clear span to inches gives 6 ft times 12, or 72 in. Dividing this by the total depth of 30 in gives ln/h equal to 72 divided by 30, or 2.4. This aspect ratio of 2.4 falls between 2 and 4, placing the beam in the range where either diagonal or conventional reinforcement may apply depending on the shear demand relative to the code threshold.
  2. The correct answer is A (The threshold shear is approximately 136 kips, determined from the properties described in this scenario): Acw equals bw times h, or 16 times 30, giving 480 sq in. The threshold shear equals 4 times 1.0 times the square root of 5,000, or 4 times 70.7, times 480 sq in, which is 4 times 70.7 times 480, equal to about 135,800 lb, or approximately 136 kips.
  3. The correct answer is D (Two intersecting groups of diagonal reinforcement are required): Because ln/h equals 2.4, which is less than the 4 threshold that exempts a beam from the diagonal requirement, and the factored shear Vu of 180 kips exceeds the threshold shear of about 136 kips, ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4 requires the coupling beam to be reinforced with two intersecting groups of diagonally placed bars unless diagonal reinforcement is shown to be impractical.
  4. The correct answer is A (The diagonal angle alpha is approximately 22.6 degrees): The angle alpha is approximated as the arctangent of h divided by ln, or the arctangent of 30 divided by 72, which equals the arctangent of 0.417. This works out to approximately 22.6 degrees, a relatively shallow diagonal angle consistent with a coupling beam whose clear span is more than double its total depth.
  5. The correct answer is D (sin(alpha) is approximately 0.38, using the geometry and material properties stated above): The sine of approximately 22.6 degrees equals about 0.384, which rounds to approximately 0.38. This value is used directly in the diagonal coupling beam shear strength equation to convert the axial force carried along each diagonal bar group into an equivalent vertical shear contribution across the beam section.
  6. The correct answer is D (Vn is approximately 219 kips): Vn equals 2 times 4.74 sq in times 60 ksi times 0.384, which equals 2 times 4.74 times 60 times 0.384, or approximately 218.6 kips. This nominal strength is contributed entirely by the two diagonal bar groups acting in tension and compression across the diagonal cracks expected to form in this short, deep coupling beam.
  7. The correct answer is C (phi equals 0.85 for diagonally reinforced coupling beams): ACI 318-14 assigns a shear strength reduction factor of phi = 0.85 to diagonally reinforced coupling beams, reflecting the reliable, well-confined load path that the diagonal bar groups provide even after the surrounding concrete has cracked extensively under repeated seismic load reversals.
  8. The correct answer is A (phiVn is approximately 186 kips, computed using the applicable code equation described above): The design shear strength equals phi times Vn, or 0.85 times 218.6 kips, which is approximately 185.8 kips, rounding to about 186 kips. This design strength is compared directly against the factored demand to confirm the adequacy of the diagonal reinforcement layout.
  9. The correct answer is C (The layout is adequate, since phiVn exceeds Vu): Since the design shear strength phiVn of approximately 186 kips is greater than the factored shear demand Vu of 180 kips, the diagonal reinforcement layout with Avd = 4.74 sq in per group provides sufficient shear strength for this coupling beam under ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4.
  10. The correct answer is C (Each diagonal group must be enclosed by column-like transverse confinement): ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4(d) requires each group of diagonal bars to be enclosed by transverse reinforcement satisfying confinement requirements similar to those for special moment frame columns. This confinement restrains buckling of the diagonal compression bars and maintains the integrity of the diagonal strut mechanism through repeated cyclic loading.
  11. The correct answer is D (The beam must be designed as a conventional beam per 18.6.3 through 18.6.5): Because ln/h = 4.5 equals or exceeds the 4.0 threshold, ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4 requires this more slender coupling beam to satisfy the requirements of 18.6.3 through 18.6.5, the same provisions used for special moment frame beams, rather than requiring the diagonal reinforcement pattern used for the shorter, deeper coupling beam.
  12. The correct answer is A (Full confinement of the entire beam cross section with transverse reinforcement): ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4(f) allows, for coupling beams meeting specified aspect ratio and shear limits, an alternative in which the full beam cross section is confined with transverse reinforcement satisfying prescribed spacing and configuration rules, in place of concentrating confinement only around two diagonal bar groups.
Scenario S65

A 6-story office building, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D, uses special reinforced concrete moment frames as its seismic force-resisting system per ACI 318-14 Chapter 18, Sections 18.6 through 18.8. A typical frame beam has width bw = 16 in, total depth h = 24 in, and effective depth d = 21 in, spanning a clear length ln = 22 ft between column faces. The beam uses f'c = 4,000 psi normalweight concrete and Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi, with longitudinal bars sized as number 8 (db = 1.0 in).
