The factored gravity shear at the beam support is Vg = 20 kips, and probable flexural strengths at the beam ends are Mpr,left = 380 kip-ft and Mpr,right = 340 kip-ft. The gross cross-sectional area is Ag = 384 sq in.

  1. The correct answer is D (Ve is approximately 53 kips, computed using the applicable code equation described above): The sum of probable moment strengths equals 380 plus 340, or 720 kip-ft, divided by the clear span of 22 ft, giving 32.7 kips from the probable moment gradient alone. Adding the gravity shear contribution of Vg = 20 kips gives Ve equal to about 32.7 plus 20, or approximately 53 kips for the governing direction.
  2. The correct answer is B (The hoop region length is 48 in from the face of support): ACI 318-14 18.6.4.1 requires hoops over a length equal to 2h measured from the face of the supporting member, where h is the total member depth. With h = 24 in, this length equals 2 times 24, or 48 in, defining the plastic hinge region at each end of the beam where closely spaced confinement is mandatory.
  3. The correct answer is B (The maximum spacing is 5.25 in, governed by d/4): The three limits are d/4 equal to 21 divided by 4, or 5.25 in; 6db equal to 6 times 1.0 in, or 6.0 in; and the fixed 6 in limit. The smallest of these three values is 5.25 in, so d/4 governs and limits the maximum hoop spacing within the plastic hinge region to 5.25 in.
  4. The correct answer is C (The probable-moment shear is at least half of Ve, and axial load is below Agf'c/20): ACI 318-14 18.6.5.2 allows Vc to be taken as zero when the shear force generated by probable moment strengths at the beam ends is at least half of the maximum required shear strength within the hinge region, and when the factored axial compressive force, including earthquake effects, is less than Ag times f'c divided by 20.
  5. The correct answer is B (The threshold is approximately 77 kips, determined from the properties described in this scenario): The threshold equals Ag times f'c divided by 20, or 384 sq in times 4,000 psi divided by 20, which equals 1,536,000 divided by 20, or 76,800 lb, approximately 77 kips. This threshold sets an upper bound on factored axial compression below which the Vc = 0 provision may still be applied for shear design within the plastic hinge region.
  6. The correct answer is C (rho_min is approximately 0.0033, governed by 200/fy, computed using the applicable code equation described above): The expression 3 times the square root of 4,000 divided by 60,000 equals 3 times 63.2 divided by 60,000, or about 0.00316. The expression 200 divided by 60,000 equals 0.00333. Since the code requires the minimum ratio to be at least both expressions, the larger value of 0.00333 governs, approximately 0.0033.
  7. The correct answer is A (The maximum reinforcement ratio is 0.025, based on the values given in this scenario): ACI 318-14 18.6.3.1 limits the longitudinal reinforcement ratio for special moment frame beams to a maximum of 0.025, applying at any section along the beam length. This upper limit helps control congestion at beam-column joints and ensures adequate ductility can still be achieved under large inelastic rotation demands.
  8. The correct answer is A (The positive moment strength must be at least one half of the negative strength): ACI 318-14 18.6.3.2 requires that the positive moment strength at a joint face be no less than one half of the negative moment strength provided at that same face. This requirement ensures the beam can develop meaningful flexural resistance and hinge formation under load reversal in either direction of seismic response.
  9. The correct answer is D (At least one fourth of the maximum end moment strength): ACI 318-14 18.6.3.2 requires that neither the positive nor the negative moment strength at any section along the beam length be less than one fourth of the maximum moment strength provided at either face of the joints, preventing an abrupt and undesirable loss of flexural capacity partway along the beam span.
  10. The correct answer is B (At least two continuous bars are required per face): ACI 318-14 18.6.3.3 requires that at least two longitudinal bars be provided continuously along both the top and bottom faces of the beam. These continuous bars help tie the beam together along its entire length and provide a minimum level of residual capacity even after significant flexural cracking has occurred near the supports.
  11. The correct answer is D (Closed hoops with seismic hooks are required in the hinge region): ACI 318-14 18.6.4 requires closed hoops, detailed with seismic hooks that engage the longitudinal bars and anchor properly into the concrete core, within the plastic hinge regions at each end of the beam. This closed hoop detailing provides confinement and restrains longitudinal bar buckling under the large inelastic strain reversals expected there.
  12. The correct answer is A (Vc = 0 is no longer mandatory, since the axial condition is not satisfied): Since 90 kips exceeds the threshold of about 77 kips, or Ag times f'c divided by 20, the axial load condition of ACI 318-14 18.6.5.2 is no longer satisfied, so the mandatory assumption of Vc = 0 within the plastic hinge region no longer applies, even though the capacity design shear Ve still governs the required transverse reinforcement.

Practice Test 14 Answer Key

Scenario S66

A 3-story medical office building, Risk Category III with Ie = 1.5, Seismic Design Category C, uses intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames as its seismic force-resisting system per ACI 318-14 18.4. A typical frame beam has width bw = 14 in, total depth h = 20 in, and effective depth d = 17.5 in, with a clear span ln = 20 ft. The beam uses f'c = 4,000 psi normalweight concrete and Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi, and longitudinal bars sized as number 7 (db = 0.875 in). Factored gravity moments at the beam ends produce Mu = 140 kip-ft. Interior gravity framing also includes a two-way flat plate slab-column connection carrying a factored gravity shear Vg = 60 kips, requiring the special IMF slab-column shear reinforcement provisions of ACI 318-14 18.14.

  1. The correct answer is D (At least one third of the negative moment strength): ACI 318-14 18.4.2.2 requires the positive moment strength at a joint face of an intermediate moment frame beam to be at least one third of the negative moment strength at that face. This requirement is less strict than the one half fraction required for special moment frame beams, consistent with the reduced ductility demand expected for intermediate systems.
  2. The correct answer is B (At least one fifth of the maximum moment strength at either joint face): ACI 318-14 18.4.2.2 requires that the moment strength at any section along an intermediate moment frame beam be no less than one fifth of the maximum moment strength provided at either joint face, a less restrictive limit than the one fourth fraction that applies to special moment frame beams under Chapter 18.6.
  3. The correct answer is C (The d/4 spacing limit is approximately 4.4 in): Dividing the effective depth of 17.5 in by 4 gives d/4 equal to 4.375 in, which rounds to approximately 4.4 in. This value is compared against the other spacing limits of 8db and 24 times the tie bar diameter, along with a fixed 12 in limit, with the smallest of these governing the maximum stirrup spacing near the beam ends.
  4. The correct answer is C (The required length is 40 in from the face of support): The required length equals 2h, or 2 times 20 in, giving 40 in measured from the face of the supporting column. Over this 40 in region at each end of the beam, closely spaced transverse reinforcement satisfying the governing spacing limits must be provided.
  5. The correct answer is D (R equals 5.0 for intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames a response modification coefficient R equal to 5.0, reflecting a moderate level of ductile detailing that falls between the R = 8.0 permitted for special moment frames and the R = 3.0 permitted for ordinary moment frames.
  6. The correct answer is D (Cd equals 4.5 for intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames a deflection amplification factor Cd equal to 4.5, which is applied to the elastic story drift computed under reduced seismic design forces to estimate the actual expected inelastic drift for comparison against the code drift limits.
  7. The correct answer is B (Omega0 equals 3.0 for intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames an overstrength factor Omega0 equal to 3.0. This value is higher than the 2.5 assigned to special moment frames, reflecting the reduced ductility of intermediate detailing and the correspondingly greater reliance on strength to limit inelastic demand.
  8. The correct answer is C (Intermediate moment frames are not permitted as the seismic force-resisting system in SDC D): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 does not permit intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames as the seismic force-resisting system for structures assigned to Seismic Design Category D, E, or F. Their use as the primary system is limited to Seismic Design Categories A, B, and C, consistent with the Category C assignment used in this project.
  9. The correct answer is C (ACI 318-14 18.14 requires shear reinforcement sized for the design story drift ratio): ACI 318-14 18.14 requires that slab-column connections of two-way slabs without beams, in structures assigned to Seismic Design Category C or higher, be checked for the design story drift ratio, with shear reinforcement provided at the connection when the computed drift ratio and gravity shear combination exceeds the specified limits.
  10. The correct answer is C (Nominal moment strength using the specified yield strength directly): ACI 318-14 18.4.2.3(a) bases the first alternative on the nominal moment strengths at the beam ends, computed using the specified yield strength fy directly rather than the 1.25 times fy overstrength factor used for probable moment strength in special moment frames, combined with tributary gravity loads along the span.
  11. The correct answer is B (The overstrength factor Omega0 amplifies the seismic shear): ACI 318-14 18.4.2.3(b) permits establishing the beam design shear force using load combinations that include the seismic load effect amplified by the system overstrength factor Omega0, rather than basing the shear directly on computed beam end moment strengths as in the first alternative method.
  12. The correct answer is A (It is permitted, provided the required slab-column connection reinforcement is furnished): Because this building is assigned to Seismic Design Category C, ACI 318-14 18.14 permits the two-way flat plate slab-column framing to participate in the seismic force-resisting system, provided the slab-column connections are furnished with the shear reinforcement required to accommodate the expected design story drift ratio alongside the gravity shear.
Scenario S67

A 2-story retail building, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category B, uses ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls as its seismic force-resisting system, classified as a bearing wall system per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1. A wall segment has length lw = 16 ft, thickness t = 10 in, and height hw = 24 ft, giving an aspect ratio hw/lw = 1.5. The wall uses normalweight concrete with f'c = 3,000 psi and Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi, reinforced with deformed bars no larger than number 5. Factored demands at the wall base are Pu = 150 kips and Vu = 90 kips. Because the wall is classified as ordinary, it is designed per the non-seismic provisions of ACI 318-14 Chapter 11 rather than the special seismic detailing of Chapter 18.

  1. The correct answer is B (The minimum ratio is 0.0012, determined from the properties described in this scenario): ACI 318-14 11.6.1 sets a minimum reinforcement ratio of 0.0012 for both vertical and horizontal reinforcement in ordinary walls when using deformed bars no larger than number 5 with a specified yield strength of at least 60,000 psi, applicable to this wall using number 5 or smaller Grade 60 deformed bars.
  2. The correct answer is B (The alternative minimum ratio is 0.0015): ACI 318-14 11.6.1 requires a minimum reinforcement ratio of 0.0015 for ordinary walls reinforced with bars larger than number 5, or with welded wire reinforcement, which is slightly higher than the 0.0012 ratio permitted specifically for deformed bars no larger than number 5.
  3. The correct answer is C (Vc is approximately 210 kips): Acv equals 10 in times 192 in, or 1,920 sq in. Vc equals 2 times 1.0 times the square root of 3,000, times 1,920, which is 2 times 54.8 times 1,920, or approximately 210,200 lb, about 210 kips, using the simplified expression permitted for ordinary walls not governed by the more detailed provisions.
  4. The correct answer is B (phi equals 0.75 for this ordinary wall, using the geometry and material properties stated above): For shear design of ordinary structural members not subject to the special seismic reduction factors of ACI 318-14 Chapter 18, the standard shear strength reduction factor phi = 0.75 applies, consistent with the general provisions of ACI 318-14 Chapter 21 for shear-controlled behavior.
  5. The correct answer is A (phiVc is approximately 158 kips, calculated from the demands given for this member): The design shear strength equals phi times Vc, or 0.75 times 210 kips, which equals approximately 157.5 kips, rounding to about 158 kips. This value represents the wall's design shear capacity from the concrete contribution alone, before any additional horizontal reinforcement contribution is added.
  6. The correct answer is D (The wall is adequate, since phiVc exceeds Vu): Since the design shear strength phiVc of approximately 158 kips exceeds the factored shear demand Vu of 90 kips, the wall provides adequate shear strength for this demand based on the concrete contribution alone, without needing to rely on the horizontal web reinforcement for additional shear capacity.
  7. The correct answer is A (R equals 4.0 for this ordinary bearing wall system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls used as a bearing wall system a response modification coefficient R equal to 4.0, reflecting the more limited ductility available compared with the R = 5.0 assigned to special reinforced concrete shear walls in the same bearing wall system classification.
  8. The correct answer is D (Cd equals 4.0 for this ordinary bearing wall system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls in a bearing wall system a deflection amplification factor Cd equal to 4.0. This value is used to amplify the elastic drift computed under the reduced seismic design forces to estimate the expected actual inelastic story drift for this system.
  9. The correct answer is A (Omega0 equals 2.5 for ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls an overstrength factor Omega0 equal to 2.5, the same value assigned to special reinforced concrete shear walls, since this overstrength factor reflects general system-level force redistribution rather than the specific ductility of the detailing used.
  10. The correct answer is C (Ordinary shear walls are not permitted as the seismic force-resisting system in SDC D): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 does not permit ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls to be used as the seismic force-resisting system for structures assigned to Seismic Design Category C, D, E, or F. Their use as the primary bearing wall system is limited to Seismic Design Categories A and B, consistent with this building's Category B assignment.
  11. The correct answer is C (Chapter 18 special detailing does not apply, since the wall is ordinary): Because this wall is classified as an ordinary reinforced concrete shear wall rather than a special or intermediate wall, it is designed under the non-seismic provisions of ACI 318-14 Chapter 11 rather than the special seismic detailing requirements, including boundary element checks and minimum web reinforcement ratios, found in Chapter 18.
  12. The correct answer is A (hw/lw is approximately 1.5, classifying the wall as relatively squat): Dividing the wall height of 24 ft by the wall length of 16 ft gives hw/lw equal to 1.5. This relatively low aspect ratio classifies the wall as comparatively squat, meaning shear behavior tends to dominate its response more than flexural behavior would for a more slender wall with a higher aspect ratio.
Scenario S68

This Concrete structure is a 4-story parking garage, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D, with a cast-in-place concrete diaphragm at each level distributing seismic forces to special reinforced concrete shear walls. The roof diaphragm measures 200 ft long by 120 ft wide, with a thickness t = 6 in and f'c = 4,000 psi normalweight concrete. The factored diaphragm shear along the 120 ft depth direction is vu = 800 plf. A collector element along an interior wall line carries a factored collector force Fc = 180 kips, already amplified by the overstrength factor Omega0 per ASCE 7-16 12.10.2.1, delivering this force into a special reinforced concrete shear wall. Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi is used for the collector.

  1. The correct answer is D (Yes, Fc already reflects the required Omega0 amplification): As stated for this scenario, the collector force Fc = 180 kips already reflects the amplification required by ASCE 7-16 12.10.2.1, which mandates that collector elements and their connections to vertical elements be designed for seismic load effects amplified by the system overstrength factor Omega0, so no further amplification of this given value is needed.
  2. The correct answer is B (As is approximately 3.3 sq in, using the geometry and material properties stated above): The required area equals Fc divided by phi times fy, or 180,000 lb divided by (0.90 times 60,000 psi), which equals 180,000 divided by 54,000, or approximately 3.33 sq in. This is the minimum longitudinal reinforcement area needed to carry the amplified collector tension force in a tension-controlled design.
  3. The correct answer is A (4 bars are needed to satisfy the required area): Three number 9 bars provide only 3.0 sq in, which falls short of the required 3.33 sq in. Four number 9 bars provide 4.0 sq in, which exceeds the required area, making four bars the minimum number needed to satisfy the collector's tension reinforcement requirement.
  4. The correct answer is D (phi equals 0.90 for tension-controlled sections): ACI 318-14 assigns a strength reduction factor phi = 0.90 to tension-controlled reinforced concrete sections, which applies to this collector element designed to carry axial tension from the amplified seismic collector force into the adjoining special reinforced concrete shear wall.
  5. The correct answer is C (ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1, the collector elements provision): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1 specifically requires collector elements, splices, and their connections to supporting vertical elements to be designed to resist earthquake load effects, including gravity loads, amplified by the system overstrength factor Omega0, which is the provision governing this collector's amplified 180 kip design force.
  6. The correct answer is C (ACI 318-14 Section 18.12 governs special diaphragm seismic provisions): ACI 318-14 Section 18.12 provides the special seismic design provisions for cast-in-place and precast concrete diaphragms in structures assigned to higher Seismic Design Categories, including minimum reinforcement, shear strength expressions, and collector detailing requirements applicable to this parking garage's roof diaphragm.
  7. The correct answer is D (Vn equals Acv times (2 lambda sqrt(f'c), plus rho_t times fy)): ACI 318-14 18.12.9 gives the nominal shear strength of a cast-in-place diaphragm in a form analogous to the wall shear strength equation, as Acv times the sum of 2 times lambda times the square root of f'c and rho_t times fy, combining a concrete contribution term with a reinforcement contribution term across the diaphragm shear area Acv.
  8. The correct answer is D (Vn shall not exceed 8 times Acv times the square root of f'c): ACI 318-14 18.12.9 caps the assumed nominal shear strength of a cast-in-place concrete diaphragm at 8 times Acv times the square root of f'c, matching the same form of upper limit that applies to special structural walls under Chapter 18.10, preventing an unrealistically high reliance on combined concrete and steel contributions.
  9. The correct answer is B (The minimum diaphragm reinforcement ratio is 0.0025, determined from the properties described in this scenario): ACI 318-14 Chapter 18 aligns the minimum reinforcement ratio for cast-in-place seismic diaphragms with the 0.0025 minimum ratio required for special structural wall web reinforcement, reflecting the diaphragm's role as a critical, essentially elastic load path element within the seismic force resisting system.
  10. The correct answer is B (The overstrength factor Omega0 governs the collector force amplification): The overstrength factor Omega0 specifically governs the amplification of collector forces under ASCE 7-16 12.10.2.1, while the redundancy factor rho instead applies more broadly to the overall horizontal seismic load effect used in general strength design load combinations, making Omega0 the correct factor for this collector force.
  11. The correct answer is C (To ensure the collector can reliably transfer force along its full length): Requiring collector splices to develop the full tension capacity of the spliced bars ensures the collector element can reliably transfer the amplified seismic force along its entire length into the vertical elements of the seismic force-resisting system, without a weak link forming at a splice location under repeated load reversal.
  12. The correct answer is C (Vu is approximately 96 kips): The total factored diaphragm shear equals the unit shear vu multiplied by the diaphragm depth over which it acts, or 800 plf times 120 ft, which equals 96,000 lb, or approximately 96 kips delivered into the shear walls resisting this level of the parking garage.
Scenario S69

A 20-story residential tower, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D, uses special reinforced concrete shear walls classified as a building frame system per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1. A critical wall has length lw = 25 ft, thickness t = 16 in, and total height hw = 220 ft. The wall is designed using the displacement-based method of ACI 318-14 18.10.6.2 for evaluating boundary element requirements, with a design displacement at the wall top of delta_u = 18 in. A strain compatibility analysis gives a neutral axis depth c = 90 in at the design displacement. At the critical section, factored demands are Mu = 25,000 kip-ft and Vu = 300 kips.

  1. The correct answer is A (delta_u/hw is approximately 0.0068, calculated from the demands given for this member): Converting hw to inches gives 220 ft times 12, or 2,640 in. Dividing the design displacement of 18 in by 2,640 in gives delta_u divided by hw equal to approximately 0.00682, which rounds to approximately 0.0068.
  2. The correct answer is A (The minimum value of delta_u/hw is 0.005, determined from the properties described in this scenario): ACI 318-14 18.10.6.2 requires that delta_u divided by hw not be taken as less than 0.005 in the displacement-based boundary element evaluation, even if the actual computed design displacement ratio for the structure is smaller than this value, ensuring a minimum level of conservatism in the method.
  3. The correct answer is C (c* is approximately 73 in): Converting lw to inches gives 25 ft times 12, or 300 in. The threshold equals 300 divided by (600 times 0.00682), or 300 divided by 4.09, which equals approximately 73.3 in, representing the neutral axis depth at which boundary elements first become required under this method.
  4. The correct answer is B (Boundary elements are required, since c exceeds the threshold c*): Since the actual neutral axis depth c = 90 in exceeds the threshold c* of approximately 73.3 in, ACI 318-14 18.10.6.2 requires special boundary elements to be provided at the compression boundary of this wall, confirming that the wall's inelastic deformation demand at the design displacement exceeds what the boundary can tolerate without confinement.
  5. The correct answer is A (The required horizontal extent is 60 in, using the geometry and material properties stated above): The first quantity, c minus 0.1lw, equals 90 minus 30, or 60 in. The second quantity, c/2, equals 90 divided by 2, or 45 in. The larger of these two values is 60 in, which governs the required horizontal extent of the boundary element from the extreme compression fiber.
  6. The correct answer is D (The required vertical extent is approximately 25.0 ft): Mu divided by (4 times Vu) equals 25,000 divided by (4 times 300), or 25,000 divided by 1,200, which equals approximately 20.83 ft. Comparing this to lw = 25 ft, the larger of the two values, 25 ft, governs the required vertical extent of the boundary element above the critical section.
  7. The correct answer is C (Mu/(4Vu) is approximately 20.8 ft, determined from the properties described in this scenario): Dividing Mu = 25,000 kip-ft by 4 times Vu, or 4 times 300 kips equal to 1,200 kips, gives 25,000 divided by 1,200, which equals approximately 20.83 ft, rounding to approximately 20.8 ft.
  8. The correct answer is A (The wall length lw governs, since 25 ft exceeds 20.8 ft): Since ACI 318-14 18.10.6.2(b) requires the larger of the two quantities, and the wall length lw of 25 ft exceeds the computed value of Mu divided by 4Vu, which is approximately 20.8 ft, the wall length lw governs and sets the required vertical extent of the boundary element above the critical section.
  9. The correct answer is C (R equals 6.0 for this building frame system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls classified as a building frame system a response modification coefficient R equal to 6.0, which is higher than the R = 5.0 assigned when the same special shear wall type instead serves as a bearing wall system.
  10. The correct answer is D (Cd equals 5.0 for this building frame system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls classified as a building frame system a deflection amplification factor Cd equal to 5.0, the same Cd value used for the bearing wall system classification of the identical special shear wall type.
  11. The correct answer is A (Omega0 equals 2.5 for this building frame system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls an overstrength factor Omega0 equal to 2.5, regardless of whether the wall system is classified as a bearing wall or a building frame system, applying consistently to force-controlled elements such as collectors in this residential tower.
  12. The correct answer is A (The displacement-based method of ACI 318-14 18.10.6.2 was applied): Because this evaluation relied on the design displacement delta_u, the ratio delta_u/hw, and the neutral axis depth c compared against a computed threshold c*, rather than comparing an extreme fiber compressive stress against 0.2 times f'c, the displacement-based method of ACI 318-14 18.10.6.2 was the method applied in this scenario.
Scenario S70

A 12-story condominium building, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D, uses coupled special reinforced concrete shear walls as its seismic force-resisting system. A coupling beam at a lower level has clear span ln = 8 ft, total depth h = 24 in, effective depth d = 21 in, and width bw = 14 in, giving an aspect ratio ln/h = 4.0. The beam uses normalweight concrete with f'c = 5,000 psi and Grade 60 reinforcement with fy = 60,000 psi, and longitudinal bars sized as number 8 (db = 1.0 in). The factored shear demand from analysis is Vu = 110 kips, gravity shear at the beam ends is Vg = 8 kips, and probable flexural strengths at the beam ends are Mpr,left = 210 kip-ft and Mpr,right = 190 kip-ft. Because ln/h = 4.0, the beam is proportioned per ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4 using conventional beam-like reinforcement following 18.6.3 through 18.6.5.

  1. The correct answer is B (The aspect ratio ln/h is approximately 4.0, computed using the applicable code equation described above): Converting the clear span to inches gives 8 ft times 12, or 96 in. Dividing this by the total depth of 24 in gives ln/h equal to 96 divided by 24, or 4.0. This aspect ratio of exactly 4.0 meets the threshold at which conventional beam-like reinforcement becomes permitted rather than mandatory diagonal reinforcement.
  2. The correct answer is B (Conventional beam reinforcement per 18.6.3 through 18.6.5 is permitted): Because ln/h equals 4.0, which meets the threshold defined in ACI 318-14 18.10.7.1, the coupling beam is permitted to be proportioned as a conventional beam following the requirements of 18.6.3 through 18.6.5, rather than being required to use the diagonal reinforcement pattern needed for shorter, more squat coupling beams below this aspect ratio.
  3. The correct answer is D (The threshold shear is approximately 95 kips, using the geometry and material properties stated above): Acw equals 14 times 24, or 336 sq in. The threshold equals 4 times 1.0 times the square root of 5,000, times 336, which is 4 times 70.7 times 336, equal to approximately 95,050 lb, or approximately 95 kips.
  4. The correct answer is B (Vu exceeds the threshold, but ln/h = 4.0 exempts the beam from the diagonal requirement): Although Vu = 110 kips exceeds the reference threshold shear of approximately 95 kips, the diagonal reinforcement requirement of ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4 only becomes mandatory for coupling beams with ln/h less than 4. Since this beam has ln/h exactly equal to 4.0, it is exempt from that mandatory diagonal reinforcement requirement.
  5. The correct answer is B (Ve is approximately 58 kips, determined from the properties described in this scenario): The sum of probable moment strengths equals 210 plus 190, or 400 kip-ft, divided by the clear span of 8 ft, giving 50 kips from the probable moment gradient. Adding the gravity shear contribution of Vg = 8 kips gives Ve equal to 50 plus 8, or approximately 58 kips for the governing direction.
  6. The correct answer is C (The hoop region length is 48 in from the face of support): The required length equals 2h, or 2 times 24 in, giving 48 in measured from the face of the wall pier at each end of the coupling beam. Over this 48 in region, closely spaced hoop reinforcement satisfying the governing spacing limits must be provided.
  7. The correct answer is A (The maximum spacing is 5.25 in, governed by d/4): The three limits are d/4 equal to 21 divided by 4, or 5.25 in; 6db equal to 6 times 1.0 in, or 6.0 in; and the fixed 6 in limit. The smallest of these three values is 5.25 in, so d/4 governs and limits the maximum hoop spacing in the plastic hinge region to 5.25 in.
  8. The correct answer is D (At least two continuous bars are required per face): ACI 318-14 18.6.3.3 requires at least two longitudinal bars to be provided continuously along both the top and bottom faces of the beam, which applies to this coupling beam since it follows the conventional beam detailing provisions of 18.6.3 through 18.6.5.
  9. The correct answer is A (The positive moment strength must be at least one half of the negative strength): ACI 318-14 18.6.3.2 requires the positive moment strength at a joint face to be at least one half of the negative moment strength at that same face, which applies to this coupling beam since it follows the conventional special moment frame beam provisions rather than the diagonal reinforcement pattern.
  10. The correct answer is A (Closed hoops with seismic hooks are required in the hinge region): ACI 318-14 18.6.4 requires closed hoops with properly detailed seismic hooks within the plastic hinge regions at each end of a conventionally detailed beam, including this coupling beam, to provide the confinement and longitudinal bar restraint needed under large inelastic strain reversals.
  11. The correct answer is B (Diagonal reinforcement is required, since ln/h is below 4 and Vu exceeds the threshold): Because ln/h equals 3.0, which is less than the 4.0 threshold that exempts a beam from the diagonal requirement, and the factored shear demand of 110 kips exceeds the reference threshold shear of approximately 95 kips, ACI 318-14 18.10.7.4 requires this alternative beam to be reinforced with two intersecting groups of diagonal bars.
  12. The correct answer is C (R equals 6.0 for this coupled special shear wall system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls classified as a building frame system a response modification coefficient R equal to 6.0, which applies to this coupled shear wall configuration where coupling beams link discrete wall piers acting together as a single building frame lateral system.

Practice Test 15 Answer Key

Scenario S71

A structural engineer is comparing three alternative reinforced masonry lateral force-resisting systems for a proposed 5-story mixed-use building assigned to Seismic Design Category C: special reinforced masonry shear walls in a building frame system, intermediate reinforced masonry shear walls in a bearing wall system, and ordinary reinforced masonry shear walls in a bearing wall system. The building has an effective seismic weight W = 6,000 kips, an importance factor Ie = 1.0, and a design spectral response acceleration parameter SDS = 0.60. The seismic response coefficient is computed as Cs = SDS divided by (R divided by Ie) for each candidate system, and the base shear is computed as V = Cs times W.

  1. The correct answer is A (Cs is approximately 0.120, using the geometry and material properties stated above): Cs equals SDS divided by (R divided by Ie), or 0.60 divided by (5.0 divided by 1.0), which equals 0.60 divided by 5.0, or 0.120. This response coefficient corresponds to the special reinforced masonry shear wall system, which uses the highest R value among the three systems being compared.
  2. The correct answer is A (V is approximately 720 kips, calculated from the demands given for this member): The design base shear equals Cs times W, or 0.120 times 6,000 kips, which equals 720 kips for the special reinforced masonry shear wall system, reflecting the reduced seismic demand associated with the higher R value and greater ductility of this system.
  3. The correct answer is D (Cs is approximately 0.171, determined from the properties described in this scenario): Cs equals SDS divided by (R divided by Ie), or 0.60 divided by (3.5 divided by 1.0), which equals 0.60 divided by 3.5, or approximately 0.171. This higher response coefficient, compared with the special shear wall system, reflects the lower R value assigned to intermediate reinforced masonry shear walls. ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 has no separate 'intermediate' tier for cast-in-place reinforced concrete shear walls; that middle ductility tier exists for reinforced masonry (and precast concrete) shear walls instead.
  4. The correct answer is B (V is approximately 1,029 kips, computed using the applicable code equation described above): The design base shear equals Cs times W, or approximately 0.171 times 6,000 kips, which equals approximately 1,029 kips for the intermediate reinforced masonry shear wall bearing wall system, higher than the 720 kips computed for the special masonry shear wall system.
  5. The correct answer is D (Cs is approximately 0.300, based on the values given in this scenario): Cs equals SDS divided by (R divided by Ie), or 0.60 divided by (2.0 divided by 1.0), which equals 0.60 divided by 2.0, or 0.300. This is the highest response coefficient among the three candidate systems, reflecting the lowest R value assigned to ordinary reinforced masonry shear walls.
  6. The correct answer is B (V is approximately 1,800 kips, using the geometry and material properties stated above): The design base shear equals Cs times W, or 0.300 times 6,000 kips, which equals 1,800 kips for the ordinary reinforced masonry shear wall bearing wall system, the highest base shear among the three candidate systems evaluated for this building.
  7. The correct answer is D (The special reinforced masonry shear wall system produces the lowest base shear): The special reinforced masonry shear wall system, with the highest R value of 5.0 among the three systems, produces the lowest computed design base shear of approximately 720 kips, reflecting the greater ductility credit given to its more rigorous seismic detailing under TMS 402/602-16.
  8. The correct answer is B (It is not permitted in Seismic Design Category C or higher): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 restricts the use of ordinary reinforced masonry shear walls in a bearing wall system to Seismic Design Categories A and B only, listing this system as not permitted for Seismic Design Category C or higher, which eliminates it as a viable option for this Category C building despite its lower computed base shear demand.
  9. The correct answer is B (It is permitted, since intermediate shear walls are allowed through SDC C): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 permits the intermediate reinforced masonry shear wall bearing wall system to be used as the seismic force-resisting system through Seismic Design Category C, making it a viable option for this building, unlike the ordinary reinforced masonry shear wall system, which is restricted to Seismic Design Categories A and B only.
  10. The correct answer is A (Cd equals 2.25 for this intermediate bearing wall system): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns a deflection amplification factor Cd equal to 2.25 to the intermediate reinforced masonry shear wall bearing wall system, which is used to estimate the expected inelastic story drift from the elastic drift computed under the reduced seismic design forces for this system.
  11. The correct answer is D (Omega0 equals 2.5 for all three shear wall systems): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns an overstrength factor Omega0 equal to 2.5 to special, intermediate, and ordinary reinforced masonry shear wall systems alike, since this factor reflects general force redistribution behavior common across these related wall system types rather than differences in ductile detailing.
  12. The correct answer is A (The special and intermediate reinforced masonry shear wall systems remain viable): Since ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 excludes the ordinary reinforced masonry shear wall bearing wall system from use in Seismic Design Category C, the engineer's viable choices narrow to the special reinforced masonry shear wall system and the intermediate reinforced masonry shear wall system, both of which are permitted for this Category C building.
Scenario S72

A 9-story reinforced concrete apartment building in Los Angeles, California (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 1.20g, SD1 = 0.65g, Seismic Design Category D) uses special reinforced concrete bearing shear walls as the sole lateral force-resisting system in both directions. A typical wall segment is lw = 20 ft (240 in) long and 16 in thick, with total height hw = 90 ft (1080 in) above the base. Concrete strength f'c = 5000 psi and reinforcement is Grade 60 (fy = 60,000 psi). At the critical base section, factored shear demand Vu = 800 kips and factored moment Mu = 14,000 ft-kips act together with a design story drift ratio of 0.015. Wall vertical and horizontal reinforcement each provide rho = 0.0025.

  1. The correct answer is B (hw/lw equals 4.5, so alpha_c equals 2.0 because the wall behaves as a slender wall under Chapter 18): hw/lw equals 1080 in / 240 in = 4.5, which is well above the 2.0 threshold in ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.4.1, so the wall is classified as slender and alpha_c equals 2.0 rather than the 3.0 value used for squat walls with hw/lw of 1.5 or less.
  2. The correct answer is C (Vn is approximately 1120 kips, matching the correctly applied ACI 318-14 shear equation): Vn = Acv(alpha_c*lambda*sqrt(f'c) + rho_t*fy) = 3840*(2.0*70.7 + 0.0025*60000) = 3840*(141.4 + 150) = 3840*291.4, which equals about 1,119,000 lb or approximately 1120 kips.
  3. The correct answer is A (phi*Vn is about 839 kips, which exceeds Vu, so the wall section is adequate in shear): phi*Vn = 0.75*1119 kips equals about 839 kips, which exceeds the factored shear demand Vu of 800 kips, so the wall section satisfies ACI 318-14's phi*Vn is greater than or equal to Vu requirement and is adequate in shear.
  4. The correct answer is D (The limit is about 2173 kips, well above the calculated Vn, so the section is acceptable): the upper limit is 8*Acv*sqrt(f'c) = 8*3840*70.7, which equals about 2,173,000 lb or approximately 2173 kips, far exceeding the calculated Vn of about 1119 kips, confirming the wall section is not overstressed under this ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.4.4 check.
  5. The correct answer is D (Two curtains are required because Vu of 800 kips exceeds the calculated 543-kip threshold): the two-curtain threshold is 2*Acv*lambda*sqrt(f'c) = 2*3840*70.7, which equals about 543,000 lb or 543 kips; since Vu of 800 kips exceeds this threshold, ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.2.2 requires two curtains of reinforcement rather than a single curtain.
  6. The correct answer is A (0.0025 in each direction, vertical and horizontal): ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.2.1 requires a minimum reinforcement ratio of 0.0025 in each direction, vertical and horizontal, for special reinforced concrete shear walls whenever the factored shear demand exceeds the threshold that would otherwise permit a reduced ratio, as is the case here.
  7. The correct answer is B (18 in, governed by the fixed 18-inch code limit rather than the geometric ratios): the three limits are lw/5 = 240/5 = 48 in, 3h = 3*16 = 48 in, and the fixed 18 in maximum specified in ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.2.2; the smallest governing value is 18 in, which controls over both geometric calculations in this case.
  8. The correct answer is C (About 26.7 in; since c of 30 in exceeds it, a boundary element is required): c_limit = lw/(600(delta_u/hw)) = 240/(600*0.015) = 240/9, which equals about 26.7 in; since the calculated neutral axis depth c of 30 in exceeds this limit, ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.2 requires a special boundary element at this wall end.
  9. The correct answer is A (20 ft, governed by the wall length lw controlling over the moment-based ratio): per ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.4(a), the boundary element must extend the greater of lw = 20 ft or Mu/(4Vu) = 14,000/(4*800) = 4.4 ft above the critical section; since 20 ft exceeds 4.4 ft, the wall length itself governs the required extension height.
  10. The correct answer is D (R = 5, Cd = 5, Omega_0 = 2.5, the bearing wall system value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 5, Cd = 5, and Omega_0 = 2.5 to a bearing wall system built entirely of special reinforced concrete shear walls, which is distinct from the building frame system value of R = 6 that applies when gravity columns carry the vertical load instead.
  11. The correct answer is C (300 kips, the correctly amplified collector force using the assigned Omega_0 factor): per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1, the amplified collector design force equals the unamplified force times Omega_0, so 120 kips times 2.5 equals 300 kips as the required design force for the collector element and its connections.
  12. The correct answer is B (It is a full wall segment since lw/bw of 15 far exceeds the 2.5 wall pier limit): lw/bw = (20 x 12)/16 = 15. ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.8 classifies a segment as a wall pier only when 2.5 < lw/bw <= 6.0 (a segment at or below 2.5 is instead designed as a column). Since 15 exceeds even the upper 6.0 wall-pier bound, this segment is treated as a full wall under the general Section 18.10.4 provisions rather than under the wall pier provisions of Section 18.10.8.
Scenario S73

A 5-story reinforced concrete office building in Memphis, Tennessee (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.55g, SD1 = 0.30g, Seismic Design Category C) uses intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames as the lateral force-resisting system in both directions. A typical frame bay spans 24 ft between column centerlines, with columns 20 in by 20 in and beams 16 in wide by 24 in deep. Concrete strength f'c = 4000 psi and reinforcement is Grade 60. At an exterior joint, factored beam shear at the column face is Vu = 45 kips and factored column axial load is Pu = 250 kips, with a clear column height of 120 in.

  1. The correct answer is C (R = 5, Cd = 4.5, Omega_0 = 3, the intermediate moment frame value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 5, Cd = 4.5, and Omega_0 = 3 to intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames, values that fall between the ordinary moment frame's R = 3 and the special moment frame's R = 8, reflecting the system's intermediate ductility.
  2. The correct answer is B (5.4 in, governed by the d/4 limit, the smallest of the four calculated values): comparing d/4 of about 5.4 in, 8db of about 8 in, 24db of about 9 in, and the fixed 12 in maximum, the smallest of the four values, 5.4 in, governs the maximum permitted hoop spacing within 2h of the column face per ACI 318-14 Section 18.4.3.3.
  3. The correct answer is D (Vc is approximately 43.5 kips, matching the concrete shear equation applied correctly here): Vc = 2*lambda*sqrt(f'c)*bw*d = 2*1.0*63.2*16*21.5, which equals 2*63.2 psi times 344 in-squared, or about 43,500 lb, approximately 43.5 kips of concrete shear contribution for this beam cross-section.
  4. The correct answer is A (Vc need not be set to zero even when earthquake-induced shear is large relative to total shear): ACI 318-14 Section 18.6.5.2 requires special moment frame column shear design to neglect Vc under certain axial load and earthquake-shear-proportion conditions, but this Vc-equals-zero provision is specific to special frames and does not apply to intermediate moment frame beams designed under Section 18.4.
  5. The correct answer is D (No, explicit joint shear strength provisions of Section 18.8 apply only to special moment frames): the joint shear strength equation Vn = gamma*sqrt(f'c)*Aj in ACI 318-14 Section 18.8 is written specifically for special moment frames; intermediate moment frames designed under Section 18.4 are not required to perform this explicit joint shear strength check.
  6. The correct answer is C (Pu of 250 kips exceeds Ag*f'c/10 of 160 kips, triggering special transverse reinforcement over lo): Ag*f'c/10 = 400 in-squared * 4000 psi / 10, which equals 160,000 lb or 160 kips; since the factored column axial load Pu of 250 kips exceeds this 160 kip threshold, ACI 318-14 Section 18.4.3 triggers special transverse reinforcement over the length lo at the column ends.
  7. The correct answer is A (20 in, governed by both the clear-height ratio and the column dimension being equal): the three candidate values are clear height/6 = 120/6 = 20 in, the maximum column dimension of 20 in, and the fixed 18 in minimum; the largest of these, 20 in, governs lo and happens to be produced identically by both the clear-height ratio and the column dimension.
  8. The correct answer is B (No, the strong column-weak beam provision applies only to special moment frames): the strong column-weak beam provision requiring the sum of nominal column moments to be at least 1.2 times the sum of nominal beam moments is specified in ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.3, which applies only to special reinforced concrete moment frames, not intermediate frames under Section 18.4.
  9. The correct answer is B (Yes, intermediate moment frames are permitted through Seismic Design Category C): per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1, intermediate reinforced concrete moment frames are a permitted seismic force-resisting system through Seismic Design Category C, so this building, assigned to SDC C, is allowed to use this system as its primary lateral force resistance.
  10. The correct answer is A (0.00333, governed by the 200/fy expression): 3*sqrt(f'c)/fy = 3*63.2/60000, which equals about 0.00316, while 200/fy = 200/60000, which equals about 0.00333; since the code takes the larger of the two values, 0.00333 governs the minimum flexural reinforcement ratio for this beam.
  11. The correct answer is D (rho = 1.0, since the redundancy factor requirement applies only to SDC D, E, and F): per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4, the redundancy factor rho requirement applies only to structures assigned to Seismic Design Category D, E, or F; because this building is assigned to SDC C, rho is taken as 1.0 without any element-removal or torsional-irregularity check being required.
  12. The correct answer is C (delta_x is approximately 1.58 in, the correctly amplified roof-level deflection value): delta_x = Cd*delta_xe/Ie = 4.5*0.35/1.0, which equals 1.575 in, approximately 1.58 in, representing the amplified deflection used to check story drift against the allowable limit for this intermediate moment frame building.
Scenario S74

A 3-story reinforced concrete retail building in Dallas, Texas (Risk Category II, Site Class C, SDS = 0.28g, SD1 = 0.12g, Seismic Design Category B) uses ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls as a bearing wall system. A typical wall segment is lw = 16 ft (192 in) long and 10 in thick, with total height hw = 36 ft (432 in) and a story height of 144 in. Concrete strength f'c = 3500 psi and reinforcement is Grade 60. Factored base shear demand on the wall is Vu = 180 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (R = 4, Cd = 4, Omega_0 = 2.5, the ordinary bearing wall system value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 4, Cd = 4, and Omega_0 = 2.5 to a bearing wall system built of ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls, values notably lower than the R = 5 assigned to special reinforced concrete shear walls in the same bearing wall configuration.
  2. The correct answer is D (Yes, ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls are permitted in Seismic Design Category A and B): per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1, ordinary reinforced concrete shear walls are permitted as the seismic force-resisting system only in Seismic Design Category A and B; since this building is assigned to SDC B, the system is permitted for use here.
  3. The correct answer is B (General Chapter 11 wall design provisions apply since Chapter 18 special provisions do not cover it): ACI 318-14 Chapter 18's special shear wall shear strength provisions, including the alpha_c coefficient equation, apply only to walls classified as special or intermediate under Chapter 18; an ordinary shear wall is instead designed using the general wall shear provisions of Chapter 11.
  4. The correct answer is A (Vc is about 227 kips, already exceeding Vu of 180 kips before any steel contribution): Vc = 2*lambda*sqrt(f'c)*Acv = 2*1.0*59.2*1920, which equals about 227,000 lb or 227 kips; since this concrete contribution alone already exceeds Vu of 180 kips, the wall has adequate shear capacity before even considering the horizontal steel contribution.
  5. The correct answer is A (Vertical 0.0012 and horizontal 0.0020, the general Chapter 11 minimum ratios): ACI 318-14 Section 11.6 sets the general minimum reinforcement ratios at 0.0012 vertical and 0.0020 horizontal for No. 5 or smaller Grade 60 deformed bars, values that are lower than the 0.0025 minimum in each direction required specifically for special shear walls under Chapter 18.
  6. The correct answer is B (No, Section 18.10.6 boundary element provisions apply only to special reinforced concrete shear walls): the special boundary element trigger criteria of ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6, both the displacement-based and stress-based methods, apply only to walls classified as special reinforced concrete shear walls, so this ordinary shear wall is not subject to that section at all.
  7. The correct answer is C (18 in, governed by the fixed code maximum rather than the 3h calculation): under the general ACI 318-14 provisions applicable to this ordinary wall, 3h = 3*10 in equals 30 in, but the fixed 18 in code maximum governs since it is the smaller of the two limits being compared for this wall's reinforcement spacing.
  8. The correct answer is D (0.9D + 1.0E, which minimizes the stabilizing dead load effect against overturning): ASCE 7-16 Section 2.3 load combination 0.9D + 1.0E is specifically intended to check overturning and uplift conditions because it minimizes the stabilizing dead load while including the full seismic effect, producing the governing case for this type of check.
  9. The correct answer is D (Delta_a is 2.88 in, exceeding the calculated drift of 1.2 in, so the design is acceptable): Delta_a = 0.020*hsx = 0.020*144, which equals 2.88 in, the allowable story drift for this Risk Category II structure per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1; since the calculated amplified drift of 1.2 in is well below this limit, the design satisfies the drift requirement.
  10. The correct answer is B (225 kips, the correctly amplified collector force using the assigned overstrength factor): per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1, the amplified collector force equals the unamplified force times Omega_0, so 90 kips times 2.5 equals 225 kips as the required amplified design force for this collector element and its connections.
  11. The correct answer is A (No, Section 18.10.8 wall pier provisions apply only to special reinforced concrete shear walls): the wall pier provisions of ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.8, which use the lw/bw not exceeding 2.5 criterion, are contained entirely within the special shear wall provisions of Section 18.10 and therefore do not apply to an ordinary reinforced concrete shear wall.
  12. The correct answer is C (The minimum is 5.76 in, less than the actual 10 in thickness, so the wall is acceptable): the minimum thickness per ACI 318-14 Section 14.5.3.1 is the greater of 1/25 of the unsupported height (144/25 = 5.76 in) or 4 in, so 5.76 in governs; since the actual thickness of 10 in exceeds this minimum, the wall satisfies the requirement.
Scenario S75

A 6-story reinforced concrete hospital in San Francisco, California (Risk Category IV, Ie = 1.5, Site Class D, SDS = 1.35g, SD1 = 0.80g, Seismic Design Category D) uses special reinforced concrete moment frames as the lateral force-resisting system. A typical interior beam-column joint has beams 18 in wide by 28 in deep framing into a 26 in square column confined on all four sides by beams at least three-quarters of the column width. Concrete strength f'c = 5000 psi and reinforcement is Grade 60. At the joint, factored column axial load Pu = 400 kips, and the sum of nominal beam moment capacities framing into the joint is 1200 ft-kips, with a story height of 144 in.

  1. The correct answer is C (gamma = 20, for joints confined on all four faces): ACI 318-14 Section 18.8.4.3 assigns gamma = 20 to joints confined on all four faces by beams at least three-quarters of the column width, the highest of the three gamma values, reflecting the added confinement provided by beams framing in from every side.
  2. The correct answer is A (Vn is approximately 956 kips, matching the joint shear equation applied correctly here): Vn = gamma*sqrt(f'c)*Aj = 20*70.7*676, which equals about 955,900 lb or approximately 956 kips, representing the nominal joint shear strength available at this fully confined interior beam-column joint.
  3. The correct answer is D (1440 ft-kips, using the required 1.2 amplification factor applied to 1200 ft-kips): ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.3.2 requires the sum of nominal column moments to be at least 1.2 times the sum of nominal beam moments, so 1.2*1200 ft-kips equals 1440 ft-kips as the minimum required sum of column flexural strengths at this joint.
  4. The correct answer is B (Vc need not be taken as zero because Pu of 400 kips exceeds the 169-kip threshold): the threshold Ag*f'c/20 = 676*5000/20, which equals 169,000 lb or 169 kips, is compared to Pu of 400 kips; since Pu exceeds this threshold, the axial load condition required to set Vc equal to zero under ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.6.2.1 is not met, so Vc need not be neglected.
  5. The correct answer is B (so is 5.33 in, falling within the required 4 to 6 in range): so = 4 + (14 - hx)/3 = 4 + (14 - 10)/3, which equals 4 + 1.33, or about 5.33 in; this value falls within the 4 in to 6 in range specified in ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.5.3 for special transverse reinforcement spacing.
  6. The correct answer is D (lo is 26 in, governed by the column cross-sectional dimension): comparing clear height/6 = 116/6, which equals about 19.3 in, the 26 in column dimension, and the fixed 18 in minimum, the largest value, 26 in, governs lo and is controlled by the column's own cross-sectional dimension in this case.
  7. The correct answer is C (R = 8, Cd = 5.5, Omega_0 = 3, the special moment frame value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 8, Cd = 5.5, and Omega_0 = 3 to special reinforced concrete moment frames, the highest R value among the concrete moment frame categories, reflecting the extensive ductile detailing required by ACI 318-14 Section 18.7 and 18.8.
  8. The correct answer is A (Delta_a is 1.44 in, the Risk Category IV allowable drift limit): Delta_a = 0.010*hsx = 0.010*144, which equals 1.44 in, the reduced allowable story drift assigned to Risk Category IV structures under ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1, reflecting the higher performance expectation for essential facilities like this hospital.
  9. The correct answer is A (0.01 to 0.06, the special moment frame column range): ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.4.1 limits the longitudinal reinforcement ratio for special moment frame columns to a range of 0.01 to 0.06, ensuring both adequate flexural capacity and constructability of the confined column section under high seismic demand.
  10. The correct answer is C (4 bars, the rectangular column minimum): ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.4.2 requires a minimum of 4 longitudinal bars for a rectangular special moment frame column, compared to a minimum of 6 bars for circular columns, reflecting the different confinement geometry between the two column shapes.
  11. The correct answer is C (SDC F, since SD1 = 0.80g meets Table 11.6-2's own SD1 >= 0.75g threshold for Risk Category IV structures): Table 11.6-1 (based on SDS) tops out at SDC D for this structure, but Table 11.6-2 assigns SDC F directly, in its own right, to any Risk Category IV structure with SD1 at or above 0.75g. Since SD1 = 0.80g here exceeds that threshold, Table 11.6-2 alone already governs at F, which is more severe than Table 11.6-1's D, so SDC F controls for this hospital.
  12. The correct answer is D (56 in, twice the beam depth as required by Section 18.6.4.1): ACI 318-14 Section 18.6.4.1 requires the special transverse reinforcement region to extend 2h from the face of the support, so 2*28 in equals 56 in, the length over which hoops must be provided at each end of this special moment frame beam.

Practice Test 16 Answer Key

Scenario S76

A 14-story reinforced concrete residential tower in Seattle, Washington (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 1.10g, SD1 = 0.60g, Seismic Design Category D) uses a pair of special reinforced concrete shear walls coupled by reinforced concrete coupling beams at every floor around an elevator core, with gravity loads for the typical floor plate carried by a separate system of reinforced concrete gravity columns rather than by the shear wall core itself. Each coupling beam has clear span ln = 6 ft (72 in) and total depth h = 36 in, giving an aspect ratio ln/h = 2.0, with width bw = 16 in. Concrete strength f'c = 6000 psi. The factored shear demand on a typical coupling beam is Vu = 280 kips.

  1. The correct answer is D (ln/h equals 2.0, and since it is not strictly less than 2, the trigger is not met): ln/h = 72/36 equals 2.0 exactly, which is not strictly less than 2 as required by ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.7.2; even though Vu of 280 kips exceeds the 4*lambda*sqrt(f'c)*Acw threshold of about 178.5 kips, both conditions must be satisfied together to mandate diagonal reinforcement.
  2. The correct answer is A (Conventional longitudinal flexural reinforcement combined with confining transverse hoops along the span): per ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.7.2, a coupling beam not required to use diagonal reinforcement may instead be designed with conventional longitudinal flexural reinforcement and confining transverse hoops satisfying the beam shear and flexural strength requirements.
  3. The correct answer is C (Vc is approximately 79.3 kips, matching the concrete shear equation applied correctly here): Vc = 2*lambda*sqrt(f'c)*bw*d = 2*1.0*77.46*16*32, which equals about 79,300 lb or approximately 79.3 kips, the concrete shear contribution for this conventionally reinforced coupling beam.
  4. The correct answer is B (Vs is approximately 294 kips, matching the stirrup demand calculated correctly from Vu and Vc): Vs = Vu/phi - Vc = 280/0.75 - 79.3, which equals 373.3 - 79.3, or about 294 kips, the required stirrup shear contribution needed in addition to the concrete contribution to satisfy the factored shear demand.
  5. The correct answer is C (R = 6, Cd = 5, Omega_0 = 2.5, the building frame system special shear wall value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 6, Cd = 5, and Omega_0 = 2.5 to a building frame system consisting of special reinforced concrete shear walls, which applies here since the coupled wall piers carry the seismic force while gravity columns support gravity loads elsewhere.
  6. The correct answer is B (They transfer shear between the wall piers, coupling them into a composite system and providing ductile energy dissipation): coupling beams transfer shear force between the two wall piers, engaging both piers to act as a composite coupled system that resists overturning more efficiently, while also providing a ductile, replaceable mechanism for seismic energy dissipation.
  7. The correct answer is A (6 in, governed by the fixed code limit rather than the bar diameter calculation): 6 times the diagonal bar diameter equals 6*1.128, which is about 6.77 in, but the fixed 6 in code limit is smaller and therefore governs the maximum spacing of confining transverse reinforcement around each diagonal bar group.
  8. The correct answer is D (Yes, since the aspect ratio of 1.67 and the shear demand both satisfy the trigger conditions): with ln reduced to 60 in, ln/h = 60/36 equals about 1.67, which is less than 2, and Vu of 280 kips still exceeds the calculated shear threshold; since both conditions of ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.7.2 are now satisfied, diagonal reinforcement becomes mandatory.
  9. The correct answer is B (It reduces each pier's individual overturning moment by converting part of it into an axial couple): coupling beam shear accumulated over the building height creates an axial tension-compression couple between the two wall piers, which resists part of the overall overturning moment directly through axial force, reducing the individual flexural moment demand each pier must resist alone.
  10. The correct answer is C (phi = 0.85, a special value permitted for diagonally reinforced coupling beams): ACI 318-14 Section 21.2.4 permits a strength reduction factor of phi = 0.85 for shear in diagonally reinforced concrete coupling beams, a higher value than the standard 0.75 factor, reflecting the more reliable, ductile shear mechanism provided by the diagonal reinforcement.
  11. The correct answer is D (Confining each diagonal bar group individually, or alternatively confining the full beam cross-section): ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.7.4 permits either confining each individual diagonal reinforcement group with its own transverse ties, or alternatively confining the full beam cross-section as a single unit, giving the engineer flexibility in detailing the confinement.
  12. The correct answer is A (No, coupling beams are typically designed without significant axial load effects under normal restraint conditions): coupling beams are typically designed for flexure and shear without significant axial load effects, because the rotational restraint provided by the connected wall piers under normal lateral loading conditions produces negligible net axial force in the beam itself.
Scenario S77

A 4-story reinforced concrete parking structure in Portland, Oregon (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.85g, SD1 = 0.45g, Seismic Design Category D) has a cast-in-place concrete diaphragm at each level, 180 ft long by 60 ft wide, that delivers seismic force to two special reinforced concrete shear walls at the building's east and west ends. The diaphragm is 8 in thick with f'c = 4000 psi. At the roof, the diaphragm design force Fpx per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.1 is 420 kips, applied uniformly along the 180 ft length. A collector element along the centerline gathers force over a 40 ft tributary length at 3.5 kips/ft before amplification.

  1. The correct answer is A (210 kips at each wall, half of the total diaphragm force): treating the diaphragm as a simple span beam under a uniformly distributed load, symmetry places half of the total Fpx of 420 kips at each support, so each end wall receives a maximum shear reaction of 210 kips.
  2. The correct answer is D (3.5 kip/ft, matching the wall reaction divided correctly by the diaphragm depth): dividing the 210 kip wall reaction by the 60 ft diaphragm depth gives 210/60, which equals 3.5 kip/ft, the unit shear that must be transferred along the depth of the diaphragm into the shear wall.
  3. The correct answer is B (vn is approximately 48.6 psi, matching the required shear stress calculated correctly here): vn = Vu/(phi*Acv) = 210,000/(0.75*5760), which equals 210,000/4320, or about 48.6 psi, the required nominal shear stress that the diaphragm slab and its reinforcement must be designed to resist at this location.
  4. The correct answer is C (The demand of 48.6 psi is well below the 126.4 psi limit, so minimum reinforcement is adequate): 2*sqrt(f'c) = 2*63.2, which equals 126.4 psi; since the required demand of 48.6 psi is well below this concrete shear capacity limit, the diaphragm slab can rely on minimum shrinkage and temperature reinforcement without needing additional dedicated shear reinforcement.
  5. The correct answer is D (The chord force is approximately 157.5 kips): M = wL^2/8 = 2.333*180^2/8, which equals 2.333*32,400/8, or about 9448 ft-kips; dividing by the diaphragm depth d of 60 ft gives 9448/60, which equals approximately 157.5 kips as the chord force at the diaphragm edge.
  6. The correct answer is C (140 kips, matching the unit force multiplied correctly by the tributary length): multiplying the given unit collector force of 3.5 kips/ft by the 40 ft tributary length gives 3.5*40, which equals 140 kips, the unamplified force the collector element must gather and deliver into the shear wall.
  7. The correct answer is B (350 kips, matching the unamplified force correctly multiplied by the overstrength factor): per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1, the amplified collector design force equals the unamplified force times Omega_0, so 140 kips times 2.5 equals 350 kips as the required design force for the collector element and its connections.
  8. The correct answer is A (Yes, the amplified collector force requirement applies since the structure is not light-frame construction): the Omega_0-amplified collector force requirement of ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1 applies to structures assigned to Seismic Design Category C through F except light-frame construction; since this concrete parking structure is assigned to SDC D and is not light-frame construction, the requirement applies.
  9. The correct answer is C (Rigid, since cast-in-place concrete diaphragms without large openings can generally be idealized as rigid): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1 permits cast-in-place concrete diaphragms without large openings or irregular geometry to be idealized as rigid absent a specific deflection calculation showing otherwise, which applies to this uniform 8 in thick slab diaphragm.
  10. The correct answer is A (9 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 180 ft dimension): the accidental torsional eccentricity equals 5 percent of the building dimension measured perpendicular to the applied seismic force, so 0.05*180 ft equals 9 ft, which must be applied at each level in the direction being analyzed.
  11. The correct answer is B (They increase, since a reduced effective depth requires higher local chord and collector forces for the same demand): reducing the effective diaphragm depth at the opening location increases the required chord and collector forces for the same applied moment and shear, since force equals moment divided by depth and shear divided by a shorter, effective width or depth.
  12. The correct answer is D (Chords resist bending as tension-compression flange forces at the edges, while collectors gather diaphragm shear): diaphragm chords resist the tension and compression flange forces produced by diaphragm bending along the diaphragm's edges, while collectors gather diaphragm shear force along the diaphragm's length and deliver it into the vertical shear walls or frames.
Scenario S78

A 10-story reinforced concrete condominium tower in Vancouver, Washington (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 1.05g, SD1 = 0.55g, Seismic Design Category D) uses special reinforced concrete shear walls as a building frame system paired with gravity columns that carry no lateral load. A critical wall segment is lw = 22 ft (264 in) long and 18 in thick, with total height hw = 100 ft (1200 in) and a story height of 144 in. Concrete strength f'c = 5000 psi and Grade 60 reinforcement. At the base, the design story drift ratio delta_u/hw = 0.012, and structural analysis gives a neutral axis depth c = 28 in. The maximum compressive stress under factored gravity plus seismic combinations is 0.22*f'c at one wall end.

  1. The correct answer is B (c_limit is about 36.7 in, exceeding c of 28 in, so no boundary element is required): c_limit = lw/(600(delta_u/hw)) = 264/(600*0.012), which equals 264/7.2, or about 36.7 in; since this exceeds the calculated neutral axis depth c of 28 in, the displacement-based method of ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.2 does not require a boundary element.
  2. The correct answer is D (Yes, a special boundary element is required because 0.22*f'c exceeds the 0.2*f'c threshold): the stress-based method of ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.3 requires a special boundary element whenever the calculated maximum compressive stress under factored loads reaches or exceeds 0.2*f'c; since 0.22*f'c exceeds this threshold, a boundary element is required by this method.
  3. The correct answer is A (A special boundary element is required overall because triggering either method's condition is sufficient): ACI 318-14 permits an engineer to use either the displacement-based method of Section 18.10.6.2 or the stress-based method of Section 18.10.6.3, and satisfying either method's trigger condition independently is sufficient to require a special boundary element overall.
  4. The correct answer is C (0.15*f'c, the code-specified discontinuation threshold used once the boundary element is provided): ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.3 specifies that once provided under the stress-based method, the special boundary element may be discontinued at the height where the calculated compressive stress drops to less than 0.15*f'c, a threshold below the 0.2*f'c trigger value.
  5. The correct answer is A (14 in, governed by c/2 since it exceeds the value of c minus 0.1*lw): c - 0.1*lw = 28 - 26.4, which equals 1.6 in, while c/2 = 28/2, which equals 14 in; since ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.4(e) requires the larger of the two values, 14 in governs the required horizontal extension of the boundary element.
  6. The correct answer is B (The minimum is 9 in, well below the actual 18 in thickness, so the wall is acceptable): hu/16 = 144/16, which equals 9 in, the minimum boundary element width required by ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.4(c); since the actual wall thickness of 18 in exceeds this minimum, the wall satisfies the boundary element width requirement.
  7. The correct answer is D (so is 4.67 in, within the required 4 to 6 in range): so = 4 + (14 - hx)/3 = 4 + (14 - 12)/3, which equals 4 + 0.67, or about 4.67 in, a value that falls within the 4 in to 6 in range required by ACI 318-14 Section 18.7.5.3 for the boundary element transverse reinforcement spacing.
  8. The correct answer is C (The special moment frame column confinement equations of Sections 18.7.5.2 through 18.7.5.4): ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.6.4(g) directs that the transverse reinforcement within a special boundary element satisfy the same confinement equations used for special moment frame columns, given in Sections 18.7.5.2 through 18.7.5.4, adapted to the boundary element geometry.
  9. The correct answer is D (They must maintain gravity load capacity while accommodating the imposed seismic story drift, per deformation compatibility): gravity columns that are not part of the seismic force-resisting system must still be designed to maintain their gravity load-carrying capacity while accommodating the imposed lateral seismic story drift, satisfying deformation compatibility requirements even though they resist no lateral force themselves.
  10. The correct answer is B (R = 6, Cd = 5, Omega_0 = 2.5, the building frame system special shear wall value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 6, Cd = 5, and Omega_0 = 2.5 to a building frame system built of special reinforced concrete shear walls, the appropriate classification here since gravity columns, not the shear walls, support the building's gravity loads.
  11. The correct answer is C (No, the flexural design reinforcement typically governs, but it must still meet the 0.0025 general wall minimum): the boundary element's vertical reinforcement is generally sized by the flexural design demand at that section rather than by a separate boundary-element-specific minimum, though it must still satisfy the general 0.0025 minimum reinforcement ratio required throughout the wall.
  12. The correct answer is A (The lap-spliced region must be enclosed by transverse reinforcement meeting the boundary element hoop spacing limits): where lap splices are used within a special boundary element rather than mechanical connections, the lap-spliced region must be enclosed by transverse reinforcement satisfying the same hoop spacing limits required for the boundary element confinement, ensuring adequate splice performance under cyclic loading.
Scenario S79

A 16-story reinforced concrete office tower in Los Angeles, California (Risk Category III, Ie = 1.25, Site Class D, SDS = 1.30g, SD1 = 0.70g, S1 = 1.00g, Seismic Design Category D) uses a dual system of special reinforced concrete moment frames around the perimeter combined with special reinforced concrete shear walls at the building core, with the moment frames designed to independently resist at least 25 percent of the prescribed seismic forces. The building plan is 150 ft by 150 ft with a story height of 144 in. At a given story, the moment frames alone resist a base shear of 320 kips out of a total design base shear of 1100 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (R = 7, Cd = 5.5, Omega_0 = 2.5, the dual system with special moment frame value): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns R = 7, Cd = 5.5, and Omega_0 = 2.5 to a dual system combining special reinforced concrete moment frames capable of independently resisting at least 25 percent of the seismic force with special reinforced concrete shear walls.
  2. The correct answer is D (The ratio is about 29.1 percent, exceeding the 25 percent minimum requirement): dividing the moment frame base shear of 320 kips by the total design base shear of 1100 kips gives 320/1100, which equals about 29.1 percent, exceeding the 25 percent minimum independent capacity required for a dual system under ASCE 7-16 Section 12.2.5.1.
  3. The correct answer is A (rho = 1.0, since the redundancy exception conditions of Section 12.3.4.2 are satisfied): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 permits rho to be taken as 1.0 when removing any single element does not reduce story strength by more than 33 percent and does not create an extreme torsional irregularity, conditions stated as satisfied for this dual system story.
  4. The correct answer is B (Eh equals Qe directly since a redundancy factor of 1.0 applies no amplification to the calculated force): with rho = 1.0, the seismic load combination E = rho*Qe + 0.2*SDS*D reduces so that the horizontal seismic effect Eh equals Qe directly, since multiplying by a redundancy factor of exactly 1.0 applies no amplification to the calculated seismic force.
  5. The correct answer is B (Delta_a is 2.16 in, the Risk Category III allowable drift limit): Delta_a = 0.015*hsx = 0.015*144, which equals 2.16 in, the allowable story drift assigned to Risk Category III structures under ASCE 7-16 Table 12.12-1, a value between the Risk Category I/II limit of 2.88 in and the Risk Category IV limit of 1.44 in.
  6. The correct answer is A (It must be verified at each story, since frames must independently resist 25 percent of story shear): the dual system independent frame capacity requirement of ASCE 7-16 Section 12.2.5.1 is a story-level requirement, meaning the moment frames must independently resist at least 25 percent of the story shear at every level of the building, not only at the base.
  7. The correct answer is C (500 kips, using the dual system Omega_0 of 2.5): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 assigns Omega_0 = 2.5 to this dual system with special moment frames, so 200 kips times 2.5 equals 500 kips as the required amplified collector design force, a lower value than would result from applying the standalone special moment frame Omega_0 of 3.0.
  8. The correct answer is D (No, dual system moment frame components still satisfy the same Section 18.7 and 18.8 special moment frame provisions): the special moment frame components of a dual system are designed exactly as a standalone special moment frame would be, satisfying the same strong column-weak beam provisions of Section 18.7.3 and the same joint shear provisions of Section 18.8 regardless of the presence of shear walls.
  9. The correct answer is A (No, dual system walls still follow the same Section 18.10 special shear wall detailing provisions): the special reinforced concrete shear wall components of a dual system are designed exactly as a standalone special shear wall building would be, satisfying the same shear strength, minimum reinforcement, and boundary element provisions of ACI 318-14 Section 18.10 regardless of the paired moment frame.
  10. The correct answer is C (7.5 ft, equal to 5 percent of the 150 ft plan dimension): the accidental torsional eccentricity equals 5 percent of the building dimension measured perpendicular to the direction of the applied seismic force, so 0.05*150 ft equals 7.5 ft for this square 150 ft by 150 ft building plan.
  11.
