The correct answer is D (Cs is approximately 0.089, governed by the additional minimum for high S1 sites): Cs = 0.5*S1/(R/Ie) = 0.5*1.00/(7/1.25), which equals 0.5/5.6, or approximately 0.089, the additional minimum seismic response coefficient required by ASCE 7-16 for sites with S1 of at least 0.6g, which governs when it exceeds the otherwise calculated Cs value.
  12. The correct answer is B (No, the same P-delta stability coefficient formula applies to any system type, using that system's own Cd value): the P-delta stability coefficient theta formula given in ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.7 applies to any seismic force-resisting system type, with the system's own Cd value substituted into the same equation, so no separate formula is used for a dual system compared to a bearing wall system.
Scenario S80

A 7-story reinforced concrete medical office building in Salt Lake City, Utah (Risk Category II, Site Class D, SDS = 0.95g, SD1 = 0.50g, Seismic Design Category D) uses special reinforced concrete shear walls as a bearing wall system. A wall segment has two piers separated by a door opening: Pier 1 is lw = 8 ft (96 in) long, Pier 2 is lw = 10 ft (120 in) long, both 14 in thick, with a common story height of 132 in and total wall height hw = 924 in. Concrete strength f'c = 4500 psi and Grade 60 reinforcement. The spread footing beneath the wall system carries a factored overturning moment Mu = 9,000 ft-kips and a factored axial dead load N = 1,800 kips, with a footing length of 30 ft and width of 8 ft.

  1. The correct answer is D (Both ratios, about 6.9 and 8.6, exceed 2.5, so neither pier needs the wall pier provisions): lw/bw for Pier 1 equals 96/14, which is about 6.9, and for Pier 2 equals 120/14, which is about 8.6; since both ratios exceed the 6.0 upper bound for the wall pier classification in ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.8, neither pier qualifies as a wall pier and both follow the general special shear wall provisions instead.
  2. The correct answer is A (The ratio is about 9.6, giving alpha_c = 2.0 since it is well above the 2.0 threshold): hw/lw for Pier 1 equals 924/96, which is about 9.6, well above the 2.0 threshold in ACI 318-14 Section 18.10.4.1, so the pier is classified as slender and alpha_c equals 2.0 for the nominal shear strength calculation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Vn is approximately 382 kips, matching the nominal shear equation applied correctly to Pier 1): Vn = Acv(alpha_c*lambda*sqrt(f'c) + rho_t*fy) = 1344*(2.0*67.08 + 0.0025*60000) = 1344*(134.16 + 150), which equals 1344*284.16, or about 381,900 lb, approximately 382 kips for Pier 1.
  4. The correct answer is C (Vn is approximately 477 kips, matching the nominal shear equation applied correctly to Pier 2): using the same coefficients as Pier 1, Vn = 1680*(134.16 + 150) = 1680*284.16, which equals about 477,400 lb, approximately 477 kips for Pier 2, proportionally larger than Pier 1's capacity due to its greater length and larger Acv.
  5. The correct answer is C (Total capacity is about 644 kips, exceeding the 600 kip demand, so the system is adequate): phi*Vn for Pier 1 equals 0.75*382, or about 286.5 kips, and phi*Vn for Pier 2 equals 0.75*477, or about 357.8 kips; summing these gives a total capacity of about 644 kips, which exceeds the total factored demand of 600 kips, so the wall system is adequate.
  6. The correct answer is B (c_limit is about 12.3 in, exceeding c of 10 in, so no boundary element is required here): c_limit = lw/(600(delta_u/hw)) = 96/(600*0.013), which equals 96/7.8, or about 12.3 in; since this exceeds the calculated neutral axis depth c of 10 in for Pier 1, a special boundary element is not required at this pier by the displacement-based method.
  7. The correct answer is D (The ratio is 3.0, calculated from a resisting moment of 27,000 ft-kips): Mr = N*(L/2) = 1800*15, which equals 27,000 ft-kips; dividing by the factored overturning moment Mu of 9,000 ft-kips gives a stability ratio of 27,000/9,000, which equals 3.0, a comfortable margin against overturning for this spread footing.
  8. The correct answer is A (e equals 5 ft, exactly matching the kern limit L/6, placing the footing at the bearing boundary): e = Mu/N = 9,000/1,800, which equals exactly 5 ft, matching the kern limit L/6 = 30/6, which also equals 5 ft; this places the resultant load exactly at the boundary between full bearing contact and partial uplift under the footing.
  9. The correct answer is B (qmax is 15 ksf, matching the trapezoidal formula applied correctly at the kern limit): qmax = (N/BL)(1 + 6e/L) = (1800/(8*30))(1 + 6*5/30) = (7.5)(1 + 1.0), which equals 7.5*2.0, or 15 ksf, the maximum bearing pressure occurring at the near edge of the footing under the eccentric load.
  10. The correct answer is D (qmin equals zero, confirming the footing is exactly at the transition between full bearing and uplift): qmin = (N/BL)(1 - 6e/L) = (7.5)(1 - 1.0), which equals 7.5*0, or zero, confirming that at exactly the kern limit, the bearing pressure at the far edge of the footing drops to zero with no tension developing, right at the transition to partial uplift.
  11. The correct answer is A (The resisting force is about 720 kips, exceeding the 600 kip sliding demand by a modest margin): the sliding resistance equals mu*N = 0.4*1,800, which equals 720 kips; comparing this to the factored sliding demand of 600 kips shows the resisting force exceeds the demand, giving a sliding safety factor of 720/600, or 1.2, a modest but acceptable margin.
  12. The correct answer is C (V is 1100 kips, matching the elastic base shear correctly divided by R for this system): V = Ve/R = 5,500/5, which equals 1,100 kips, the design base shear obtained by reducing the elastic, unreduced base shear by the response modification coefficient R of 5 assigned to this special reinforced concrete bearing wall system.

Practice Test 17 Answer Key

Scenario S81

A one story wood framed retail building in Denver has an exterior shear wall line 32 ft long divided into four 8 ft blocked wood structural panel segments, each 9 ft tall, sheathed with 7/16 in Structural I OSB and 8d common nails at full penetration into 2x framing. Segment 1 uses 6 in on center edge nailing with a tabulated ASD level nominal unit shear capacity of 400 plf. Segment 2 uses 4 in on center edge nailing at 530 plf nominal. Segment 3 uses 3 in on center edge nailing at 610 plf nominal. Segment 4 uses 2 in on center edge nailing at 770 plf nominal. An ASD reduction factor of 2.0 converts each nominal value to an allowable unit shear capacity, and the field nails are spaced 12 in on center in every segment.

  1. The correct answer is B (200 plf, the nominal value divided by the ASD reduction factor of 2.0): the allowable unit shear equals the tabulated nominal value divided by the stated ASD reduction factor of 2.0, so 400 plf divided by 2.0 equals 200 plf. This is the capacity a designer compares directly against an ASD level unit shear demand. The other options either skip the reduction or apply the wrong divisor.
  2. The correct answer is C (305 plf, the nominal value divided by the ASD reduction factor of 2.0): dividing the 610 plf nominal unit shear by the stated ASD reduction factor of 2.0 gives an allowable capacity of 305 plf. This matches the same conversion method used for every segment in this wall line. The remaining choices apply no reduction, an unrelated subtraction, or the reduction in the wrong direction.
  3. The correct answer is A (3,080 lb, from an allowable unit shear of 385 plf over 8 ft): Segment 4 allowable unit shear is 770 plf divided by 2.0, which equals 385 plf, and multiplying by the 8 ft segment length gives 385 times 8 equals 3,080 lb. Option A is the only one built from the correct segment capacity and length. The other totals mix up segments or misapply the reduction factor.
  4. The correct answer is B (9,240 lb, the sum of all four segment capacities at 8 ft each): the allowable unit shears are 200, 265, 305, and 385 plf, and multiplying each by its 8 ft length and summing gives 1,600 plus 2,120 plus 2,440 plus 3,080, which totals 9,240 lb. This sums the actual segment contributions rather than assuming one uniform value. The other totals either use wrong unit shears or an incorrect assumed average.
  5. The correct answer is C (225 plf, from dividing the total demand by the 32 ft wall length): unit shear demand equals the total story shear divided by the sheathed wall length, so 7,200 lb divided by 32 ft equals 225 plf. This demand is then compared segment by segment against each segment's allowable capacity. The other results divide by the wrong length or use the wrong operation entirely.
  6. The correct answer is D (Segment 1, since 225 plf exceeds its 200 plf capacity): Segment 1 has the lowest allowable capacity at 200 plf, and the 225 plf uniform demand exceeds that value while staying below every other segment's capacity. Under a uniform distribution assumption the weakest segment governs the adequacy of the entire line. Segments 2 through 4 all have capacities above the 225 plf demand and are not overstressed.
  7. The correct answer is D (2 in on center, giving 385 plf allowable): only the 2 in on center spacing produces an allowable unit shear of 385 plf, which is the sole value among the four options at or above the 350 plf target. The 3 in spacing falls just short at 305 plf. This shows how tighter edge nail spacing directly increases the tabulated shear capacity of a wood structural panel wall.
  8. The correct answer is A (Compliant, since 1.125 is well below the 3.5 to 1 maximum for blocked panels): SDPWS permits a maximum height to width aspect ratio of 3.5 to 1 for a blocked wood structural panel shear wall segment, and this segment's ratio of 9 ft divided by 8 ft equals 1.125, far below that limit. No shear capacity reduction for aspect ratio applies here. The other statements describe limits that do not match the blocked panel provision.
  9. The correct answer is C (It decreases to 2 to 1 for unblocked construction): SDPWS limits an unblocked wood structural panel shear wall segment to a maximum height to width aspect ratio of 2 to 1, compared with 3.5 to 1 for a blocked segment. Removing blocking reduces the diaphragm action of the panel edges and lowers the permitted slenderness of the segment. This is why designers often block panels intentionally to allow narrower, taller segments.
  10. The correct answer is B (A species specific gravity adjustment factor must reduce the tabulated value): SDPWS Table 4.3A values are tabulated for Douglas fir larch or southern pine framing, and a lower specific gravity species requires a specific gravity adjustment factor applied to reduce the tabulated nominal unit shear. Substituting a lower density species without this adjustment would overstate the wall's true capacity. Increasing panel thickness or nail spacing does not address the framing density difference.
  11. The correct answer is C (Field nailing holds the panel field to framing but the tabulated edge nailing governs shear capacity): the tabulated nominal unit shear values in SDPWS Table 4.3A are governed by the edge nail spacing at panel boundaries, while field nailing at a standard 12 in spacing secures the panel interior to framing and prevents buckling between edges. Field nailing is required but is not the variable that drives the listed shear capacity. Edge nailing spacing is what changes between the four segments in this scenario.
  12. The correct answer is A (Tighter spacing increases the number of nails resisting shear along each panel edge): reducing the edge nail spacing places more fasteners along each panel edge over the same wall length, increasing the number of nails engaged in transferring shear from the panel into the framing. This is why SDPWS Table 4.3A shows higher nominal unit shear values as edge spacing decreases from 6 in down to 2 in. Panel thickness and framing species are separate variables not changed by nail spacing.
Scenario S82

A one story wood framed gymnasium in Sacramento has a rectangular roof diaphragm 100 ft long in the direction of applied wind load and 60 ft deep. The diaphragm resists a total ASD level uniform lateral load of 400 plf along its 100 ft length, delivered to shear walls at each end. Wood ledger members along the 100 ft edges act as diaphragm chords, and one chord splice is located 20 ft from the west end. A collector element along the same 100 ft edge frames into a single interior shear wall segment 10 ft long located 30 ft from the west end.

  1. The correct answer is B (500,000 ft-lb, from wL squared over 8 with w = 400 plf and L = 100 ft): the diaphragm spans as a simple beam between the two end shear walls, so the maximum moment equals w times L squared divided by 8, which is 400 times 100 squared divided by 8, giving 500,000 ft-lb. This moment occurs at midspan, 50 ft from each end. The other values misapply the span length or double count the result.
  2. The correct answer is A (8,333 lb, from dividing the moment by the 60 ft depth): chord force equals the diaphragm moment divided by the diaphragm depth, so 500,000 ft-lb divided by 60 ft equals approximately 8,333 lb. This represents the tension or compression force the chord member and its splices must be designed to resist at the point of maximum moment. The other options misapply the depth or confuse moment with force directly.
  3. The correct answer is A (320,000 ft-lb, from w times x times (L minus x) over 2 with x = 20 ft): the moment at a distance x from a simply supported uniformly loaded span equals w times x times the quantity L minus x, divided by 2, so 400 times 20 times 80 divided by 2 equals 320,000 ft-lb. This is lower than the 500,000 ft-lb midspan moment because 20 ft is off center. Option D uses the midspan distance by mistake rather than the actual 20 ft splice location.
  4. The correct answer is A (5,333 lb, from dividing the 320,000 ft-lb moment by the 60 ft depth): dividing the local moment of 320,000 ft-lb by the 60 ft diaphragm depth gives a splice design force of approximately 5,333 lb, less than the 8,333 lb force at midspan since the splice sits off center. Sizing the splice connection for the actual force at its location, not the peak midspan force, avoids an unnecessarily conservative or unconservative design. The remaining options confuse the local and peak values or misapply the depth.
  5. The correct answer is C (20,000 lb, from w times L over 2 with w = 400 plf and L = 100 ft): for a uniformly loaded simple span diaphragm, each end reaction equals half the total load, and the total load is w times L, so 400 times 100 divided by 2 equals 20,000 lb at each end support. This reaction is the starting shear value used when tracing diaphragm shear along the span toward the interior collector. The other totals misapply the load or reaction formula.
  6. The correct answer is A (8,000 lb, from 20,000 lb minus 400 plf times 30 ft): the diaphragm shear decreases linearly from the end reaction as the uniform load is picked up along the span, so the shear at 30 ft equals the 20,000 lb reaction minus 400 plf times 30 ft, which is 20,000 minus 12,000, equaling 8,000 lb. This remaining shear is the value the collector element must gather at that section. The other results use the wrong distance or the wrong sign.
  7. The correct answer is C (800 plf, from dividing the collected force by the 10 ft length): the average collector drag force equals the total force gathered, 8,000 lb, divided by the 10 ft length of the collecting shear wall segment, giving 800 plf. This is the axial force per foot the collector element and its connections to the wall below must transfer. The other options confuse the collected shear value with an unrelated applied load or misapply the division.
  8. The correct answer is A (A chord resists diaphragm bending as tension and compression at the boundary): a diaphragm chord functions like the flange of a horizontal beam, resisting the tension and compression forces created by diaphragm bending at the boundary parallel to the direction of load. A collector, in contrast, gathers diaphragm shear along a discontinuous shear wall line and delivers it as an axial drag force. These are distinct force paths even though both often use the same physical member.
  9. The correct answer is B (The splice is a discontinuity in the load path and typically has lower capacity): a splice connection introduces a discontinuity in the continuous wood chord member, relying on fasteners, plates, or straps rather than continuous wood fiber, and this connection typically has lower capacity than the surrounding solid member. Because of this, splice locations are frequently the controlling design check for chord adequacy even when the member itself has ample capacity elsewhere along its length.
  10. The correct answer is A (Chord force follows the parabolic moment diagram, peaking at midspan where moment is maximum): chord force equals the diaphragm moment at a given location divided by the diaphragm depth, and for a uniformly loaded simple span the moment diagram is parabolic, reaching its maximum at midspan and reducing to zero at each support. Because depth stays constant, the chord force follows this same parabolic shape, which is why the splice force calculated at 20 ft was lower than the midspan value. Shear, not chord force, is highest at the supports.
  11. The correct answer is B (A collector is needed to gather diaphragm shear over the wall's tributary length into the shorter wall): when a shear wall does not extend the full length of a diaphragm boundary, the diaphragm shear tributary to that discontinuous length must be gathered, or dragged, into the shorter wall segment through a collector element acting in axial tension and compression. The diaphragm chord resists bending moment at the boundary and does not by itself collect shear into an interior wall. This is why the 10 ft wall segment in this scenario needs its own collector force.
  12. The correct answer is B (It carries chord tension or compression from bending plus axial drag force from collecting): a single continuous boundary member commonly serves as both the diaphragm chord, resisting bending induced tension or compression, and as the collector, carrying an additional axial drag force where it gathers shear into a discontinuous wall segment. Both force effects are axial in the member and are combined at the design location where both actions occur. This dual role is why boundary member design in wood diaphragms must consider both chord and collector demands together.
Scenario S83

A three story wood framed apartment building in Portland has a shear wall line with a sheathed length of 10 ft and a story height of 9 ft at every level. The ASD level story shears are 6,000 lb at the roof, 10,000 lb at the third floor, and 14,000 lb at the second floor, each applied at its respective level above the story below. The wall's dead load resisting moment arm is half the 10 ft wall length, and the total ASD level dead load resisting each story's overturning is 2,400 lb per story acting at that same 5 ft arm.

  1. The correct answer is A (54,000 ft-lb, from 6,000 lb times the 9 ft story height): the overturning moment at the base of a story equals the story shear acting above that level multiplied by the story height, so 6,000 lb times 9 ft equals 54,000 ft-lb at the base of the top story. This moment must be resisted by the wall's hold-down and dead load couple at that level. The other values misapply the height or double the correct result.
  2. The correct answer is A (4,200 lb, from dividing the net moment after subtracting the dead load moment by the wall length): the dead load resisting moment is 2,400 lb times 5 ft, or 12,000 ft-lb, and subtracting this from the 54,000 ft-lb overturning moment gives a net moment of 42,000 ft-lb; dividing by the 10 ft wall length gives a hold-down tension of 4,200 lb. This is the net force the hold-down device at the base of the third story must be sized to resist. The other options either ignore the dead load contribution, use it in isolation, or double the correctly calculated result.
  3. The correct answer is C (30,000 lb, summing all three story shears of 6,000, 10,000, and 14,000 lb): the cumulative shear resisted at the base of the lowest story equals the sum of every story shear applied above and at that level, so 6,000 plus 10,000 plus 14,000 equals 30,000 lb. This cumulative value is what the ground floor shear wall segment and its connections must be designed to transfer into the foundation. The other totals omit one or more of the three story shears.
  4. The correct answer is A (468,000 ft-lb, summing 162,000 plus 180,000 plus 126,000 ft-lb): the cumulative overturning moment at the base of the first story sums each story's shear multiplied by its own true height above that base: the roof shear of 6,000 lb acts through 27 ft (three story heights) giving 162,000 ft-lb, the third floor shear of 10,000 lb acts through 18 ft giving 180,000 ft-lb, and the second floor shear of 14,000 lb acts through 9 ft giving 126,000 ft-lb. Summing these three individual contributions, 162,000 + 180,000 + 126,000, gives the correct total of 468,000 ft-lb.
  5. The correct answer is A (Cumulative overturning moment increases at lower stories from stacked shear above): each lower story must resist the overturning moment generated by every story shear acting above it, not just the shear applied directly at that level, so the cumulative moment and resulting hold-down demand grow substantially at lower stories. This is why multi-story wood shear wall hold-down forces typically increase toward the base of the building even when wall length and dead load resistance stay constant at every level. The dead load resisting moment alone cannot offset this growing cumulative demand.
  6. The correct answer is C (A continuous load path, sometimes called a tension tie or rod system): stacking hold-down devices and tension rods from the roof through each floor level down to the foundation, aligned at the same wall location, creates a continuous vertical tension load path often implemented with a continuous rod tie-down system. Without this continuity, tension force calculated at an upper story would have no way to reach the foundation. Collectors and diaphragm chords serve horizontal force transfer functions and are distinct from this vertical tension path.
  7. The correct answer is A (Only dead load directly tributary to the overturning wall segment can resist its own overturning): only the dead load that is structurally tributary to the specific overturning wall segment, delivered through the framing directly above and connected to that wall, can be relied upon to resist that wall's own overturning moment. Using the building's total seismic weight would overstate the resisting moment by including load that has no direct connection to the wall being checked. This is a fundamental limitation on how much resisting moment a designer may claim in a hold-down calculation.
  8. The correct answer is C (Rod diameter must increase to resist the larger cumulative tension demand at lower stories): since the cumulative overturning moment and resulting hold-down tension demand increase toward the base of a multi-story building, the anchor rod or bolt providing that tension capacity must also increase in diameter or grade at lower stories to safely resist the larger force. Selecting a single rod size for the entire building height would either underdesign the lower stories or grossly overdesign the upper stories. This tapering approach is standard practice for continuous tie-down rod systems.
  9. The correct answer is A (The net tension demand decreases because more moment is resisted by dead load): the net hold-down tension demand is calculated by subtracting the dead load resisting moment from the total overturning moment before dividing by the wall length, so increasing the dead load resisting moment, while the overturning moment stays constant, directly reduces the net tension the hold-down device must resist. This is why designers sometimes add strategic dead load, such as heavier floor sheathing near a shear wall, specifically to reduce hold-down demand. The overturning moment itself is a separate calculation unaffected by dead load changes.
  10. The correct answer is B (Overturning creates a tension and compression couple while shear is a separate in-plane force effect): lateral load applied to a shear wall segment produces two distinct effects: an in-plane shear force resisted by the sheathing and its nailing, and an overturning moment that creates a tension and compression couple resisted by the hold-down and the opposite end stud or post. These effects are calculated independently and both must be checked, since a wall could have adequate shear capacity but an undersized hold-down, or the reverse. Neither effect substitutes for the other in a complete design.
  11. The correct answer is A (Load and capacity must be expressed in the same basis, either ASD or LRFD, to compare directly): a meaningful capacity check requires the calculated demand and the published capacity to be expressed on the same basis, either both ASD level or both LRFD level, since manufacturer catalogs often list both an ASD allowable value and a separate LRFD design value for the same hold-down device. Comparing an ASD level demand against an LRFD level capacity without an appropriate factor would produce an incorrect and unconservative conclusion about whether the device is adequate.
  12. The correct answer is A (3,000 plf, from dividing the cumulative shear by the wall length): the required unit shear capacity equals the cumulative shear demand divided by the sheathed wall length, so 30,000 lb divided by 10 ft equals 3,000 plf. This is the minimum allowable unit shear the second story wall segment's sheathing and nailing schedule must be designed to provide. The other values misapply the wall length or the cumulative shear value.
Scenario S84

A one story reinforced CMU masonry warehouse in Phoenix has an exterior wall with window openings that create masonry piers between openings. Each pier is 8 in thick, nominal actual thickness, 32 in wide, and has an unsupported height of 12 ft between the foundation and the roof diaphragm, which provides lateral support at the top. The wall is fully grouted with a radius of gyration for the 8 in nominal CMU wall of 2.4 in, and TMS 402 classifies the effective height factor for a wall pier laterally supported top and bottom as 1.0.

  1. The correct answer is B (12 ft, from applying the stated effective height factor of 1.0): the effective height equals the unsupported height multiplied by the effective height factor, and since this scenario states an effective height factor of 1.0 for a wall pier laterally supported at both top and bottom, the effective height equals 12 ft times 1.0, or 12 ft. This is the value used directly in the slenderness ratio calculation for the pier. The other factors describe different support conditions not stated in this scenario.
  2. The correct answer is B (60, from dividing the 144 in effective height by the 2.4 in radius of gyration): converting the 12 ft effective height to 144 in and dividing by the 2.4 in radius of gyration gives a slenderness ratio of 144 divided by 2.4, which equals 60. This slenderness ratio is the key parameter TMS 402 uses to classify the pier and to determine whether additional slenderness effects must be considered in the design. Option A mistakenly uses only 12 in rather than converting the full 12 ft height.
  3. The correct answer is C (No, since a slenderness ratio of 60 does not exceed the actual 99 threshold for reinforced masonry): TMS 402 requires explicit slenderness effects, such as moment magnification, to be evaluated for a reinforced masonry wall once its slenderness ratio, height divided by radius of gyration, exceeds 99. A ratio of 60 for this pier stays below that threshold, so the additional slenderness procedure is not triggered, though the pier's design must still satisfy the standard strength provisions.
  4. The correct answer is A (Slenderness increases secondary moments from axial load acting through lateral deflection): a slender pier under combined axial compression and lateral out of plane bending develops additional secondary moment as the axial load acts through the lateral deflection of the pier, an effect commonly called the P-delta or moment magnification effect. This secondary moment adds to the primary bending moment from lateral load and must be accounted for once the slenderness ratio exceeds the threshold that triggers this consideration. It does not directly change the applied axial load itself.
  5. The correct answer is A (The slenderness ratio would double to 120): since slenderness ratio is directly proportional to effective height, and effective height equals unsupported height multiplied by the effective height factor, doubling the effective height factor from 1.0 to 2.0 for a cantilevered condition doubles the effective height from 12 ft to 24 ft, which in turn doubles the slenderness ratio from 60 to 120. This illustrates why maintaining a reliable lateral support connection at the diaphragm is structurally important for controlling pier slenderness.
  6. The correct answer is A (Out of plane slenderness governs about the weak axis, which is the thickness direction): out of plane bending of a wall pier occurs about its weak axis, which corresponds to the thin wall thickness direction rather than the wider in-plane pier width direction, so the radius of gyration governing slenderness for this loading is the smaller value associated with the wall's cross sectional thickness. Using the larger width direction radius of gyration would understate the true slenderness and could unconservatively suggest the pier is less slender than it actually is for out of plane bending.
  7. The correct answer is B (Full grouting typically increases the effective cross section, which can improve the radius of gyration compared to partial grouting): fully grouting a masonry wall fills the interior cells with solid grout, engaging a larger and more uniform effective cross section than a partially grouted wall where ungrouted cells contribute little stiffness, and this generally improves the section properties, including radius of gyration, used in a slenderness calculation. A partially grouted wall with the same nominal thickness typically has a smaller effective radius of gyration because less of its cross section is structurally engaged.
  8. The correct answer is A (The slenderness ratios are identical since slenderness depends on thickness direction radius of gyration and height, not pier width): since the out of plane slenderness ratio depends on the effective height and the thickness direction radius of gyration, and both piers share the same thickness, grouting, height, and effective height factor, their slenderness ratios are identical regardless of the difference in pier width. Pier width affects axial load capacity and in-plane behavior but does not enter into the out of plane slenderness ratio calculation for a given wall thickness.
  9. The correct answer is B (The lower limit triggers additional analysis while the higher limit represents a hard ceiling beyond which the wall is not permitted regardless of calculation): TMS 402 sets a lower slenderness ratio threshold, near 30, above which a reinforced masonry wall's design must explicitly account for slenderness effects such as moment magnification, while it also sets a separate, higher absolute maximum slenderness ratio that a wall may not exceed under any circumstance, regardless of how rigorous the analysis is. This two-tier approach recognizes that some slender walls can still be safely designed with additional analysis, while extremely slender walls are simply not permitted.
  10. The correct answer is A (Increasing the pier's effective thickness or reducing its unsupported height to reduce slenderness): since slenderness ratio depends directly on effective height and radius of gyration, an engineer facing an unacceptable moment magnification would typically consider increasing the pier's effective thickness, which improves radius of gyration, or reducing its unsupported height, for example by adding an intermediate lateral support, both of which directly lower the slenderness ratio. Simply increasing reinforcement bar size addresses flexural capacity but does not change the underlying slenderness ratio driving the magnification.
  11. The correct answer is B (Higher axial load combined with slenderness produces a larger secondary moment through the P-delta effect): the secondary, or P-delta, moment in a slender pier results from the axial load acting through the lateral deflection caused by out of plane bending, so a higher level of superimposed axial load, combined with the same lateral deflection, produces a larger secondary moment than a lightly loaded pier of identical slenderness. This is why both slenderness ratio and axial load level must be considered together when assessing whether a slender masonry pier is adequate under combined loading.
  12. The correct answer is A (2.2 in, which is slightly less than the stated 2.4 in value): dividing the 7.625 in actual wall thickness by the square root of 12, approximately 3.46, gives a radius of gyration of about 2.2 in, slightly less than the 2.4 in value stated for this scenario, which reflects a small additional contribution from the wall's grouted and reinforced cross section beyond a simple solid rectangle approximation. Using the raw thickness directly or squaring it instead of taking the square root would produce a value with no physical basis for radius of gyration.
Scenario S85

A reinforced CMU bearing wall in a Kansas City school building has a bond beam lintel spanning a 10 ft wide door opening, with the lintel bearing 8 in onto masonry at each end so the clear span is 10 ft and the design span for flexure is taken as 10.67 ft center to center of bearing. The lintel carries a factored uniform load of 800 plf from tributary wall weight and roof reaction above the opening, and the fully grouted lintel is 8 in wide by 16 in deep with two number 5 bottom bars providing the flexural reinforcement.

  1. The correct answer is A (11,381 ft-lb, from wL squared over 8 with w = 800 plf and L = 10.67 ft): maximum moment for a uniformly loaded simple span beam equals w times L squared divided by 8, so 800 times 10.67 squared divided by 8 equals approximately 11,381 ft-lb. This is the moment the bottom reinforcement of the lintel must be designed to resist at midspan. Using the shorter 10 ft clear span instead of the stated 10.67 ft design span would understate the true design moment.
  2. The correct answer is B (4,268 lb, from half of 800 plf times the 10.67 ft design span): the maximum shear at each support of a uniformly loaded simple span beam equals half the total load, and the total load is 800 plf times 10.67 ft, giving 8,536 lb, so half of that value is approximately 4,268 lb. This shear value is used to check whether the lintel needs shear reinforcement in addition to its flexural bottom bars. The other options misapply the load or the division by two.
  3. The correct answer is B (Adequate bearing length limits the bearing stress transferred into the masonry below the lintel end): a minimum bearing length at each end of a lintel ensures that the reaction force is spread over a sufficient masonry area to keep the resulting bearing stress within the allowable compressive stress of the masonry beneath the lintel end. Too short a bearing length would concentrate the reaction force over too small an area, risking local crushing of the masonry even if the lintel itself has adequate flexural and shear capacity.
  4. The correct answer is B (Design span accounts for the lintel extending onto its bearing at each end, measured center to center of bearing): the design span for flexure in a simply supported beam or lintel is conventionally measured center to center of bearing at each support rather than only across the clear opening, so half the bearing length at each end, four inches in this case, adds to the 10 ft clear span to produce the 10.67 ft design span used in this scenario. This captures the true distance between the effective points of support reaction.
  5. The correct answer is A (It provides horizontal reinforcement that helps distribute out of plane and diaphragm forces along the wall): a continuous bond beam course running through a reinforced masonry wall, including where it forms a lintel over an opening, provides horizontal reinforcement that helps tie the wall together, distribute concentrated loads, and often serves as part of the diaphragm chord or collector load path where the wall meets the roof or floor diaphragm. This dual role means the bond beam lintel's reinforcement must sometimes be checked against more than just the local flexural demand over the opening.
  6. The correct answer is A (Bottom placement maximizes the reinforcement's distance from the compression zone, increasing the internal moment arm): under downward gravity load, a simply supported lintel develops tension in its bottom fiber and compression in its top fiber, so placing the flexural reinforcement as close to the bottom extreme fiber as cover requirements allow maximizes the internal moment arm between the tension steel and the compression zone near the top, which directly increases the section's flexural capacity for a given amount of steel. Placing the bars at middepth would significantly reduce this effective moment arm.
  7. The correct answer is A (Full grouting engages the entire masonry cross section to develop the reinforcement and transfer stresses): fully grouting the lintel cross section ensures that the reinforcing bars are fully embedded and bonded within a continuous solid masonry and grout matrix, allowing the section to properly develop the reinforcement, transfer bond stresses, and resist the combined flexural and shear demands calculated for the lintel. A partially grouted section, with hollow cores away from the reinforcement, would not reliably engage the full cross section assumed in the flexural design.
  8. The correct answer is B (The design moment would increase roughly with the square of the span, more than the span ratio alone): because maximum moment for a uniformly loaded simple span equals w times L squared divided by 8, moment is proportional to the square of the span length rather than to the span directly, so increasing the design span from 10.67 ft to 14 ft, a ratio of about 1.31, increases the moment by roughly 1.31 squared, or about 1.73 times, a larger increase than the span ratio alone would suggest. This nonlinear relationship is why wider openings require disproportionately larger lintel sections or reinforcement.
  9. The correct answer is A (Shear demand at the face of support is the maximum value the section must resist beyond the support region itself): shear demand in a uniformly loaded beam decreases linearly from its maximum value at the support toward zero at midspan, and using the face of support location, rather than crediting some reduction within the bearing region itself, gives the maximum shear the lintel section outside the support must actually resist. This is a conservative and standard convention that avoids relying on any shear reduction benefit within the bearing zone that some codes permit only under specific conditions.
  10. The correct answer is B (Reinforced masonry members must satisfy code minimum reinforcement and detailing rules beyond a basic strength check to ensure ductile, reliable behavior): reinforced masonry members such as this bond beam lintel are governed by TMS 402 provisions that require satisfying a minimum reinforcement ratio, adequate bar development length so the reinforcement can reach its intended stress without slipping, and other detailing rules beyond simply confirming the section has enough flexural strength for the calculated moment. These additional provisions exist to ensure ductile, predictable behavior and reliable transfer of force between the reinforcement and the surrounding masonry, which a bare strength check alone does not guarantee.
  11. The correct answer is A (The design moment and required reinforcement both decrease in direct proportion to the load reduction): since maximum moment for a uniformly loaded simple span beam is directly proportional to the applied uniform load for a fixed span, reducing the load from 800 plf to 500 plf, a ratio of 0.625, reduces the design moment by that same ratio, and the required flexural reinforcement area decreases correspondingly since it is derived directly from the reduced design moment. This straightforward proportionality is a useful check when evaluating how sensitive a lintel's reinforcement demand is to changes in tributary load.
  12. The correct answer is A (Increasing depth improves both flexural capacity, through a larger internal moment arm, and shear capacity, through a larger effective shear area): increasing the depth of a lintel cross section while holding width and reinforcement constant increases the internal moment arm between the tension reinforcement and the compression zone, improving flexural capacity, and it also increases the effective cross sectional area available to resist shear stress, improving shear capacity as well. This is why increasing lintel depth is a common and effective strategy when a wider opening or heavier tributary load produces demands that exceed the capacity of a shallower section.

Practice Test 18 Answer Key

Scenario S86

A one story wood roof diaphragm bears on a reinforced CMU masonry wall in a Salt Lake City distribution center. The roof diaphragm delivers an ASD level shear of 500 plf into the masonry wall through a wood ledger bolted to the wall face with 3/4 in diameter anchor bolts spaced 24 in on center, each bolt having an allowable single shear capacity of 1,100 lb based on the specified embedment and edge distance into fully grouted masonry.

  1. The correct answer is B (1,000 lb, from multiplying the 500 plf unit shear by the 24 in spacing converted to 2 ft): each anchor bolt resists the diaphragm shear tributary to its spacing along the ledger, so converting the 24 in spacing to 2 ft and multiplying by the 500 plf unit shear gives 500 times 2, or 1,000 lb per bolt. This tributary force is what each bolt's allowable capacity must meet or exceed. The other options either skip the unit conversion or apply an unrelated multiplier.
  2. The correct answer is A (Adequate, since the 1,100 lb allowable capacity exceeds the 1,000 lb demand): since the calculated demand of 1,000 lb per bolt is less than the stated allowable single shear capacity of 1,100 lb per bolt, the anchor bolt connection has adequate capacity for the given diaphragm shear with a small margin remaining. No additional factor of safety beyond the allowable capacity value itself is required for this straightforward ASD comparison. The connection passes this specific check as described.
  3. The correct answer is A (1,400 lb demand, which exceeds the 1,100 lb capacity and is inadequate): the new tributary demand per bolt equals the increased 700 plf unit shear multiplied by the 2 ft spacing, giving 700 times 2, or 1,400 lb, which exceeds the 1,100 lb allowable capacity of each bolt. This means the existing bolt size, spacing, or embedment would need to be revised, for example by reducing the spacing or using a larger diameter bolt, to adequately resist the increased diaphragm shear.
  4. The correct answer is B (26.4 in, from dividing the 1,100 lb capacity by the 500 plf shear and converting to inches): the maximum allowable spacing equals the bolt's allowable capacity divided by the unit shear demand, so 1,100 lb divided by 500 plf gives 2.2 ft, which converts to 26.4 in. Any spacing at or below this value keeps the tributary demand per bolt within the bolt's allowable capacity. The other options misapply the division or the unit conversion.
  5. The correct answer is B (Insufficient edge distance can cause the masonry to split or spall before the bolt reaches its rated capacity): an anchor bolt placed too close to a free edge of masonry can cause the masonry to split, crack, or spall under load before the bolt itself reaches its full rated shear or tension capacity, since the surrounding masonry does not have enough material to develop the necessary breakout resistance. This is why manufacturers and codes specify a minimum edge distance, along with minimum embedment, as conditions under which the tabulated allowable capacity for an anchor bolt is valid.
  6. The correct answer is A (Wood has very low bending strength across its grain, making a ledger prone to splitting under out of plane anchorage load applied perpendicular to the wall): when an anchor bolt through a wood ledger is loaded in a direction that bends the ledger across its grain, perpendicular to the wood fibers, the ledger has very low resistance to this type of bending and is prone to splitting along the grain well before reaching the load implied by the bolt's shear capacity alone. This cross grain bending vulnerability is a well recognized weak link in wood ledger to masonry wall anchorage connections and is why supplemental hardware is often required to avoid relying on the wood ledger in this manner.
  7. The correct answer is A (Using steel anchors, straps, or continuity ties that engage the diaphragm directly rather than loading the ledger across its grain): a common solution is to provide steel anchors, straps, or continuity ties that transfer the out of plane wall anchorage force directly into the diaphragm framing or sheathing, bypassing reliance on the wood ledger's weak cross grain bending capacity. Simply increasing anchor bolt diameter within the same ledger detail does not address the underlying cross grain bending vulnerability, since the wood fiber weakness, not the bolt itself, is typically the governing concern.
  8. The correct answer is A (Fully grouted masonry provides a solid, continuous bearing material around the embedded anchor, allowing it to develop its rated capacity): an anchor bolt embedded in fully grouted masonry is surrounded by a solid, continuous material that can develop the bearing and confinement needed for the bolt to reach its rated shear capacity, while an anchor placed in an ungrouted or hollow masonry cell would have little surrounding material to transfer load into, resulting in a much lower actual capacity than the tabulated value. This is why manufacturer and code tabulated anchor capacities in masonry are typically conditioned on full grout encasement of the embedded anchor.
  9. The correct answer is B (In-plane diaphragm shear and out of plane wall anchorage are distinct force effects acting in different directions that must each be checked): in-plane diaphragm shear delivered along the length of the wall acts parallel to the wall surface, while out of plane wall anchorage force acts perpendicular to the wall surface to keep the wall from separating from the diaphragm under lateral load normal to the wall, and these are physically distinct force effects with separate load paths and connection requirements. A connection detail adequate for one effect does not automatically provide adequate capacity for the other, so both must be evaluated independently at the same wall to diaphragm interface.
  10. The correct answer is B (49 bolts, from dividing the 96 ft length by the 2 ft spacing and adding one for the starting bolt): for bolts spaced uniformly along a length with one bolt placed at each end, the number of spaces equals the total length divided by the spacing, which is 96 ft divided by 2 ft, giving 48 spaces, and the number of bolts equals the number of spaces plus one, giving 49 bolts total. This plus one adjustment reflects that a fence post style count requires one more bolt than the number of intervals between them.
  11. The correct answer is A (The required spacing based on bolt shear capacity would be unchanged, since that calculation does not depend on ledger material): the anchor bolt spacing required to satisfy the diaphragm shear demand depends on the diaphragm unit shear and the bolt's own allowable shear capacity, not on the ledger material itself, so replacing a wood ledger with a steel angle or channel would not change the bolt spacing calculation for in-plane shear transfer. The benefit of a steel ledger instead addresses the separate cross grain bending vulnerability relevant to out of plane wall anchorage, a different force effect from the in-plane shear calculation performed in this scenario.
  12. The correct answer is B (Adequate embedment depth is needed for the anchor to develop its full rated capacity without pulling out of the masonry): the tabulated allowable shear and tension capacities for an anchor bolt in masonry are established based on a specific minimum embedment depth, since insufficient embedment would allow the anchor to pull out of the surrounding masonry, or the masonry around a shallow embedment to break out, before the anchor reaches its intended rated capacity. Specifying and verifying adequate embedment depth is therefore a necessary condition for the anchor bolt's published capacity values to actually apply in the completed connection.
Scenario S87

A reinforced CMU masonry shear wall in a Miami condominium is located in a building assigned to Seismic Design Category D. The wall is 8 in nominal thickness, fully grouted, with vertical reinforcement of number 5 bars and horizontal joint reinforcement plus bond beam bars at intermediate courses. TMS 402 requires a maximum vertical and horizontal reinforcement spacing of 48 in for special reinforced masonry shear walls in this seismic design category, and a minimum reinforcement ratio in each direction of 0.0007, with a combined minimum ratio of 0.002.

  1. The correct answer is A (Compliant, since 32 in is less than the 48 in maximum spacing): since the vertical bar spacing of 32 in is less than the stated maximum permitted spacing of 48 in for special reinforced masonry shear walls in this seismic design category, the vertical reinforcement spacing satisfies the maximum spacing requirement given in this scenario. This maximum spacing limit exists to ensure a reasonably distributed pattern of vertical reinforcement across the wall length, and 32 in provides a comfortable margin below the 48 in ceiling.
  2. The correct answer is A (0.116 square inches per foot, from dividing the bar area by the spacing in feet): reinforcement area per foot of wall length equals the bar's cross sectional area divided by its spacing measured in feet, so 0.31 square inches divided by 32 in converted to 2.67 ft gives approximately 0.116 square inches per foot. This value is what gets compared against the minimum reinforcement ratio requirement once converted to a ratio using the wall's gross cross sectional area per foot of length.
  3. The correct answer is A (0.00121, from dividing 0.116 square inches by the 96 square inch gross area): reinforcement ratio equals the reinforcement area per foot divided by the gross cross sectional area per foot, so 0.116 square inches divided by 96 square inches gives approximately 0.00121. This ratio is then compared against the stated minimum individual direction ratio of 0.0007 to confirm the vertical reinforcement alone satisfies the minimum requirement in that direction.
  4. The correct answer is B (Satisfies the minimum, since 0.00121 exceeds the 0.0007 individual direction minimum): since the calculated vertical reinforcement ratio of approximately 0.00121 exceeds the stated minimum individual direction ratio of 0.0007, the vertical reinforcement alone satisfies that specific minimum requirement. This individual direction check is separate from, and in addition to, the combined minimum ratio of 0.002 that considers both vertical and horizontal reinforcement together, which must also be checked before the wall's reinforcement is confirmed adequate.
  5. The correct answer is A (0.00211 combined, which satisfies the 0.002 combined minimum): adding the vertical ratio of approximately 0.00121 to the horizontal ratio of 0.0009 gives a combined ratio of approximately 0.00211, which exceeds the stated combined minimum reinforcement ratio of 0.002 for this wall. Both the individual direction minimums and the combined minimum must be satisfied together, and this wall's reinforcement pattern meets all three checks based on the values given in this scenario.
  6. The correct answer is A (The individual minimums ensure adequate reinforcement in each direction, while the combined minimum ensures sufficient total reinforcement for overall ductile wall behavior): requiring a minimum reinforcement ratio in each individual direction ensures that neither the vertical nor the horizontal reinforcement alone falls below a baseline level needed for crack control and load distribution in that direction, while the additional combined minimum ensures the wall has sufficient total reinforcement across both directions together to support the ductile inelastic behavior expected of a special reinforced masonry shear wall during a design level seismic event.
  7. The correct answer is B (Adequate cover protects reinforcement from corrosion and ensures proper bond development between the bar and surrounding grout): minimum cover requirements for reinforcement in masonry, similar in purpose to cover requirements in reinforced concrete, ensure that the surrounding grout provides adequate protection against corrosion of the steel over the building's service life and ensures there is sufficient grout thickness around the bar for proper bond and stress transfer between the reinforcement and the masonry assembly. Without adequate cover, reinforcement is more vulnerable to environmental exposure and may not fully develop its intended strength.
  8. The correct answer is A (Special reinforced walls require more restrictive detailing to achieve the ductile inelastic response expected under higher seismic demand): buildings assigned to higher seismic design categories are expected to undergo larger inelastic demands during a design level earthquake, and a special reinforced masonry shear wall is designed and detailed with more restrictive reinforcement spacing, minimum ratios, and other requirements specifically to provide the ductile, energy dissipating behavior needed to safely accommodate this larger inelastic demand without brittle failure. An ordinary reinforced masonry shear wall, permitted only in lower seismic design categories with correspondingly lower ductility demand, is not required to meet these same restrictive provisions.
  9. The correct answer is A (Noncompliant, since 54 in exceeds the stated 48 in maximum spacing): increasing the vertical bar spacing from 32 in to 54 in would exceed the stated 48 in maximum spacing requirement for special reinforced masonry shear walls in this seismic design category, making the wall noncompliant with that specific spacing limit even if the resulting reinforcement ratio happened to still satisfy the minimum ratio requirements. Maximum spacing and minimum ratio are separate, independently enforced requirements, and satisfying one does not excuse noncompliance with the other.
  10. The correct answer is A (A maximum spacing limit prevents excessively wide gaps between bars even if the overall average ratio happens to be satisfied by fewer, larger bars): a minimum reinforcement ratio could theoretically be satisfied using a small number of widely spaced, larger diameter bars, which would leave large unreinforced regions of the wall between bars, so a separate maximum spacing limit ensures the reinforcement is reasonably distributed across the wall's length or height regardless of bar size, providing more uniform crack control and load transfer than a ratio requirement could guarantee on its own. Both requirements work together rather than one substituting for the other.
  11. The correct answer is B (The reinforcement ratio would increase, since the larger bar area increases reinforcement area per foot without changing the gross wall area): since reinforcement ratio equals reinforcement area per foot divided by the wall's gross cross sectional area per foot, and substituting a larger number 6 bar, with an area of 0.44 square inches, in place of the number 5 bar, at 0.31 square inches, at the same 32 in spacing increases the reinforcement area per foot while the gross wall area stays unchanged, the resulting reinforcement ratio would increase proportionally with the larger bar area.
  12. The correct answer is A (TMS 402 minimum reinforcement and spacing requirements for masonry walls apply broadly to control cracking and provide minimum ductility regardless of the primary in-plane shear function): the minimum reinforcement ratio and maximum spacing requirements in TMS 402 for a special reinforced masonry shear wall apply to the wall's distributed reinforcement pattern generally, serving to control shrinkage and temperature cracking, provide minimum ductility, and support both in-plane and out of plane demands the wall may experience, rather than applying only to one specific loading direction. A wall designed primarily as an in-plane shear wall must still satisfy these general distributed reinforcement provisions in addition to any specific boundary element or shear reinforcement required for its in-plane demand.
Scenario S88

A one story wood roof diaphragm in a big box retail store in Sacramento spans between concrete tilt-up walls. Out of plane wall anchorage forces must be transferred from the concrete wall into the wood diaphragm using a subdiaphragm because the main roof diaphragm framing does not align directly with the wall anchor locations. A subdiaphragm 60 ft wide, measured parallel to the wall, and 20 ft deep, measured perpendicular to the wall, is used to collect the anchorage force, applying an ASD level out of plane wall anchorage demand of 420 plf along the wall.

  1. The correct answer is A (3 to 1, from dividing the 60 ft span by the 20 ft depth): subdiaphragm aspect ratio is typically expressed as span divided by depth, so dividing the 60 ft span, measured parallel to the wall, by the 20 ft depth, measured perpendicular to the wall, gives a ratio of 3 to 1. This ratio must be compared against the maximum aspect ratio permitted for a wood subdiaphragm under the applicable building code provisions for wall anchorage.
  2. The correct answer is B (Noncompliant, since 3 to 1 exceeds the 2.5 to 1 maximum aspect ratio limit): since the subdiaphragm's calculated span to depth aspect ratio of 3 to 1 exceeds the maximum permitted 2.5 to 1 ratio for a wood subdiaphragm collecting out of plane wall anchorage force, this configuration does not comply and must be revised, typically by reducing the subdiaphragm span, increasing its depth, or reconfiguring the framing to create a smaller aspect ratio subdiaphragm.
  3. The correct answer is A (24 ft, from dividing the 60 ft span by the maximum 2.5 aspect ratio): solving for the depth that produces exactly the maximum permitted aspect ratio involves dividing the 60 ft span by the maximum ratio of 2.5, giving 60 divided by 2.5, or 24 ft. Increasing the subdiaphragm depth from the original 20 ft to at least 24 ft would bring the configuration into compliance with the maximum span to depth aspect ratio limit for a wood subdiaphragm.
  4. The correct answer is B (25,200 lb, from multiplying the 420 plf demand by the 60 ft width parallel to the wall): the total wall anchorage force collected by the subdiaphragm equals the unit anchorage demand multiplied by the length of wall it serves, which is the 60 ft width parallel to the wall, so 420 plf times 60 ft equals 25,200 lb. This total force must then be transferred through the subdiaphragm's chords and into the continuous cross tie system extending into the main diaphragm.
  5. The correct answer is B (A subdiaphragm collects out of plane wall anchorage force locally when the main diaphragm framing does not align with the anchor locations, then delivers it into the main diaphragm system through cross ties): when the primary roof framing members do not directly align with the locations where out of plane wall anchorage forces must be collected, a subdiaphragm uses a smaller, localized diaphragm action within a defined portion of the roof to gather the anchorage force and deliver it into the main diaphragm's continuous cross tie system, which then carries the force across the building to the opposite wall or an internal line of resistance. This avoids requiring every roof framing member to align precisely with every anchor location.
  6. The correct answer is A (Continuous cross ties carry the collected wall anchorage force in tension across the diaphragm to a point of resistance on the opposite side of the building): continuous cross ties act in tension to carry the out of plane wall anchorage force collected by the subdiaphragm across the width or depth of the main diaphragm, ultimately delivering that force to an adequate point of resistance such as the opposite wall or an internal shear wall line, completing the load path that began with the wall pulling away from the diaphragm under out of plane loading. Without this continuity, the anchorage force gathered locally would have nowhere reliable to go.
  7. The correct answer is A (A subdiaphragm's more restrictive limit reflects its role in a critical, brittle failure sensitive load path for wall anchorage, where excessive flexibility is less tolerable): because a subdiaphragm plays a critical role in the out of plane wall anchorage load path, a connection historically associated with brittle failures when inadequately designed, codes impose a more restrictive maximum aspect ratio on subdiaphragms than on a main diaphragm resisting general in-plane lateral load. This tighter limit helps ensure the subdiaphragm behaves with enough stiffness and reliability to properly collect and transfer the anchorage force without excessive local flexibility undermining the connection.
  8. The correct answer is A (36,000 lb, from multiplying the increased 600 plf demand by the 60 ft width): multiplying the increased unit anchorage demand of 600 plf by the unchanged 60 ft subdiaphragm width gives a new total collected force of 600 times 60, or 36,000 lb, an increase proportional to the increase in unit demand since the width used to calculate tributary force did not change. This larger force would then need to be checked against the subdiaphragm chord and cross tie capacities to confirm they remain adequate.
  9. The correct answer is B (A subdiaphragm develops its own internal bending action while collecting anchorage force, requiring boundary chords to resist that local bending just as a main diaphragm does): a subdiaphragm functions as its own localized diaphragm, developing bending moment as it spans between its own boundaries while collecting the out of plane wall anchorage force, and this local bending action requires its own chord elements at the subdiaphragm boundaries to resist the resulting tension and compression forces, similar in principle to how a main diaphragm requires chords at its own boundaries. The main diaphragm's chords, located at the overall building perimeter, do not automatically serve this local subdiaphragm function.
  10. The correct answer is A (Subdiaphragms are a design technique developed specifically to address the critical wall anchorage connection, not a general purpose tool for in-plane force distribution): the subdiaphragm concept was developed specifically to solve the practical problem of collecting out of plane wall anchorage force at locations where the main diaphragm framing does not align with the wall anchors, a connection recognized as historically vulnerable to brittle failure, and it is not generally used as a technique for distributing ordinary in-plane lateral forces elsewhere in a building, which instead rely on the main diaphragm and its chords and collectors as described in earlier scenarios in this book.
  11. The correct answer is A (The required minimum depth would decrease to 16 ft, since 40 ft divided by 2.5 equals 16 ft): since the maximum aspect ratio is defined as span divided by depth, reducing the span from 60 ft to 40 ft while holding the aspect ratio at its maximum permitted value of 2.5 to 1 reduces the corresponding minimum required depth proportionally, giving 40 divided by 2.5, or 16 ft, a smaller minimum depth than the 24 ft required for the original 60 ft span. A shorter subdiaphragm span allows a shallower depth while still satisfying the same aspect ratio limit.
  12. The correct answer is A (Out of plane wall anchorage and in-plane diaphragm shear act in different directions relative to the wall and arise from different aspects of the seismic or wind demand on the wall): out of plane wall anchorage force arises from the tendency of a wall to separate from the diaphragm under lateral acceleration perpendicular to the wall's own plane, while in-plane diaphragm shear arises from lateral force applied parallel to the wall being distributed along the diaphragm to shear walls at each end, and these represent physically distinct load effects governed by separate code provisions and requiring separate load paths, even though both ultimately originate from the same overall wind or seismic event affecting the building.
Scenario S89

A five story mixed use building in Los Angeles has four stories of wood framed residential construction built atop a one story reinforced masonry and concrete podium level used for retail and parking. The podium level structural concrete diaphragm at the top of the podium collects the ASD level lateral shear from all four wood stories above, totaling 60,000 lb, and must transfer this force into the masonry shear walls of the podium level below through a distinct transfer diaphragm condition at the wood to podium interface.

  1. The correct answer is B (A transfer diaphragm redirects lateral force between two dissimilar structural systems with different stiffness and wall layouts above and below): a transfer diaphragm is specifically the diaphragm located at the interface between two dissimilar vertical lateral force resisting systems, in this case flexible wood shear walls above and stiffer masonry shear walls below, often with a different wall layout at each level, and its role is to collect the lateral force from the system above and redistribute it into the different wall configuration of the system below. An ordinary diaphragm within a single material story does not perform this redistribution between two different structural systems.
  2. The correct answer is B (60,000 lb, the full stated combined shear from all four wood stories above): the podium level transfer diaphragm must be designed to collect and redistribute the entire combined lateral shear delivered from all stories above it, which this scenario states directly as 60,000 lb, since the transfer diaphragm sits at the single interface where the accumulated force from the wood framed stories above must pass through into the different wall system below. Dividing or multiplying this stated total would not reflect the actual demand at this critical interface.
  3. The correct answer is A (The podium level, used for retail and parking, typically has fewer and differently located shear walls than the more numerous unit demising walls available in the wood framed residential stories above): a retail and parking podium level typically requires large open spaces with widely spaced columns and few interior walls to accommodate parking aisles and retail floor plans, resulting in shear walls located differently and often more sparsely than the many unit demising and corridor walls available for lateral resistance throughout the wood framed residential stories above. This mismatch in wall layout between the two systems is a primary reason a transfer diaphragm is needed to redistribute the collected force from the upper wall locations into the different lower wall locations.
  4. The correct answer is A (A sharp stiffness discontinuity between flexible wood construction above and stiff masonry construction below can concentrate seismic demand and displacement incompatibility at the transfer level): a large abrupt change in lateral stiffness between the more flexible wood framed stories above and the much stiffer reinforced masonry podium level below can create a soft or weak story type condition and concentrate seismic displacement demand and force effects at the transfer level, a well recognized structural vulnerability in podium type buildings. This is why seismic design provisions for this configuration go beyond simply aligning wall locations and also address the dynamic implications of this vertical stiffness discontinuity.
  5. The correct answer is A (Podium level walls accumulate the lateral demand from every story above them plus their own story's tributary load, making them cumulatively the most heavily loaded elements): similar to the cumulative overturning and shear demand seen in a multi-story shear wall line, the podium level masonry shear walls in this scenario must resist both the lateral force generated by their own story's mass and the accumulated 60,000 lb of transferred force collected from all four wood framed stories above, making the total demand on these walls the sum of every story's contribution above and at the podium level itself, typically the largest cumulative demand anywhere in the building.
  6. The correct answer is B (The connection at the wood to concrete or masonry interface must transfer significant accumulated force between two dissimilar materials with different stiffness and connection detailing needs): the interface between the lowest wood framed shear wall story and the concrete or masonry transfer diaphragm below involves connecting two structurally dissimilar materials with different stiffness, thermal movement, and connection hardware requirements, while also transferring the largest accumulated lateral shear and overturning demand found anywhere in the wood framed portion of the building. This combination of dissimilar materials and peak accumulated demand is why this specific connection interface receives particular design attention beyond what a typical wood to wood connection within the upper stories would require.
  7. The correct answer is A (Overturning moment accumulates with height above the point being checked, so the podium level walls must resist the combined moment contribution of every story above them): just as a multi-story wood shear wall must resist the cumulative overturning moment generated by every story shear acting above the level being checked, the podium level masonry shear walls in this mixed use building must resist the cumulative overturning moment generated by all four wood framed stories above, in addition to any overturning generated within the podium level itself, since overturning moment fundamentally accumulates with height above the point of interest regardless of whether the stories above share the same construction material as the level being checked.
  8. The correct answer is A (The total collected force would increase, since it accumulates from a larger number of stories each contributing lateral shear): since the transfer diaphragm must collect and redistribute the cumulative lateral shear from every wood framed story above it, increasing the number of stories from four to six, while each story continues to contribute a similar level of lateral shear, increases the total accumulated force the transfer diaphragm and its supporting podium shear walls must be designed to resist. This directly illustrates why building height above a transfer level is a critical parameter in podium building lateral design.
  9. The correct answer is A (Stiffness compatibility affects how load actually distributes among the podium walls and interacts with the diaphragm's own flexibility, which a strength check alone does not capture): how the transferred lateral force actually distributes among the individual podium level masonry shear walls depends on the relative stiffness of those walls and the flexibility of the transfer diaphragm itself, since a rigid diaphragm distributes load in proportion to relative wall stiffness while a flexible diaphragm distributes load based on tributary area, so evaluating stiffness compatibility, not just individual wall strength, is necessary to correctly determine how much of the transferred force each podium level wall actually receives.
  10. The correct answer is B (A discontinuous lateral force resisting system between two dissimilar construction types requires a deliberately designed transfer mechanism to maintain a complete and reliable load path): this entire scenario illustrates the broader structural principle that whenever the lateral force resisting system changes configuration or material between stories, such as wood framed shear walls transitioning to masonry shear walls at this podium interface, the load path is not automatically continuous and a deliberately designed transfer mechanism, in this case the podium level transfer diaphragm, is required to maintain a complete and reliable path for lateral force from the roof down to the foundation. Without this deliberate transfer design, the discontinuity in the lateral system would leave an incomplete load path.
  11. The correct answer is A (The transfer diaphragm's chords and collectors must resist forces from its own story plus the entire accumulated demand transferred down from all stories above, unlike an upper story diaphragm that resists only its own story's demand): an ordinary diaphragm within one of the upper wood framed stories only needs to resist the lateral demand generated by that single story, while the podium level transfer diaphragm must resist both its own story's lateral demand and the full accumulated 60,000 lb of transferred force collected from every wood framed story above it, so its chords and collectors are generally sized for a substantially larger combined force than those found at any single upper story. Material type alone, whether concrete, masonry, or wood, does not by itself reduce the actual force the element must resist.
  12. The correct answer is A (Wood diaphragms and shear walls collect and resist upper story lateral force, deliver it into the podium level transfer diaphragm, which redistributes it into the podium masonry shear walls, which in turn transfer the combined force into the foundation): the complete load path in this scenario begins with lateral force generated within each wood framed story, collected by that story's wood diaphragm and delivered into its own wood shear walls, which then deliver the cumulative shear down through each story to the podium level transfer diaphragm, which in turn redistributes the accumulated force into the differently configured masonry shear walls at the podium level, and those masonry shear walls finally transfer the combined cumulative lateral and overturning demand into the building's foundation, completing an unbroken load path from roof to ground.
Scenario S90

A single story retail building in Seismic Design Category D has a wood framed exterior wall line divided into three blocked wood structural panel shear wall segments: Segment A is 12 ft long, Segment B is 5 ft long, and Segment C is 3 ft long, all sheathed with 7/16 in OSB at a uniform 8 ft wall height. The line resists a total ASD level seismic story shear of 8,400 lb. The nominal unit shear capacity of the specified nailing schedule from SDPWS Table 4.3A is 870 plf, and an ASD reduction factor of 2.0 applies to obtain allowable capacity.

  1. The correct answer is D (420 plf, based on dividing the total shear by 20 ft of wall): the unit shear demand equals total shear divided by total sheathed length, so 8,400 lb divided by 20 ft equals 420 plf. This treats the three segments as a single design length for the average demand calculation. The other totals do not match the stated 12+5+3 ft sheathed length of this wall line.
  2. The correct answer is D (435 plf allowable, which exceeds the 420 plf demand and is adequate): allowable capacity equals the nominal 870 plf divided by the ASD reduction factor of 2.0, giving 435 plf. Since 435 plf exceeds the 420 plf average demand, the standard nailed segments are adequate. The other values misapply or omit the stated reduction factor.
  3. The correct answer is C (2.67 to 1, the shear capacity reduction factor must be applied): the height to width ratio equals 8 ft divided by 3 ft, or 2.67. Because this exceeds 2 to 1 but remains within the 3.5 to 1 limit for blocked wood structural panel walls, SDPWS requires the unit shear capacity to be reduced by a factor based on the segment width and height. The other ratios do not correspond to this 8 ft by 3 ft segment.
  4. The correct answer is D (326 plf, after applying a 0.75 reduction factor to the allowable value): the reduction factor equals 2 times 3 ft divided by 8 ft, or 0.75, and this factor multiplies the full unit shear capacity. Starting from the 435 plf allowable capacity, 0.75 times 435 plf gives approximately 326 plf. The other options either skip the required reduction or misapply it.
  5. The correct answer is D (2,217 lb, from an overturning moment of 8,000 ft-lb minus 450 lb): the overturning moment equals the 1,000 lb force times the 8 ft height, giving 8,000 ft-lb, and dividing by the 3 ft width gives 2,667 lb before the dead load credit. Subtracting 0.9 times 500 lb, or 450 lb, gives a net hold down tension of approximately 2,217 lb. The other values omit the width division or the dead load reduction.
  6. The correct answer is C (Segment C only, because its 8 ft to 3 ft ratio exceeds 2 to 1): Segment A has a ratio of 0.67 and Segment B has a ratio of 1.6, both under 2 to 1, while Segment C has a ratio of 2.67. Only segments exceeding 2 to 1 while remaining within 3.5 to 1 require the SDPWS capacity reduction. The remaining options misidentify which segment triggers the reduction.
  7. The correct answer is A (2,175 lb, using the full allowable capacity times the segment length): multiplying the 435 plf allowable unit shear by the 5 ft segment length gives 2,175 lb of total design shear that the sill plate connection must transfer. The other values use the nominal capacity, a fraction of capacity, or omit the length multiplication.
  8. The correct answer is D (725 lb per bolt, dividing the total shear by three bolts): with three anchor bolts equally spaced along the sill plate sharing the load equally, each bolt resists 2,175 lb divided by 3, or approximately 725 lb. The other options assume an incorrect bolt count for this 5 ft segment at 24 in spacing.
  9. The correct answer is A (The 870 plf capacity requires edge nailing at all panel edges, which needs blocking): SDPWS tabulated capacities at this level rely on continuous edge nailing along horizontal panel joints, which can only be achieved with blocking members behind those joints.
