Unblocked construction cannot develop this capacity because the horizontal joint has no framing to nail into. The other options misstate the purpose or applicability of blocking.
  10. The correct answer is D (Edge nailing develops the shear transfer between panels and framing where load is highest): shear flow in a sheathed wall concentrates along panel edges and boundary elements, so closer nail spacing there is needed to transfer the higher unit shear into the framing. Field nails mainly hold the panel flat and resist buckling rather than carry primary shear. The other options mischaracterize the load path through the panel.
  11. The correct answer is C (0.18 in, summing all four listed deflection components): the SDPWS shear wall deflection equation adds bending, shear (nail slip), and anchorage slip contributions together, so 0.02 plus 0.05 plus 0.08 plus 0.03 equals 0.18 in total. The other totals omit one or more required components of the deflection equation.
  12. The correct answer is C (600 lb, equal to the difference the wall cannot pick up directly): the diaphragm delivers 300 plf times 6 ft, or 1,800 lb, over the collection zone, while the 4 ft wall directly resists 300 plf times 4 ft, or 1,200 lb. The collector must carry the remaining 600 lb axially into the wall. The other values represent the full delivered shear or the directly resisted portion rather than the true collector demand.

Practice Test 19 Answer Key

Scenario S91

A single story wood framed warehouse has plan dimensions of 100 ft by 60 ft with a blocked wood structural panel roof diaphragm sheathed with 15/32 in OSB. Masonry shear walls run along both 60 ft edges, spaced 100 ft apart, and resist a total seismic force of 24,000 lb applied uniformly along the 100 ft diaphragm span. The specified nailing schedule provides a nominal unit shear capacity of 640 plf, with an ASD reduction factor of 2.0 to obtain allowable capacity.

  1. The correct answer is C (240 plf, dividing the total force by the 100 ft span): the uniform line load equals the total applied force divided by the diaphragm span in the direction of loading, so 24,000 lb divided by 100 ft equals 240 plf. The diaphragm acts as a simple beam spanning between the two shear wall lines. The other divisors do not match the given 100 ft span.
  2. The correct answer is D (12,000 lb at each end, equal to half the total applied force): for a simply supported diaphragm with a uniform load, each end reaction equals half the total load, so 24,000 lb divided by 2 equals 12,000 lb. This is the maximum shear the diaphragm delivers into each shear wall line. The other values either use the full load or an incorrect fraction of it.
  3. The correct answer is D (200 plf, dividing the reaction by the 60 ft depth): unit shear at the diaphragm boundary equals the maximum shear force divided by the diaphragm depth, so 12,000 lb divided by 60 ft equals 200 plf. This is the demand that must be resisted by the specified boundary nailing. The other divisors do not correspond to the stated 60 ft diaphragm depth.
  4. The correct answer is A (Allowable capacity is 320 plf, which exceeds the 200 plf demand and is adequate): dividing the 640 plf nominal capacity by the ASD reduction factor of 2.0 gives an allowable capacity of 320 plf. Since 320 plf exceeds the 200 plf demand at the diaphragm boundary, the specified nailing schedule is adequate. The other values misapply the reduction factor or produce an inadequate comparison.
  5. The correct answer is D (Unit shear demand is highest near the diaphragm boundary and decreases toward the center): in a simply supported diaphragm under uniform load, shear is maximum at the supports and decreases toward midspan, so the heaviest blocked nailing is needed near the shear walls. Interior zones with lower unit shear demand may use a lighter schedule where permitted. The other options do not reflect the actual shear distribution in the diaphragm.
  6. The correct answer is A (1.67 to 1, well within the 4 to 1 limit for blocked diaphragms): dividing the 100 ft span by the 60 ft depth gives an aspect ratio of 1.67 to 1, which is comfortably within the 4 to 1 maximum permitted for blocked wood structural panel diaphragms. The other ratios do not correspond to these plan dimensions.
  7. The correct answer is B (Yes, because 1.67 is still less than the 3 to 1 unblocked limit): even under the more restrictive unblocked diaphragm aspect ratio limit of 3 to 1, the diaphragm's actual 1.67 to 1 ratio remains compliant. The blocked versus unblocked distinction affects unit shear capacity values more significantly than it affects this particular geometry check. The other options misstate the comparison or the existence of a limit.
  8. The correct answer is C (300,000 ft-lb, using one eighth of the load times span squared): the maximum moment for a simply supported beam under uniform load equals wL squared divided by 8, so 240 plf times 100 ft squared divided by 8 gives 300,000 ft-lb. The other options use an incorrect moment coefficient for this loading and support condition.
  9. The correct answer is D (5,000 lb, dividing the moment by the 60 ft depth): chord force equals the diaphragm bending moment divided by the diaphragm depth, so 300,000 ft-lb divided by 60 ft gives 5,000 lb. This axial force must be developed in the perimeter chord member and its splices at midspan. The other values use an incorrect depth or fail to divide by the depth at all.
  10. The correct answer is C (5,000 lb, matching the full calculated chord force): a chord splice must transfer the entire axial tension or compression force present at its location, so the splice connection must be designed for the full 5,000 lb chord force calculated at midspan. The other values arbitrarily reduce or inflate the required splice capacity without engineering basis.
  11. The correct answer is D (2,000 lb, using half of the opening width): the collector force equals the unit shear demand times the distance over which shear must be dragged into the continuous diaphragm, so 200 plf times 10 ft, or half the 20 ft opening width, gives 2,000 lb. The other values use the full opening width or an unrelated fraction of it.
  12. The correct answer is D (As flexible, permitting distribution by tributary area without a rigidity calculation): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.1 permits wood structural panel diaphragms supported by concrete or masonry shear walls to be idealized as flexible without additional calculation, so load is distributed to the walls based on tributary area rather than relative stiffness. The other options describe classifications that would require additional analysis not needed here.
Scenario S92

A two story masonry office building uses a fully grouted 8 in nominal CMU (7.625 in actual thickness) reinforced shear wall 20 ft long with f'm equal to 2,000 psi. Vertical reinforcement consists of single No. 5 bars (area 0.31 sq in) spaced at 32 in on center, with reinforcement grade fy equal to 60,000 psi. Design uses TMS 402/602-16 provisions: Vm = 4.0 An sqrt(f'm) for this squat wall condition, Vs = 0.5 (Av fy dv)/s, and an effective depth dv = 0.8 times the wall length. The wall resists an ASD equivalent design shear of 95,000 lb, with strength shear reduction factor phi = 0.8.

  1. The correct answer is A (1,830 sq in, using the full wall length and actual thickness): for a fully grouted wall, net area equals the actual thickness times the full wall length, so 7.625 in times 240 in gives 1,830 sq in. The other values use an incorrect thickness or an incorrect fraction of the wall length.
  2. The correct answer is D (about 327,000 lb): substituting An equal to 1,830 sq in and the square root of 2,000 psi, approximately 44.7, into Vm = 4.0 An sqrt(f'm) gives 4.0 times 1,830 times 44.7, or approximately 327,000 lb. The other options represent fractions of this correctly computed value rather than the actual product.
  3. The correct answer is A (192 in, using 0.8 times the wall length): the effective depth equals 0.8 times the 240 in wall length, giving 192 in, which is then used in the steel contribution formula. The other values apply an incorrect multiplier to the wall length.
  4. The correct answer is D (About 55,800 lb, using the formula and input values stated in the scenario): substituting the given values, 0.5 times 0.31 times 60,000 times 192, all divided by 32, gives approximately 55,800 lb. The other options represent half or double this correctly computed value.
  5. The correct answer is B (Vn about 383,000 lb, phi Vn about 306,000 lb): nominal shear strength equals Vm plus Vs, or 327,000 lb plus 55,800 lb, giving approximately 383,000 lb, and multiplying by phi equal to 0.8 gives approximately 306,000 lb of design shear strength. The other options either omit the steel contribution or misapply the reduction factor.
  6. The correct answer is A (Adequate, since design strength substantially exceeds the demand): the calculated design shear strength of approximately 306,000 lb is well above the 95,000 lb design demand, so this wall has ample reserve shear capacity. The other options misstate the comparison or introduce an unnecessary condition.
  7. The correct answer is D (The coefficient decreases from 4.0 toward 1.75 as the pier becomes more slender): TMS 402 reduces the masonry shear contribution coefficient as the M divided by V dv ratio increases from a squat condition toward a more flexural, slender pier condition, reflecting reduced shear friction capacity as flexural cracking dominates behavior. The other options misstate the direction or basis of this reduction.
  8. The correct answer is B (Minimum reinforcement controls shrinkage cracking and provides ductile, distributed behavior): minimum reinforcement ratios help distribute cracking, provide post cracking ductility, and ensure a wall does not behave in a brittle, unreinforced manner even where calculated demand is low. The other options misstate the purpose or scope of this requirement.
  9. The correct answer is A (Fully grouted construction increases An by using the full wall thickness across the entire length): when all cells are grouted, the net cross sectional area equals the full nominal thickness times the wall length, unlike partially grouted walls where only grouted cells and face shells contribute. The other options misstate how grouting affects the net area calculation.
  10. The correct answer is D (Special reinforced walls require closer maximum spacing to improve ductility and confinement): special reinforced masonry shear walls carry more stringent detailing requirements, including tighter reinforcement spacing limits, to achieve the greater ductility expected for use in higher seismic design categories. The other options misstate the comparison between wall classifications.
  11. The correct answer is D (Shear is distributed to each pier in proportion to its relative lateral stiffness): interconnected wall piers along a common line share the total lateral shear in proportion to their relative stiffness, which depends on pier geometry, height to length ratio, and support conditions. The other options describe distribution methods that do not reflect actual structural behavior.
  12. The correct answer is D (Flexural governance allows ductile yielding of reinforcement before any shear failure occurs): designing a wall so flexural yielding of vertical reinforcement precedes any shear failure produces ductile, more predictable behavior and avoids the brittle, sudden failure associated with shear controlled response. The other options misstate the seismic design intent behind this hierarchy.
Scenario S93

A low rise unreinforced clay brick masonry wall, 9.625 in nominal thickness and 15 ft (180 in) long, carries in plane lateral shear with no reinforcement present, per TMS 402/602-16 Chapter 9 provisions for allowable stress design of plain (unreinforced) masonry. The wall resists an actual in plane shear force of 42,000 lb, and the allowable in plane shear stress Fv for this masonry and mortar type is 45 psi. The wall also carries an axial dead load of 80,000 lb and an in plane overturning moment of 120 ft-kips (1,440,000 in-lb) at its base.

  1. The correct answer is C (1,732.5 sq in): net area equals the actual wall thickness of 9.625 in times the 180 in wall length, giving 1,732.5 sq in. The other values apply incorrect thickness or length assumptions to this solid unreinforced wall.
  2. The correct answer is C (24.2 psi): dividing the 42,000 lb shear force by the 1,732.5 sq in net area gives an actual average shear stress of approximately 24.2 psi. The other values do not result from this division with the given force and area.
  3. The correct answer is A (Satisfied, because 24.2 psi is less than the 45 psi allowable stress): since the actual shear stress of approximately 24.2 psi remains below the 45 psi allowable value, this unreinforced masonry wall satisfies the in plane shear check by allowable stress design. The other options misstate the comparison or introduce an irrelevant condition, since unreinforced walls have no reinforcement by definition.
  4. The correct answer is C (51,975 cu in): section modulus equals thickness times length squared divided by six, so 9.625 times 180 squared divided by 6 gives approximately 51,975 cu in. The other values misapply the formula for this rectangular cross section.
  5. The correct answer is C (27.7 psi): dividing the 1,440,000 in-lb moment by the 51,975 cu in section modulus gives a flexural stress of approximately 27.7 psi. The other values do not result from this correct division.
  6. The correct answer is C (46.2 psi): dividing the 80,000 lb axial dead load by the 1,732.5 sq in net area gives an average compressive stress of approximately 46.2 psi. The other values do not result from this correct division with the given loads.
  7. The correct answer is D (No net tension develops, since compression of 46.2 psi exceeds the 27.7 psi tension): because axial compressive stress acts to reduce the effect of flexural tension at the same fiber, and 46.2 psi of compression exceeds 27.7 psi of flexural tension, the net stress remains compressive with no tension developing. The other options misstate the direction or magnitude of the combined stress.
  8. The correct answer is D (Unreinforced masonry lacks ductility and can fail suddenly once cracking capacity is exceeded): plain masonry has essentially no post cracking ductility, so once its tensile or shear capacity is exceeded it can fail abruptly, which is unacceptable for the seismic force resisting system in higher seismic design categories. The other options misstate the applicability or the underlying reason for the restriction.
  9. The correct answer is A (Unreinforced walls have essentially no ductile reserve capacity once cracking initiates): without reinforcement to carry tension and provide post cracking behavior, an unreinforced wall's capacity is governed by the brittle cracking of the masonry and mortar bond, offering little to no ductile reserve. The other options misstate the comparative ductility of unreinforced versus reinforced masonry.
  10. The correct answer is D (Stronger mortar types provide higher bond and shear strength needed to justify the allowable stress): higher strength mortar types such as Type S or Type M develop greater bond and shear resistance between masonry units, which is necessary to support the allowable shear stress values used in design of unreinforced walls. The other options misstate the structural role of mortar type.
  11. The correct answer is D (A low R factor reflects minimal expected inelastic energy dissipation before failure): the response modification factor R is assigned based on a system's expected ductility and energy dissipation capacity, and because plain unreinforced masonry has little ductile reserve, it is assigned a low R factor requiring design for higher elastic level forces. The other options misstate the basis for R factor assignment.
  12. The correct answer is D (Bond beams control shrinkage cracking but do not by themselves change the unreinforced strength classification): horizontal bond beam reinforcement placed primarily for shrinkage and temperature crack control does not automatically convert a wall into a reinforced masonry element for strength design unless it is explicitly designed and detailed as part of the reinforced shear resisting mechanism. The other options misstate this distinction.
Scenario S94

A single story big box retail building has 24 ft tall reinforced CMU bearing walls supporting a flexible wood structural panel roof diaphragm spanning 120 ft between parallel shear wall lines. The site has SDS equal to 1.0 and Risk Category II with Ie equal to 1.0. The tributary out of plane wall weight Wp for anchorage design is 1,200 plf. Steel wall anchors connect the CMU walls directly to the diaphragm framing at 4 ft on center, bypassing the wood ledger in direct tension.

  1. The correct answer is C (480 plf, using 0.4 SDS Ie Wp, using the stated input values): the general wall anchorage force per ASCE 7-16 12.11.1 equals 0.4 times SDS times Ie times Wp, so 0.4 times 1.0 times 1.0 times 1,200 plf gives 480 plf. The other options apply a different coefficient than the general provision requires.
  2. The correct answer is C (960 plf, which governs over the 480 plf general provision): ka = 1 + Lf/100 = 1 + 120/100 = 2.2, which is capped at the maximum value of 2.0. The flexible diaphragm anchorage force then equals 0.4 times ka times SDS times Ie times Wp, so 0.4 times 2.0 times 1.0 times 1.0 times 1,200 plf gives 960 plf, which is larger than and therefore governs over the 480 plf general force. Because ka happens to cap out at 2.0 for this span, the result is the same as if a flat 0.8 coefficient had been used, but that flat coefficient is not itself the general formula and would understate the force on a shorter diaphragm span where ka stays below 2.0.
  3. The correct answer is A (Flexible diaphragms amplify out of plane demand on the wall to diaphragm connection): a flexible wood diaphragm responds dynamically to the heavy concrete or masonry wall's out of plane inertial force differently than a rigid diaphragm, and this interaction has historically produced larger anchorage demands, which the code addresses with the higher 0.8 SDS Ie Wp force. The other options misstate the physical basis or scope of this requirement.
  4. The correct answer is C (2.0, since the uncapped result of 2.2 exceeds the cap): computing 1 plus 120 divided by 100 gives 2.2, but ASCE 7-16 caps this diaphragm amplification factor ka at a maximum value of 2.0, so the applicable value is 2.0. The other options either fail to compute the formula correctly or ignore the required cap.
  5. The correct answer is B (960 plf, which exceeds the 240 plf minimum and governs the diaphragm design): substituting ka equal to 2.0 into 0.4 ka SDS Ie Wp gives 0.4 times 2.0 times 1.0 times 1.0 times 1,200 plf, or 960 plf, which is well above the 240 plf minimum of 0.2 SDS Ie Wp and therefore controls the diaphragm design force at this anchorage. The other values misapply the formula or the comparison to the minimum.
  6. The correct answer is C (3,840 lb per anchor): multiplying the 960 plf governing anchorage force by the 4 ft anchor spacing gives 3,840 lb of required tension capacity per anchor. The other values do not result from this correct multiplication.
  7. The correct answer is B (a ledger loaded in cross grain bending is prone to brittle splitting failure): wood ledgers subjected to cross grain bending or cross grain tension from wall anchorage forces have historically split in a brittle manner, so codes require the anchorage load path to bypass the ledger through direct steel ties or straps connected to the diaphragm framing. The other options misstate the structural reasoning or scope of this requirement.
  8. The correct answer is D (2.5 to 1, using the formula and input values stated in the scenario): SDPWS limits the span to depth aspect ratio of a subdiaphragm used for out of plane wall anchorage force collection to 2.5 to 1, which is more restrictive than the aspect ratio permitted for the main diaphragm, to keep subdiaphragm deflection and force distribution reasonable. The other options misstate this specific subdiaphragm limit.
  9. The correct answer is B (it transfers wall anchorage force across the diaphragm to develop a complete load path): continuity ties extend the wall anchorage force across the diaphragm so it can be resolved by cross ties, subdiaphragms, or the opposite wall line, completing the load path required by ASCE 7-16 for out of plane wall anchorage. The other options misstate the function or applicability of this tie.
  10. The correct answer is B (it lacks a positive, direct, and redundant tension path independent of ledger cross grain capacity): current provisions require a positive direct connection, typically a steel strap or anchor, that develops the out of plane tension force without relying on the ledger's weak cross grain capacity, since ledger only connections have historically failed in this mode. The other options misstate the requirement or its scope.
  11. The correct answer is D (Adequate, since 4,500 lb exceeds the 3,840 lb demand): because the strap's rated allowable tension capacity of 4,500 lb exceeds the calculated 3,840 lb anchor demand, this connection satisfies the out of plane wall anchorage requirement. The other options misstate this direct capacity comparison.
  12. The correct answer is B (Section 12.11.2.1, anchorage of concrete or masonry walls to flexible diaphragms): this specific ASCE 7-16 provision addresses the required out of plane anchorage force for concrete or masonry walls connected to flexible diaphragms, distinct from the general anchorage requirement and from the diaphragm flexibility classification section. The other sections address unrelated aspects of the seismic design procedure.
Scenario S95

A two story wood framed apartment building has a 10 ft wide exterior wood structural panel shear wall segment stacked continuously through both 9 ft tall stories. At the roof diaphragm, this segment resists an applied lateral force of 3,000 lb, and at the level 2 floor diaphragm it resists an additional applied lateral force of 4,500 lb. A dead load reaction of 1,200 lb acts at the tension end of this segment at its base. Nailing options include a lighter schedule with nominal capacity 640 plf and a heavier schedule with nominal capacity 1,640 plf, each using an ASD reduction factor of 2.0.

  1. The correct answer is C (300 plf): the upper story shear equals the 3,000 lb roof force alone, since no additional force has yet accumulated below the roof, and dividing by the 10 ft segment width gives 300 plf. The other values do not result from this correct division.
  2. The correct answer is B (Allowable capacity is 320 plf, which exceeds the 300 plf demand and is adequate): dividing the 640 plf nominal capacity by the ASD reduction factor of 2.0 gives 320 plf, which exceeds the 300 plf upper story demand, so the lighter nailing schedule is adequate for this level. The other options misapply the reduction factor or the comparison.
  3. The correct answer is C (750 plf): the lower story cumulative shear equals the 3,000 lb roof force plus the 4,500 lb floor force, giving 7,500 lb, and dividing by the 10 ft segment width gives 750 plf. The other values do not correctly sum both applied forces before dividing by the segment width.
  4. The correct answer is A (allowable capacity is 820 plf, adequate, because cumulative shear increases toward the base): dividing 1,640 plf by the ASD reduction factor of 2.0 gives 820 plf, which exceeds the 750 plf lower story demand. Lower stories typically require heavier nailing schedules because story shear accumulates from all diaphragm levels above, making the base of the wall the most heavily loaded location. The other options misapply the reduction factor or misstate the reason for the heavier schedule.
  5. The correct answer is A (0.9 to 1, no reduction factor is required): dividing the 9 ft story height by the 10 ft segment width gives an aspect ratio of 0.9 to 1, which is well below the 2 to 1 threshold that would trigger the SDPWS capacity reduction factor for narrow wall segments. The other ratios do not correspond to this 9 ft by 10 ft panel geometry.
  6. The correct answer is C (94,500 ft-lb, summing both force contributions): the overturning moment equals 3,000 lb times 18 ft, or 54,000 ft-lb, plus 4,500 lb times 9 ft, or 40,500 ft-lb, giving a total of 94,500 ft-lb. The other options omit one force's contribution or apply an incorrect height to one of the forces.
  7. The correct answer is B (8,370 lb): dividing the 94,500 ft-lb moment by the 10 ft width gives 9,450 lb, and subtracting 0.9 times the 1,200 lb dead load, or 1,080 lb, gives a net hold down tension of 8,370 lb. The other options either omit the dead load credit or apply it incorrectly.
  8. The correct answer is C (Overturning tension must be resolved and transferred at each level where it develops): in a multi story shear wall, net uplift tension develops at each floor and must be collected and transferred continuously down to the foundation, which is why hold downs or a continuous tie rod system are needed at each level rather than only at the base. The other options misstate how overturning tension develops through a multi story wall.
  9. The correct answer is A (it provides a direct, continuous tension load path from roof to foundation with minimal elongation): a continuous rod system connects tension rods through each floor level with minimal takeup, providing a direct and stiff continuous tension path from the roof level down to the foundation, reducing cumulative elongation compared to stacked individual hold down brackets. The other options misstate its structural function.
  10. The correct answer is B (the lower story governs, because cumulative shear from all levels above is largest there): since story shear accumulates going down the building, the lower story experiences the largest unit shear demand and governs the required nailing schedule for that portion of the wall. The other options misstate which story controls or the underlying reason.
  11. The correct answer is C (Collectors and their connections are often designed for Omega0 amplified forces due to their critical role): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1 requires collector elements and their connections in structures assigned to higher seismic design categories to be designed for seismic forces amplified by the overstrength factor Omega0, recognizing their critical role in the load path. The other options misstate the scope or applicability of this amplification.
  12. The correct answer is A (double sided sheathing can achieve a comparable capacity by combining the shear resistance of both sides): SDPWS permits combining the unit shear capacities of wood structural panels applied to both faces of the same framing, subject to specific provisions, offering an alternative to a single heavier one sided nailing schedule for meeting a high unit shear demand. The other options misstate how double sided sheathing capacity is determined.

Practice Test 20 Answer Key

Scenario S96

A single story school gymnasium measures 150 ft by 90 ft in plan, with masonry bearing walls along both 90 ft edges spaced 150 ft apart and a wood structural panel roof diaphragm spanning between them. The diaphragm resists a total seismic design force of 54,000 lb applied uniformly along the 150 ft span. A 30 ft by 15 ft subdiaphragm near one wall collects out of plane wall anchorage forces at a tributary rate of 500 plf. Nailing schedule options include nominal capacities of 320 plf, 640 plf, and 870 plf, each with an ASD reduction factor of 2.0.

  1. The correct answer is C (360 plf): dividing the 54,000 lb total force by the 150 ft span gives a uniform line load of 360 plf applied along the diaphragm's length. The other values do not result from this correct division.
  2. The correct answer is B (as flexible by default, permitting tributary area distribution without further calculation): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.1 permits a wood structural panel diaphragm supported by masonry or concrete shear walls to be idealized as flexible without additional calculation, so load is distributed to the walls by tributary area. The other options misstate the default classification available for this diaphragm and wall combination.
  3. The correct answer is B (distributed equally by tributary area, since both walls support half the diaphragm span): because the diaphragm is flexible and the walls are symmetric about the diaphragm span, each wall line receives half the total tributary seismic force, independent of the walls' relative stiffness. The other options describe distribution methods that would apply to a rigid diaphragm or that are otherwise inapplicable here.
  4. The correct answer is C (27,000 lb): for a simply supported diaphragm under uniform load, each end reaction equals half the total load, so 360 plf times 150 ft, divided by 2, gives 27,000 lb at each wall line. The other values do not result from this correct calculation.
  5. The correct answer is B (300 plf demand, satisfied by the 640 nominal schedule with 320 plf allowable): dividing the 27,000 lb reaction by the 90 ft depth gives a unit shear demand of 300 plf, and the 640 plf nominal schedule provides an allowable capacity of 320 plf after the ASD reduction factor, which just satisfies the demand while the 320 nominal schedule at 160 plf allowable would not. The other options misstate the demand or select an unnecessarily heavier schedule.
  6. The correct answer is D (1,012,500 ft-lb): maximum moment for a simply supported beam under uniform load equals wL squared divided by 8, so 360 plf times 150 ft squared divided by 8 gives 1,012,500 ft-lb. The other options apply an incorrect moment coefficient.
  7. The correct answer is C (11,250 lb): chord force equals the maximum diaphragm moment divided by the diaphragm depth, so 1,012,500 ft-lb divided by 90 ft gives 11,250 lb. The other values do not result from this correct division.
  8. The correct answer is A (re-entrant corners experience stress concentration and force redistribution requiring reinforcement): an irregular plan with a re-entrant corner creates a discontinuity in the diaphragm that concentrates forces and requires additional collectors or chords to redistribute shear and moment around the geometric irregularity. The other options misstate the structural behavior at this type of plan irregularity.
  9. The correct answer is B (7,500 lb maximum shear, 500 plf unit shear demand): the subdiaphragm's maximum shear equals half the total tributary load, or 500 plf times 30 ft divided by 2, giving 7,500 lb, and dividing by the 15 ft subdiaphragm depth gives a unit shear demand of 500 plf. The other options do not result from this correct calculation.
  10. The correct answer is A (ratio is 2.0 to 1, which complies with the 2.5 to 1 limit): dividing the 30 ft span by the 15 ft depth gives an aspect ratio of 2.0 to 1, which satisfies the more restrictive 2.5 to 1 maximum permitted for subdiaphragms. The other ratios do not correspond to this 30 ft by 15 ft geometry.
  11. The correct answer is A (it transfers collected wall anchorage force across the diaphragm to complete the load path): a continuity tie spanning the full diaphragm width carries the out of plane wall anchorage force collected by the subdiaphragm across to the opposite wall line, completing the required load path for anchorage forces. The other options misstate its structural function.
  12. The correct answer is B (The chord member is checked for axial force while nailing transfers shear into the wall separately): the perimeter framing member carries axial chord tension or compression from diaphragm bending, which must be checked independently from the boundary nailing that transfers diaphragm shear into the supporting wall, since these are distinct force paths acting on the same member. The other options misstate the relationship between these two design demands.
Scenario S97

A three story masonry parking structure has an exterior wall line interrupted by window openings, creating three identical fully grouted 8 in nominal CMU wall piers, each 4 ft wide and 10 ft tall, with f'm equal to 2,000 psi. Each pier contains two No. 4 bars (area 0.20 sq in each) as flexural reinforcement at the pier ends, spaced with shear reinforcement at 16 in on center, fy equal to 60,000 psi. The wall line resists a total shear of 45,000 lb, with shear strength reduction factor phi_v = 0.8 and flexural strength reduction factor phi_b = 0.9.

  1. The correct answer is C (366 sq in): net area equals the actual thickness of 7.625 in times the 48 in pier width, giving 366 sq in. The other values apply an incorrect thickness or width to this fully grouted pier.
  2. The correct answer is D (about 65,500 lb): substituting An equal to 366 sq in and the square root of 2,000 psi, approximately 44.7, into the formula gives 4.0 times 366 times 44.7, or approximately 65,500 lb. The other options represent fractions of this correctly computed value.
  3. The correct answer is B (about 14,400 lb): the effective depth dv equals 0.8 times 48 in, or 38.4 in, and substituting into the formula gives 0.5 times 0.2 times 60,000 times 38.4, divided by 16, or approximately 14,400 lb. The other options represent fractions or multiples of this correctly computed value.
  4. The correct answer is A (Vn about 79,900 lb, phi Vn about 63,900 lb): nominal shear strength equals Vm plus Vs, or 65,500 lb plus 14,400 lb, giving approximately 79,900 lb, and multiplying by phi_v equal to 0.8 gives approximately 63,900 lb of design shear strength per pier. The other options omit the steel term or use incorrect arithmetic.
  5. The correct answer is B (15,000 lb per pier, split equally among all three identical piers): because relative stiffness distribution assigns shear in proportion to each pier's stiffness, and all three piers share identical geometry, boundary conditions, and material properties, they have equal stiffness and therefore share the 45,000 lb total shear equally, giving 15,000 lb per pier. The other options misstate the number of piers sharing load or the distribution basis.
  6. The correct answer is A (adequate, since design strength substantially exceeds the demand): the pier's design shear strength of approximately 63,900 lb is well above the 15,000 lb shear demand assigned to it, so this pier has ample reserve shear capacity. The other options misstate the comparison or introduce an unnecessary precondition.
  7. The correct answer is C (75,000 ft-lb): the pier demand moment equals the 15,000 lb shear times half the 10 ft height, or 15,000 times 5, giving 75,000 ft-lb, consistent with a fixed-fixed pier where inflection occurs at mid-height. The other values do not result from this correct calculation.
  8. The correct answer is A (design flexural strength is about 67.3 ft-kips, which is less than the 75 ft-kip demand): multiplying the 74.8 ft-kip nominal flexural strength by phi_b equal to 0.9 gives approximately 67.3 ft-kips, which falls short of the 75 ft-kip demand moment, indicating this pier is governed by flexure and would need additional vertical reinforcement at its ends. The other options misstate this comparison or its validity.
  9. The correct answer is B (flexure governs, since its design strength does not yet satisfy the demand): because the pier's flexural design strength of approximately 67.3 ft-kips does not meet its 75 ft-kip demand while its shear design strength has ample reserve, flexure controls this pier's design and additional vertical end reinforcement would be needed to increase Mn. The other options misstate which check is unsatisfied.
  10. The correct answer is A (satisfied, since 0.00109 exceeds the 0.0007 minimum): the pier's actual reinforcement ratio of approximately 0.00109 is greater than the stated 0.0007 code minimum for load bearing masonry shear walls, so the minimum reinforcement requirement is satisfied independent of the flexural strength shortfall found earlier. The other options misstate this comparison.
  11. The correct answer is B (the h/t ratio evaluates the pier's resistance to out of plane buckling under slenderness effects): a relatively tall, narrow masonry pier can be susceptible to out of plane buckling under combined axial and lateral loading, so TMS 402 requires checking the height to thickness ratio and associated slenderness effects as a separate limit state from in plane shear and flexural strength. The other options misstate the purpose of this check.
  12. The correct answer is B (a special reinforced system provides greater ductility and detailing needed in higher seismic design categories): ASCE 7-16 Table 12.2-1 requires more stringent detailing and assigns a higher R factor to special reinforced masonry shear walls because of their greater expected ductility, and permits or requires this classification based on the structure's seismic design category and risk category. The other options misstate the basis for this system selection.
Scenario S98

A 4-story steel-framed office building in Salt Lake City, Utah is designed as Risk Category II with a Seismic Design Category of D, SDS = 1.00, and Ie = 1.0. The lateral force-resisting system consists of Special Concentrically Braced Frames (SCBF), R = 6, in the north-south direction and Special Moment Frames (SMF), R = 8, in the east-west direction. The roof diaphragm is a 3 in, 20-gage composite metal deck with 2.5 in normal-weight concrete topping, spanning 100 ft in the direction of analysis with a diaphragm depth of 40 ft. A single SCBF line at the east end resists roof diaphragm force delivered through a 50 ft long collector beam carrying a uniform diaphragm shear of 0.6 kip/ft. A 12 in thick reinforced CMU exterior wall panel, weighing 125 psf, spans vertically between the roof diaphragm and the floor below, with wall anchors spaced at 4 ft on center and a tributary anchorage height of 12 ft. All members use ASTM A992 steel (Fy = 50 ksi, Ry = 1.1) per AISC 341. Design per ASCE 7-16, IBC 2018, and AISC 358.

  1. The correct answer is C (30 kips, calculated using the full 50 ft collector tributary length and the diaphragm shear): the collector element accumulates diaphragm shear force along its full tributary length before delivering it to the SCBF connection. Multiplying the uniform diaphragm shear of 0.6 kip/ft by the full 50 ft collector length gives 30 kips, which is the axial drag force the collector must carry into the brace connection. This unamplified force reflects the diaphragm inertial demand only, before the SCBF overstrength factor is applied in the next step. AISC and ASCE 7-16 both require collectors to be sized for this accumulated tributary shear, not a partial or averaged length.
  2. The correct answer is B (60 kips, applying the specified SCBF overstrength factor of 2.0 to the collector force): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1 requires collector elements and their connections in structures assigned to Seismic Design Category D and higher to be designed for the seismic load effect including overstrength, Emh. Multiplying the calculated 30 kip collector force by the SCBF Omega0 of 2.0 gives a required connection design force of 60 kips. This overstrength requirement protects the brittle collector connection from failing before the ductile SCBF brace yields, consistent with capacity design philosophy under AISC 341.
  3. The correct answer is C (31.25 kips, obtained by dividing the maximum diaphragm moment by the stated diaphragm depth): treating the diaphragm as a simple beam spanning 100 ft under a uniform load of 1.0 kip/ft, the maximum moment equals wL squared divided by 8, which is 1.0 times 100 squared divided by 8, or 1250 kip-ft. Dividing this moment by the diaphragm depth of 40 ft gives a chord tension force of 31.25 kips at the diaphragm boundary. This chord force must be resisted by a continuous chord element, such as a beam or bond beam, spliced adequately to develop the full tension force per ASCE 7-16 diaphragm design provisions.
  4. The correct answer is B (6.0 kips, based on the stated 4 ft anchor spacing and 12 ft tributary height): the tributary seismic weight Wp assigned to each wall anchor is found by multiplying the wall unit weight by the tributary height and the tributary width. Using 125 psf times 12 ft times 4 ft gives 6000 lb, or 6.0 kips, per anchor. This tributary weight becomes the basis for the ASCE 7-16 Equation 12.11-1 anchorage force calculation performed in the next step. Using an incorrect anchor spacing directly changes the tributary weight and therefore the resulting anchorage force.
  5. The correct answer is B (2.4 kips, applying the 0.4 times SDS times Ie times Wp anchorage coefficient): ASCE 7-16 Equation 12.11-1 requires the wall anchorage force Fp to equal 0.4 times SDS times Ie times Wp. Substituting SDS = 1.00, Ie = 1.0, and Wp = 6.0 kips gives Fp equal to 0.4 times 6.0, or 2.4 kips, which exceeds the 0.2 Wp minimum floor value of 1.2 kips so the 0.4 coefficient governs. Each wall anchor connecting the CMU panel to the roof diaphragm must be designed for at least this force. This out-of-plane anchorage requirement is independent of the in-plane diaphragm chord and collector forces calculated for the steel lateral system.
  6. The correct answer is C (Reduced Beam Section connection, which removes material from the beam flange near the column): AISC 358 prequalifies the Reduced Beam Section, or RBS, connection specifically because a portion of each beam flange is trimmed in a curved cutout pattern a short distance from the column face. This deliberate flange reduction is the defining feature that distinguishes RBS from the other listed connection types, which either keep the flange intact or add reinforcing plates instead of removing material. RBS connections are widely used in SMF systems within the size and span-to-depth limits AISC 358 establishes for this detail. The other three connection types achieve inelastic control through different geometric or material means.
  7. The correct answer is B (It relocates the expected plastic hinge location away from the column face into the beam): trimming the beam flange width creates a locally weakened section with reduced plastic moment capacity at a defined distance from the column face. Under seismic loading, this weakened section yields first, forcing the plastic hinge to form within the beam span rather than immediately at the more vulnerable beam-to-column weld region. Moving the hinge away from the connection reduces the moment and strain demand delivered to the column face and its welds during large inelastic seismic excursions. This mechanism is the core seismic performance benefit that AISC 358 credits to the RBS connection.
  8. The correct answer is B (Project two lines at 30 degrees outward from the first row of fasteners or weld): the Whitmore section method establishes the effective gusset plate width for a yielding check by drawing two lines at 30 degrees from the vertical, starting at the first row of bolts or the start of the weld connecting the brace to the gusset. The width where these two lines intersect a section perpendicular to the brace axis defines the Whitmore section width. This approach recognizes that stress spreads out from the concentrated connection point rather than remaining confined to the bolt or weld group width alone. The Whitmore section is then used with the gusset plate thickness and yield stress to check tension or compression yielding capacity.
  9. The correct answer is B (236.5 kips, computed using the expected yield stress with the Ry factor applied): AISC 341 capacity design requires gusset plate and connection elements adjacent to a ductile brace to be sized for the brace expected strength, computed as Ry times Fy times Ag. Substituting Ry = 1.1, Fy = 50 ksi, and Ag = 4.30 in^2 gives 1.1 times 50 times 4.30, or 236.5 kips. This expected strength, rather than the nominal specified yield strength alone, ensures the connection can develop the brace full ductile capacity without prematurely fracturing. Using the specified Fy without Ry, or an incorrect Ry value, understates or overstates the true expected demand the connection must resist.
  10. The correct answer is A (Ties must transfer a minimum specified tie force to maintain a continuous horizontal load path): ASCE 7-16 Section 1.4, General Structural Integrity, establishes minimum tie force requirements intended to give the structure a continuous, redundant load path independent of the specific lateral forces computed for wind or seismic design in other chapters. These minimum ties apply regardless of whether a calculated collector or chord force happens to be small, because their purpose is robustness against disproportionate or progressive collapse rather than resisting a specific calculated lateral demand. The tie force requirement therefore does not depend on a collector force threshold or on the presence of redundant elements elsewhere on the same line. This general integrity provision works alongside, not instead of, the calculated seismic collector and chord design forces.
  11. The correct answer is B (Column splices must provide a minimum specified tie capacity independent of the calculated design loads): ASCE 7-16 Section 1.4 general structural integrity provisions require vertical tie continuity through column splices so that a column retains a minimum tension capacity even where calculated seismic or gravity demands might otherwise be smaller. This minimum tie requirement exists to prevent a local connection failure from propagating into a broader structural collapse, and it applies in addition to the axial and shear forces separately calculated for the SMF and SCBF load combinations. The requirement is not waived simply because a ductile lateral system such as SMF or SCBF is present. Column splices must therefore satisfy both the calculated design forces and this independent minimum integrity tie capacity.
  12. The correct answer is B (Each connection must be designed to develop the expected strength of the adjoining ductile element): AISC 341 capacity design philosophy requires that connections adjoining a ductile yielding element, such as an SCBF brace or an SMF beam plastic hinge region, be designed to remain elastic while the ductile element itself yields and dissipates energy. This means the gusset plate, collector connection, and column splice must each be checked against the expected strength of the adjacent brace, beam, or diaphragm element rather than only the code-level design force. Applying this principle inconsistently, such as only at the diaphragm-to-collector interface, would leave a weak link in the load path that could fail before the intended ductile mechanism engages. Coordinating every connection along the path in this way is what ensures the seismic load path performs as designed from the diaphragm through the foundation.
Scenario S99

A 3-story steel office building assigned to Risk Category II (Ie = 1.0) has SDS = 1.00g and is assigned to Seismic Design Category D. The lateral system consists of special concentrically braced frames (SCBF, R = 6, Cd = 5, Omega0 = 2) at each end of the building, spaced 100 ft apart along a 150 ft plan dimension. The roof diaphragm is rigid, concrete filled metal deck. The total roof diaphragm design force Fpx is 80 kips, split equally between the two braced frame lines, each collecting shear over the 100 ft building width. A steel collector beam runs 50 ft from mid diaphragm to each braced frame, picking up a uniform diaphragm shear of 400 plf. The brace carries an unamplified seismic axial force QE of 150 kips from the ELF analysis. The diaphragm bending moment at midspan is 900 kip-ft over a 100 ft chord depth, and the collector's tributary dead load reaction is 15 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (20 kips, the required axial strength for the collector connection to the frame): The collector force equals the uniform diaphragm shear multiplied by the collector length, so 400 plf times 50 ft gives 20,000 lb, or 20 kips. This is the axial tension or compression demand the collector and its connection to the braced frame must be designed to resist. Because the frame reaction is assumed to occur entirely at the connected end, the full collected force accumulates there rather than being distributed along the member.
  2. The correct answer is C (12 kips, the required axial strength at the collector splice location): Because the collector force builds linearly from zero at the free end to the full 20 kips at the frame connection, the force at any point equals the unit shear times the distance from the free end. At 30 ft from the free end, the force is 400 plf times 30 ft, which equals 12,000 lb, or 12 kips. The splice must therefore be designed for this intermediate axial force, not the full 20 kip end force.
  3. The correct answer is D (9 kips, the required chord force at diaphragm midspan): Diaphragm chord force is computed using the beam analogy, where the diaphragm bending moment is resisted by a tension-compression couple at the two chords separated by the diaphragm depth. Dividing the moment by the depth gives 900 kip-ft divided by 100 ft, which equals 9 kips. This chord force must be developed by continuous reinforcement or a steel chord member running the full diaphragm length.
  4. The correct answer is A (300 kips, the required strength of the brace connection considering seismic overstrength): The special seismic load combinations require the brace connection to be designed using Omega0 times the unamplified horizontal seismic force QE, rather than QE alone. Multiplying 150 kips by Omega0 = 2 gives 300 kips. This capacity-design amplification protects the connection from failing before the brace itself yields or buckles under the actual earthquake demand.
  5. The correct answer is D (5 percent of the weight of the smaller connected portion, per Section 12.1.3): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.1.3 requires that continuity ties be designed for a force not less than 5 percent of the weight of the smaller of the two connected portions. This minimum applies regardless of any smaller computed seismic force, ensuring a baseline level of positive interconnection along the entire load path. The requirement exists to prevent reliance on friction or incidental bearing alone.
  6. The correct answer is A (Omega0 times QE replaces the horizontal seismic force QE in the load combination for capacity design.): The special seismic load combinations replace the ordinary horizontal seismic force effect Eh, which equals rho times QE, with the overstrength seismic force Emh, which equals Omega0 times QE. This substitution is required specifically for elements like brace connections that must remain elastic while the brace itself is allowed to yield or buckle. Cd and R govern deflection and system-level force reduction, not the connection amplification used here.
  7. The correct answer is B (4 kips, of shear transferred from the diaphragm into the collector over that segment): The diaphragm delivers force into the collector at a uniform rate equal to the unit shear, so the force transferred over any given segment equals the unit shear times that segment's length. Multiplying 400 plf by 10 ft gives 4,000 lb, or 4 kips, which is the shear transfer demand across that specific 10 ft length of the shear connection between deck and collector.
  8. The correct answer is C (400 plf, the diaphragm unit shear delivered to each braced frame line): Each braced frame line receives half of the total roof force, so 80 kips divided by 2 equals 40 kips per line. Dividing that force by the 100 ft tributary building width gives 0.4 kips per foot, which equals 400 plf. This unit shear is the value used to compute collector and chord forces along that frame line.
  9. The correct answer is C (The connection must develop the brace's expected strength or the Omega0-amplified force, whichever governs.): AISC 341 capacity design requires connections in the seismic load path to be designed for either the expected strength of the yielding element or the Omega0-amplified seismic force, whichever provides the more reliable, capacity-protected result. This ensures the connection remains elastic while the brace itself is the intended ductile fuse. Designing only for wind or unamplified seismic force would risk a brittle connection failure before the brace can perform as intended.
  10. The correct answer is B (26 kips, the required horizontal seismic design force including the redundancy factor rho): The horizontal seismic force effect Eh equals rho times QE for ordinary (non-overstrength) load combinations, so multiplying the 20 kip collected force by rho = 1.3 gives 26 kips. This amplified force is combined with the applicable gravity and vertical seismic load terms when checking the collector element itself under the basic seismic load combinations rather than the overstrength combinations.
  11. The correct answer is A (The horizontal seismic force is multiplied by rho before combining it with gravity and vertical loads.): Under ASCE 7-16 Section 12.4.2, the horizontal seismic force effect Eh is defined as rho times QE, where rho accounts for structural redundancy in the seismic force-resisting system. This amplified horizontal term is then combined with the gravity loads and the vertical seismic load effect Ev to form the complete seismic load combination used for ordinary element design, distinct from the overstrength combinations used for capacity-protected elements.
  12. The correct answer is D (3 kips, the vertical seismic load effect Ev combined with the collector's dead load reaction): The vertical seismic load effect is computed as Ev = 0.2 SDS D, so multiplying 0.2 by 1.00 and then by the 15 kip tributary dead load gives 3 kips. This vertical term is added to or subtracted from the dead load in the governing load combinations, depending on whether it increases or relieves the net demand on the collector connection.
Scenario S100

A 4-story steel office building (Risk Category II, Ie = 1.0) has SDS = 0.90g and is assigned to Seismic Design Category D. The lateral system is a perimeter special moment frame (SMF, R = 8, Cd = 5.5, Omega0 = 3) using prequalified reduced beam section connections per AISC 358. A typical SMF beam is a W24x76 (Fy = 50 ksi, Zx = 200 in^3, A992 steel with Ry = 1.1), and the connection design uses a probable maximum moment factor Cpr = 1.15. The distance between assumed plastic hinge locations on each side of the column is Lh = 20 ft, the hinge is located 2.5 ft from the column face, and the gravity shear at the hinge location under the seismic load combination is 5 kips. A steel roof diaphragm collector delivers a diaphragm shear of 250 plf over its 40 ft length into an SMF column, and the frame's unamplified column axial force from the ELF analysis is 80 kips.

  1. The correct answer is A (1054 kip-ft, the probable maximum moment Mpr at the plastic hinge): Mpr is computed as Cpr times Ry times Fy times Zx, so multiplying 1.15 by 1.1, then by 50 ksi, then by 200 in^3, gives 12,650 kip-in. Dividing by 12 to convert to kip-ft results in approximately 1,054 kip-ft. This probable maximum moment represents the expected flexural demand at the hinge once the beam has strain-hardened beyond its nominal plastic capacity.
  2. The correct answer is D (105 kips, the shear at the plastic hinge due to the probable moments alone): The moment-induced shear at each hinge equals twice the probable maximum moment divided by the distance between hinges, assuming equal and opposite moments develop at each end. Computing 2 times 1,054 kip-ft divided by 20 ft gives approximately 105 kips. This value represents only the shear generated by the beam's flexural yielding, before adding any gravity load contribution.
  3. The correct answer is C (110 kips, the total design shear at the plastic hinge location): The total shear demand at the hinge combines the shear generated by the probable maximum moments with the gravity shear present under the applicable seismic load combination. Adding the 105 kip moment-induced shear to the 5 kip gravity shear gives a total of 110 kips. This combined shear governs the beam web and connection shear design at the hinge location.
  4. The correct answer is B (1317 kip-ft, the required flexural strength at the face of the column): The moment at the column face exceeds the hinge moment because the hinge shear acts over the distance between the hinge and the column face, adding shear times distance to the hinge moment. Multiplying 105 kips by 2.5 ft gives about 262.5 kip-ft, and adding that to the 1,054 kip-ft hinge moment gives approximately 1,317 kip-ft. This is the flexural demand the column, panel zone, and weld must be designed to resist.
  5. The correct answer is D (10 kips, the required axial strength of the collector delivered into the SMF column): The collector axial force equals the uniform diaphragm shear multiplied by the collector length, so 250 plf times 40 ft gives 10,000 lb, or 10 kips. This axial force must be developed at the connection between the collector beam and the SMF column, in addition to any moment frame beam forces already carried by that connection.
  6. The correct answer is C (Attachments, welds, and discontinuities are restricted within the region expected to undergo inelastic straining.): The protected zone is the region of the beam expected to undergo significant inelastic straining during a design earthquake, typically near the reduced beam section. AISC 341 restricts welds, bolt holes, and other discontinuities within this zone because they can act as stress risers that trigger premature fracture during the large inelastic rotations the connection must sustain.
  7. The correct answer is B (240 kips, the required column axial strength considering seismic overstrength amplification): Capacity-protected elements such as SMF columns are designed using the overstrength seismic load combination, which substitutes Omega0 times QE for the ordinary seismic force term. Multiplying the 80 kip unamplified axial force by Omega0 = 3 gives 240 kips. This amplified axial demand, combined with the applicable gravity loads, governs the column's required compressive and tensile strength.
  8. The correct answer is A (The connection has demonstrated adequate inelastic rotation capacity through prior cyclic qualification testing.): AISC 358 prequalification is based on extensive cyclic testing that demonstrates a connection can achieve the required inelastic rotation without brittle failure, within specified geometric and material limits. Using a prequalified connection allows engineers to design within those limits without conducting project-specific qualification testing, since the connection's ductile performance has already been validated experimentally.
  9. The correct answer is B (20 kips, the minimum required continuity tie force at that connection): The minimum continuity tie force under ASCE 7-16 Section 12.1.3 is 5 percent of the weight of the smaller connected portion. Multiplying 0.05 by the 400 kip weight gives 20 kips, which must be provided at that connection regardless of whether a smaller force is computed from the actual seismic analysis at that location.
  10. The correct answer is D (To prevent excessive shear distortion of the column web before the beam reaches its plastic capacity.): The panel zone shear strength check ensures the column web within the beam-column joint can resist the combined shear generated by the framing beams without excessive distortion. Excessive panel zone distortion can prevent the beam from developing its intended plastic hinge and can compromise the overall stability and strength of the connection under cyclic loading.
  11. The correct answer is C (To reduce the moment demand at the column face below the beam's probable maximum moment.): The reduced beam section intentionally weakens the beam flanges at a location away from the column face, forcing the plastic hinge to form there instead of at the connection itself. This reduces the moment that reaches the column face and the beam flange welds, protecting the connection from the higher moment demand associated with the beam's full probable maximum moment.
  12. The correct answer is A (4 studs, the minimum number of shear studs required along the collector length): The minimum number of studs equals the total required shear force divided by the design strength of a single stud. Dividing the 10 kip collected force by the 2.5 kip capacity of each stud gives exactly 4 studs. This is the minimum number needed to positively transfer the full collector force from the concrete diaphragm into the steel collector beam.

Practice Test 21 Answer Key

Scenario S101

A 6-story reinforced concrete residential building (Risk Category II, Ie = 1.0) has SDS = 1.20g and is assigned to Seismic Design Category D. The lateral system consists of special reinforced concrete shear walls per ACI 318-14 Chapter 18 (R = 5, Cd = 5, Omega0 = 2.5). The post-tensioned concrete roof diaphragm is rigid, measuring 200 ft by 80 ft in plan. A concrete collector strip along one shear wall line picks up a uniform diaphragm shear of 600 plf over a 35 ft length, delivering the force into a shear wall pier. The diaphragm bending moment at midspan is 1,600 kip-ft over an 80 ft chord depth. A coupling beam linking two wall piers carries an unamplified seismic shear QE of 60 kips from the ELF analysis. The exterior concrete wall weighs 100 psf, with a tributary height of 10 ft assigned to each out-of-plane wall anchor, and each anchor has a design strength of 6 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (21 kips, the required axial strength of the collector strip at the shear wall pier): The collector axial force equals the uniform diaphragm shear multiplied by the collector length, so 600 plf times 35 ft gives 21,000 lb, or 21 kips. This is the tension or compression force that the collector reinforcement and its connection into the shear wall pier must be designed to develop across the full collector length.
  2. The correct answer is D (20 kips, the required chord force at diaphragm midspan): Diaphragm chord force is found by dividing the diaphragm bending moment by the chord-to-chord depth, consistent with the beam analogy used for diaphragm design. Dividing 1,600 kip-ft by 80 ft gives exactly 20 kips. This force must be developed by continuous chord reinforcement running along the full length of the diaphragm edge.
  3. The correct answer is C (150 kips, the required design shear strength of the coupling beam connection): Coupling beam connections in special reinforced concrete shear walls are capacity-protected elements designed using the overstrength seismic load combination. Multiplying the 60 kip unamplified shear by Omega0 = 2.5 gives 150 kips, which represents the design shear strength the connection must provide so that it does not fail before the coupling beam yields as intended.
  4. The correct answer is B (480 plf, the out-of-plane wall anchorage design force): The anchorage force is Fp = 0.4 times ka times SDS times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.4 by 1.0, then by 1.20, then by 1.0, then by 1,000 plf gives 480 plf. Checking the minimum of 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp gives only 200 plf, which is less than 480 plf, so the 480 plf value governs the wall anchorage design.
  5. The correct answer is A (12.5 ft, the maximum permitted spacing between out-of-plane wall anchors): The maximum anchor spacing equals the anchor's design strength divided by the anchorage demand per unit length of wall. Dividing the 6,000 lb anchor capacity by the 480 plf demand gives 12.5 ft. Anchors placed no farther apart than this spacing ensure the wall's out-of-plane seismic force is adequately transferred into the diaphragm.
  6. The correct answer is B (When the calculated extreme fiber compressive strain exceeds the code's limiting strain under the design displacement.): ACI 318-14 Section 18.10 requires special boundary elements when the calculated extreme fiber compressive strain, determined using a nonlinear analysis at the design displacement, exceeds the code's specified limiting compressive strain. This displacement-based approach directly targets walls expected to experience significant inelastic curvature demand, rather than relying solely on axial load or reinforcement ratio alone.
  7. The correct answer is C (To keep diaphragm and wall elements positively interconnected so forces transfer along the intended load path.): Continuity ties keep diaphragm and wall elements positively interconnected, so that seismic forces can reliably transfer from the diaphragm into the vertical seismic force-resisting elements without relying on friction, bearing, or incidental contact alone. This positive interconnection is essential to maintaining a complete and dependable seismic load path throughout the structure.
  8. The correct answer is D (15 kips, the minimum required continuity tie force at that connection): The minimum continuity tie force is 5 percent of the weight of the smaller connected portion. Multiplying 0.05 by the 300 kip wall panel weight gives 15 kips, which is the minimum tie force required at this connection regardless of the magnitude of the actual computed seismic demand at that location.
  9. The correct answer is D (9 kips, the collector force at that point along its length): Because the collector force accumulates linearly from zero at the free end to the full collected force at the shear wall connection, the force at any intermediate point equals the unit shear times the distance from the free end. Multiplying 600 plf by 15 ft gives 9,000 lb, or 9 kips, at that specific location along the collector strip.
  10. The correct answer is B (Reinforcing dowels or mechanical connectors sized for the full calculated collector force crossing the joint.): Even within the same material, a construction joint or interface between separately placed concrete elements represents a potential discontinuity in the load path. Reinforcing dowels or mechanical connectors, sized for the full calculated collector or chord force crossing that joint, are needed to ensure the force is reliably transferred rather than relying on bond or friction alone.
  11. The correct answer is A (27 kips, the required design axial strength including the redundancy factor rho): The horizontal seismic force effect under the basic seismic load combinations equals rho times QE, so multiplying the 21 kip collected force by rho = 1.3 gives approximately 27 kips. This amplified force is combined with the applicable gravity and vertical seismic load terms when checking the collector-to-wall connection under the ordinary seismic load combinations.
  12. The correct answer is C (To provide reliable ductile shear behavior and prevent sliding shear failure under repeated load reversals.): Diagonally reinforced coupling beams are used because conventionally reinforced short, deep coupling beams are prone to sliding shear failure and strength degradation under the repeated load reversals of an earthquake. Diagonal reinforcement groups carry the shear more directly through axial tension and compression in the diagonal bars, providing more reliable ductile behavior over many cycles.
Scenario S102

A one-story tilt-up concrete warehouse (Risk Category II, Ie = 1.0) has SDS = 0.60g and is assigned to Seismic Design Category D. The roof diaphragm is a flexible wood structural panel diaphragm per SDPWS 2015, spanning Lf = 120 ft between concrete tilt-up shear wall lines, resulting in a diaphragm amplification factor ka capped at its maximum permitted value. The tilt-up concrete wall weighs 90 psf, with a tributary height of 10 ft assigned to each out-of-plane wall anchor. Each individual wall anchor (steel strap or hold-down) has a design strength of 2,160 lb. A continuous cross tie within a 40 ft deep subdiaphragm collects the anchorage force over a 30 ft length of wall. The diaphragm boundary nailing at the shear wall line is designed for a unit shear of 300 plf.

  1. The correct answer is C (2 the diaphragm amplification factor ka to be used in the anchorage force equation): The amplification factor is computed as ka = 1 plus Lf divided by 100, so 1 plus 120 divided by 100 gives 2.2. However, ASCE 7-16 caps ka at a maximum value of 2.0, so the value actually used in the anchorage force equation for this diaphragm span is 2.0, not the uncapped 2.2 result.
  2. The correct answer is A (432 plf, the out-of-plane wall anchorage design force): The anchorage force is Fp = 0.4 times ka times SDS times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.4 by 2.0, then by 0.60, then by 1.0, then by 900 plf gives 432 plf. Checking the minimum of 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp gives 360 plf, which is less than 432 plf, so the 432 plf value governs this wall anchorage design.
  3. The correct answer is D (5 ft, the maximum permitted spacing between out-of-plane wall anchors): The maximum anchor spacing equals the anchor's design strength divided by the anchorage demand per foot of wall. Dividing 2,160 lb by 432 plf gives exactly 5.0 ft, which is the maximum spacing at which the steel straps or hold-downs can be placed while still transferring the required out-of-plane anchorage force into the roof diaphragm.
  4. The correct answer is B (13 kips, the required continuous cross tie force within the subdiaphragm): The cross tie must collect the total anchorage force generated along the tributary length of wall it serves, so multiplying the 432 plf demand by the 30 ft tributary wall length gives approximately 12,960 lb, or about 13.0 kips. This cross tie distributes the concentrated wall anchorage force into the depth of the main roof diaphragm.
  5. The correct answer is D (Rigid diaphragms, where the diaphragm span does not amplify the anchorage demand.): The ka amplification factor accounts for the increased local acceleration and anchorage demand that can occur with flexible diaphragms of significant span. For rigid diaphragms, this amplification is not appropriate, so ka = 1.0 applies, reflecting that the diaphragm's own flexibility does not add to the out-of-plane wall anchorage demand in that case.
  6. The correct answer is B (To transfer concentrated wall anchorage forces into the main diaphragm using a shorter, stiffer local segment.): A subdiaphragm is a localized diaphragm segment, with its own chords and boundary members, used to collect the concentrated out-of-plane wall anchorage force over a shorter span before delivering it into the main roof diaphragm. This approach avoids requiring the entire main diaphragm to be treated as a single short-span element just to satisfy the wall anchorage load path.
  7. The correct answer is A (Adequate, since the demand does not exceed the boundary nailing's design capacity here.): Since the actual diaphragm unit shear demand of 280 plf is less than the boundary nailing's design capacity of 300 plf, the connection is adequate at that location. The comparison between demand and capacity is a straightforward check, and the boundary nailing capacity does not need to independently exceed the separate out-of-plane wall anchorage force Fp, since that is a different load path.
  8. The correct answer is C (They specifically distribute the concentrated out-of-plane wall anchorage force into the diaphragm, independent of in-plane shear.): Continuous cross ties serve a specifically different function from ordinary chords and collectors: they distribute the concentrated out-of-plane wall anchorage force generated at the wall line into the depth of the diaphragm, independent of the diaphragm's normal in-plane shear and chord forces. Both force systems must be checked and designed for at the same locations.
  9. The correct answer is B (518 plf, the out-of-plane wall anchorage design force with the revised tributary weight): Using the revised tributary weight, Fp = 0.4 times ka times SDS times Ie times Wp becomes 0.4 times 2.0 times 0.60 times 1.0 times 1,080 plf, which equals approximately 518 plf. Since this exceeds the corresponding minimum bound, 518 plf governs the anchorage design force for this larger tributary wall height.
  10. The correct answer is D (Cross-grain bending in wood ledgers has historically caused brittle splitting failures during earthquakes.): Wood has very low strength perpendicular to its grain, and ledgers loaded in cross-grain bending have historically split in a brittle manner during past earthquakes, leading to wall anchorage failures and partial roof or wall collapse. Current code provisions require steel anchors that avoid inducing cross-grain bending or tension in wood ledgers.
  11. The correct answer is C (360 plf, the minimum out-of-plane wall anchorage force, independent of SDS): The lower-bound minimum anchorage force is 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.2 by 2.0, then by 1.0, then by 900 plf gives 360 plf. This minimum value acts as a floor, ensuring the anchorage design force never falls below 360 plf regardless of how small SDS might otherwise make the computed demand.
  12. The correct answer is A (18.4 kips, the required strength of the wall anchor connection at that corner): Combining two perpendicular 13 kip forces using the square root of the sum of the squares gives the square root of 13 squared plus 13 squared, which equals 13 times the square root of 2, or approximately 18.4 kips. This orthogonal combination approach avoids the overly conservative result of simply adding the two forces directly, which would give 26 kips.
Scenario S103

A 2-story reinforced masonry retail building (Risk Category II, Ie = 1.0) has SDS = 1.00g and is assigned to Seismic Design Category D. The lateral system is reinforced masonry shear walls designed per TMS 402/602-16 (R = 5, Cd = 3.5, Omega0 = 2.5). The roof diaphragm is untopped steel deck, classified as flexible per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.1.1, spanning Lf = 100 ft between shear wall lines. The masonry bearing wall weighs 75 psf, with a tributary height of 14 ft assigned to each out-of-plane wall anchor, and each anchor has a design strength of 4,200 lb. A bond-beam collector within a masonry wall pier picks up a uniform diaphragm shear of 500 plf over a 24 ft length. The diaphragm bending moment at midspan is 800 kip-ft over a 60 ft chord depth.

  1. The correct answer is A (2 the diaphragm amplification factor ka for the flexible steel deck diaphragm): The amplification factor is computed as ka = 1 plus Lf divided by 100, so 1 plus 100 divided by 100 gives exactly 2.0. Since this value does not exceed the code's maximum permitted cap of 2.0, the full calculated value of 2.0 is used directly in the out-of-plane wall anchorage force equation for this diaphragm.
  2. The correct answer is C (840 plf, the out-of-plane wall anchorage design force): The anchorage force is Fp = 0.4 times ka times SDS times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.4 by 2.0, then by 1.00, then by 1.0, then by 1,050 plf gives 840 plf. Checking the minimum of 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp gives 420 plf, which is less than 840 plf, so the 840 plf value governs this masonry wall anchorage design.
  3. The correct answer is B (5 ft, the maximum permitted spacing between out-of-plane wall anchors): The maximum anchor spacing equals the anchor's design strength divided by the anchorage demand per foot of wall. Dividing 4,200 lb by 840 plf gives exactly 5.0 ft, which is the maximum spacing at which the steel deck anchors can be placed along the masonry wall while still satisfying the required out-of-plane anchorage demand.
  4. The correct answer is D (12 kips, the required axial strength of the bond-beam collector): The collector axial force equals the uniform diaphragm shear multiplied by the collector length, so 500 plf times 24 ft gives 12,000 lb, or 12 kips. This is the tension or compression demand that the bond-beam reinforcement within the masonry pier must be designed to develop across the full length of the collector.
  5. The correct answer is D (13.3 kips, the required chord force at diaphragm midspan): Diaphragm chord force is found by dividing the diaphragm bending moment by the chord-to-chord depth. Dividing 800 kip-ft by 60 ft gives approximately 13.3 kips, which is the tension or compression force that the chord reinforcement along the diaphragm edge must be designed to develop at midspan.
  6. The correct answer is A (It must be fully developed and spliced continuously across the full collector length.): Collector reinforcement, including bond-beam reinforcement acting as a collector, must be continuous and adequately developed or spliced across the full length over which the calculated axial force occurs. A discontinuity or inadequately developed splice would prevent the calculated tension or compression force from being reliably transferred along the collector.
  7. The correct answer is C (Flexible diaphragms can produce larger local accelerations and wall demands than rigid diaphragms of similar span.): Flexible diaphragms, unlike rigid ones, can amplify local accelerations near the middle of their span relative to what a rigid diaphragm would experience, increasing the out-of-plane demand on connected walls. The ka factor accounts for this dynamic behavior by increasing the computed anchorage force as the diaphragm span increases, up to the code's specified cap.
  8. The correct answer is B (420 plf, the minimum out-of-plane wall anchorage force, independent of SDS): The lower-bound minimum anchorage force is 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.2 by 2.0, then by 1.0, then by 1,050 plf gives 420 plf. This minimum value acts as a floor below which the computed anchorage design force is not permitted to fall, regardless of the calculated SDS-based value.
  9. The correct answer is B (5 kips, the collector force at that point along its length): Since the collector force accumulates linearly from zero at the free end to the full collected force at the wall pier, the force at any intermediate point equals the unit shear times the distance from the free end. Multiplying 500 plf by 10 ft gives 5,000 lb, or 5 kips, at that particular location along the collector.
  10. The correct answer is C (They should engage the diaphragm through a subdiaphragm or continuous tie so the force spreads out.): Wall anchors are required to engage the diaphragm framing directly, or through a subdiaphragm or continuous cross tie, so that the concentrated out-of-plane wall anchorage force is spread into the diaphragm rather than concentrating at a single fastener or a small, isolated area of deck or sheathing.
  11. The correct answer is A (30 kips, the required design strength of the collector connection with seismic overstrength applied): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.10.2.1 generally requires collectors in structures other than light-frame construction to be designed using the overstrength seismic load combination. Multiplying the 12 kip unamplified collector force by Omega0 = 2.5 gives 30 kips, which is the required design strength of the collector-to-wall-pier connection.
  12.
