The correct answer is D (Welded or fastened deck connections sized for the full collector force, anchored into grouted masonry cells.): Because the collector force must cross from the steel deck diaphragm into the masonry wall, welded or mechanically fastened connections between the deck and a ledger or angle, combined with embedded anchor bolts or bond-beam dowels into fully grouted masonry cells, are essential to reliably carry the full calculated collector force across this dissimilar-material interface.
Scenario S104

A 5-story steel office building (Risk Category II, Exposure Category C) uses a basic wind speed V = 140 mph (ultimate design wind speed) per ASCE 7-16 Chapters 26 to 30, with Kzt = 1.0 and Kd = 0.85 for the Main Wind Force Resisting System directional procedure. The lateral system consists of steel ordinary concentrically braced frames (OCBF) at each end of the building, spanning a 120 ft building width. The total wind base shear delivered to the roof diaphragm is 60 kips, split equally between the two braced frame lines. A steel collector beam 40 ft long delivers a uniform diaphragm shear of 250 plf into one braced frame. The diaphragm bending moment at midspan under wind loading is 500 kip-ft over a 100 ft chord depth. The braced frame's diagonal brace carries a factored wind axial force of 45 kips.

  1. The correct answer is A (250 plf, the unit diaphragm shear delivered to each braced frame line, in pounds per foot): Each braced frame line receives half of the total wind base shear, so 60 kips divided by 2 equals 30 kips per line. Dividing that force by the 120 ft tributary building width gives 0.25 kips per foot, which equals 250 plf. This unit shear is used to compute the wind-induced collector and chord forces along that frame line.
  2. The correct answer is B (10 kips, the required axial strength of the collector beam): The collector axial force equals the uniform diaphragm shear multiplied by the collector length, so 250 plf times 40 ft gives 10,000 lb, or 10 kips. This is the wind-induced axial demand the collector and its connection to the braced frame must be designed to resist under the governing wind load combination.
  3. The correct answer is D (5 kips, the required diaphragm chord force at midspan under wind loading): Diaphragm chord force is found by dividing the diaphragm bending moment by the chord-to-chord depth, the same beam analogy used for seismic diaphragm design. Dividing 500 kip-ft by 100 ft gives exactly 5 kips, which the chord reinforcement or steel chord member must be designed to develop under the wind load case.
  4. The correct answer is C (45 kips, the required strength of the brace connection under the governing wind combination): Since Omega0 is a seismic-specific capacity design factor, it is not applied when a wind load combination governs the connection design. The brace connection is therefore designed directly for the factored wind axial force of 45 kips, without any additional ductility-based amplification applied on top of that factored wind force.
  5. The correct answer is D (No, Omega0 is a seismic-specific factor and is not applied to wind load combinations.): Omega0 is a system overstrength factor defined and used exclusively within the seismic provisions of ASCE 7-16 Chapter 12, to provide capacity-protected design for elements in the seismic load path. It has no defined role or applicability within the separate wind load provisions, so wind-governed connections are designed for the actual factored wind force without any such amplification.
  6. The correct answer is C (No, that section is seismic-specific; wind anchorage uses the actual computed wind pressure instead.): Section 12.11.1 is located within the seismic design provisions of ASCE 7-16 and defines a seismic-specific wall anchorage force based on a percentage of wall weight. When wind governs a wall's out-of-plane connection design, the anchorage force is instead derived from the actual computed wind pressure, using the applicable MWFRS or components and cladding procedure, reacting on the wall's tributary area.
  7. The correct answer is A (3.75 kips, the collector force at that point along its length): Since the collector force accumulates linearly from zero at the free end to the full collected force at the braced frame, the force at any intermediate point equals the unit shear times the distance from the free end. Multiplying 250 plf by 15 ft gives 3,750 lb, or 3.75 kips, at that specific location along the collector.
  8. The correct answer is B (The Directional Procedure of Chapter 27, using velocity pressure and pressure coefficients for this building.): The Directional Procedure in ASCE 7-16 Chapter 27 is the general MWFRS procedure applicable to buildings of any height, using velocity pressure computed from Kz, Kzt, Kd, and V, combined with directional external and internal pressure coefficients. The Envelope Procedure is limited to low-rise buildings meeting specific height and shape criteria, which does not apply to this 5-story building.
  9. The correct answer is C (43 psf, the velocity pressure qz at roof level, in pounds per square foot): Squaring 140 mph gives 19,600, and multiplying 0.00256 by 1.0, by 1.0, and by 0.85 gives approximately 0.002176. Multiplying that result by 19,600 gives approximately 42.65 psf, which rounds to approximately 43 psf. This velocity pressure is then combined with the appropriate pressure coefficients to determine the design wind pressure at that location.
  10. The correct answer is A (The factored wind-induced diaphragm shear collected along the member, without ductility-based overstrength amplification.): A wind-governed collector connection is designed for the factored wind-induced diaphragm shear collected along the member length, using the applicable wind load factor from the governing load combination, without any ductility-based overstrength amplification such as Omega0. That amplification is specific to capacity-protected seismic design, not to wind-governed connection design.
  11. The correct answer is B (They apply to seismic design only, since Section 12.1.3 sits within the seismic provisions.): Section 12.1.3 is located within Chapter 12 of ASCE 7-16, the chapter devoted to seismic design requirements, and its continuity tie provisions are specific to the seismic load path. While good engineering practice may still call for continuity of members under wind loading, this particular code requirement is a seismic provision rather than a wind provision.
  12. The correct answer is D (80.5 kips, the total factored wind force applied to that lower story wall panel): The design wind pressure equals the velocity pressure times the combined pressure coefficient sum, so 43 psf times 1.3 gives approximately 55.9 psf. Multiplying that pressure by the 120 ft by 12 ft tributary wall area, or 1,440 square feet, gives approximately 80,500 lb, or approximately 80.5 kips, applied to that wall panel.
Scenario S105

An existing 2-story unreinforced masonry building undergoing a seismic retrofit is assigned SDS = 0.80g and Seismic Design Category D (Risk Category II, Ie = 1.0). The existing flexible wood diaphragm spans Lf = 100 ft between masonry wall lines, and new steel through-bolt anchors are being added per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.11 to correct the original lack of positive wall anchorage. The masonry wall weighs 1,000 plf tributary to each anchor, and each new anchor has a design strength of 3,200 lb. A rooftop mechanical unit weighing 8 kips is rigidly mounted at roof level (z/h = 1.0) with ap = 1.0 and Rp = 2.5 for the component anchorage force calculation per ASCE 7-16 Chapter 13. Separately, a computer structural analysis model reports a collector axial force of 45 kips at a braced frame connection, while an independent hand calculation using tributary diaphragm shear and collector length estimates 40 kips at the same location.

  1. The correct answer is B (2 the diaphragm amplification factor ka for the existing flexible wood diaphragm): The amplification factor is computed as ka = 1 plus Lf divided by 100, so 1 plus 100 divided by 100 gives exactly 2.0. Since this value does not exceed the code's maximum permitted cap of 2.0, the full calculated value of 2.0 applies directly in the retrofit anchorage force equation.
  2. The correct answer is D (640 plf, the required out-of-plane wall anchorage force for the new retrofit anchors): The anchorage force is Fp = 0.4 times ka times SDS times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.4 by 2.0, then by 0.80, then by 1.0, then by 1,000 plf gives 640 plf. Checking the minimum of 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp gives 400 plf, which is less than 640 plf, so the 640 plf value governs this retrofit anchorage design.
  3. The correct answer is A (5 ft, the maximum permitted spacing between the new retrofit wall anchors): The maximum anchor spacing equals the anchor's design strength divided by the anchorage demand per foot of wall. Dividing 3,200 lb by 640 plf gives exactly 5.0 ft, which is the maximum spacing at which the new steel through-bolt anchors can be installed along the URM wall during this retrofit.
  4. The correct answer is C (3.1 kips, the seismic design force Fp for the rooftop mechanical unit): Equation 13.3-1 gives Fp = 0.4 times ap times SDS times Ie times Wp divided by Rp, multiplied by the quantity 1 plus 2 times z over h. Computing 0.4 times 1.0 times 0.80 times 1.0 times 8 kips, dividing by 2.5, and multiplying by 3 (since 1 plus 2 times 1.0 equals 3) gives approximately 3.07 kips, or approximately 3.1 kips.
  5. The correct answer is A (It governs directly, since it falls between the minimum and maximum bounds established for it.): ASCE 7-16 Section 13.3.1 establishes minimum and maximum bounds on the computed component force Fp. Since the computed value of approximately 3.07 kips falls between the 1.92 kip minimum and the 10.24 kip maximum, the computed value governs directly and does not need to be adjusted up to the maximum or down to the minimum bound.
  6. The correct answer is D (The results are reasonably consistent, about 12 percent apart, so the computer output is likely credible.): A difference of about 12 percent between an independent hand calculation and a computer model output is typically within the range expected from simplifying assumptions in a hand calculation, such as ignoring minor stiffness effects. This level of agreement suggests the computer output is likely credible, though the engineer should still understand the source of the remaining difference before finalizing the design.
  7. The correct answer is B (12.5 percent, the percent difference between the computer model and hand calculation results): The percent difference is computed as the difference between the two values divided by the reference value. Subtracting 40 kips from 45 kips gives 5 kips, and dividing 5 kips by the 40 kip hand calculation result gives 0.125, or 12.5 percent, which is the percent difference between the two results.
  8. The correct answer is C (Friction and masonry bond have proven unreliable during earthquakes, requiring a ductile mechanical anchor instead.): Numerous past earthquakes have demonstrated that unreinforced masonry walls relying only on friction or masonry bond for their connection to a diaphragm are prone to sudden, brittle out-of-plane failure and collapse. Current code philosophy therefore requires positive, ductile mechanical anchors specifically designed and detailed to resist the calculated out-of-plane anchorage force.
  9. The correct answer is D (400 plf, the minimum out-of-plane wall anchorage force, independent of SDS): The lower-bound minimum anchorage force is 0.2 times ka times Ie times Wp, so multiplying 0.2 by 2.0, then by 1.0, then by 1,000 plf gives 400 plf. This minimum value establishes a floor for the required retrofit anchorage force regardless of how small the SDS-based computed value might otherwise be.
  10. The correct answer is B (The component amplification factor, reflecting the component's dynamic response and flexibility.): The factor ap represents the component amplification factor, which reflects how the flexibility and dynamic response characteristics of the component itself, relative to the supporting structure's motion, can amplify the seismic force the component experiences. A more flexible or resonant component is assigned a higher ap value than a rigid one.
  11. The correct answer is C (ASCE 7-16 Chapter 15, often combined with Chapter 13 for the attachment connection force.): ASCE 7-16 Chapter 15 provides the seismic design requirements specific to nonbuilding structures, including tanks supported on or within another structure. The attachment or anchorage force connecting that nonbuilding structure to the supporting building structure is often determined using the component force provisions of Chapter 13, applied in conjunction with Chapter 15.
  12. The correct answer is A (No, Fp under Section 12.11.1 is a self-contained component force not subject to rho.): The redundancy factor rho applies to the horizontal seismic force effect used for the building's overall seismic force-resisting system under the basic seismic load combinations. The out-of-plane wall anchorage force Fp defined in Section 12.11.1 is a separate, self-contained, prescribed component-level force with its own factors, including ka, and is not additionally multiplied by rho.

Practice Test 22 Answer Key

Scenario S106

A steel-framed one-story building in Exposure C uses an isolated square spread footing beneath a moment-frame column base plate. The footing plan dimension is B = 8 ft square, thickness t = 2.5 ft, and 150 pcf concrete is used. The service column dead load reaction is N_D = 180 kips. Wind load analysis gives an overturning moment at the base of the footing M_ot = 250 kip-ft and a concurrent lateral shear V = 32 kips. Allowable soil bearing pressure qa = 3000 psf, the coefficient of friction between footing base and soil is mu = 0.40, and the passive pressure resultant available against the footing side, already including its own factor of safety, is Pp = 8 kips.

  1. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 24 kips.): The footing weight equals the concrete unit weight times the footing volume: 150 pcf times (8 ft x 8 ft x 2.5 ft) equals 150 times 160 cubic feet, or 24,000 lb, which is 24 kips. This self-weight must be added to the column dead load reaction to find the total vertical load available to resist overturning and provide sliding friction.
  2. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 204 kips.): Total resisting vertical load equals the column dead load reaction plus the footing self-weight: N = 180 kips + 24 kips = 204 kips. This combined weight, not just the column reaction alone, is what generates the resisting moment against overturning and the normal force used in the sliding friction calculation.
  3. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 816 kip-ft.): The resisting moment about the toe equals the total vertical load times the distance from the toe to the footing centroid, which is B/2 for a footing rotating about its edge: 204 kips times 4 ft equals 816 kip-ft. This moment is compared directly against the applied overturning moment to establish the factor of safety.
  4. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 3.26, which satisfies the requirement.): The factor of safety against overturning equals the resisting moment divided by the driving moment: 816 kip-ft divided by 250 kip-ft equals 3.26. Since 3.26 exceeds the commonly required minimum of 1.5, the footing has ample margin against rotational overturning failure about its toe.
  5. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 81.6 kips.): Frictional resistance at the footing base equals the coefficient of friction times the total vertical load: 0.40 times 204 kips equals 81.6 kips. This frictional component is the primary source of sliding resistance and is combined with any available passive pressure to obtain the total resistance against lateral sliding.
  6. The correct answer is C (89.6 kips total resistance, factor of safety 2.80): Total sliding resistance is the frictional resistance plus passive pressure: 81.6 kips + 8 kips equals 89.6 kips. The factor of safety against sliding equals 89.6 kips divided by the applied shear of 32 kips, which equals 2.80, comfortably exceeding a typical minimum of 1.5.
  7. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1.23 ft.): Eccentricity equals the overturning moment divided by the total vertical load: e = M/N = 250 kip-ft divided by 204 kips, which equals approximately 1.23 ft. This value is then compared to the kern limit of B/6 to determine whether the entire footing base remains in bearing or a portion lifts off the soil.
  8. The correct answer is C (e is about 1.23 ft, under the 1.33 ft kern limit, so the base fully bears.): The kern distance for a footing of width B is B/6, which here equals 8 ft divided by 6, or 1.33 ft. Since the calculated eccentricity of 1.23 ft is smaller than 1.33 ft, the resultant falls within the middle third of the footing and the entire base area remains in bearing, so the standard combined axial-plus-bending pressure formula applies rather than the partial-area formula.
  9. The correct answer is C (about 6120 psf, which exceeds the allowable pressure): With full-base bearing, maximum pressure equals N/A plus Mc/I: 204/64 plus (250 times 4) divided by 341.3, which is about 3.19 ksf plus 2.93 ksf, or roughly 6120 psf. This exceeds the allowable bearing pressure of 3000 psf, showing that even though overturning and sliding both pass comfortably, the bearing pressure check governs and the footing plan area must be increased.
  10. The correct answer is D (about 258 psf, positive, consistent with full-base bearing): Minimum pressure equals N/A minus Mc/I: 3.19 ksf minus 2.93 ksf, which equals about 0.26 ksf or 258 psf. Because this value is positive rather than negative, no portion of the footing lifts off the soil, confirming the earlier conclusion from the eccentricity-versus-kern comparison that the full footing base is in contact with the soil.
  11. The correct answer is B (increase the footing plan dimensions to reduce both the average and eccentric bearing pressure): Bearing pressure depends on the vertical load divided by plan area, plus a bending term that also decreases as plan dimensions grow, so enlarging the footing footprint directly reduces both terms in the q = N/A plus Mc/I expression. Footing thickness affects punching shear and flexural capacity but does not change the soil bearing pressure distribution, so it does not correct this particular failing check.
  12. The correct answer is C (overturning is least critical, sliding is next, bearing pressure is the most critical and fails): Overturning has the largest margin at a factor of safety of 3.26, sliding follows at 2.80, and the bearing pressure check is the only one that actually fails, at roughly twice the allowable value. This ordering shows that a comfortable overturning and sliding margin does not guarantee an adequate bearing pressure result, since bearing depends on plan area rather than only on the moment and shear demands.
Scenario S107

A steel special moment frame building assigned to Seismic Design Category D bears on an isolated square spread footing, B = 9 ft square, thickness t = 3.0 ft, 150 pcf concrete. Service column dead load is N_D = 300 kips. The horizontal seismic overturning moment effect at the base of the footing is E_m = 520 kip-ft and the concurrent seismic shear is V = 44 kips. For the overturning check, the load combination 0.9D + 1.0E applies. For the bearing pressure check, the allowable stress design combination D + 0.7E applies, with allowable bearing pressure qa = 4500 psf. Coefficient of friction mu = 0.35 and passive resistance Pp = 10 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (footing weight 36.45 kips, total dead load 336.45 kips): Footing weight equals 150 pcf times the footing volume: 150 times (9 ft x 9 ft x 3.0 ft) equals 150 times 243 cubic feet, or 36,450 lb, which is 36.45 kips. Adding this to the 300 kip column dead load gives a total unfactored service dead load of 336.45 kips, which is the starting point before applying the 0.9 dead load factor required for the overturning check.
  2. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 302.8 kips.): The 0.9D + 1.0E combination intentionally reduces the stabilizing dead load to its minimum likely value, since a smaller dead load makes the overturning check more conservative: 0.9 times 336.45 kips equals 302.8 kips. This reduced value, not the full service dead load, must be used to compute the resisting moment against seismic overturning.
  3. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1363 kip-ft.): Resisting moment about the toe equals the reduced vertical load times half the footing width: 302.8 kips times 4.5 ft equals about 1363 kip-ft. This resisting moment is compared against the full 1.0E seismic overturning moment demand, since the 0.9D + 1.0E combination applies no reduction factor to the earthquake effect itself.
  4. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 2.62, which satisfies the requirement.): Factor of safety equals resisting moment divided by the seismic overturning moment: 1363 kip-ft divided by 520 kip-ft equals about 2.62. Since this exceeds the commonly applied minimum of 1.5, the footing has adequate margin against seismic overturning even after the dead load has been reduced by the 0.9 factor required for this load combination.
  5. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 106.0 kips.): Frictional resistance equals the coefficient of friction times the vertical load used for the overturning combination: 0.35 times 302.8 kips equals about 106.0 kips. Using the reduced 0.9D axial load is conservative for sliding as well, since a smaller normal force produces less frictional resistance against the applied seismic shear.
  6. The correct answer is B (the calculated result is approximately 116.0 kips total, factor of safety 2.64.): Total sliding resistance equals friction plus passive pressure: 106.0 kips plus 10 kips equals 116.0 kips. Dividing by the applied seismic shear of 44 kips gives a factor of safety of approximately 2.64, indicating the footing has ample margin against sliding under the seismic load case.
  7. The correct answer is D (N = 336.45 kips, M = 364 kip-ft): The D + 0.7E combination uses the full, unreduced dead load together with 0.7 times the seismic effect: N equals 336.45 kips and M equals 0.7 times 520 kip-ft, or 364 kip-ft. This combination is intended to represent a realistic simultaneous dead and seismic loading for checking allowable soil bearing pressure rather than the worst-case overturning scenario.
  8. The correct answer is C (e is about 1.08 ft, under the 1.5 ft kern limit, so the base fully bears.): Eccentricity equals M/N: 364 kip-ft divided by 336.45 kips equals about 1.08 ft. The kern distance for a 9-ft-wide footing is 9/6, or 1.5 ft, and since 1.08 ft is smaller than 1.5 ft, the resultant lies within the kern and the full footing base remains in contact with the soil, so the standard combined bearing pressure formula applies.
  9. The correct answer is D (about 7150 psf, which exceeds the allowable pressure): Maximum bearing pressure equals N/A plus Mc/I. With A = 81 sf, c = 4.5 ft, and I = 546.75 ft to the fourth power, the result is 336.45/81 plus (364 times 4.5)/546.75, or about 4.15 ksf plus 3.00 ksf, roughly 7150 psf. This exceeds the allowable bearing pressure of 4500 psf, so the bearing check governs and the footing plan area needs to be increased even though both overturning and sliding pass.
  10. The correct answer is B (the calculated result is approximately about 1158 psf, positive, confirming full-base bearing.): Minimum pressure equals N/A minus Mc/I: 4.15 ksf minus 3.00 ksf, which equals about 1.16 ksf or 1158 psf. Because this value is positive, no uplift occurs anywhere along the footing base, which is consistent with the earlier finding that the eccentricity of 1.08 ft falls inside the kern distance of 1.5 ft.
  11. The correct answer is D (0.9D + 1.0E minimizes dead load to test overturning, while D + 0.7E checks realistic soil pressure.): The 0.9D + 1.0E combination reduces the dead load to its lower-bound likely value specifically to make overturning and net uplift checks conservative, since less stabilizing weight is harder to counteract the seismic moment. The D + 0.7E allowable stress combination instead retains the full expected dead load together with a reduced seismic effect to represent a realistic combined service-level loading appropriate for checking soil bearing pressure against an allowable value.
  12. The correct answer is C (bearing pressure governs, failing at about 1.6 times allowable, so plan dimensions should grow.): Overturning passes at a factor of safety of 2.62 and sliding passes at 2.64, but the bearing pressure of about 7150 psf exceeds the 4500 psf allowable by roughly 1.6 times, so the bearing check governs. Because bearing pressure depends on the vertical load divided by plan area plus a bending term that also decreases with a larger footprint, enlarging the footing plan dimensions is the most direct way to bring the design into compliance.
Scenario S108

A reinforced concrete cantilever retaining wall supports a level granular backfill with friction angle phi = 32 degrees and unit weight gamma = 115 pcf, retaining a total height H = 12 ft measured from the top of the wall to the bottom of the footing. All calculations are per foot of wall length. The footing is Bw = 8 ft wide, with a 1.5-ft-thick toe, a stem averaging 1.5 ft thick over a 10.5-ft height above the footing, a 5-ft-long heel, and a 1.5-ft-thick footing slab, all using 150 pcf concrete. Coefficient of friction at the base mu = 0.45, passive resistance at the toe Pp = 1.8 kips per foot, and allowable bearing pressure qa = 3500 psf.

  1. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 0.307.): The Rankine active pressure coefficient for a level backfill equals tan squared of (45 degrees minus phi/2): tan squared of (45 minus 16) equals tan squared of 29 degrees, which equals about 0.307. This coefficient converts the vertical effective overburden stress at any depth into the horizontal active earth pressure acting against the back of the wall.
  2. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 2.54 kips per foot.): The Rankine active resultant force per unit length of wall equals one-half times Ka times gamma times H squared: 0.5 times 0.307 times 0.115 kcf times 144 square feet equals about 2.54 kips per foot. This triangular pressure distribution increases linearly with depth and is zero at the top of the retained soil.
  3. The correct answer is A (acts at H/3 = 4.0 ft above the base, giving a moment of about 10.2 kip-ft.): For a triangular pressure distribution with zero pressure at the top, the resultant acts at one-third of the retained height above the base, or 12 ft divided by 3 equals 4.0 ft. The overturning moment about the toe equals the resultant force times this arm: 2.54 kips per foot times 4.0 ft equals about 10.2 kip-ft per foot of wall.
  4. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 10.2 kips per foot.): Stem weight equals 1.5 ft times 10.5 ft times 0.150 kcf, or about 2.36 kips per foot. Footing weight equals 8 ft times 1.5 ft times 0.150 kcf, or 1.80 kips per foot. Heel soil weight equals 5 ft times 10.5 ft times 0.115 kcf, or about 6.04 kips per foot. Summing these three components gives a total resisting vertical load of about 10.2 kips per foot.
  5. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 45.7 kip-ft per foot.): Summing each weight times its arm from the toe gives the total resisting moment: the stem contributes 2.36 kips times 2.25 ft, the footing contributes 1.80 kips times 4.0 ft, and the heel soil contributes 6.04 kips times 5.5 ft, which together total about 45.7 kip-ft per foot of wall, well above the 10.2 kip-ft per foot overturning demand.
  6. The correct answer is C (about 4.49, which satisfies the requirement by a wide margin): The factor of safety against overturning equals the resisting moment divided by the overturning moment: 45.7 kip-ft divided by 10.2 kip-ft equals about 4.49. This is well above the typical minimum requirement of 1.5, indicating this wall has a comfortable margin against rotation about the toe under the level backfill loading condition.
  7. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 4.59 kips per foot.): Frictional resistance equals the coefficient of friction times the total vertical load: 0.45 times 10.2 kips per foot equals about 4.59 kips per foot. This friction component acts at the interface between the footing base and the underlying soil and is the primary source of resistance against the applied active earth pressure force.
  8. The correct answer is A (about 6.39 kips per foot total, factor of safety about 2.51): Total sliding resistance equals friction plus passive pressure: 4.59 kips per foot plus 1.8 kips per foot equals about 6.39 kips per foot. Dividing by the active pressure demand of 2.54 kips per foot gives a factor of safety of about 2.51, which comfortably exceeds the typical minimum requirement of 1.5.
  9. The correct answer is D (e is about 0.52 ft, under the 1.33 ft kern limit, so the base fully bears.): The distance from the toe to the resultant equals the net moment (resisting minus overturning) divided by the total weight: (45.7 minus 10.2) divided by 10.2, or about 3.48 ft. The eccentricity from the footing centerline is 4.0 ft minus 3.48 ft, or about 0.52 ft, which is smaller than the kern distance of 8/6, or 1.33 ft, so the full footing base stays in bearing contact.
  10. The correct answer is A (about 1770 psf, which satisfies the allowable pressure): Maximum pressure equals N/A plus Mc/I, where A = 8 sf per foot of wall, c = 4.0 ft, and I = 42.67 ft to the fourth power. This gives 10.2/8 plus (10.2 times 0.52 times 4.0)/42.67, which totals about 1.77 ksf, or 1770 psf. Since this is well below the allowable bearing pressure of 3500 psf, the bearing check passes with margin, consistent with the comfortable overturning and sliding factors of safety already found.
  11. The correct answer is A (the calculated result is approximately about 780 psf, positive, confirming no uplift.): Minimum pressure equals N/A minus Mc/I: 1.275 ksf minus 0.495 ksf, which equals about 0.78 ksf or 780 psf. Since this value is positive, the entire base remains in bearing contact with no tension or separation anywhere, matching the earlier conclusion that the eccentricity falls within the middle third of the footing.
  12. The correct answer is A (all three checks pass, with sliding having the smallest margin, near a factor of 2.51.): Overturning has a factor of safety of about 4.49, sliding has a factor of safety of about 2.51, and the maximum bearing pressure of about 1770 psf is well under the allowable 3500 psf. All three checks pass, and sliding has the smallest numerical margin of the three, even though it still exceeds the typical minimum requirement of 1.5 by a comfortable amount.
Scenario S109

A second cantilever retaining wall retains a granular backfill with friction angle phi = 30 degrees and unit weight gamma = 120 pcf. The total retained height used for the Rankine active pressure calculation is H = 11 ft. All values are per foot of wall length. The footing is Bw = 7 ft wide with a 1.5-ft toe, a stem averaging 1.5 ft thick over a 9.5-ft height, a 4-ft heel, and a 1.5-ft-thick footing slab, all 150 pcf concrete. In front of the toe, the soil provides Df = 3 ft of embedment available to mobilize passive resistance, and a factor of safety of 1.5 is required on the passive resistance before it may be counted toward sliding resistance. The friction angle mobilized at the footing base is 0.7 times phi, and allowable bearing pressure qa = 2500 psf.

  1. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 3.00.): The Rankine passive pressure coefficient equals tan squared of (45 degrees plus phi/2): tan squared of (45 plus 15) equals tan squared of 60 degrees, which equals 3.00. Passive pressure coefficients are always significantly larger than active pressure coefficients for the same friction angle, which is why even a modest embedment depth can mobilize meaningful resistance against sliding.
  2. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1.62 kips per foot.): The passive resultant force per unit length equals one-half times Kp times gamma times Df squared: 0.5 times 3.00 times 0.120 kcf times 9 square feet equals 1.62 kips per foot. This is the theoretical full passive resistance available if the full embedment depth mobilizes passive conditions with no reduction for uncertainty.
  3. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1.08 kips per foot.): The allowable passive resistance equals the full passive resultant divided by the required factor of safety: 1.62 kips per foot divided by 1.5 equals 1.08 kips per foot. Reducing the theoretical passive resistance by its own factor of safety accounts for uncertainty in soil disturbance, future excavation in front of the wall, and the displacement needed to fully mobilize passive pressure.
  4. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 2.42 kips per foot.): The Rankine active resultant equals one-half times Ka times gamma times H squared: 0.5 times 0.333 times 0.120 kcf times 121 square feet equals about 2.42 kips per foot. This resultant acts at one-third of the 11-ft retained height above the footing base, or about 3.67 ft, and produces the overturning moment used in the sliding and overturning checks.
  5. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 8.27 kips per foot.): Stem weight equals 1.5 ft times 9.5 ft times 0.150 kcf, or about 2.14 kips per foot. Footing weight equals 7 ft times 1.5 ft times 0.150 kcf, or 1.575 kips per foot. Heel soil weight equals 4 ft times 9.5 ft times 0.120 kcf, or 4.56 kips per foot. Summing these gives a total resisting vertical load of about 8.27 kips per foot.
  6. The correct answer is D (mu is about 0.384, giving a frictional resistance of about 3.18 kips per foot): The mobilized friction angle equals 0.7 times 30 degrees, or 21 degrees, and mu equals the tangent of that angle, which is about 0.384. Multiplying by the total vertical weight gives a frictional resistance of 0.384 times 8.27 kips per foot, or about 3.18 kips per foot, acting against the applied active pressure force at the base of the footing.
  7. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 1.31, which fails the requirement.): With friction resistance alone, the factor of safety equals 3.18 kips per foot divided by 2.42 kips per foot, or about 1.31. Since this is below the typical minimum requirement of 1.5, friction alone is insufficient for this wall, which is why the allowable passive resistance in front of the toe must also be counted to achieve an adequate sliding safety margin.
  8. The correct answer is C (about 4.26 kips per foot total, factor of safety about 1.76): Total sliding resistance including passive pressure equals 3.18 kips per foot plus 1.08 kips per foot, or about 4.26 kips per foot. Dividing by the active force of 2.42 kips per foot gives a factor of safety of about 1.76, which now satisfies the typical minimum requirement of 1.5, confirming that the passive resistance at the toe is necessary for this wall to be adequate against sliding.
  9. The correct answer is C (about 3.73, which satisfies a minimum requirement of 1.5): The overturning moment equals the active force times its arm above the base: 2.42 kips per foot times 3.67 ft, or about 8.87 kip-ft per foot. The resisting moment from summing each component weight times its arm from the toe totals about 33.1 kip-ft per foot, giving a factor of safety of about 3.73, well above the minimum requirement of 1.5.
  10. The correct answer is A (e is about 0.57 ft, under the 1.17 ft kern limit, so the base fully bears.): The distance from the toe to the resultant equals the net moment divided by the total weight: (33.1 minus 8.87) divided by 8.27, or about 2.93 ft. The eccentricity from the centerline is 3.5 ft minus 2.93 ft, or about 0.57 ft, which is smaller than the kern distance of 7/6, or 1.17 ft, so the entire footing base remains in bearing contact.
  11. The correct answer is C (about 1758 psf, which satisfies the allowable pressure): Maximum pressure equals N/A plus Mc/I, where A = 7 sf per foot of wall, c = 3.5 ft, and I = 28.58 ft to the fourth power. This gives 8.27/7 plus (8.27 times 0.57 times 3.5)/28.58, which totals about 1.76 ksf, or 1758 psf, comfortably below the allowable bearing pressure of 2500 psf.
  12. The correct answer is B (sliding is most sensitive, passing only with passive resistance, so future excavation must not remove that soil.): The sliding check fails at a factor of safety of only 1.31 using friction alone but passes at 1.76 once the allowable passive resistance is included, making it the check most dependent on that assumption. Because passive resistance depends on soil remaining in place in front of the toe, any future excavation, utility trench, or grade lowering in that area would reduce or eliminate this resistance and could cause the wall to fail the sliding check in service.
Scenario S110

A cast-in-place concrete basement wall for a concrete shear wall building is restrained at the top by the ground floor diaphragm and at the bottom by the basement floor slab, so the wall cannot rotate or translate under lateral earth pressure. The wall retains a granular backfill with friction angle phi = 33 degrees and unit weight gamma = 118 pcf over a full retained height H = 14 ft between the two supports. Because the wall is restrained against the outward movement needed to mobilize the active condition, the geotechnical report requires the at-rest earth pressure coefficient Ko to be used rather than the Rankine active coefficient Ka. All values are per foot of wall length.

  1. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 0.455.): The Jaky approximation for the at-rest coefficient is Ko = 1 minus sine of phi: 1 minus sine of 33 degrees equals 1 minus 0.545, which equals about 0.455. This coefficient applies to soil that has not been allowed to deform laterally, which is the case for a wall rigidly restrained at both the top and bottom.
  2. The correct answer is D (Ka is about 0.295, and using Ka instead of Ko would understate the actual pressure): The Rankine active coefficient equals tan squared of (45 minus phi/2): tan squared of (45 minus 16.5) equals tan squared of 28.5 degrees, or about 0.295, which is smaller than the at-rest coefficient of 0.455. Because a restrained wall cannot deflect enough to mobilize the lower active pressure state, using Ka for this wall would understate the true lateral pressure and could result in an under-designed wall.
  3. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 752 psf.): The at-rest pressure at any depth equals Ko times gamma times that depth, so at the maximum depth of 14 ft the pressure equals 0.455 times 118 pcf times 14 ft, which equals about 752 psf. This peak value occurs at the base of the wall since the at-rest pressure distribution, like the active pressure distribution, increases linearly with depth from zero at the top of the retained soil.
  4. The correct answer is B (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 5.27 kips per foot.): For a triangular pressure distribution, the resultant force equals one-half times the peak pressure times the height: 0.5 times 0.752 ksf times 14 ft equals about 5.27 kips per foot. This total resultant force is what the wall, together with its top and bottom supports, must resist over the full retained height.
  5. The correct answer is B (the active resultant is about 3.41 kips per foot, with the at-rest force 1.55 times larger.): The Rankine active resultant equals one-half times Ka times gamma times H squared: 0.5 times 0.295 times 0.118 kcf times 196 square feet equals about 3.41 kips per foot. Dividing the at-rest resultant of 5.27 kips per foot by this active resultant gives a ratio of about 1.55, confirming that at-rest pressure on a restrained wall is substantially larger than active pressure on a wall free to rotate, roughly matching the ratio of their pressure coefficients.
  6. The correct answer is B (the calculated result is approximately H/3 = 4.67 ft above the base.): A triangular pressure distribution that is zero at the top of the retained soil and maximum at the base has its resultant acting at one-third of the height above the base: 14 ft divided by 3 equals about 4.67 ft. This location is needed to determine how the total resultant force is distributed between the top and bottom supports of the restrained wall.
  7. The correct answer is A (the bottom support carries two-thirds of the resultant and the top support carries one-third): For a simply supported span with a triangular load that is zero at one support and maximum at the other, summing moments about the top support shows the support nearer the peak load carries two-thirds of the total resultant while the far support carries the remaining one-third. Since the at-rest pressure is maximum at the base, the bottom support (the basement slab) carries two-thirds of the total force and the top support (the diaphragm) carries one-third.
  8. The correct answer is B (about 3.51 kips per foot to the slab and about 1.76 kips per foot to the diaphragm): The bottom reaction equals two-thirds of the total resultant: two-thirds of 5.27 kips per foot is about 3.51 kips per foot, delivered into the basement floor slab. The top reaction equals one-third of the total resultant, or about 1.76 kips per foot, delivered into the ground floor diaphragm, which must then transfer this force through the diaphragm to the building's lateral force-resisting system.
  9. The correct answer is B (the diaphragm must resist and transfer this at-rest reaction as an added in-plane force.): The basement wall spans vertically between the two floor levels acting as its supports, so whatever portion of the earth pressure resultant is assigned to the top support must physically be delivered into the diaphragm at that level as an in-plane force. This force adds to any other lateral demands already being transferred through that diaphragm and must be included in its design regardless of whether its source is soil pressure, wind, or seismic load.
  10. The correct answer is C (the active coefficient Ka, since sufficient outward rotation would fully mobilize the active state): The at-rest condition applies specifically when a wall cannot move enough to allow the backfill to reach its lower-bound active pressure state. A cantilever wall free to rotate about its base typically deflects enough at the top to fully mobilize active conditions, so the smaller Rankine active coefficient Ka becomes appropriate rather than the larger at-rest coefficient Ko used for a rigidly restrained wall like the one in this basement scenario.
  11. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 55 percent higher.): The ratio of the at-rest resultant to the active resultant was found to be about 1.55, meaning the at-rest force is 55 percent larger than the active force would have been. This is a direct consequence of Ko being significantly larger than Ka for the same friction angle, and it illustrates why correctly identifying whether a wall is restrained or free to rotate has a substantial effect on the design lateral load.
  12. The correct answer is D (this wall spans between two supports, analyzed as a flexural member, not a rigid gravity body.): A cantilever retaining wall relies on its own weight and footing to resist overturning and sliding as a rigid body, with the earth pressure resultant compared against resisting moments and frictional resistance. A wall restrained at both the top and bottom instead behaves as a flexural member spanning between those supports, so the correct approach is to find the reactions delivered to each support from the applied earth pressure distribution, which then become design demands on the diaphragm above and the slab or footing below.

Practice Test 23 Answer Key

Scenario S111

A wood-framed building on driven piles has a rigid pile cap supporting 6 piles arranged in three lines of 2 piles each, with the three lines located at distances of 4 ft on either side of the group centroid and one line through the centroid (c = minus 4 ft, 0 ft, and plus 4 ft). Lateral load analysis gives a total axial load at the cap of N = 450 kips, an overturning moment about the centroid of M = 1200 kip-ft, and a total lateral shear of V = 60 kips, all assumed to be resisted equally by the piles for shear and by an eccentric axial couple for the moment, consistent with a rigid pile cap. Each pile has an allowable compression capacity of 100 kips, an allowable uplift (tension) capacity of 40 kips, and an allowable lateral (shear) capacity of 12 kips.

  1. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 64 square feet.): Sigma c squared is computed by summing the square of each pile's distance from the centroid over all piles: two piles at plus 4 ft each contribute 16 square feet, two piles at minus 4 ft each contribute 16 square feet, and two piles at 0 ft contribute nothing, giving a total of 4 times 16, or 64 square feet. This sum is the denominator in the moment distribution formula used to find the axial force added to or subtracted from each pile line.
  2. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 75 kips per pile.): The average axial load per pile from the concentric vertical load equals the total load divided by the number of piles: 450 kips divided by 6 equals 75 kips per pile. This average value applies equally to every pile before the additional axial force from the overturning moment is superimposed on the piles farther from the centroid.
  3. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 75 kips.): The additional axial force from the overturning moment equals M times c divided by Sigma c squared: 1200 kip-ft times 4 ft divided by 64 square feet equals 75 kips. This additional force adds to the compression side of the group and subtracts from the tension side, forming a couple that resists the applied overturning moment.
  4. The correct answer is A (150 kips per pile, which exceeds the allowable compression capacity): At the c = plus 4 ft line, the total pile force equals the average load plus the moment-induced force: 75 kips plus 75 kips equals 150 kips per pile. This exceeds the stated allowable compression capacity of 100 kips per pile, indicating the piles at this line are overstressed in compression under the given loading.
  5. The correct answer is D (75 kips per pile, which satisfies the allowable compression capacity): At the centroidal pile line, the distance c equals zero, so the moment produces no additional axial force there, leaving only the average axial load: 75 kips per pile. Since 75 kips is well below the 100 kip allowable compression capacity, these two piles satisfy the axial capacity check comfortably.
  6. The correct answer is B (0 kips per pile, at the boundary between compression and tension, with no net uplift): At the c = minus 4 ft line, the moment-induced force subtracts from the average axial load: 75 kips minus 75 kips equals 0 kips per pile. This means these piles are exactly at the boundary between compression and tension under this particular loading, with no net uplift force to check against the 40 kip allowable tension capacity.
  7. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 10 kips per pile.): Assuming equal distribution of lateral shear among all piles in a rigid cap, each pile carries the total shear divided by the number of piles: 60 kips divided by 6 equals 10 kips per pile. This value is then compared against each pile's allowable lateral capacity, which is a separate check from the axial compression and tension capacities.
  8. The correct answer is C (10 kips is less than 12 kips, so the lateral capacity check passes): Since the calculated lateral shear demand of 10 kips per pile is less than the allowable lateral capacity of 12 kips per pile, every pile in the group satisfies the lateral capacity check, even though the axial compression check at the outer pile line does not.
  9. The correct answer is C (the compression check governs, since the outer pile line at 150 kips exceeds the 100 kip capacity.): The lateral shear demand of 10 kips per pile and the tension condition of 0 kips at the governing line both satisfy their respective allowable capacities, but the compression demand of 150 kips per pile at the outer line exceeds the 100 kip allowable compression capacity, making the axial compression check the governing constraint that requires a design revision.
  10. The correct answer is D (increase the outer pile lines' distance from the centroid, raising Sigma c squared, cutting the moment force.): Since the moment-induced axial force per pile equals M times c divided by Sigma c squared, increasing the spacing of the outer pile lines from the centroid increases Sigma c squared faster than it increases the individual c value in the numerator, which reduces the moment-induced force at each pile line and lowers the peak compression demand at the outermost piles.
  11. The correct answer is D (the moment force becomes 50 kips, giving 125 kips per pile, still over the 100 kip capacity.): With M reduced to 800 kip-ft, the moment-induced force equals 800 times 4 divided by 64, which is 50 kips. Adding this to the average axial load of 75 kips gives a total of 125 kips per pile at the outer line, which still exceeds the 100 kip allowable compression capacity, showing that a moderate reduction in the design moment alone is not enough to resolve this overstress without also adjusting pile spacing or the number of piles.
  12. The correct answer is A (a rigid cap forces all pile heads to translate and rotate together as an axial push-pull couple.): When a pile cap is rigid, it does not bend under the applied loads, so all pile heads connected to it are forced to move and rotate together as a unit. This kinematic constraint means the overturning moment is resisted mainly by a couple of increased compression on one side and decreased compression or tension on the other, following the same P/n plus or minus Mc/Sigma c squared logic used for eccentrically loaded footings, rather than by individual pile bending.
Scenario S112

A masonry building on driven piles has a single pile cap supporting a row of 4 piles spaced 3 ft on center. The geotechnical report gives an allowable lateral capacity of 8 kips per pile if the pile head is free to rotate (a free-head condition), based on a limiting lateral deflection. If the piles are instead rigidly embedded into the pile cap so the pile head cannot rotate (a fixed-head condition), the allowable lateral capacity increases to 14 kips per pile for the same limiting deflection. Because the piles are spaced closely relative to their diameter, a group efficiency factor of 0.90 must be applied to the fixed-head capacity when checking the group as a whole. The total lateral shear delivered to the pile cap is V = 48 kips.

  1. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 12 kips per pile.): With the total lateral shear distributed equally among the 4 piles in the row, each pile carries the total load divided by the number of piles: 48 kips divided by 4 equals 12 kips per pile. This demand is then compared against the allowable capacity under whichever head condition, free or fixed, actually exists at the top of the piles.
  2. The correct answer is D (32 kips total, which is inadequate for the 48 kip demand): The total free-head capacity equals the per-pile allowable capacity times the number of piles: 8 kips times 4 equals 32 kips. Since this total capacity is less than the 48 kip lateral demand, the pile group would be inadequate if the piles were connected to the cap with a free-head, or pinned, condition.
  3. The correct answer is B (56 kips total, which is adequate for the 48 kip demand): The total fixed-head capacity without any group reduction equals 14 kips times 4 piles, or 56 kips. Since 56 kips exceeds the 48 kip demand, the group would be adequate under a fixed-head condition before considering the group efficiency factor required for the close pile spacing in this project.
  4. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 12.6 kips per pile.): The group-reduced allowable capacity equals the fixed-head per-pile capacity times the group efficiency factor: 14 kips times 0.90 equals 12.6 kips per pile. This reduction accounts for the overlapping zones of soil disturbance and stress between closely spaced piles, which reduces the lateral resistance each pile can develop compared to an isolated pile.
  5. The correct answer is B (50.4 kips total, which is adequate for the 48 kip demand): The total group-reduced capacity equals 12.6 kips times 4 piles, or 50.4 kips. Since this exceeds the 48 kip lateral demand, although only by a modest margin, the pile group is adequate under a fixed-head condition even after applying the required group efficiency reduction for close spacing.
  6. The correct answer is B (12 kips is less than 12.6 kips, so the individual pile check passes as well.): Since the equally distributed per-pile demand of 12 kips is less than the group-reduced allowable capacity of 12.6 kips per pile, the individual pile check passes with a small margin, which is consistent with the total group capacity of 50.4 kips exceeding the total demand of 48 kips by the same proportional amount.
  7. The correct answer is A (restraining the head creates a stiffer system with two moment points, reducing deflection for the same load.): A free-head pile behaves like a cantilever with a single point of maximum moment near the ground surface, while restraining rotation at the top of a fixed-head pile splits the bending moment between the pile head and a point below grade, reducing the peak moment and the lateral deflection for the same applied load. This stiffer, better-distributed behavior allows a larger lateral load before the pile reaches the same limiting deflection used to establish the free-head capacity.
  8. The correct answer is B (the pile must be embedded deep enough into a rigid cap, with dowels developing full moment continuity.): A true fixed-head condition requires the pile to be embedded into the pile cap by a sufficient development length, typically with reinforcing dowels or the pile's own reinforcement extending into the cap concrete, so that the connection can transfer moment rather than behaving as a simple pin. Without this embedment and reinforcement, the connection would behave closer to a free-head condition regardless of the intended design assumption.
  9. The correct answer is C (the total capacity returns to 56.0 kips, an added 5.6 kips of margin over the reduced value.): Without any group efficiency reduction, the total capacity returns to 14 kips times 4 piles, or 56.0 kips, compared to the group-reduced value of 50.4 kips found earlier. The difference of 5.6 kips represents the margin of safety that was given up specifically because of the close pile spacing in the original design, illustrating the direct cost of tight spacing on lateral capacity.
  10. The correct answer is D (the piles must be detailed for a true fixed-head condition, since free-head capacity alone is inadequate.): The free-head total capacity of 32 kips is well below the 48 kip demand, while the fixed-head total capacity, even after the required group efficiency reduction, reaches 50.4 kips, which is adequate. This means the connection detail between the pile heads and the cap must actually achieve a fixed-head, moment-resisting condition, since a free-head or pinned connection would leave the group significantly understrength.
  11. The correct answer is A (closely spaced piles develop overlapping soil resistance zones, reducing each pile's individual lateral resistance.): Lateral pile capacity depends on the passive soil resistance mobilized in front of each pile as it deflects, and when piles are spaced closely this resistance zone from one pile overlaps with its neighbor, reducing the resistance any single pile can develop compared to an isolated pile. Axial capacity depends primarily on skin friction along the pile shaft and end bearing at the tip, which involves a different, though related, group interaction, so the specific 0.90 factor given in this problem is applied where it was stated, to the lateral capacity.
  12. The correct answer is B (no, since the reduced group capacity of 50.4 kips is less than the 55 kip demand.): The group-reduced fixed-head capacity was found to be 50.4 kips, which is less than an increased demand of 55 kips, so the pile group would no longer be adequate under the higher lateral load. This would require either adding piles to the row, increasing pile spacing to avoid the group efficiency reduction, or specifying a larger pile diameter to increase the individual fixed-head capacity.
Scenario S113

A building assigned to Seismic Design Category C has two adjacent isolated column footings, Footing A and Footing B, that are not otherwise restrained against relative horizontal movement by a structural slab or other bracing. Footing A carries a total service dead plus live column load of 380 kips, and Footing B carries a total service dead plus live column load of 240 kips. Per the foundation engineer's design criteria, a tie beam connecting the two footings must be designed for a minimum horizontal force equal to 10 percent of the larger footing's total column load. The proposed tie beam is 14 in wide by 20 in deep, reinforced with 2 No. 6 longitudinal bars of Grade 60 reinforcing steel (fy = 60 ksi), spanning a clear distance of 22 ft between the two footings, and is designed as a tension-controlled member with a strength reduction factor phi = 0.9.

  1. The correct answer is A (Footing A governs since it carries the larger load, giving a minimum tie force of 38 kips): The code criterion is based on 10 percent of the larger of the two connected footing loads, and since Footing A carries 380 kips compared to Footing B's 240 kips, Footing A governs: 0.10 times 380 kips equals 38 kips. This is the minimum horizontal force the tie beam must be designed to resist, independent of the actual calculated seismic collector or diaphragm force, unless that calculated force is larger.
  2. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 0.88 square inches.): The total reinforcing area equals the number of bars times the area of each bar: 2 bars times 0.44 square inches per bar equals 0.88 square inches. This total area of steel is what resists the axial tension force in the tie beam once the concrete has cracked and the reinforcement alone is assumed to carry the tension.
  3. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 47.5 kips.): The design tension capacity for a tension-controlled reinforced concrete member equals phi times the steel area times the yield strength: 0.9 times 0.88 square inches times 60 ksi equals about 47.5 kips. This capacity represents the tie beam's ability to resist a pure axial tension force once the reinforcement reaches yield.
  4. The correct answer is B (the capacity exceeds the demand, with a factor of safety of about 1.25): The factor of safety, or margin, equals the design capacity divided by the required demand: 47.5 kips divided by 38 kips equals about 1.25. Since the capacity exceeds the demand, the tie beam reinforcement is adequate for the minimum tension force required by the 10 percent criterion.
  5. The correct answer is A (lateral and seismic forces reverse direction, so the tie beam may act as a compression strut too.): Wind and seismic lateral forces reverse direction over the duration of an event, so a tie beam connecting two footings must be capable of resisting the same force acting in either direction: as tension pulling the footings together, or as compression pushing them apart. A compression check for this same beam would need to verify that its unsupported length does not lead to buckling before reaching the required compressive force, which is a separate check from the tension yield capacity calculated above.
  6. The correct answer is A (Footing B now governs, giving a minimum tie force of 42 kips): Since the 10 percent criterion is always based on the larger of the two connected footing loads, if Footing B's load increases to 420 kips it becomes the larger footing and now governs: 0.10 times 420 kips equals 42 kips. This slightly higher required force would need to be checked against the same 47.5 kip tension capacity already calculated for the existing reinforcement.
  7. The correct answer is B (the existing capacity of 47.5 kips still exceeds 42 kips, though with a reduced margin.): The unchanged capacity of 47.5 kips still exceeds the revised demand of 42 kips, giving a reduced but still acceptable factor of safety of about 1.13. If additional margin were desired, adding a third No. 6 bar would increase the steel area to 1.32 square inches and raise the design tension capacity to about 0.9 times 1.32 times 60, or roughly 71.3 kips.
  8. The correct answer is A (a tie beam prevents relative horizontal movement between footings, restraint the individual checks do not provide.): Overturning, sliding, and bearing checks each evaluate an individual footing's stability under its own applied loads, but they do not prevent that footing from sliding or shifting relative to an adjacent footing. A tie beam physically restrains this relative horizontal movement between footings, which is particularly important where footings might otherwise be pushed apart or together by differing soil conditions or unbalanced lateral forces during a seismic event.
  9. The correct answer is D (when the footings are already restrained by a structural slab on grade or other adequate bracing.): The scenario specifically states that ties are required because these two footings are not otherwise restrained against relative horizontal movement. If a properly designed structural slab on grade, grade beam, or other adequate bracing already provided this restraint, a separate dedicated tie beam meeting the 10 percent criterion would not be necessary, since its purpose would already be served by the existing element.
  10. The correct answer is A (the percentage rule sets a code minimum robustness force, independent of the calculated analysis result.): Minimum percentage-based tie force requirements exist as a robustness provision, ensuring adjacent footings remain interconnected even in situations where a structural analysis might calculate a very small or negligible actual force. This type of minimum requirement is applied as a lower bound, meaning the tie beam must be designed for whichever is larger, the calculated force from the actual structural analysis or the code minimum percentage-based force.
  11. The correct answer is B (the larger calculated force of 55 kips governs, since the percentage-based value is only a minimum requirement): Since the percentage-based tie force represents a code minimum rather than an upper bound, whenever the actual calculated force from the structural analysis exceeds this minimum, the larger calculated value of 55 kips must govern the final tie beam design rather than the 38 kip minimum, which would leave the beam under-designed for its actual collector force demand.
  12. The correct answer is B (the tension check passes near a factor of 1.25, but a compression buckling check is still needed.): The tension capacity of 47.5 kips exceeds the 38 kip minimum required force, giving an adequate factor of safety of about 1.25 for that direction of loading. However, because lateral and seismic forces reverse direction, the same tie beam must also be checked as a compression strut for buckling under the same force before the design can be considered complete, which is a distinct calculation from the tension yield check already performed.
Scenario S114

An outdoor pad-mounted electrical transformer, classified as a nonbuilding structure, is supported on an isolated square spread footing with plan dimension B = 6 ft square and thickness t = 2.0 ft, using 150 pcf concrete. The service dead load of the equipment and its support frame is N_D = 60 kips. Wind load analysis on the exposed equipment produces an overturning moment at the base of the footing of M_ot = 140 kip-ft and a concurrent lateral shear of V = 18 kips. Allowable soil bearing pressure qa = 2500 psf, coefficient of friction mu = 0.50, and passive resistance at the footing edge Pp = 4 kips.

  1. The correct answer is B (footing weight 10.8 kips, total N = 70.8 kips): Footing weight equals 150 pcf times the footing volume: 150 times (6 ft times 6 ft times 2.0 ft) equals 150 times 72 cubic feet, or 10,800 lb, which is 10.8 kips. Adding this to the 60 kip equipment dead load gives a total vertical resisting load of 70.8 kips.
  2. The correct answer is C (resisting moment 212.4 kip-ft, factor of safety about 1.52, which just satisfies a minimum of 1.5): The resisting moment equals N times B/2: 70.8 kips times 3.0 ft equals 212.4 kip-ft. Dividing by the overturning moment of 140 kip-ft gives a factor of safety of about 1.52, which just barely satisfies the typical minimum requirement of 1.5, leaving essentially no additional margin.
  3. The correct answer is C (total resistance 39.4 kips, factor of safety about 2.19): Frictional resistance equals mu times N: 0.50 times 70.8 kips equals 35.4 kips. Adding the passive resistance of 4 kips gives a total sliding resistance of 39.4 kips, and dividing by the applied shear of 18 kips gives a factor of safety of about 2.19, comfortably satisfying the typical minimum requirement of 1.5.
  4. The correct answer is A (e is about 1.98 ft, exceeding B/6 = 1.0 ft, so part of the base lifts off.): Eccentricity equals M/N: 140 kip-ft divided by 70.8 kips equals about 1.98 ft. Since the kern distance for this 6-ft-wide footing is 6/6, or 1.0 ft, and the calculated eccentricity of 1.98 ft is much larger, the resultant falls well outside the middle third of the footing, meaning a portion of the base lifts off the soil and only part of the footing area remains in bearing contact.
  5. The correct answer is B (the partial-area formula applies, since the standard formula would predict impossible negative pressure at the heel.): When the eccentricity exceeds B/6, the standard N/A plus Mc/I formula produces a negative pressure at the heel, implying the soil is pulling down on the footing there, which is physically impossible since soil cannot resist tension. The correct approach recognizes that only a portion of the footing remains in contact with the soil and uses the partial-area formula, q_max = 2N divided by (3 times L times (B/2 minus e)), which accounts for the reduced effective bearing area.
  6. The correct answer is A ((B/2 minus e) is about 1.02 ft, giving a maximum bearing pressure of about 7690 psf): B/2 minus e equals 3.0 ft minus 1.98 ft, or about 1.02 ft. Substituting into the partial-area formula gives q_max equals 2 times 70.8 divided by (3 times 6 times 1.02), which equals 141.6 divided by 18.41, or about 7690 psf, using the reduced effective bearing width rather than the full 6-ft footing width.
  7. The correct answer is B (the maximum pressure is roughly three times the allowable value, so the bearing check fails badly): The calculated maximum bearing pressure of about 7690 psf is roughly three times the allowable bearing pressure of 2500 psf, representing a severe bearing failure rather than a marginal one. This is a direct consequence of the large eccentricity relative to the footing width, which concentrates the entire vertical load onto a small effective bearing area at the toe.
  8. The correct answer is C (increase the footing plan dimensions, since a wider footing raises the resisting arm and cuts eccentricity.): Increasing the footing's plan dimensions increases the resisting moment (which grows with B/2) and, importantly, increases the kern distance B/6 relative to the same overturning moment, which reduces the ratio of eccentricity to width. A larger footing is more likely to keep the resultant within the kern, avoiding the punishing partial-area bearing formula altogether, while simultaneously improving the overturning factor of safety margin.
  9. The correct answer is C (the eccentricity stays 1.98 ft, still over the new 1.5 ft kern, so uplift persists.): Eccentricity equals M/N, and since neither the moment nor the axial load changes in this comparison, the eccentricity remains about 1.98 ft. The kern distance for the wider 9-ft footing becomes 9/6, or 1.5 ft, and since 1.98 ft still exceeds 1.5 ft, uplift still occurs at this plan size. Widening the footing alone does not fully resolve the eccentricity issue.
  10. The correct answer is D (such a large eccentricity relative to modest dead load suggests the equipment bracing itself should be reconsidered.): An eccentricity nearly double the kern distance suggests the overturning moment is very large relative to the supported dead load, which is common for lightweight, top-heavy equipment structures exposed to wind. In such cases, simply enlarging the footing may require an impractically large footprint, so alternatives such as adding ballast weight, bracing the equipment support to reduce the moment arm, or anchoring the equipment differently are often more practical than footing enlargement alone.
  11. The correct answer is C (overturning barely passes, sliding passes comfortably, and bearing pressure fails badly, so bearing governs the redesign.): The overturning factor of safety of about 1.52 barely meets the minimum requirement of 1.5, the sliding factor of safety of about 2.19 passes comfortably, and the bearing pressure of about 7690 psf is roughly three times the allowable 2500 psf, representing the most severe failure of the three checks. This makes bearing pressure the clearly governing check that must drive any redesign.
  12. The correct answer is C (the moment-to-dead-load ratio is far larger for this light equipment pad, pushing eccentricity past the kern.): The determining factor is purely the ratio of eccentricity to footing width: when e exceeds B/6, part of the footing lifts off and the partial-area formula is required, and when e is less than B/6, the standard combined formula applies. This equipment pad has a relatively small supported dead load compared to its overturning moment, producing a large eccentricity, while the heavier building footings analyzed earlier had enough dead load relative to their moments to keep the resultant within the kern.
Scenario S115

A cantilever retaining wall retains a granular backfill with friction angle phi = 30 degrees and unit weight gamma = 115 pcf, over a total retained height H = 10 ft. A uniform surcharge load qs = 300 psf, representing an adjacent parking area, is applied at the ground surface behind the wall and extends across the full width of the backfill, including directly above the footing heel. All values are per foot of wall length. The footing is Bw = 6.5 ft wide, with a 1.3-ft toe, a stem averaging 1.2 ft thick over an 8.5-ft height, a 4.0-ft heel, and a 1.5-ft-thick footing slab, all 150 pcf concrete. Coefficient of friction mu = 0.35, passive resistance Pp = 1.0 kip per foot, and allowable bearing pressure qa = 2400 psf.

  1. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1.92 kips per foot.): The active resultant from the soil alone equals one-half times Ka times gamma times H squared: 0.5 times 0.333 times 0.115 kcf times 100 square feet equals about 1.92 kips per foot. This triangular pressure component acts at H/3, or about 3.33 ft, above the base of the footing.
  2. The correct answer is C (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 100 psf.): The lateral pressure from a uniform surcharge equals Ka times the surcharge intensity, applied uniformly over the full retained height: 0.333 times 300 psf equals about 100 psf. Unlike the soil's own triangular pressure distribution, this surcharge pressure does not vary with depth since the surcharge load itself is constant across the ground surface.
  3. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1.0 kip per foot.): For a uniform (rectangular) pressure distribution, the resultant force equals the pressure times the height over which it acts: 0.100 ksf times 10 ft equals 1.0 kip per foot. This resultant acts at the mid-height of the wall, H/2, rather than at H/3 as with the triangular soil pressure distribution.
  4. The correct answer is A (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately about 11.4 kip-ft per foot.): The overturning moment from the soil component equals 1.92 kips per foot times 3.33 ft, or about 6.4 kip-ft per foot, and the moment from the surcharge component equals 1.0 kip per foot times 5.0 ft, or 5.0 kip-ft per foot. Summing these two contributions gives a total overturning moment of about 11.4 kip-ft per foot acting on the wall.
  5. The correct answer is C (the surcharge weight over the heel adds to the resisting load, partly offsetting its added driving force.): Because the uniform surcharge is stated to extend across the full backfill width, including directly above the footing heel, its weight over that heel area acts as an additional vertical load contributing to the resisting moment, in the same way the heel soil weight does. This partially offsets the additional overturning moment the same surcharge produces through the increased lateral pressure, though the two effects do not necessarily cancel out completely.
  6. The correct answer is D (the calculated value for this case works out to approximately 1.2 kips per foot.): The additional vertical load from the surcharge over the heel equals the surcharge intensity times the heel length: 0.300 ksf times 4.0 ft equals 1.2 kips per foot. This load acts at the same horizontal location as the heel soil weight, at the centroid of the heel, and is added to the other resisting weight components when computing the total resisting moment.
  7. The correct answer is C (about 2.69, which satisfies a minimum requirement of 1.5): The factor of safety against overturning equals the resisting moment divided by the total overturning moment: 30.66 kip-ft per foot divided by 11.4 kip-ft per foot equals about 2.69. This value already incorporates both the surcharge's contribution to the driving overturning moment and its contribution to the resisting vertical weight over the heel, and it comfortably satisfies the typical minimum requirement of 1.5.
  8. The correct answer is C (frictional resistance about 2.84 kips per foot, total resistance about 3.84 kips per foot): Frictional resistance equals mu times the total vertical load: 0.35 times 8.10 kips per foot equals about 2.84 kips per foot. Adding the passive resistance of 1.0 kip per foot gives a total sliding resistance of about 3.84 kips per foot, which is then compared against the total active plus surcharge driving force to determine the factor of safety against sliding.
  9. The correct answer is C (about 1.32, which fails the minimum requirement of 1.5): The factor of safety against sliding equals the total resistance divided by the total driving force: 3.84 kips per foot divided by 2.92 kips per foot equals about 1.32. Since this is below the typical minimum requirement of 1.5, the sliding check fails for this wall, even though the overturning check passed comfortably, showing that the surcharge's added driving force is enough to push the sliding check below its required margin.
  10. The correct answer is A (add a shear key below the footing base to mobilize passive resistance beyond the toe embedment.): A shear key, a short vertical concrete projection extending below the footing base into the soil, mobilizes additional passive resistance along its own embedded height, directly increasing the total sliding resistance without significantly changing the footing's plan dimensions or the resisting moment used in the overturning check. This makes it a targeted solution for a wall that already has adequate overturning capacity but insufficient sliding resistance.
  11. The correct answer is A (e = 0.87 ft is under B/6 = 1.08 ft, so the base bears fully.): Since the kern distance for this 6.5-ft-wide footing is 6.5/6, or about 1.08 ft, and the calculated eccentricity of 0.87 ft is smaller, the full base remains in bearing and the standard combined formula applies: q_max equals N/A plus Mc/I, which works out to about 2250 psf, comfortably below the allowable bearing pressure of 2400 psf.
  12. The correct answer is D (sliding governs and fails, since the surcharge cuts the sliding factor of safety below 1.5.): Overturning passed at a factor of safety of about 2.69 and the maximum bearing pressure of about 2250 psf remained below the 2400 psf allowable, but the sliding factor of safety of about 1.32 fell below the required minimum of 1.5. The surcharge increased both the driving lateral force and the resisting vertical weight over the heel, but for this wall's geometry the increase in driving force at the sliding interface outweighed the modest increase in frictional resistance from the added weight, making sliding the governing failure mode introduced by the surcharge.

Practice Test 24 Answer Key

Scenario S116

An existing three-story steel special moment frame office building is being evaluated for seismic retrofit. The building is Risk Category II with Site Class D soil, Ss = 1.25g, and S1 = 0.55g (Fa = 1.0, Fv = 1.5). Story heights are 14 ft (ground story, which contains an open parking level), 13 ft, and 13 ft, for a total roof height of 40 ft. Total seismic weight is 4,500 kips, distributed equally as 1,500 kips at each of three levels. Lateral story stiffnesses are 8,000 kip/in (story 1), 13,000 kip/in (story 2), and 14,000 kip/in (story 3, roof).
