  1. The correct answer is D (Sds = 0.833g and Sd1 = 0.550g): Sds = (2/3)(1.0)(1.25g) = 0.833g and Sd1 = (2/3)(1.5)(0.55g) = 0.550g. These are the two-thirds-scaled design spectral acceleration parameters used throughout the ELF procedure. The other options incorrectly skip the two-thirds factor or misapply Fa and Fv to the wrong period parameter.
  2. The correct answer is D (Seismic Design Category D): with Sds = 0.833g and Sd1 = 0.550g, both exceed the 0.50g and 0.20g thresholds respectively in ASCE 7-16 Tables 11.6-1 and 11.6-2, placing the building in SDC D. Category E would require S1 at least 0.75g, which is not the case here, so D governs rather than a higher or lower category.
  3. The correct answer is A (A soft story (Type 1a) irregularity exists because story 1 stiffness is less than 70 percent of story 2): 70 percent of 13,000 kip/in is 9,100 kip/in, and the actual story 1 stiffness of 8,000 kip/in falls below that threshold. It does not meet the more severe extreme soft story threshold of 60 percent, since 8,000 kip/in exceeds 60 percent of 13,000 kip/in (7,800 kip/in), so Type 1a governs rather than Type 1b or no irregularity at all.
  4. The correct answer is D (Modal response spectrum analysis is required because ELF is not permitted with this irregularity): Table 12.6-1 prohibits the equivalent lateral force procedure for structures in SDC D through F with a Type 1a or 1b vertical irregularity that exceed two stories or 30 ft in height. This building is three stories and 40 ft tall, so it exceeds that threshold and modal response spectrum analysis becomes the required minimum procedure rather than ELF or the simplified method.
  5. The correct answer is B (Ta = 0.54 seconds): Ta = Ct(hn)^x = 0.028(40)^0.8. Computing 40 raised to the 0.8 power gives approximately 19.1, so Ta = 0.028(19.1) = 0.536 seconds, which rounds to 0.54 seconds. The other values result from misapplying the exponent or using the wrong roof height.
  6. The correct answer is D (Cs = 0.104): Cs = Sds/(R/Ie) = 0.833/(8/1.0) = 0.104. This value does not need to exceed Cs,max computed from Sd1/(T(R/Ie)) = 0.55/(0.536 x 8) = 0.128, and it exceeds the minimum of 0.044(Sds)(Ie) = 0.037, so the base value of 0.104 governs the design.
  7. The correct answer is D (V = 469 kips): V = CsW = 0.104(4,500 kips) = 468.9 kips, which rounds to 469 kips. This base shear represents the total design lateral force applied through the equivalent lateral force procedure before it is distributed vertically among the three floor levels.
  8. The correct answer is C (Fx = 232 kips): Cvx = wxhx/(sum of wihi) = (1,500 x 40)/121,500 = 0.494, so Fx = CvxV = 0.494(469 kips) = 231.6 kips, which rounds to 232 kips. This is the largest of the three level forces because the roof carries the largest product of weight and height in the vertical distribution.
  9. The correct answer is D (rho = 1.3 by default unless specific redundancy conditions are demonstrated): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.3.4.2 sets the default redundancy factor at 1.3 for structures in SDC D through F unless the engineer demonstrates that removal of an individual lateral element would not create an extreme torsional irregularity and that each story resists at least 35 percent of the story shear along each principal axis. A soft story condition makes satisfying that second condition unlikely, so 1.3 is the reasonable working value.
  10. The correct answer is A (0.020hsx, or 3.36 inches): Table 12.12-1 assigns an allowable drift of 0.020hsx to Risk Category I and II structures that do not qualify for the more permissive 0.025hsx category. With hsx = 14 ft = 168 in, the allowable drift is 0.020(168) = 3.36 in, which governs the retrofit stiffness target for the soft ground story.
  11. The correct answer is C (Add new stiff and ductile lateral elements, such as steel braced frames, at the open ground story): introducing new braced frames or reinforced concrete walls at the deficient ground story directly increases story stiffness and strength where the irregularity originates, correcting both the soft story condition and the associated drift and P-delta concerns. Reducing upper floor weight, changing rho, or reclassifying Risk Category do not address the underlying stiffness discontinuity.
  12. The correct answer is D (Story 1 stiffness increases to at least 70 percent of story 2 stiffness after retrofit): the Type 1a soft story definition in Table 12.3-2 is triggered when a story's stiffness falls below 70 percent of the story above, so raising story 1 stiffness to meet or exceed that ratio removes the irregularity classification. Weight changes and redundancy factor adjustments do not by themselves alter the stiffness ratio that defines this irregularity.
Scenario S117

A new elevated steel water storage tank is supported on four braced steel legs arranged in a 20 ft square, providing fire suppression water for an adjacent facility (Risk Category III, Ie = 1.25). The site has Sds = 0.90g and Sd1 = 0.45g (Seismic Design Category D). The tank and its contents have an operating weight of 850 kips, with the center of gravity located 60 ft above grade. The engineer is designing the braced-leg tower and its foundation anchorage for seismic lateral forces.

  1. The correct answer is B (Chapter 15, Seismic Design Requirements for Nonbuilding Structures): an elevated tank on its own dedicated support tower is a nonbuilding structure rather than a building or a component attached to one, so Chapter 15 provisions, including Table 15.4-2 response modification coefficients, apply directly to the tower and tank design.
  2. The correct answer is A (R = 3.0): Table 15.4-2 assigns elevated tanks, vessels, bins, or hoppers on braced or unbraced legs a response modification coefficient of 3.0, reflecting the limited ductility and post-yield behavior expected of this structural configuration compared to a typical building lateral system.
  3. The correct answer is C (Risk Category IV, Ie = 1.50, the essential-facility category for water storage tanks required to maintain fire-suppression pressure): ASCE 7-16 Table 1.5-1 explicitly lists water storage facilities and pump structures required to maintain water pressure for fire suppression as Risk Category IV, since their failure directly undermines the emergency response capability of the facilities they serve, corresponding to an Importance Factor of Ie = 1.50.
  4. The correct answer is C (Cs = 0.375): Cs = Sds/(R/Ie) = 0.90/(3.0/1.25) = 0.90/2.4 = 0.375. This coefficient is notably higher than a typical building value because the low response modification coefficient assigned to braced-leg elevated tanks reflects their reduced ductility compared to a code-recognized building lateral system.
  5. The correct answer is C (V = 319 kips): V = CsWp = 0.375(850 kips) = 318.75 kips, which rounds to 319 kips. This total lateral force must be resisted by the four braced legs and transferred through the tower into the foundation.
  6. The correct answer is A (M = 19,140 kip-ft): for a single concentrated mass at the top of the tower, the overturning moment is approximated as M = V(h) = 319 kips(60 ft) = 19,140 kip-ft. This moment must be resisted by the tension and compression forces developed in the four legs and ultimately by the foundation.
  7. The correct answer is B (F = 479 kips): using simple statics, the added axial force per tension-side leg equals M divided by the product of the number of legs per side and the spacing, F = 19,140 kip-ft/(2 legs x 20 ft) = 478.5 kips, which rounds to 479 kips. This overturning-induced force must be added to or subtracted from the gravity load carried by each leg.
  8. The correct answer is B (F = 79.8 kips): with four symmetric legs sharing the total base shear equally, each leg carries V/4 = 319 kips/4 = 79.75 kips, which rounds to 79.8 kips. This direct shear is combined with the bracing configuration to design the leg and brace connections.
  9. The correct answer is D (Convective sloshing mode response of the stored liquid): unlike large ground-supported tanks where the convective (sloshing) mode of the liquid contributes meaningfully to lateral demand, an elevated tank on a rigid support tower is typically evaluated as a single concentrated impulsive mass, making the sloshing contribution a secondary consideration compared to overturning, shear, and anchorage design.
  10. The correct answer is B (Ev = 0.20(Sds)(D) = 0.18D): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.4.2.2 defines the vertical seismic load effect as Ev = 0.2SdsD, so with Sds = 0.90g, Ev = 0.2(0.90)D = 0.18D. This vertical component is added to or subtracted from the dead load in the governing seismic load combinations.
  11. The correct answer is B (Seismic load governs because it produces the larger controlling lateral demand): structural design combinations require checking both wind and seismic load cases and designing for whichever produces the larger demand on each element, so with the stated seismic base shear exceeding the wind base shear, seismic governs the tower and leg design rather than wind or an average of the two.
  12. The correct answer is A (Designing anchor bolts and the foundation for net uplift when overturning tension exceeds the gravity reaction): when the calculated overturning tension in a leg exceeds the dead load compression normally carried by that leg, the net effect is uplift, requiring anchor bolts and foundation elements specifically designed to resist that tension rather than relying on gravity alone.
Scenario S118

A rooftop packaged HVAC unit weighing 8 kips is being anchored on the roof of a five-story office building, Risk Category II (Ip = 1.0 for this component). The roof is 60 ft above grade, and the site has Sds = 0.75g. The unit is rigidly mounted (not vibration isolated), with component amplification factor ap = 2.5 and component response modification factor Rp = 2.5 per ASCE 7-16 Table 13.6-1. The engineer must determine the seismic design force for the unit's anchorage to the roof curb.

  1. The correct answer is C (Chapter 13, Seismic Design Requirements for Nonstructural Components): mechanical equipment such as a rooftop HVAC unit that is supported by, but not itself a primary part of, the building structure is classified as a nonstructural component, so its anchorage design is governed by Chapter 13 rather than the building-level provisions of Chapter 12.
  2. The correct answer is A (Ip = 1.0): per ASCE 7-16 Section 13.1.3, a component importance factor of 1.5 applies only to designated seismic systems in Risk Category IV structures, components containing hazardous materials, or components needed for continued operation of a Risk Category IV facility. Since none of those conditions apply to this ordinary rooftop unit, Ip = 1.0 governs.
  3. The correct answer is D (z/h = 1.0): the attachment elevation z is measured relative to the base of the structure, and since the HVAC unit sits at the roof, which is the highest point of the structure, z equals h and the ratio z/h is 1.0, producing the maximum height amplification permitted in the Fp equation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Fp = 7.2 kips): Fp = 0.4(ap)(Sds)(Wp)/(Rp/Ip) x (1 + 2z/h) = 0.4(2.5)(0.75)(8)/(2.5/1.0) x (1 + 2(1.0)) = 2.4 x 3 = 7.2 kips. This is the base equation result before checking it against the code maximum and minimum limits.
  5. The correct answer is A (Fp,max = 9.6 kips): Fp,max = 1.6(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 1.6(0.75)(1.0)(8) = 9.6 kips. This upper bound caps the design force regardless of the component location or dynamic amplification calculated from the base Fp equation.
  6. The correct answer is C (Fp,min = 1.8 kips): Fp,min = 0.3(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 0.3(0.75)(1.0)(8) = 1.8 kips. This lower bound ensures a minimum design force is applied even for components with a low elevation ratio or favorable ap and Rp values.
  7. The correct answer is B (7.2 kips, because it falls between the minimum and maximum limits): since the calculated base value of 7.2 kips lies between Fp,min of 1.8 kips and Fp,max of 9.6 kips, no capping is required and the base equation result of 7.2 kips is used directly as the design anchorage force.
  8. The correct answer is D (1.2 kips): the vertical seismic effect on the component follows the same form as the building-level Ev term, 0.2(Sds)(Wp) = 0.2(0.75)(8) = 1.2 kips. This vertical force is combined with the horizontal Fp in the anchorage design load combinations.
  9. The correct answer is A (Fp must be applied independently in each of two orthogonal horizontal directions at the center of gravity of the component): Section 13.3.1 requires the horizontal seismic force to be applied at the component's center of gravity in the direction that produces the most critical load effect, evaluated in each of two orthogonal horizontal directions rather than a single fixed direction.
  10. The correct answer is C (The unit is not exempt because its weight far exceeds the light-component exemption threshold): Section 13.1.4 exempts only mechanical and electrical components below specific low weight and force thresholds, generally on the order of a few hundred pounds. An 8,000 lb rooftop unit is well above that threshold and must be positively anchored for the calculated Fp regardless of its roof location or Ip value.
  11. The correct answer is D (The component amplification factor ap would typically increase to reflect the flexible, resonant-prone mounting): Table 13.6-1 assigns a higher ap value to equipment on vibration isolators because the flexible mounting can amplify the component's dynamic response relative to a rigidly attached unit, increasing the resulting Fp even though Sds, Wp, and z/h remain unchanged.
  12. The correct answer is B (Design the anchor bolts, curb, and attachment connecting the unit to the roof structure for the combined Fp and vertical force): the governing Fp represents the horizontal seismic demand that the anchorage system, including bolts, curb framing, and the connection to the roof structure, must be sized to resist in combination with the applicable vertical seismic and gravity effects.
Scenario S119

A two-story unreinforced masonry (URM) bearing wall building constructed in 1948 is undergoing seismic evaluation for a proposed change in occupancy. The site has Site Class D soil, Ss = 1.5g, and S1 = 0.6g (Fa = 1.0, Fv = 1.5), and the building is currently Risk Category II. The exterior masonry walls are connected to a flexible wood diaphragm spanning 40 ft between walls. Tributary wall weight for anchorage design is 0.8 kip per foot of wall length. The engineer is evaluating whether the existing wall-to-diaphragm anchorage is adequate.

  1. The correct answer is B (Sds = 1.00g, Sd1 = 0.60g, Seismic Design Category D): Sds = (2/3)(1.0)(1.5g) = 1.00g and Sd1 = (2/3)(1.5)(0.6g) = 0.60g. Both values place the building in Seismic Design Category D per Tables 11.6-1 and 11.6-2, since neither S1 reaches the 0.75g threshold that would trigger Category E.
  2. The correct answer is A (Plain unreinforced masonry shear walls are restricted or prohibited as the seismic force-resisting system in SDC D): TMS 402/602-16 imposes significant restrictions on plain, unreinforced masonry as a seismic force-resisting element in higher Seismic Design Categories, which is why existing URM buildings in SDC D typically require either supplemental reinforced elements or an entirely new lateral system during retrofit rather than reliance on the original unreinforced walls.
  3. The correct answer is C (Inadequate anchorage of masonry walls to flexible diaphragms, allowing out-of-plane wall collapse): the historically dominant failure mode in URM buildings is out-of-plane collapse of exterior walls due to insufficient positive anchorage connecting the walls to flexible wood diaphragms, which is why wall anchorage design is the central focus of most URM seismic retrofit programs.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ka = 1.40): Ka = 1 + Lf/100 = 1 + 40/100 = 1.40, which is less than the code-imposed upper limit of 2.0, so the calculated value governs directly without being capped.
  5. The correct answer is A (Fp = 0.448 klf): Fp = 0.4(1.00)(1.0)(1.40)(0.8 klf) = 0.448 klf. This anchorage force per foot of wall must be transferred through positive ties into the diaphragm rather than relying on the wall's out-of-plane bending capacity alone.
  6. The correct answer is D (Minimum is 0.224 klf, so 0.448 klf governs since it is larger): the minimum anchorage force is 0.2(1.40)(1.0)(0.8 klf) = 0.224 klf, which is less than the calculated value of 0.448 klf. Since the code requires anchorage design for at least the calculated force and not less than the minimum, the larger value of 0.448 klf governs.
  7. The correct answer is A (Relying on cross-grain bending of a wood ledger to resist the anchorage tension force): wood ledgers loaded in cross-grain bending have repeatedly failed in earthquakes because wood is weak perpendicular to grain, so accepted retrofit practice uses steel straps, rods, or hardware that engage the diaphragm framing directly in tension rather than depending on a ledger's cross-grain bending strength.
  8. The correct answer is A (A subdiaphragm with continuous cross ties): subdiaphragms are smaller structural units within the main diaphragm that use continuous cross ties to gather anchorage forces from individual joists and deliver them into the main diaphragm's continuous chords, completing the load path from the wall anchors to the rest of the lateral system.
  9. The correct answer is B (Collectors, or drag struts, that gather diaphragm shear into discrete shear wall segments): where the diaphragm shear generated along a wall line exceeds the capacity of a shorter shear wall segment, collector elements are required to gather and transfer the additional diaphragm shear into that segment, completing the load path from the diaphragm to the vertical lateral system.
  10. The correct answer is B (rho = 1.3, the default value unless specific redundancy conditions are demonstrated): Section 12.3.4.2 applies the default redundancy factor of 1.3 to structures in SDC D through F unless the engineer specifically demonstrates the redundancy conditions are satisfied, and a bearing wall building being evaluated for deficiencies typically does not have that demonstration readily available.
  11. The correct answer is C (Fp = 0.560 klf): Fp = 0.4(1.00)(1.25)(1.40)(0.8 klf) = 0.560 klf. Raising the Importance Factor from 1.0 to 1.25 increases the required anchorage force proportionally, illustrating why a change in occupancy that raises Risk Category directly increases the seismic retrofit demand on existing wall anchorage.
  12. The correct answer is A (The existing anchorage should be assumed inadequate for the calculated Fp demand and requires new anchors or straps designed for the governing force): original 1948-era URM construction predates modern positive anchorage requirements, so the evaluation should assume the existing connections do not meet the calculated Fp demand and specify new steel anchors or straps engineered for the governing anchorage force established in this scenario.
Scenario S120

An open steel pipe rack supporting process piping with hazardous contents (Risk Category III, Ie = 1.25) is braced with ordinary concentric bracing at three levels, 8 ft, 16 ft, and 24 ft above grade. The site has Sds = 0.65g. The total operating weight of the rack structure and supported piping is 250 kips, distributed equally among the three bracing levels. The engineer is designing the braced frame and its foundation for seismic lateral forces per ASCE 7-16 Chapter 15.

  1. The correct answer is D (Section 15.5.6, Pipe Racks): ASCE 7-16 includes a dedicated provision within Chapter 15 for pipe racks, directing the engineer to design the supporting structure as a nonbuilding structure using the response modification coefficients appropriate to its bracing type.
  2. The correct answer is D (Risk Category III, Ie = 1.25): structures containing sufficient quantities of hazardous materials to pose a substantial risk to the public if released are assigned Risk Category III per Table 1.5-1, corresponding to Ie = 1.25 rather than the ordinary Ie = 1.00 used for standard occupancies.
  3. The correct answer is B (R = 3.25): Table 15.4-2 assigns ordinary concentrically braced frames used as the lateral system for nonbuilding structures a response modification coefficient of 3.25, reflecting their more limited ductility compared to a special concentrically braced frame in a building application.
  4. The correct answer is B (Cs = 0.25): Cs = Sds/(R/Ie) = 0.65/(3.25/1.25) = 0.65/2.60 = 0.25. This value directly reflects the relatively low R value assigned to ordinary concentrically braced frames in nonbuilding applications.
  5. The correct answer is B (V = 62.5 kips): V = CsWp = 0.25(250 kips) = 62.5 kips. This total force must be distributed vertically among the three bracing levels and resisted by the braced frame and its foundation.
  6. The correct answer is A (Fx = 31.3 kips): with each level carrying 83.3 kips, Cvx for the top level is (83.3 x 24)/4,000 = 0.50, so Fx = 0.50(62.5 kips) = 31.25 kips, which rounds to 31.3 kips. This is the largest of the three level forces because the top level carries the greatest product of weight and height.
  7. The correct answer is C (M = 1,167 kip-ft): summing Fx(hx) for all three levels gives 31.25(24) + 20.83(16) + 10.42(8) = 750 + 333.3 + 83.3 = 1,166.6 kip-ft, which rounds to 1,167 kip-ft. This moment must be resisted by the braced frame columns and transferred into the foundation.
  8. The correct answer is A (A separate Chapter 13 nonstructural component check of the piping distribution system, including Fp for the pipe supports): even though the supporting rack structure is designed as a nonbuilding structure under Chapter 15, the piping itself is treated as a distribution system component under Section 13.6.8, requiring its own Fp calculation for the pipe supports and bracing along the rack.
  9. The correct answer is D (Flexible couplings or expansion loops that accommodate relative movement without overstressing the pipe): piping that crosses a location where relative seismic or thermal movement is expected needs flexible couplings or expansion loops so the pipe can accommodate that movement without developing excessive stress or losing support, rather than rigid connections that would restrain the pipe against the anticipated movement.
  10. The correct answer is B (Seismic, because it produces the larger controlling base shear in this case): with the given site conditions and hazardous piping contents producing Ie = 1.25, the calculated seismic demand of 62.5 kips exceeds the stated wind demand of 40 kips, so seismic loading governs the design of the braced frame rather than wind or a simple sum of the two independent load cases.
  11. The correct answer is C (Checking that footing uplift or net tension due to overturning is resisted by dead load or tension anchors): with a significant overturning moment relative to a lightly loaded isolated footing, the design must confirm that any net uplift or tension generated at the footing is adequately resisted by the available dead load or by tension anchors, rather than relying solely on a standard bearing pressure check.
  12. The correct answer is C (Nonbuilding structures like ordinary concentrically braced pipe racks are assigned lower R values reflecting less ductile detailing than a comparable building system): Table 15.4-2 generally assigns nonbuilding structural systems lower R values than their building-system counterparts in Table 12.2-1, reflecting typically less ductile detailing and connection requirements, which produces a comparatively higher Cs and base shear for the same Sds and weight.

Practice Test 25 Answer Key

Scenario S121

A fire protection sprinkler main is routed along the roof level of a four-story hospital building, 48 ft above grade (Risk Category IV). The tributary weight of the piping segment being anchored is 3 kips, and the site has Sds = 0.60g. Per ASCE 7-16 Table 13.6-1, fire protection sprinkler piping has ap = 2.5 and Rp = 4.5, and as a designated seismic system in a Risk Category IV facility the component importance factor is Ip = 1.5. The engineer is determining the seismic design force for the piping's lateral bracing.

  1. The correct answer is D (It is a designated seismic system required to function in a Risk Category IV facility): ASCE 7-16 Section 13.1.3 assigns Ip = 1.5 to designated seismic systems, including life-safety fire suppression piping, that are required to remain functional after an earthquake in a Risk Category IV structure such as a hospital, distinct from the building's own structural Importance Factor.
  2. The correct answer is B (z/h = 1.0): since the piping is attached at the roof, the highest point of the structure, the attachment elevation z equals the total structure height h, producing a ratio of 1.0 and the maximum height amplification in the Fp equation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Fp = 1.8 kips): Fp = 0.4(ap)(Sds)(Wp)/(Rp/Ip) x (1 + 2z/h) = 0.4(2.5)(0.60)(3)/(4.5/1.5) x (1 + 2(1.0)) = 0.6/3.0 x 3 = 0.6 x 3 = 1.8 kips. This base result must still be checked against the code's maximum and minimum limits.
  4. The correct answer is D (Fp,max = 4.32 kips): Fp,max = 1.6(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 1.6(0.60)(1.5)(3) = 4.32 kips. This upper bound caps the force regardless of the height ratio and the dynamic amplification computed in the base Fp equation.
  5. The correct answer is A (Fp,min = 0.81 kips): Fp,min = 0.3(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 0.3(0.60)(1.5)(3) = 0.81 kips. This floor value ensures a minimum design force even for components at a lower elevation or with more favorable ap and Rp values.
  6. The correct answer is B (1.8 kips, since it falls between the minimum and maximum limits): because the calculated value of 1.8 kips lies between the minimum of 0.81 kips and the maximum of 4.32 kips, no capping applies and 1.8 kips is used directly as the governing design force for the piping bracing.
  7. The correct answer is D (Flexible couplings that permit relative movement without overstressing the pipe): piping crossing a building seismic joint or connecting to equipment with different dynamic characteristics requires flexible couplings that accommodate the anticipated relative movement, preventing the pipe from being overstressed by restraint against that movement.
  8. The correct answer is C (Distributed among the individual lateral bracing points along the pipe run according to their tributary length): the total Fp calculated for a tributary segment of piping represents the overall seismic demand for that segment, which is then distributed among the discrete lateral bracing points spaced along the pipe run according to each brace's tributary length.
  9. The correct answer is D (It is not exempt because designated seismic systems in Risk Category IV facilities do not qualify for the general small-component exemptions): Section 13.1.3 specifically excludes designated seismic systems serving Risk Category IV structures from the general exemptions otherwise available to small or low-demand components, requiring positive seismic bracing regardless of pipe size.
  10. The correct answer is D (±0.36 kips): the vertical seismic effect follows 0.2(Sds)(Wp) = 0.2(0.60)(3) = 0.36 kips, applied as an addition to or subtraction from the gravity load in the governing combined load check for the piping supports.
  11. The correct answer is A (Ip applies specifically to the nonstructural component's seismic system classification and may differ from the building's overall structural Ie): the component importance factor Ip is determined independently based on the component's own designated seismic system status and function, per Section 13.1.3, and is not automatically the same numerical value as the building's structural Importance Factor Ie used in the Chapter 12 base shear calculation.
  12. The correct answer is A (The elevated Ip value of 1.5 for the designated life-safety system in a Risk Category IV facility increases the required Fp compared to a standard Ip of 1.0): because Fp is directly proportional to Ip in the numerator and inversely proportional through the Rp/Ip term, raising Ip from 1.0 to 1.5 for this designated seismic system increases the resulting design force well above what an otherwise identical piping run in an ordinary office building would require.
Scenario S122

An existing six-story reinforced concrete building has a ground-floor lobby with widely spaced columns. Above the lobby, a special reinforced concrete shear wall is offset out-of-plane from the wall configuration below, creating a transfer diaphragm at the second floor. The building is Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D, with Sds = 0.70g and Sd1 = 0.40g. The shear wall immediately above the transfer level carries a design story shear of 600 kips, and the transfer diaphragm accumulates 400 kips of collected shear into one collector element.

  1. The correct answer is D (Out-of-plane offset irregularity (Type 4 plan irregularity)): Table 12.3-1 defines this condition as occurring when a discontinuity exists in a lateral force resistance path, such as an out-of-plane offset of a vertical resisting element, which precisely matches the shear wall configuration described above the transfer diaphragm in this building.
  2. The correct answer is B (In-plane discontinuity in vertical lateral force-resisting element, Type 4): an offset shear wall carried through a transfer diaphragm triggers two separate classifications at once, a Table 12.3-1 Type 4 plan irregularity (out-of-plane offset, viewed in the horizontal plan) and a Table 12.3-2 Type 4 vertical irregularity (in-plane discontinuity, an offset of the element within the building's plan greater than the element's own length, viewed story to story), since both tables describe the identical physical condition of a wall whose load path is discontinuous between stories and must be picked up by the diaphragm below.
  3. The correct answer is D (The supporting elements must be designed using the special seismic load combinations that include the system overstrength factor Omega0): Section 12.3.3.3 requires that columns, beams, and other elements supporting a discontinuous shear wall or braced frame be designed for the special load combinations incorporating the overstrength factor, recognizing the concentrated and potentially amplified demand these elements must carry.
  4. The correct answer is D (Omega0 = 2.5): Table 12.2-1 assigns special reinforced concrete shear walls an overstrength factor of 2.5, reflecting the expected margin between the design force level and the actual force the wall can develop before yielding, which the transfer elements must be designed to resist.
  5. The correct answer is C (F = 1,500 kips): the amplified transfer force equals Omega0 times the design story shear, F = 2.5(600 kips) = 1,500 kips. This amplified demand reflects the intent of the overstrength requirement to protect the critical transfer elements from behaving as the weak link in the load path.
  6. The correct answer is C (It is not prohibited for this Risk Category and SDC, but it does require the special overstrength design provisions): Section 12.3.3.2 restricts certain extreme irregularities primarily for Risk Category IV structures or SDC E and F, but for this Risk Category II, SDC D building, the Type 4 irregularity is permitted provided the supporting elements are designed using the special overstrength load combinations discussed previously.
  7. The correct answer is B (The equivalent lateral force procedure is permitted without restriction): Table 12.6-1 only prohibits the equivalent lateral force procedure above the two-story/30 ft height threshold for structures with a vertical irregularity of Type 1a, 1b, 2, or 3 of Table 12.3-2, or a horizontal irregularity of Type 1a or 1b of Table 12.3-1. A Type 4 vertical irregularity is not on that restricted list, so this six-story building with only a Type 4 discontinuity keeps the equivalent lateral force procedure available and instead must satisfy the Omega0 overstrength design requirements of Section 12.3.3.3, consistent with Q1458's own conclusion in this same cluster.
  8. The correct answer is A (rho = 1.3, the default value, since a transfer configuration is unlikely to satisfy the specific redundancy exception conditions): because the redundancy exception in Section 12.3.4.2 requires demonstrating that each story resists a minimum percentage of shear without an extreme torsional irregularity resulting from element loss, a building with a transfer discontinuity is unlikely to satisfy this demonstration, so the default value of 1.3 typically applies.
  9. The correct answer is B (The overstrength factor Omega0): Section 12.10.2.1 requires collector elements, their splices, and their connections to supporting vertical elements in SDC C through F to be designed for the special seismic load combinations that include the system overstrength factor Omega0, recognizing collectors as critical load path elements similar to transfer elements.
  10. The correct answer is C (F = 1,000 kips): the amplified collector force equals Omega0 times the collected diaphragm shear, F = 2.5(400 kips) = 1,000 kips. This amplified force must be developed through the collector element and its connections into the supporting shear wall or column.
  11. The correct answer is C (The existing transfer diaphragm and its supporting columns, given the significantly amplified overstrength-level design forces): because the amplified transfer force of 1,500 kips and collector force of 1,000 kips substantially exceed the original code-level design forces the existing diaphragm and columns were likely designed for, these elements are the most probable candidates requiring strengthening during the seismic retrofit.
  12. The correct answer is B (Add new lateral force-resisting elements that are continuous from the roof to the foundation, eliminating the reliance on the transfer diaphragm): rather than strengthening the existing discontinuous configuration for amplified overstrength forces, introducing new vertically continuous lateral elements removes the underlying vertical irregularity altogether, providing a more robust and code-conforming load path than relying on a strengthened transfer condition.
Scenario S123

A structural engineer is hand-checking the results of a three-dimensional computer model built for a three-story steel special moment frame office building, Risk Category II, Seismic Design Category D. The site has Sds = 0.75g and Sd1 = 0.45g, the roof is 36 ft above grade, and the total seismic weight is 3,200 kips (R = 8, Ie = 1.0). The computer model reports a total seismic base shear of 385 kips and a Rayleigh-method fundamental period of 0.90 seconds.

  1. The correct answer is D (Ta = 0.49 seconds): Ta = Ct(hn)^x = 0.028(36)^0.8. Computing 36 raised to the 0.8 power gives approximately 17.6, so Ta = 0.028(17.6) = 0.492 seconds, which rounds to 0.49 seconds. This is the code-prescribed baseline period used for the hand-check ELF calculation.
  2. The correct answer is C (k = 1.0): per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.3, the distribution exponent k equals 1.0 for structures with a period of 0.5 seconds or less, applies as a linear interpolation between 1.0 and 2.0 for periods between 0.5 and 2.5 seconds, and equals 2.0 for periods of 2.5 seconds or more. Since Ta = 0.49 seconds falls at or below the 0.5 second threshold, k = 1.0 governs.
  3. The correct answer is A (Cs = 0.09375): Cs = Sds/(R/Ie) = 0.75/(8/1.0) = 0.09375. This base value must still be checked against Cs,max, computed from Sd1, before it is confirmed as governing the hand-check calculation.
  4. The correct answer is D (V = 300 kips): V = CsW = 0.09375(3,200 kips) = 300 kips. This is the value the hand-check calculation predicts as the total design base shear using standard code parameters.
  5. The correct answer is D (The values differ by about 28 percent, which is large enough to flag a likely modeling error requiring investigation): 385 kips divided by 300 kips equals approximately 1.283, or about 28 percent higher than the hand-check value. A discrepancy of this magnitude exceeds the tolerance normally expected between a properly configured model and a code hand-check, so it should prompt the engineer to review the model's input weight, R value, or Cs parameters for an error.
  6. The correct answer is B (Cu = 1.4): per ASCE 7-16 Table 12.8-1, a coefficient Cu of 1.4 applies when Sd1 is 0.4g or greater, which is the case here with Sd1 = 0.45g. This coefficient establishes the maximum multiple of Ta permitted for the code-recognized fundamental period.
  7. The correct answer is C (CuTa = 0.69 seconds): CuTa = 1.4(0.49 seconds) = 0.686 seconds, which rounds to 0.69 seconds. This value represents the maximum period the engineer is permitted to use for computing Cs when a computed (Rayleigh) period is available.
  8. The correct answer is B (0.69 seconds, the capped value of CuTa, since the computed period exceeds this upper limit): ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.2 permits use of a computed period in lieu of Ta, but only up to the limit of CuTa. Since the model's Rayleigh period of 0.90 seconds exceeds the 0.69 second cap, the engineer must use 0.69 seconds rather than the full computed value, preventing an artificially low base shear from an uncapped long period.
  9. The correct answer is C (Enough modes must be included to capture at least 90 percent of the actual mass participation in each orthogonal horizontal direction): Section 12.9.1.1 requires the analysis to include a sufficient number of modes to obtain a combined modal mass participation of at least 90 percent of the actual structural mass in each of the two orthogonal horizontal directions being analyzed, which the engineer should verify was satisfied in the model.
  10. The correct answer is C (Whether a 5 percent accidental mass eccentricity was applied at each level per ASCE 7-16 Section 12.8.4.2): accidental torsion is modeled by displacing the center of mass at each level by 5 percent of the building dimension perpendicular to the applied seismic force, and this input is a commonly overlooked model setting that must be specifically confirmed during a hand-check review.
  11. The correct answer is A (The 100 percent-30 percent orthogonal combination rule per Section 12.5.3): structures in SDC D through F with certain plan irregularities, including nonparallel lateral force-resisting systems, are required to apply 100 percent of the seismic force in one direction combined with 30 percent in the orthogonal direction, and the engineer should confirm the model applied this combination correctly rather than analyzing each direction independently.
  12. The correct answer is B (The modal analysis results, including drifts and member forces, must be scaled up to meet the required minimum base shear): Section 12.9.1.4 requires that when the modal response spectrum base shear falls below the specified percentage of the ELF base shear, the forces (and in some cases drifts) from the modal analysis must be scaled upward to satisfy that minimum, ensuring the more detailed analysis does not understate the code-intended design demand.
Scenario S124

A single-story elementary school building with an occupant load exceeding 250 (Risk Category III) has a suspended acoustical tile ceiling at the underside of the roof, 16 ft above the floor. The site has Sds = 0.55g. The ceiling system weighs 3 psf, with ap = 1.0 and Rp = 2.5 per ASCE 7-16 Table 13.5-1, and the ceiling component importance factor is Ip = 1.0. The engineer is determining the seismic bracing force for the ceiling grid.

  1. The correct answer is A (Risk Category III): Table 1.5-1 assigns Risk Category III to elementary and secondary school facilities with an occupant load greater than 250, reflecting the substantial number of occupants at risk compared to an ordinary Risk Category II occupancy.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ie = 1.25 for the structure, while Ip = 1.0 for this ordinary, non-designated ceiling component): the structural Importance Factor Ie for a Risk Category III building is 1.25 per Table 1.5-2, but the component importance factor Ip is determined separately per Section 13.1.3, and since this suspended ceiling is not a designated seismic system, Ip remains 1.0 regardless of the building's overall Risk Category.
  3. The correct answer is A (z/h = 1.0): because the ceiling is attached at the roof level, which is the highest point of this single-story structure, the attachment elevation z equals the full structure height h, giving a ratio of 1.0 and the maximum height amplification in the Fp equation.
  4. The correct answer is A (Fp = 0.79 psf): Fp = 0.4(ap)(Sds)(Wp)/(Rp/Ip) x (1 + 2z/h) = 0.4(1.0)(0.55)(3)/(2.5/1.0) x (1 + 2(1.0)) = 0.264 x 3 = 0.792 psf, which rounds to 0.79 psf. This base value must still be checked against the code's maximum and minimum bounds.
  5. The correct answer is A (Fp,max = 2.64 psf): Fp,max = 1.6(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 1.6(0.55)(1.0)(3) = 2.64 psf. This upper bound applies regardless of the height ratio and dynamic amplification calculated in the base Fp equation.
  6. The correct answer is B (Fp,min = 0.50 psf): Fp,min = 0.3(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 0.3(0.55)(1.0)(3) = 0.495 psf, which rounds to 0.50 psf. This lower bound ensures a minimum design force is applied even for a component with a favorable elevation ratio or ap and Rp combination.
  7. The correct answer is A (0.79 psf, since it falls between the minimum and maximum limits): because the calculated base value of 0.79 psf lies between the minimum of approximately 0.50 psf and the maximum of 2.64 psf, no capping is required and the base equation result governs the ceiling bracing design directly.
  8. The correct answer is A (No, because that particular small-area exemption pathway applies specifically to Risk Category I or II conditions, which does not describe this Risk Category III school): certain limited nonstructural exemptions in ASCE 7-16 are tied to Risk Category I or II conditions, so a Risk Category III school does not qualify for that specific small-area ceiling exemption even if an individual space were small, requiring the ceiling bracing to be designed for the calculated Fp.
  9. The correct answer is B (Adequate perimeter clearance or a seismic separation joint between the ceiling grid and the wall): suspended ceiling systems require sufficient clearance at the perimeter walls, consistent with recognized ceiling seismic bracing standards referenced through Chapter 13, to accommodate relative movement between the ceiling grid and the surrounding structure without causing damage or grid displacement.
  10. The correct answer is B (Diagonal splay wires or rigid compression struts at specified spacing connecting the grid to the structure above): standard ceiling seismic bracing consists of diagonal splay wires or rigid compression struts installed at a specified grid spacing to transfer the calculated lateral force Fp from the suspended grid into the structure above, supplementing the vertical support wires that carry gravity load alone.
  11. The correct answer is C (Light fixtures independently supported require their own separate component check and are not automatically included in the ceiling grid's Wp used above): light fixtures that are independently supported from the structure above, rather than relying on the ceiling grid itself, are treated as separate nonstructural components requiring their own Fp calculation and are not folded into the ceiling system's own tributary weight Wp used in this scenario.
  12. The correct answer is C (The ceiling grid must be braced for the governing Fp of 0.79 psf using perimeter clearance and diagonal bracing consistent with recognized ceiling seismic bracing practice): the governing Chapter 13 design force of 0.79 psf establishes the demand that the ceiling grid's diagonal bracing, compression struts, and perimeter clearance detailing must be designed to accommodate, rather than assuming any blanket exemption based on Ip, occupancy, or building height alone.
Scenario S125

A new 45-kip steel equipment tank is being mounted on the roof of an existing four-story building, 48 ft above grade. The building's roof level alone carries 250 kips of seismic weight, and the site has Sds = 0.68g. The tank has ap = 2.5, Rp = 2.5, and Ip = 1.0. The engineer must first determine which ASCE 7-16 procedure applies to a nonbuilding structure supported on top of another structure, then compute the resulting seismic design force.

  1. The correct answer is C (Section 15.3.2, nonbuilding structures supported by other structures): ASCE 7-16 Section 15.3.2 specifically addresses nonbuilding structures that are supported on top of another structure, such as a tank mounted on a building roof, establishing the weight-ratio criterion used to determine whether Chapter 13 component procedures or a combined Chapter 15 dynamic analysis is required.
  2. The correct answer is A (62.5 kips): the 25 percent weight threshold equals 0.25 times the roof's seismic weight, 0.25(250 kips) = 62.5 kips. This threshold is compared against the tank's own weight to determine whether the simplified Chapter 13 component approach or a combined Chapter 15 dynamic analysis is required.
  3. The correct answer is C (The Chapter 13 nonstructural component procedure, since the tank weight is less than the 25 percent threshold): because the tank's 45 kip weight is less than the calculated 62.5 kip threshold, Section 15.3.2 permits treating the tank using the Chapter 13 nonstructural component procedure rather than requiring a combined dynamic model of the tank and supporting building.
  4. The correct answer is A (z/h = 1.0): since the tank is attached at the roof, the highest point of the building, the attachment elevation z equals the total building height h, producing a ratio of 1.0 and the maximum height amplification permitted in the Fp equation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Fp = 36.7 kips): Fp = 0.4(ap)(Sds)(Wp)/(Rp/Ip) x (1 + 2z/h) = 0.4(2.5)(0.68)(45)/(2.5/1.0) x (1 + 2(1.0)) = 12.24 x 3 = 36.72 kips, which rounds to 36.7 kips. This base value must still be checked against the code's maximum and minimum bounds.
  6. The correct answer is D (Fp,max = 48.96 kips): Fp,max = 1.6(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 1.6(0.68)(1.0)(45) = 48.96 kips. This upper bound caps the design force regardless of the height ratio and dynamic amplification calculated in the base equation.
  7. The correct answer is A (Fp,min = 9.18 kips): Fp,min = 0.3(Sds)(Ip)(Wp) = 0.3(0.68)(1.0)(45) = 9.18 kips. This lower bound ensures a minimum design force even for components with a favorable elevation ratio or ap and Rp combination.
  8. The correct answer is C (36.7 kips, since it falls between the minimum and maximum limits): because the calculated base value of 36.7 kips lies between the minimum of 9.18 kips and the maximum of 48.96 kips, no capping is required and the base equation result of 36.7 kips governs the tank's anchorage design.
  9. The correct answer is B (The local roof framing and diaphragm connections that transfer the tank's Fp force into the building's seismic force-resisting system): the calculated Fp represents the force that must be delivered from the tank's anchorage through the local roof framing and diaphragm into the building's overall lateral system, so this load path must be specifically checked in addition to the tank's own anchor design.
  10. The correct answer is A (The tank's operating weight must be added to the building's overall seismic weight W for the story below the roof): because the tank is now permanently supported by the building, its operating weight becomes part of the effective seismic weight the building's own lateral system must resist, and it must be included in the building-level ELF base shear calculation for the level below the roof and downward.
  11. The correct answer is C (A combined dynamic model treating the tank and supporting building as a single interacting system per Section 15.3.2 would be required instead of the simpler Chapter 13 component approach): once a rooftop nonbuilding structure's weight reaches or exceeds 25 percent of the supporting structure's weight at that level, Section 15.3.2 requires a combined analysis capturing the dynamic interaction between the tank and the building rather than treating the tank as an independent Chapter 13 component.
  12. The correct answer is C (A relatively light rooftop item has minimal dynamic interaction with the supporting structure and can be reasonably treated as an independent component, while a heavy one meaningfully alters the combined system's dynamic response): the 25 percent weight threshold in Section 15.3.2 reflects the practical engineering judgment that a comparatively light nonbuilding structure does not significantly change the dynamic behavior of its supporting structure and can be analyzed independently using Chapter 13, whereas a heavier one requires an integrated model to properly capture their combined dynamic response.
